PREFACE 

Owing to the complexity of hospital work and the everincreasing scope of the nurse's responsibility, in the last few years the feeling has been growing that the curriculum should be broadened and that the hospital should be a school for the teaching of nursing as well as a place for the care of the sick.

In order to carry on her work intelligently, it is necessary for the nurse to have a knowledge of the sciences underlying the various hospital procedures. Since the study of elementary chemistry makes the work more understandable, and therefore more interesting, it seems wise to give this course early in the preliminary period as a foundation for other sciences. This helps the student of physiology and materia medica to understand the processes of digestion, the action of drugs as medicines, antidotes and disinfectants, and the reasons for certain diets in metabolic disturbances, while a new meaning will be given to various procedures included in hospital housekeeping such as cleaning, laundering and the removal of stains.

As the title page suggests, the book has been written primarily for student nurses. It is hoped, however, that it will find a wider usefulness and to this end the illustrative material and questions have been chosen, not only from the experience of nurses, but from that of other students who have an interest in the household processes of a home or an institution. 

We wish to thank Dr. Grace MacLeod of the Nutrition 

Department, Teachers College, Columbia University, for

THE  AND THEIR APPLICATION A TEXT-BOOK FOR NURSES

THE  AND THEIR APPLICATION CHAPTER I INTRODUCTION Chemistry-What It Is.-The study of materials, what they are made of, their relation to each other and their behavior, is known as the science of chemistry. The earliest interest men had in materials was due to their belief that other things could be turned into gold. These early alchemists, as they were called, expended all their efforts in unsuccessful attempts to obtain gold in this way. For this reason they delayed for many years the important discoveries that came later through other kinds of experiments and out of which the subject of chemistry, as we know it to-day, has sprung.

The Work of the Chemist.-The work of the chemist consists in finding out the nature or composition of both familiar and unfamiliar materials and their uses. Many times something strange and unknown is made as the result of experiments and, later on, the same material is found to occur naturally. Again, it is frequently found that a material can be made artificially that was first known through its occurrence in nature. For example ultramarine, used commonly as a pigment and for bluing, was first known as lapis lazuli, a semi-precious blue stone valued for its color. The chemist must not only learn the composition of a material but he must also find its relation to other materials and the way it behaves under different conditions. 1

The Value of Chemistry.-The chief value of chemistry comes not so much in the importance of this vast fund of knowledge for its own sake as for the many applications made of it. There is no process, industrial, household, or physiological, that is without close connection with chemistry. The improvement of the soap making industry has been made possible only through the work of the chemist. 

Better soap could be made because he found ways of purifying the materials necessary for the process and he discovered the correct amounts to use. The development of ways of treating oils to improve their consistency or odor increased the amounts and varieties of fatty materials available for use and so lowered the cost of soap. various activities going on within every household have an equally close relation to chemistry. Baking soda and sour milk are frequently used together-sometimes with good results-but often the biscuit or cake has an unexpected and disagreeable color, odor and taste. A knowledge of chemistry explains the condition necessary for success and the reason for the failure. Again, when cleaning marble or porcelain surfaces, one is directed not to use acid. The importance of this precaution is very much better understood and more often heeded if the cleaner knows a little chemistry. (See Chapter IX.) 

The The science of nutrition has been founded on the knowledge gained from the study of the composition of foods and from the study of the body's requirements for and use of food.

Its tremendous stride in the past years has paralleled the development of food and physiological chemistry.

The increasing knowledge of the causes of disease, which has made preventive medicine possible, has come through the study of chemistry as well as bacteriology and the other biological sciences. These sciences have also contributed to the knowledge of results caused by disease and, with the

INTRODUCTION 

increasing information about metabolic disturbances, treatment through a diet as well as by drugs has been furthered. 

The Value of Chemistry to a Nurse.-The study of chemistry often seems very remote in its usefulness to a nurse, especially when she first begins training. There are many ways, however, in which such knowledge will prove helpful. It enables her to study physiology and nutrition. more intelligently. For example, it helps her to understand why starch must be digested before the body can use it and what happens to it during digestion which makes it possible for the body to use it. Again, the fact that it is a fuel food and requires oxygen before its function can be fulfilled cannot really be understood without some knowledge of chemistry. It helps to make clear the means by which the body takes care of waste products and protects itself against toxic materials. The formation of indican is an excellent example of this protective ability possessed by the body and it seems the more marvelous when it is realized that out of two substances, indole and sulphuric acid, each of which, if left alone, can cause great injury to the body, there is made a substance, indican, which the body then proceeds to eliminate. The clotting ability of the blood and the function of calcium compounds or thrombokinase to accomplish it are understood much better. 

Clinical tests, such as blood sugar tests, a urine analysis for albumen, or a gastric analysis for acidity take on real meanings. The subject of materia medica becomes more than a matter of memory for many of the names of the various drugs have become familiar through the study of chemistry. The uses of certain antidotes, as starch for iodine poisoning, or milk of lime for acid poisoning are seen to have a definite chemical significance. Hospital procedures are carried out much more intelligently. 

Disinfectants, as Dakin's solution, are used and cared for with more understanding. Institutional housekeeping

becomes more efficient as explanations are found for following very definite directions. The reason why sugar should not be put into fruit until the cooking is nearly if not entirely completed is learned. What happens when soap is used with hard water and why "softening the water" helps to prevent this result is explained. 

But knowledge is not the only benefit to be obtained through the study of chemistry. Through the performance of experiments a technic is acquired which is valuable in many ways. It increases one's ability to follow directions. It makes for a greater accuracy in the observation of results and in the records made of them. It develops an ability to interpret observations that have been made. 

Last but not least, with the performance of experiments, there come a nervous and a muscular control and steadiness which are most essential. These are all qualities which enhance one's ability to carry on any profession, whatever it may be. For nurses they are particularly valuable.

CHAPTER II  

Substances. On all sides we are surrounded by different forms of matter or materials, as we commonly call them. 

Chemists usually speak of these materials as substances, the term being applied to anything which occupies space or takes up room. We realize, at once, the many different varieties that exist. Among those familiar to a nurse there are the different materials out of which hospital equipment is made, as glass, nickel, iron and the mercury in thermometers. In bedding or bandaging materials we have wool, cotton, linen and silk. Among the different substances in food materials are sugar, starch, proteins and fat. There are the many disinfectants, for example, iodine and Dakin's solution; medicines, such as Epsom salt; cleansing agents, like soap and water. Because of the great number of substances that either occur naturally or can be made, it has been found more convenient to gather them together in a few groups. 

It is possible to narrow these substances down to three groups and to classify them as elements, compounds or mixtures.

Elements. Consider the iron which forms so large a part of hospital appliances. In it we have a substance which, so far as we now know, is composed of one kind of material only. Therefore it can neither be separated at will into simpler substances nor can it be changed into another substance without the addition of some other material. It is in its simplest form, or an elementary 5

substance, and so it has been called an element. The number of elements known to-day is comparatively small, there being ninety-two now known. Others formerly were included in the list but have since been found to contain more than one kind of material. It may be that future. experiments will make it necessary to exclude some we now believe to be elements. For that reason we say "so far as we now know each one in the present list is composed of only one kind of material." While one of the elements, radium, cannot be separated at will it undergoes a spontaneous and continuous disintegration into other kinds of particles, its radio-activity depending on this disintegration. 

We, therefore, include the phrase "at will," when defining an element as something which cannot be split into simpler substances. Of this list of elements only two, mercury and bromine, are liquids. About eleven are gases, the more familiar ones being oxygen, hydrogen, nitrogen and chlorine. The remainder are solids. Many of them are very rare, only about twenty occurring commonly either free or in combination with other substances. There are about fifteen elements entering into the composition of the body. 

They are as follows: For an adult 5 ft. 8 in. tall and weighing 154 lbs. the body weight is the sum of the weights represented below. The total value of these body constituents is $18. Oxygen. 100.10 lbs. Carbon. 27.72 66 65% 18% 66 Hydrogen. 15.4 10% Nitrogen. 4.62 66 3% Sulphur. 0.385

Most elements have been named for some definite reason. 

Iodine, for example, means violet and this name has been given because, when heated, a violet vapor is formed. Carbon comes from carbo, a Latin word meaning coal of which it forms a large part. Bromine comes from a Greek word meaning stench and, because of the disagreeable odor, the name is very appropriate. On looking up the derivation of each name it will be found that in most, if not all, cases it has a very definite significance. 

On the basis of their characteristics the elements have been classified either as metals or non-metals. With metals we associate a luster, especially when polished. They are able, also, as a rule, to conduct heat and electricity well. 

The majority of solid elements possess these characteristics. 

Among them are iron, tin, zinc, silver, gold, copper, platinum, sodium, potassium, calcium, radium, arsenic, lead, nickel and aluminium, more commonly called aluminum. 

The gas, hydrogen, although it neither has a luster nor conducts heat or electricity, behaves in many other ways like a metal and so is included in the list. Mercury, one of the two liquid elements, is also a metal. The non-metals do not possess these characteristics. Among them are the solids carbon, sulphur, phosphorus and iodine. With the exception of hydrogen the gaseous elements, as chlorine, nitrogen and oxygen are non-metallic, likewise bromine, the other liquid element. The same element, however, can show both metallic and non-metallic characteristics with one or the other usually predominating. For example, carbon shows non-metallic properties when in combination with hydrogen and metallic properties when in combination with oxygen.

Compounds. In contrast with the elements there is another kind of substance called a "compound" substance or a compound. Ordinary table salt is an example of such a substance. Unlike an element it is composed of more than one kind of substance, each being an element and called

the constituents of the compound. For example, the elements sodium and chlorine are the constituents of sodium chloride, which is the chemical name for table salt. As a compound is composed of two or more elements, it can be separated into simpler substances or its constituents, but never by mechanical means. This is another way in which elements and compounds differ from each other. Another characteristic of compounds is that the elements of which the compound is composed no longer possess the characteristic they would have if each were by itself. For example, the element sodium is a solid, soft enough to be easily cut and having a silvery luster. It is dangerous to handle and causes a burn that is very bad and difficult to treat. 

Chlorine, the other constituent of sodium chloride, is a greenish yellow gas with a disagreeable odor and if inhaled in large amounts is poisonous. In fact, it was one of the first of the "poison gases" to be used during the World War.

In sodium chloride, or "table salt," we have these two elements but neither retains its own charactertistic. The identity of each has been lost and we have a substance which neither burns nor is a poison. It is a white, crystalline solid with a salty taste, is one of the important substances in the body and is constantly being ingested with food. Still another characteristic of a compound is that the constituents or elements, of which it is composed, are always present in a constant proportion. Sodium chloride is not only composed of sodium and chlorine, but there is always a certain definite amount of each, approximately 

1 part by weight of sodium and 1.54 parts by weight of chlorine. The actual number of grams may vary but the proportion is always the same. This fact will be referred to later as the law of definite proportion. To emphasize the different characteristics of a compound, suppose we illustrate with another very familiar substance, water Water is composed of two elements, hydrogen and oxygen,

and it can be separated into its two constituents, though not by mechanical means. Both hydrogen and oxygen are colorless, tasteless, odorless gases and yet in water we have a liquid, which if pure is still colorless, tasteless and odorless. In other words, the identity of each of the constituents has been lost when the compound, water, has been formed. Again, in forming water from hydrogen and oxygen, a certain definite weight of each is necessary, 1 part by weight of hydrogen and 8 parts by weight of oxygen. 

If water is split into its constituents, hydrogen and oxygen are always obtained in the proportion of 1 part by weight of the former and 8 parts by weight of the later. In other words, the composition of the compound never varies, either in the kinds of constituents composing it or in the proportion by weight in which each occurs. 

The number of compounds is very great. There are not only many possibilities of combinations among the ninety or more elements but often the same elements can be constituents of more than one compound. Both water and hydrogen peroxide are composed of the same elements, hydrogen and oxygen. Carbon and oxygen are constituents of carbon monoxide and also carbon dioxide. Both calomel and bichloride of mercury or "corrosive sublimate" contain mercury and chlorine. In each of these cases the compounds are very different in their characteristics and serve different purposes. Take, for example, water and hydrogen peroxide.

In the former we have a tasteless liquid which gives up its oxygen under extreme conditions only. The latter substance is a liquid with a disagreeable metallic taste, giving up some of its oxygen with great readiness and its uses being dependent upon this fact. Carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide are very unlike each other. While both are gases, the former is a deadly poison and will burn, the blue flame above a coal fire being burning carbon monoxide or "coal gas." The latter is non-poisonous, and not only will it not burn but

it will not permit other materials to burn and so is used in certain types of fire extinguishers. It cannot be the kind of constituents which accounts for the difference in characteristics for they are the same in both cases. While both water and hydrogen peroxide contain hydrogen and oxygen, the proportions by weight are as follows: Water 

Hydrogen peroxide Hydrogen 

1 part by weight 

1 part by weight

Oxygen 

8 parts by weight 

16 parts by weight

The difference in the proportion results in a difference in characteristics even though the constituents are the same in both cases. The same is true of carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide, also, of calomel and corrosive sublimate. 

Compounds are sometimes named for very definite reasons and the names may reveal the constituents present in them. At this point we shall make note of only two general rules for naming. The ending, "ide," implies that no elements are present in the compound except those mentioned in the name. The following are examples:

Sodium chloride (table salt) contains sodium and chlorine (suggested in the second part of the name).

Calcium oxide (quicklime) contains calcium and oxygen. 

Aluminium chloride contains aluminium and chlorine.

If water were named in the same way it might be called hydrogen oxide. 

There are two other endings, "ite" and "ate" which also have very definite meanings. In both cases they imply that the compound contains oxygen as well as the elements mentioned in the name. Compounds having the "ite" ending have less oxygen in proportion to the other elements present than have the compounds whose names end in “ate.”

For the present, however, we shall remember that both signify the presence of oxygen. The following are examples:

Potassium nitrite contains potassium, nitrogen (suggested in the second part of the name) and oxygen.

Magnesium sulphate (Epsom salt) contains magnesium, sulphur (suggested in the second part of the name) and oxygen. 

Sodium carbonate (washing soda) contains sodium, carbon and oxygen. 

Silver nitrate contains silver, nitrogen and oxygen. 

Calcium sulphate (plaster of Paris) contains calcium, sulphur and oxygen.

The name of a compound can also give information about its composition through a prefix or through some ending other than those previously mentioned.

Mixtures. The third type of substance is one called a mixture. As the name implies, it contains more than one kind of material, in this case each being called a component. 

In this respect it is like a compound and unlike an element. 

There are, however, a number of ways in which a mixture is unlike a compound. To bring out the difference more clearly suppose we consider butter and sugar creamed together as a typical example. Each component in the mixture of butter and sugar still retains its own identity. 

We are aware of the greasiness, color and taste of the butter. 

The sugar, especially if granulated, gives a characteristic texture to the mixture because of its crystalline form and it can also be detected by its sweetness. In this respect a mixture is very different from a compound for the constituents of the latter lose their own characteristics and can no longer be recognized readily. Again, the components of a mixture can be put together in any proportion and this is not true of a compound. Butter and sugar can be mixed in any desired proportion. In many cases the components of a mixture can be separated from each other by mechanical means. This is not so easily illustrated with butter and sugar as with sugar and sand. In this mixture we have two substances, one of which is soluble in water and the other insoluble. To separate them, water can be added, the sugar dissolving in it and the sand settling to the bottom.

The liquid containing the sugar can then be poured off carefully from the sand. Finally the liquid can be boiled down to recover the sugar, as in making candy. While it is not always possible to separate the components of a mixture by mechanical means, the constituents of a compound can never be separated in this way. Still another distinction. between mixtures and compounds is that in the latter the constituents are always elements. In a mixture, the components may be either elements, compounds or even other mixtures. Air is a mixture of gases some of which, as oxygen and nitrogen, are elements and some of which, as carbon dioxide and water vapor, are compounds. Milk is a mixture containing different compounds, among them being fat, sugar, casein, vitamines, water and other compounds which, for the present, we shall call ash constituents. Egg nog is a mixture made up in part of other mixtures, milk and eggs. 

Physical Changes. Substances do not always remain the same under all conditions. They can undergo different kinds of changes which, for convenience, are classified into two general types, physical changes and chemical changes. 

Let us first consider the physical changes, taking the transformation of water into either steam or ice as an example.

In this type of change the form of the substance may become different. When water boils, steam, an invisible gas, is formed. What people ordinarily call steam is really the condensed liquid itself in very tiny drops. When water freezes we no longer have a liquid but in its place there is a solid substance, ice. Whether water is in the form of the invisible gas, steam, or the liquid, or the cake of ice, its composition is the same. It is a compound composed of 1 part by weight of hydrogen and 8 parts by weight of oxygen. This is another characteristic of a physical change, namely, that the composition of the substance is not affected by the change. Still another characteristic is that the original form can sometimes be restored by bringing back the

original conditions. Water vapor or steam can be made liquid again if the gas is cooled. This is what happens to the water vapor in the air when it rains, or to the steam coming frorn the tea kettle when it forms the cloud usually called steam. The cake of ice will melt into the liquid, water, if the temperature is raised. It is not always possible to restore a substance to its original form. Cutting a loaf of bread into slices results only in a physical change for the composition of the bread has not been affected. The slices, however, cannot again be transformed into the whole loaf. A physical change, then, is one which does not bring about a change in the composition of the material. It is often called a temporary change because in many cases, if the form has been changed, it is possible to restore the original form. 

Chemical Changes.—In a chemical change, on the other hand, the composition of the substance is always affected and its characteristics become very different. Heating sugar is a good example of this type of change. It is a white, crystalline substance, odorless, sweet and soluble in water.

As it is heated it melts and becomes brown in color. The color grows darker, it becomes thicker, bubbles and puffs up and finally there is left a black mass. The bubbling is due to gases which are some of the new substances formed from the original sugar which was a white solid. The black solid which is left is tasteless and insoluble in water, whereas the sugar was white, sweet and exceedingly soluble. In other words, the identity of the original sugar has been lost, new substances having been formed with entirely different characteristics. In this particular instance we cannot imagine ever being able to restore the original sugar.

In some cases the original substance can again be obtained but only by bringing about another chemical change. A chemical change, therefore, is often called a permanent change. It always results in the formation of one or more

new substances whose characteristics differ from those of the original substance.

Some Evidences of Chemical Changes.-The fact that a chemical change has occurred can often be observed from changes in the general appearance. Sometimes a gas is formed which causes the material to bubble or puff up, as in the case of sugar when it is heated. As the gas tries to escape from the medium in which it has been formed it. causes a bubbling often spoken of as "effervesence." 

Charged waters are sometimes called effervescent waters and their appearance is also due to the escape of gases from them. Another common evidence of a chemical change is a marked difference in color-though this is not always an infallible sign. Again, as the sugar is heated it first becomes brown and finally black. There may be a change in taste or odor. The gaseous products formed from the sugar are pungent and the black solid is tasteless. There may be a difference in solubility, a soluble substance being changed into one that is insoluble, or an insoluble substance forming one that is soluble. The formation of the black insoluble residue on heating the white soluble sugar is an example of the formation of an insoluble substance. During the digestion of starch, chemical changes occur which result in the formation of a soluble substance, glucose, from the insoluble starch. Sometimes there is a change in temperature. When water is added to quicklime, slaked lime is formed because of a chemical change and with it there is a marked rise in temperature. A chemical change can occur, however, without any of these evidences being noticed. 

Some Aids to Chemical Changes.-Chemical changes can be promoted by different means. Heat is perhaps the most common aid to be employed. It will hasten the speed of all chemical changes. Food is cooked to increase its palatability and digestibility and sometimes to improve its

appearance as in the case of meat. The chemical changes which bring about these results are helped by heat. Too hot an iron will scorch the material being pressed because of a chemical change aided by the heat. Another condition favorable to certain chemical changes is light. Silver nitrate solution must be kept in dark bottles as it is very susceptible to light. Otherwise, it will deteriorate because of a chemical change which is occurring in it. Sometimes the material must be in solution in water. A Seidlitz powder remains unchanged so long as it is dry; but when water is added a chemical change takes place at once, in this case a marked evidence being the effervescence that occurs. Again, the only condition necessary for some chemical changes is contact between the substances undergoing the change. 

Nitric acid is sometimes used to burn off a wart and accomplishes its purpose by bringing about a chemical change as it touches the tissue to be destroyed. Another means of helping a chemical change is through the use of another substance called a catalytic agent. This is a substance which affects the speed of the chemical change, but after the change has occurred, the catalytic agent itself is unaltered. There are different kinds of catalytic agents. In the preparation of oxygen from potassium chlorate, manganese dioxide is used because the oxygen can be obtained more easily and at a lower temperature; but after the action has taken place the manganese dioxide is left in its original condition. Living cells, whether they are in a plant or an animal organism, have the ability to manufacture substances which help chemical changes by acting as catalytic agents. These substances are called enzymes. Yeast cells, for example, manufacture an enzyme called zymase which helps glucose or grape sugar to form carbon dioxide and alcohol. In the lining of the stomach there are cells able to manufacture an enzyme called pepsin which helps the chemical changes occurring in the digestion of such foods

as meat and eggs. Still another way of bringing about a chemical change is by means of electricity. If an electric current is allowed to pass through certain kinds of compounds when in solution, for example silver nitrate, the substance will undergo a chemical change. In the case of the silver nitrate, free silver will be formed and articles suspended in the solution can be coated with the silver. 

The silver plating industry is dependent upon this. In some instances mechanical energy, as a jar, or blow, will cause a chemical change. Explosives, for example, must be handled carefully or they will undergo a chemical change with such violence that an explosion results. 

Kinds of Chemical Change.-While chemical changes can be called by different names depending upon the point of view from which they are considered we shall at present distinguish between four kinds. When water is added to quicklime (calcium oxide) the two substances combine to form one new substance, slaked lime (calcium hydroxide). When iodine is spilled on a starched apron a blue-black stain results due to the formation of a new substance (starch iodide) through the combination of the starch and the iodine. This kind of chemical change in which two or more substances combine to form one new substance is called combination or sometimes synthesis. It can be represented by a condensed statement as follows: 

Quicklime + water 

Starch + iodine slaked lime starch iodide

Each word represents a substance, those to the left of the arrow being the substances undergoing the chemical change, and those to the right, the substances resulting from the change or the products of it. The chemical change itself is often called the reaction and in the condensed statement it is represented by the arrow. When interpreting

such a statement in words the arrow is translated by a word appropriate to the kind of change. For example: 

Quicklime and water react to form or combine to form slaked lime

In another type of chemical change, from one substance two or more new substances can be formed. This is commonly called decomposition. For example, glucose or grape sugar can be decomposed into carbon dioxide and alcohol helped by the enzyme, zymase, which the yeast furnishes. 

Cane sugar on heating can be decomposed into carbon and gases of different kinds. From hydrogen peroxide water and oxygen can be formed. Glucose 

Cane sugar 

Hydrogen peroxide carbon dioxide + alcohol carbon + gases water+ oxygen 

A third kind of chemical change is one called simple replacement or displacement. The reaction between zinc and hydrochloric acid is an example. Zinc (an element) and hydrochloric acid (a compound) undergo a chemical change forming hydrogen (an element) and zinc chloride (a compound). For this reason zinc should not be cleaned with an acid. Zinc hydrochloric acid - hydrogen + zinc chloride.

In every displacement reaction an element and a compound react to form a new element and a new compound. We shall refer later to other examples.

In the fourth kind of chemical change, two compounds react together to form two new compounds. This is called double replacement or double decomposition. Fat and lye undergo a chemical change forming soap and glycerine as products. All four substances are compounds. 

Fatlye soap glycerine Silver nitrate and sodium chloride react together to form

silver chloride and sodium nitrate. The name of each substance implies that it is a compound. Because of this reaction taking place when both of the reacting substances are in solution, silver nitrate, when it is being used as an irrigant, should never be placed in a container which previously has held "salt" solution without first rinsing the container very thoroughly: for the "salt" solution reacts with the silver nitrate in this way and so renders the latter ineffective. 

Silver nitrate + sodium chloride-silver chloride + sodium nitrate 

Properties of Substances.-When the term "property" is used with reference to a substance by it is meant a characteristic, trait or distinguishing mark. Just as different brands of goods are recognized by their peculiar trade marks, so a chemical substance can be recognized and related to other substances by its properties. Suppose a white solid has been formed as the product of an experiment. If it behaves with silver nitrate in a way that is known to be peculiar to a group of compounds called chlorides, we know that our product is a chloride. If it also makes a nonluminous flame yellow, an effect that is characteristic of all compounds containing sodium, we know that our product is not only a chloride but one also containing sodium, therefore, sodium chloride. A knowledge of the properties of different substances, then, is essential in finding out what has taken place when a chemical change has occurred. It is also necessary for the identification of a new substance in order to find out what group of substances it is related to and to recognize it as an individual member of the group. 

In human families there are often outstanding family characteristics which make it possible to recognize an individual as belonging to a particular family. At the same time each member of the family has certain traits or characteristics peculiar only to himself. We can think of the chemical families in much the same way.

Properties can be classified as physical and chemical, the basis for this distinction being whether they can be observed without causing the substance to undergo a chemical change or not. Any property we can observe without causing a change in the composition of the substance is a physical property. For example, the condition in which the substance exists at ordinary temperature conditions, 68° F. or 

20° C., whether it is a solid, a liquid, or a gas, is a physical property. This is called its physical state. Reference has already been made to the physical state of the different elements. Some other physical properties peculiar to solids are their melting point, their hardness and whether they are crystalline or not. Peculiar to a liquid are the freezing point, boiling point and whether it will dissolve other substances. Peculiar to a gas is its odor. Any one of the three, solid, liquid, or gas, may have color, taste, or be soluble. In all cases these properties are called physical properties because the substance does not undergo a chemical change when the property is observed or studied. 

Chemical properties, on the other hand, can be studied only by causing the substance to undergo a chemical change. 

The effect of heat, if it brings about a chemical change instead of merely causing the substance to melt or boil, is a chemical property. The different, substances that a certain substance will react with and what happens as a result are chemical properties.

SUMMARY 

Substance.-Synonymous with material or matter. 

Element.-A substance composed of only one kind of material.

Compound.-A substance composed of two or more constituents (elements) which are always in a definite proportion by weight. The identity of each constituent is lost.

Mixture.-A substance composed of two or more components (elements, compounds or mixtures) whose proportion can be varied. The components retain their own identities. 

Physical Change.-Does not alter the composition of the substance though its appearance may be changed.

Chemical Change.-Alters the composition of the substance. 

Combination.-A chemical change in which two or more sub stances react to form one new substance.

Decomposition.-A chemical change in which one substance forms two or more new substances.

Displacement.-A chemical change in which an element and a compound react to form a new element and a new compound. 

Double Decomposition.-A chemical change in which two compounds react to form two new compounds. 

Enzyme.-A substance made by living cells of either plants or animals which helps a chemical change but after the change has occurred it (the enzyme) is unaltered. 

Physical Properties.-Those characteristics of a substance that can be studied without causing a chemical change to occur in the substance.

Chemical Properties.-Those characteristics of a substance that can only be studied by causing a chemical change to occur in the substance.

EXPERIMENTS 1. The Bunsen Burner 

Unscrew and examine your gas burner. Make a sketch of the burner and indicate where the gas and the air enter. Learn how to increase and diminish the supply of each. Close the air holes, then turn on and light the gas. Note the appearance of the flame. Does it deposit soot on cold porcelain? Gradually open the air holes and note the effect on the flame. Does it deposit soot? Practice lighting the burner with the air holes closed and then regulating the air supply until you can readily obtain a good non-luminous flame. 

2. Physical and Chemical Changes 

(a) Examine a platinum wire noting the color, luster and flexibility. Hold the wire in the non-luminous flame for a few moments. Note the result. Remove, and when cool compare its properties with those first noted. Has there been any permanent change in the properties of the platinum? Classify as physical or chemical any change you observed under the influence of heat. Why are platinum needles used for transferring cultures in bacteriology? (b) Examine a piece of bright copper wire, noting its color, luster and flexibility. Heat the wire in a non-luminous flame and when cool compare its properties with those first noted. 

Examine the black substance formed on the surface. 

Can you scrape it off? Is it copper? Reason. Is it soot? Reason. What change does this experiment illustrate?

(c) Put a little naphthalene in a test tube and heat gently. 

After it has boiled 2-3 minutes remove the flame and cool. Compare the deposit on the side of the tube with the original substance. Has the naphthalene undergone a physical or a chemical change? Give a reason for your answer. What general deduction can you make from these experiments? (d) Put a little red precipitate in a test-tube and heat. During the heating introduce a glowing splinter in the tube and note the result. If the splinter glows more brightly or bursts into flame, oxygen is present. Scrape the deposit from the inside of the tube and examine it. What is it? These two elements were separated from the red precipitate. What kind of a change occurred? Why? 

Name it. What kind of a substance is red precipitate? Give a chemical name for it. 

3. Mixtures and Compounds 

(a) Place a teaspoon of powdered sulphur and a little less than half the quantity of iron filings in a dry mortar and mix thoroughly. Place a small quantity on a sheet of white paper and examine it with a lens. Is it a homogeneous mass? Run a magnet along the underside of the paper. What is the result? 

Can the iron and the sulphur be separated from each other?

Place a small portion of the iron and sulphur in a test-tube and add 5cc. of carbon bisulphide (caution! carbon bisulphide is very inflammable!). Mix and filter the liquid through a dry filter paper in an evaporating dish. Allow it to stand until the liquid has evaporated and examine what is left in the dish. 

What is it? Examine the residue on the filter paper. 

Is sulphur soluble in carbon bisulphide? Is iron? you been able to do because of the difference in the solubility of iron and sulphur? Have you used physical or chemical means? 

How would you classify the material you have been experimenting with? Give as many reasons as possible. 

What is it? 

What have (b) Place the remainder of the iron and the sulphur in a testtube and heat, holding the tip of the test-tube in the hottest part of the flame. Heat steadily until the mass glows. Remove from the flame and observe carefully. Does it continue to glow? Then continue heating for a minute or two. Cool, break the tube and examine the contents. How would you try to see if iron is still present? Make the test. Result. How would you try to prove the presence of sulphur? Make the test. Result. What kind of

a substance is the mass? Give your reasons. Can you name it?

4. Double Decomposition, Precipitation and Filtration 

Dissolve a little salt (sodium chloride) in water in a test-tube. 

Add a little silver nitrate solution (has been made in the same manner by dissolving solid silver nitrate in water). The cloudy appearance is due to the formation of minute particles of silver chloride which is not soluble in water. Place a stopper in the mouth of the test-tube and shake. Note how the small particles cling together to former larger ones. An insoluble substance forming in and separating out from a liquid in this way is called a precipitate. There is another product formed in the chemical change, but it is soluble and cannot be seen. What is it? What kind of a chemical change has occurred? Give your reason.

The precipitate can be separated from the liquid by filtering. 

The liquid running through the filter paper is called the filtrate. 

What does it contain? What would happen if you put a solution of silver nitrate you were going to use as an irrigant into a container which had previously held a “salt” solution? What should always be done?

CHAPTER III  

Law of Definite Proportions.—We have already referred to the fact that water is a compound made up of the elements hydrogen and oxygen in the very definite proportion. of 1 part by weight of hydrogen and 8 parts by weight of oxygen. Neither does the composition of bichloride of mercury vary. It is always composed of mercury and chlorine in the proportion of 1 part by weight of the former to approximately 0.35 parts by weight of the latter. From the quantitative study of the composition of many other compounds, in every case it has been found true that a compound is always composed of the same elements whose proportions by weight never change, whatever their actual weights may be. The statement of this fact is called the 

Law of Definite Proportions and it is one of the fundamental laws of chemistry.

Weight Relations. The quantitative study of the amount of a given element in the different compounds containing it has also revealed another important fact. 

The constituents are not only present in a definite proportion by weight in each compound, but there seems to be a close relationship between these weights in different compounds. For example, let us take the following compounds in which we have the elements oxygen, sulphur and chlorine combined in one case with hydrogen, in another case with carbon and again with mercury. The numbers represent the parts by weight. 24

Water 

Hydrogen sulphide 

Hydrogen chloride 

Carbon dioxide ... 

Carbon disulphide 

Carbon tetrachloride 

Mercuric oxide 

Mercuric sulphide 

Bichloride of mercury 

composed of hydrogen (1) and oxygen (8) composed of hydrogen (1) and sulphur (16) composed of hydrogen (1) and chlorine (35.5) composed of carbon (1) and oxygen (2.66) composed of carbon (1) and sulphur (5.33) composed of carbon (1) and chlorine (11.8) composed of mercury (1) and oxygen (0.08) composed of mercury (1) and sulphur (0.16) composed of mercury (1) and chlorine (0.35)

Tabulating these figures as follows, we have: 

1 part by weight of hydrogen combines with 

1 part by weight of carbon combines with 

1 part by weight of mercury combines with 

Parts by weight 

Oxygen Sulphur Chlorine 16 35.5 ..... 2.66 5.33 11.8 0.08 16 0.35 

As each set of figures is on a different basis, 1 part by weight of either hydrogen or carbon or mercury, there is no apparent relationship. Suppose we put the proportions by weight on the common basis of 1 part by weight of hydrogen, thus making in each case 8 parts by weight of oxygen, 16 parts by weight of sulphur and 35.5 parts by weight of chlorine. The parts by weight of carbon or mercury will then be as follows: 

Oxygen Sulphur Chlorine 

1 part by weight of hydrogen combines with.... 8 

3 parts by weight of carbon combines with... 

100 parts by weight of mercury combines with.. 16 35.5 16 35.5 16 35.5 

A close relationship becomes apparent at once when the figures are put on this common basis. Whether 8 represents the parts by weight of oxygen, 16 the parts by weight of sulphur, or 35.5 the parts by weight of chlorine, in each case 

3 parts by weight of carbon are necessary to form the corresponding compound. Again, the compound methane is composed of 1 part by weight of hydrogen and 3 parts by weight of carbon. In the above set of figures this relationship is also represented. Other examples might be included showing the same close relationship existing between the proportions by weight.

Atomic Theory.-Naturally the question arises, how can these facts be accounted for? Can the unchanging composition of compounds and the definite weight relationship of the elements be accidental? The fact that these characteristics are not true of a few compounds only but are common to all disproves this idea. It is impossible under ordinary conditions to see details that would serve as an explanation. 

In 1802, John Dalton, an English schoolmaster, advanced a theory that offered a solution. He suggested the idea that each element might be composed of minute, invisible particles, each having weight since it was a mass of material, even though very small. For example, he imagined the element oxygen to be made up of minute oxygen particles, all of them being alike in every respect and having the same weight. We can imagine the same thing to be true of carbon, mercury, chlorine or any of the other elements. While each oxygen particle is like every other oxygen particle and each carbon particle like all the other carbon particles, the oxygen particles and the carbon particles are not alike and their weights are different. In other words, all the particles of the same element are similar, but particles of different elements are unlike each other, either in weight or other characteristics. They were called atoms from a Greek word meaning "uncut."

When Dalton first conceived this idea of the make-up of elements, it was not possible to prove the existence of atoms, and so it was called the Atomic Theory. By a theory is meant an imaginary condition of affairs which, if true, would help to account for definitely known facts. 

It is an attempt to explain a fact. A law, on the other hand, is a statement of a known fact, as the Law of Definite 

Proportions. With the development of experimental technic, means have been devised by which proof has been obtained that atoms really do exist. It has also been found possible to measure them and to study their structure.

Because of the results obtained, we no longer can accept as true the old idea that an atom is the smallest particle into which an element can be divided. We now know that in their turn atoms are made up of still smaller particles which are related to each other and to a central unit or nucleus very much as the earth and other planets are related to the sun. We now define an atom as being the smallest particle of an element that can take part in a chemical reaction or enter into the formation of a compound.

Atomic Weights.-The weight of the atom is spoken of as its atomic weight. On referring to the table of elements, a figure representing the atomic weight will be found to the right of each name. For example, 12 represents the atomic weight of carbon, 1.008 the atomic weight of hydrogen, 35.46 that of chlorine. It does not mean that an atom of carbon actually weighs 12 ounces or grams or grains. 

Because of the minute size of the atom, it is not possible to weigh it as one would a pound of sugar. The figure in each case is abstract and represents the relative weight of the atom, implying a comparison with some standard. This standard now is oxygen. Again, on consulting the table of elements, at the top of the table will be found, oxygen = 16. 

This is definitely a statement that oxygen is not only the standard but that 16 has been chosen to represent the weight of an atom of oxygen. In selecting some one element as the standard for comparison, the most natural choice would have been the lightest element. Hydrogen, which is the lightest of all the elements, was the one first selected as the standard. For a number of reasons, however, it was found that oxygen made a more convenient basis for comparison. More elements combine with it than with hydrogen and so the figures representing their atomic weights could be found more easily. The numbers found by comparison with oxygen are either whole numbers or

have comparatively simple decimals. This, again, makes for more accurate work when calculating the results of quantitative experiments. The question now arises, what was the reason for fixing 16 as the atomic weight of oxygen.

It is based upon the results of experiments in which equal volumes of hydrogen, the lightest of all elements, and oxygen were weighed under exactly the same conditions of temperature and pressure. It was found that oxygen was 

15.879 times as heavy as an equal volume of hydrogen. To assign to hydrogen, therefore, as its atomic weight a number that would not be less than 1, 16 would be the smallest whole number that could be used to represent oxygen. The atomic weight of hydrogen would then be 1.008. The same relationship exists between the weights of equal volumes of the two gases, whichever set of figures is used: Hydrogen 1 Oxygen 15.879 or 16 1.008 

The atomic weights of all the other elements, then, are relative to oxygen with 16 definitely chosen to represent the weight of its atom. For example, if the atomic weight of oxygen is 16, then the atomic weight of carbon is 12. The carbon atom, therefore, is slightly lighter than the oxygen atom. The atomic weight of mercury is 200.6 if the atomic weight of oxygen is 16. The atomic weight of an element may be defined as a fixed number representing the smallest proportion by weight in which the element occurs in its compounds.

Symbols. For writing the names of elements and compounds there is a method of abbreviation sometimes spoken of as "chemical shorthand." For the name of each element, a symbol is used which not only represents the name of that element but also one atomic weight of it. The symbol consists of either one letter or two, usually chosen from

the Latin name, though sometimes this is very similar to the English name. If one letter, it is always written as a capital; if two, the first one is capitalized, as H (hydrogen), He (helium). O is the symbol for oxygen, being an abbreviation not only for the name of the element but also representing one atomic weight of it. In the same way C represents one atomic weight of carbon, and S one atomic weight of sulphur. In many cases, there are two or more elements beginning with the same letter. For example, we have carbon, calcium, chlorine, cobalt and copper. In order to distinguish between them, it is necessary to use for a symbol not only the first letter but also some other letter from the name. For carbon, which occurs most widely in combination, the letter C is used as the symbol, for calcium Ca, chlorine Cl, cobalt Co, and copper Cu from the Latin word cuprum. The symbols of other elements can be found in the table of elements. 

Molecules.-Atoms of elements combine themselves to form larger particles whose characteristics are different from the characteristics of the separate atoms composing them. These particles have been named molecules from a word meaning "little mass." They are the smallest particles into which a substance can be divided and still retain the properties of a larger mass of the substance. 

They are the smallest particles of matter capable of existing independently. For example, suppose we separated a glass of water into drops. These would still resemble the water before it had been divided. Let us imagine that, if we had a sufficiently fine instrument, we could continue the division of the drop of water. We would, in time, reach the point beyond which we could not further separate the droplets into smaller particles of water. The smallest particle still retaining the characteristics of water would be a molecule of water. If the molecule were further divided, we would have atoms of the elements hydrogen and oxygen

of which water is composed. Just as an atom is often defined as being the smallest particle of an element entering into a chemical change so a molecule can be defined as being the smallest particle of a substance entering into a physical change.

We can imagine being able to separate either elements or compounds into these small particles that still resemble the original substance. As an element is a substance composed of only one kind of material its molecules must be composed of one kind of atom only. For example, experi ments have shown that the molecule of oxygen must consist of two oxygen atoms. The molecules of all the gaseous elements consist of two atoms. In many cases molecules of elements are represented as if they contained only one atom. The term molecule, however, is used if the particle being represented is one existing independently in the free state. This will be referred to later. The molecule of a compound, on the other hand, contains different kinds of atoms since it is composed of more than one kind of material. For example, the molecule of water contains both hydrogen and oxygen atoms.

Molecular Weights.-The weight of the molecule is spoken of as the molecular weight. As it consists of the sum of the weights of the atoms of which it is composed, it is also represented by an abstract number which is relative to oxygen. For example, water is composed of hydrogen and oxygen, the proportion by weight being approximately 1 of hydrogen to 8 of oxygen. If 16 is chosen to represent the atomic weight of oxygen, then 2 must represent the proportion by weight of hydrogen present in a molecule of water. The molecule of water consists, then, of 2 atoms of hydrogen and 1 atom of oxygen. Its molecular weight would, therefore, be 18. The molecular weight of oxygen is 32 as its molecule contains 2 atoms of oxygen.

Formulas. There is an abbreviated way of representing

a molecule of either an element or a compound. It is called a formula and consists of a collection of the symbols which represent the kind of atoms of which the molecule is composed. If there is more than one of any kind of atom, the number is written at the lower right hand side of the symbol. Without any number one atom is represented. 

Suppose we illustrate with the following: Substance 

1 Molecule Contains 

Formula Water 

2 atoms hydrogen

H₂O 1 atom oxygen 

Hydrogen peroxide 

2 atoms hydrogen

H2O2 

2 atoms oxygen

Oxygen 

2 atoms oxygen 02 

Silver nitrate 1 atom silver 1 atom nitrogen AgNO3 

3 atoms oxygen 

Sodium chloride 1 atom sodium NaCl 1 atom chlorine 

Bichloride of mercury 1 atom mercury HgCl2 

2 atoms chlorine Calomel 1 atom mercury HgCl 1 atom chlorine The formula of a substance always represents not only the kinds of elements of which it is composed but it also definitely represents the number of each kind of atom which one molecule contains. 

Chemical Changes in Terms of Atoms and Molecules.—

We have already defined a chemical change as being one which alters the composition of the substance or substances undergoing the change. Thinking of substances as being masses of molecules, we can now interpret a chemical change as consisting in the rearrangement of atoms to form new kinds of molecules. Since each molecule always has an unchangeable composition there must always be an exact

amount of the kinds of molecules undergoing the chemical change in order to provide the correct number of atoms to form the new molecules, otherwise some of the original molecules remain unchanged. The common use of baking soda with sour milk is an excellent example. Through a chemical reaction between the baking soda and a compound in the sour milk called lactic acid, the desired result is produced. A definite amount of baking soda, however, can react with only a definite amount of lactic acid. There can be no variation in the proportion of either ingredient. Frequently entirely too much soda is used for the amount of lactic acid in the milk. The unexpected, poor results, such as a very disagreeable odor and taste and the yellow color of the product, possibly a biscuit or a muffin, are due to the excess of baking soda which has no lactic acid with which to react. The fact that there must be definite amounts of substances to react together to form definite amounts of new substances is a restatement in different words of the law of definite proportions previously referred to. 

Conservation of Energy.-We think of a person as being very energetic or, in some cases, as having little energy according to the amount of work of one kind or another that he can turn out. In other words, energy can be defined as the capacity or the ability to do work. Inanimate objects as well as living beings possess it. Running water, for example, can turn a wheel and a coiled spring can make a watch run. Energy may take different forms some of which are very familiar although we may not have thought of them as energy. The energy of motion to which we have just referred in the case of the running water is frequently called kinetic energy. Other active forms of energy are heat, light, electrical energy and sound. In the coiled spring is an energy which is stored up, becoming changed into an active form as it makes the many cogs in the

machinery of the watch move. This stored energy is called potential energy. All substances are believed to have energy stored in them, a definite amount for each. This particular kind of potential energy is called chemical energy. 

Very careful experiments have showed that we cannot create or destroy energy. We can, however, change its form or its location. This fact is known as the law of conservation of energy. A few familiar examples will make its truth apparent. The kinetic energy of the running water is transferred to the energy of motion of a wheel. 

This wheel may be a portion of a dynamo by which the energy of motion is transformed into electrical energy. By using an electric pad the electrical energy is transformed into heat energy, the usefulness of the pad being dependent upon this change. By using other devices, electrical energy can be used for the treatment of rheumatism or for the destruction of tissue, its effectiveness depending on its transformation into heat and chemical energy within the body. When winding a watch, the energy of motion is transformed into the potential energy of the coiled spring. 

This in turn is gradually transformed into energy of motion in the turning cogs and into sound which we hear as the ticking. Because the energy often becomes invisible to us as a result of these changes, we think it has been destroyed. 

Radio is only one illustration of the recapturing of energy which had apparently disappeared.

From the standpoint of chemistry, energy is most important, for no chemical change takes place without an energy change accompanying it. In many cases the chemical change is brought about for the sake of the energy rather than for the new substances that are produced. When wood is burned as a fuel, for example, it is for the production of heat and not for the ashes and other gaseous products carried off through the chimney.

The energy value of a substance is often measured in

terms of its ability to produce heat, the unit of measurement being called a calorie. It represents the amount of heat necessary to raise 1 kilogram of water 1 Centigrade degree or 1 pound of water 4 Fahrenheit degrees. The difference between the Centigrade and Fahrenheit thermometer scales will be explained in a later chapter. The value of fuels to supply heat is frequently spoken of as their caloric value. The ability of foods to furnish energy to the body is measured by calories. The release of the heat or energy to the body comes through a transformation of chemical energy into heat energy, or the energy of motion (mechanical energy).

Law of the Indestructibility of Matter.-There is another important characteristic common to all chemical changes. In every chemical change that occurs, the total weight of the material undergoing the chemical change always equals the total weight of the new products. There are exactly the same number and kinds of atoms after the change has taken place as there were in the beginning. 

The only difference is in their arrangement. In other words, matter can neither be created nor destroyed. This is known as the law of conservation or indestructibility of matter. It often seems hard to believe, for example, when we think of the log of wood which has been burned and compare with it the pile of ashes so much smaller in quantity than the original wood. During the burning there were also gaseous products formed. If they had been collected, their weight together with the weight of the ashes would have been greater than the original weight of the wood for, when anything burns, there is a combination of the material being burned, in this case wood, with the oxygen of the air. The total weight of the wood and the oxygen would equal the total weight of the ashes and the gaseous products. 

Equations. We have already made use of a condensed statement for representing a chemical change. By using

formulas to represent the substances undergoing the change and the resulting products, we can further abbreviate this statement. It is called an equation, and the same general form is used for writing it. Suppose we wished to represent the decomposition of hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen. We should need to know the formulas for all the substances, hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂), water (H₂O), oxygen (O2). The equation as first written would then be as follows: 

H2O2 

Skeleton equation

H2O + O2 In every chemical change, however, there are exactly the same number and kind of atoms in the substances undergoing the change as in the products resulting from it. An equation when correctly written must, therefore, show neither loss nor gain of any kind of atom. On examining the above equation, we see at once that on the right hand side a total of 3 oxygen atoms (2 in the molecule of oxygen and 1 in the molecule of water) have been represented, but on the left hand side there are only 2 atoms of oxygen in the molecule of hydrogen peroxide. The equation must, therefore, be "balanced": that is, we must represent a number of molecules of hydrogen peroxide that will supply the correct number of atoms of hydrogen and oxygen for the water and oxygen which are formed. The equation will then show no loss or gain of any kind of atom. 

Balanced equation 2 H₂O₂ 2 H₂O + O2 H2O2 

The equation should be interpreted as follows: Two mole cules of hydrogen peroxide decompose to form 2 molecules of water and 1 molecule of oxygen.

One way of proving whether an equation has been correctly balanced is to check each kind of atom as it appears in the original substances with the same kind of atom in

the products formed.

For example, in this equation we have: 

A total of 4 hydrogen atoms (on the left hand side) a total of 4 hydrogen atoms (on the right hand side).

A total of 4 oxygen atoms (on the left hand side) = a total of 

4 oxygen atoms (on the right hand side).

Therefore, the total weight of the materials undergoing the change is equal to the total weight of the products formed and the equation is written correctly.

Radicals. Reference has already been made to the chemical reaction between solutions of silver nitrate and sodium chloride (see kinds of chemical changes) in which the products are silver chloride (AgCl) and sodium nitrate 

(NaNO3). The equation is as follows: 

NaCl + AgNO3 

AgCl + NaNO3

On comparing the number of each kind of atom represented on both sides of the equation, we find that the equation is correctly balanced as first written. We also notice that the molecule of both silver nitrate (AgNO,) and sodium nitrate (NaNO3) contain in common 1 nitrogen atom and 3 oxygen atoms. Such a grouping of atoms which are associated together in both the original substance and the product and seem to act as a unit is called a radical. 

There are many different kinds of radicals which will be referred to from time to time. Each has a name, the NO, radical being called the nitrate radical. Compounds whose molecules contain it are called nitrates, as silver nitrate, sodium nitrate and potassium nitrate.

Motion of Molecules.-Motion is a characteristic common to all molecules whether of elements or compounds. 

There is a distinction, however, in the rapidity with which the molecules can move. One of the factors which has a great influence on the speed of molecular motion is the closeness of the molecules to each other. This can be realized at once, especially if we picture the effect of

crowded conditions on our own ability to move. Imagine a room so filled with people that each person can move with great difficulty. This is similar to the condition of the molecules which make up a solid substance. Contrast with this a room comfortably filled in which each person can move about with comparative freedom. Similar to this is the condition of molecules in a liquid. Again, in a room containing very few people, each can move about with great ease and rapidity, just as the molecules of a gas can move. The distinction between a solid, a liquid and a gas. then, is essentially a distinction between the space between the molecules of the substance and their rapidity of motion. 

In a solid they are very closely packed together and move with difficulty. In a liquid they are less closely packed together and so have greater freedom of movement. In a gas there is considerable space between the molecules and they can move rapidly. Temperature is another factor influencing the speed of molecular motion, heat increasing and cold retarding it. 

Capillary Attraction.-If a small tube is placed in a liquid that can wet it, it will be noticed that the liquid inside the tube will be at a higher level than the liquid surrounding the tube. The smaller the diameter of the tube is, the higher will be the level to which the liquid inside it can rise. This phenomenon is called capillarity from a word meaning "hair like" because of the extreme fineness of the tubes in which the rise is very noticeable. Molecules of the same kind tend to cling together because of a force called cohesion. For example, water molecules tend to cling together. Through a force called adhesion molecules of different kinds tend to cling together. When a small glass tube is put in water, the water rises in the tube because the force of adhesion which causes glass molecules and water molecules to cling together is greater than the force of cohesion which would keep the water molecules together and result in the level of the liquid remaining unchanged.

There are many uses made of this capillary attraction. In an oil lamp, the oil is drawn up into the wick because of it. 

In the same way a wound is drained by using a gauze wick. 

The absorbent effect of blotting paper or absorbent cotton is due to capillarity. In obtaining blood for a coagulation test, it is possible to draw a drop of blood up into a "capillary tube" for the same reason.

Conduction-Convection-Radiation.-Heat is moved from one spot to another by either conduction, convection or radiation. To explain transference of heat by conduction, suppose we imagine a silver spoon with its bowl placed in hot water. The molecules of silver are in a state of slow motion as the spoon is a solid substance. In the bowl of the spoon this motion is increased because of the heat. 

These molecules which have been made to move more rapidly in turn set up a greater vibration or motion in the molecules next them, and these vibrations are passed on from one molecule to the next until in time the heat or energy has been moved from the part first affected through the entire spoon. All materials do not conduct heat with the same readiness. We have already made a distinction between elements, classifying them as metallic or nonmetallic, partly on the basis of their ability to conduct heat. 

Metals, therefore, are as a rule very good conductors of heat. Solids in general are better than liquids because their molecules are closer together. Gases are the poorest conductors of heat because of the great space between their molecules. A few of the more common substances are listed below in the order of their ability to conduct heat. Silver Copper Mercury Stone

Aluminum Glass Tin Water Iron

Felt 

German silver

Air

This difference in the ability of substances to conduct heat has many applications. The construction of fireless cookers and refrigerators is dependent upon it, materials being used which are poor conductors. A vacuum between the walls of a thermos or "vacuum" bottle has increased the ability to keep constant the temperature of its contents. 

The warmth of woolen clothing is due to the air spaces in it which prevent the body heat from being conducted away so rapidly. Several light layers of clothing are warmer than one heavy layer for the same reason. A flaxseed poultice will retain its heat for a longer time if bicarbonate of soda is included in the ingredients. This is because of a gas which is produced which in turn prevents the heat from being conducted away so rapidly. 

The effect of heat upon air or water is a good illustration of transference of heat by convection. When either air or water is heated, that which has become hot rises and is replaced by a cold layer. As it in turn becomes hot, it, too, rises and there is set up a current of moving air or water. 

As the molecules of both liquids and gases are less closely packed together than are the molecules of a solid, they can change their relative position with considerable freedom. As the molecules become hot they move upward and are replaced by colder molecules. The heat is moved with the molecule. In a hot water boiler, although the source of the heat is below the boiler, the heat first becomes noticeable at the top of it. When ventilating a room by opening windows, the ventilation is more effective if the window is open at both the top and bottom because this gives a higher outlet for the used air which is warmer and therefore rises. 

To illustrate transference of heat by radiation, suppose we imagine a person standing in the sunlight and becoming hot because of heat received from it. A medium called "ether" is believed to fill all spaces between the different planets and the sun, also spaces between molecules and

between the particles of which atoms are composed. The sun is a very hot body and its molecules are in a state of exceedingly rapid motion. This motion of the sun's molecules sets up waves in the ether which move in a straight line and so rapidly that they can pass through the ether without heating it. The heat only becomes apparent again when the waves strike some body capable of absorbing them, for example, the person standing in the sunlight. These waves set up in the ether are of different lengths and produce varying effects. The longest are those which produce electrical effects and are now made use of in wireless and radio. There are short ones which are capable of affecting the optic nerve and so we see light. There are still shorter ether waves which cannot affect the optic nerves but which can cause chemical changes. These are called "actinic" rays and they are of great value medicinally.

Heat is not transferred from a fireplace to a room by conduction because air is a very poor conductor. The room is not heated by convection for the heat moved in this manner passes up the chimney. The room is heated by radiation. Warmth is felt from an electric light bulb. 

This warmth comes by radiation rather than by convection or conduction. As there is a vaccum within the bulb, the heat cannot be moved by convection. Neither can it be moved by conduction as glass and a vacuum are both poor heat conductors.

SUMMARY 

Law of Definite Proportions.-A substance is always composed of the same elements united in a definite proportion by weight. 

Law of Conservation of Matter.-Matter can neither be created. nor destroyed. The total weight of materials undergoing a chemical change equals the total weight of the products resulting from it. 

Law of Conservation of Energy.-Energy can neither be created nor destroyed though its form can be changed.

Atoms. The smallest particles of an element which can enter into the formation of a compound.

Atomic Weight.-A fixed number representing the smallest proportion by weight in which an element occurs in its compound. 

Molecule. The smallest particle of a substance that can exist independently and have the same characteristics a larger mass of the substance possesses.

Molecular Weight.-A fixed number which is the sum of the weights of the atoms contained in a molecule of either an element or a compound. 

Symbol.-An abbreviation which represents not only the name of an element but also 1 atomic weight of it. 

Formula.-An abbreviation which represents not only the name of an element or a compound but also 1 molecular weight of it.

Equation. An abbreviation for a chemical change which represents the exact number and kind of molecules undergoing a complete reaction and the exact number and kind of molecules resulting from it.

Solids. Substances whose molecules are so closely packed together their motion is too slight to be noticeable.

Liquids. Substances whose molecules have greater freedom of movement because they are less closely packed together.

Gases. Substances whose molecules have great freedom and rapidity of movement because of space between the molecules.

Capillary Attraction.—The ability of a liquid within a tube to rise to a level that is higher than that of the liquid surrounding a tube.

Conduction. The transference of heat by being moved from one molecule to another.

Convection. The transference of heat by being moved with the molecule.

Radiation. The transference of heat without the molecule by vibrations set up in ether waves.
