CHAPTER IV OXYGEN 

History. As early as the eighth century A.D. the Chinese believed the air to contain two components. They knew that one, which they called "yin," was more active than the other. They also believed that it was concerned with certain changes occurring in metals and combined with burning charcoal and sulphur. While others had obtained oxygen from different substances, it was not until 1777 that Lavoisier, a French chemist, completed the experimental proof that it was a component of the air. Previous to this, a Swedish apothecary named Scheele had obtained the gas from at least seven different substances. He also recognized the fact that atmospheric oxygen combined with many substances, among them being linseed oil, phosphorus, hydrogen and metals. However, he failed to publish the results of his experiments and make known his conclusion before Priestley in 1777 made oxygen by heating mercuric oxide and discovered the brilliance with which substances burned in it. While he did not at first fully realize the significance of his results or see any connection between the gas he had obtained and the air, Priestley has usually been credited with the discovery of this element. Lavoisier gave it its name, oxygen, derived from a Greek word meaning "acid producer," because at that time it was thought to be an essential constituent of all sour substances. 

Occurrence.-Oxygen occurs widely both in the free state and in compounds in combination with other elements. We 43

can think of the air as being diluted oxygen for 23.2% of it consists of this element, the remainder being a mixture of other gases, mainly nitrogen. The reasons for calling air a mixture rather than a compound will be discussed later (see Chapter VIII). Oxygen is a constituent of many different compounds. We have already referred to its presence in water, 89% of its weight being due to this element. 

It forms 50% of the weight of the stones, rocks and soil which constitute the surface layer of the earth. The fact that it forms so large a part of both water and earth materials accounts for there being little evidence of chemical changes going on in spite of the constant contact between the earth's surface and the oxygen of the air. Sixty-five per cent of the weight of the human body is due to oxygen, a large number of the compounds in the various body tissues and fluids containing it. It occurs in food materials in varying amounts, there being more in starch and sugar than in fat. The difference in the usefulness of food materials as a fuel is due in great part to the amount of oxygen already present, the smaller the amount in proportion to hydrogen and carbon the greater being the fuel value. The absence of oxygen in food materials or condiments is the exception rather than the rule. Sodium chloride is an example of one containing no oxygen. A large number of the substances used medicinally, for example salol and aspirin, contain oxygen. Many disinfectants owe their usefulness not only to the presence of oxygen but also to the ability of the compound to release it. Potassium permanganate is an example of such a substance. Reference has already been made to the significance of the "ate" ending in the naming of compounds. Hydrogen peroxide is another example of a compound useful as a disinfectant because of the oxygen it can furnish. The term "oxide" in a name always means that the compound contains oxygen. The significance of the "ide" ending has already been explained (see Chapter II).

OXYGEN 

45 Preparation. For laboratory study, oxygen can be obtained by heating certain of the compounds containing it. 

Red precipitate or mercuric oxide (HgO) was one of the substances from which it was first obtained. By means of heat this compound can be decomposed into its two constituent elements, mercury and oxygen. If a glowing splinter is introduced in the test-tube in which the mercuric oxide is heated, the presence of free oxygen will be indicated by the brighter glow of the splinter. On scraping the gray deposit from the inside of the tube it will be recognized at once as mercury. 2 HgO →→→→ 2 Hg + O2 A cheaper source of oxygen is another of its compounds, potassium chlorate (KClO3). On heating, it is decomposed into free oxygen (O2) and potassium chloride (KCl). 2 KClO3 - 2 KCl + 3 02 By using a mixture of manganese dioxide (MnO₂) and potassium chlorate, the free oxygen can be obtained with greater ease and at a temperature of 200° C. instead of 

450°C. if the potassium chlorate is used alone. Although the manganese dioxide also contains oxygen, experiments have shown that after the action has taken place it remains unchanged. In other words, it acts as a catalytic agent (see Chapter II). In this case the chemical change of decomposition is brought about by the aid of both heat and a catalytic agent. If the manganese dioxide is represented in the equation, it should be included both in the reacting substances and in the products of the reaction to show that it is unchanged after the action is over. 2 KClO3 + MnO2 

2 KCl + 302 + MnO2

As pure oxygen has many important uses it has been necessary to devise methods of preparation which are both practical and cheap. While it can be obtained with ease

from the materials commonly used in the laboratory they are too expensive to be made use of commercially. A number of different industrial methods have been developed. 

In one, water acidified with sulphuric acid is decomposed into its constituents, hydrogen and oxygen, by passing an electric current through it. In another method, air which is a mixture mainly of oxygen and nitrogen is separated into these components in a mechanical way which will be referred to later (see Chapter VIII). 

Physical Properties.-Knowing that oxygen is one of the components of the air we are aware of certain of its physical properties. Under ordinary conditions of temperature and pressure it is a gas, colorless, tasteless and odorless. By greatly increasing the pressure and lowering the temperature it is possible to liquefy it. It is this property which makes it possible to separate it from the other gases in the air. It is not extremely soluble in water. For this reason it can be collected over water when obtaining it by the decomposition of oxygen compounds. It is, however, soluble to a slight degree. This characteristic is important because of the fact that as oxygen passes from the interior of the lung cells through their membranes and those of the capillary walls into the blood stream it is in solution. 

A very small amount of the oxygen required by the body can be carried in solution because it is only slightly soluble. 

While fishes need oxygen the amount is small and that which is ordinarily in solution in water is adequate for their needs. They cannot, however, live in water that has been boiled as the oxygen which was in solution has been driven off during the boiling. Oxygen is heavier than hydrogen, the difference in weight being the basis on which 16 was chosen to represent the atomic weight of the former (see Chapter III). It is also slightly heavier than the air. 

Chemical Properties.-The chemical behavior of pure oxygen is similar to that of the mixture air, which contains

OXYGEN 

47 it, but it is very much more pronounced. Under ordinary temperature conditions it will react with a few elements in a sufficiently vigorous manner to make the chemical change apparent. For example, phosphorus, when exposed to either air or pure oxygen, forms a white trail or cloud consisting of a new substance, phosphorus pentoxide (P2O5). 

In the dark a glowing of the phosphorus is an evidence that the chemical change is taking place. By applying heat to it the action is made to take place with great vigor. Because of the readiness with which phosphorus combines with oxygen to form phosphorus pentoxide, it is necessary to keep it covered with a liquid (water). Its use in match heads is also dependent upon its great activity. The equation representing the combination of phosphorus and oxygen is as follows: 4 P5022 P2O5 Under ordinary temperature conditions the reaction of oxygen with some elements is so slight that considerable time must elapse before the result is noticed. When iron is exposed to the air or pure oxygen there is a very gradual formation of a red deposit of iron rust or iron oxide (Fe₂O₂) through the combination of the two elements. If the temperature of the iron is raised to a high degree, however, and it is at once placed in pure oxygen, there is an action so vigorous as to cause the production of solid particles hot enough to be incandescent and a shower of sparks results. When cool these particles of magnetic oxide of iron (Fe3O4) are black and brittle. In both cases oxides have been formed but they are different compounds, possessing different characteristics. 4 Fe + 302 3 Fe + 2 02 2 Fe2O3 Fe3O4 

Iron rust or ferrous oxide 

Magnetic oxide of iron

With many elements no action is evident under ordinary temperature conditions. Carbon, sulphur, silver, gold, or

platinum, for example, can remain in contact with oxygen without any evident change. In some cases, however, if the temperature is raised, a chemical change will take place. 

Carbon will glow and gradually disappear because of the formation of a gaseous product, carbon dioxide (CO₂). 

Sulphur will burn with a blue flame forming a gas, sulphur dioxide (SO2). The familiar choking odor is not the smell of sulphur but is due to the product which has been formed. C + O2 S+ O2 CO₂ SO2 Silver, gold and platinum remain unchanged even on heating. For this reason platinum can be used for needles or small containers in experimental work requiring heat. The tarnish on silver is not due to the formation of an oxide. 

It consists of silver sulphide which has been formed through a reaction with hydrogen sulphide, a component of natural and artificial gas. 2 Ag + H₂S Ag₂S+H₂ Ag₂S + H2 Compounds, if they consist wholly or in part of elements which will react with oxygen, will also react with it in many cases. Gasoline is composed of the elements carbon and hydrogen. If heated carbon dioxide (CO2) and water (H2O) will be produced, the former through the combination of the carbon with oxygen, the latter a product of the union of hydrogen and oxygen.

Elements and compounds capable of reacting with oxygen can often take the oxygen necessary for the reaction from an oxygen compound. Some compounds will give up part or all of their oxygen with great readiness; others lose it with greater difficulty. Hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂) parts very easily with some of its oxygen, its uses being dependent upon this fact. It is also difficult to keep for any length of time, especially after a bottle has been opened. 2 H₂O₂ 2 H₂O + O2 H2O2

OXYGEN 

49 Slow and Rapid Oxidation.-Every chemical change in which oxygen has combined with either an element or compound is commonly spoken of as an oxidation. If the oxidation is complete, the products are oxides, that is, in every case they are compounds composed of oxygen and one other element. For example, when charcoal is heated until it begins to glow it combines with oxygen. In other words, it is undergoing oxidation and the product being formed is carbon dioxide. When alcohol, which is a compound composed of carbon, hydrogen and a small amount of oxygen, is being burned the chemical change taking place is one of oxidation. The carbon of the alcohol combines with oxygen to form carbon dioxide and the hydrogen combining with oxygen forms water. Both these products can be proved to be present by performing appropriate tests. Reference has already been made to the fact that every chemical change results not only in the production of new material products but also in a transformation of energy (see Chapter III). In the case of oxidation, chemical energy is being changed to heat energy, or energy of motion. Under some conditions, the oxidation takes place so rapidly that the heat which is produced cannot be radiated away as fast as it is formed. Therefore, it accumulates and the temperature of the mass rises. Such an oxidation is called rapid oxidation. The phenomenon we commonly speak of as "burning" or "combustion" is really a rapid oxidation. The burning log of wood is an example. Very often, as in this case, energy in the form of light as well as heat may result. There are other cases of oxidation, however, which take place so slowly that the heat is radiated away as rapidly as it is formed. Therefore, there is no chance for it to accumulate and the temperature of the material undergoing oxidation remains unchanged. This is called a slow oxidation. The fallen tree rotting in the forest is undergoing this type of oxidation. Whether it is

rapid or slow, heat is a result and, if the change is complete, the ultimate products are oxides. In many cases it is necessary to apply heat to the material in order to make the oxidation take place rapidly enough for the energy change and the material change to be apparent. This temperature at which a substance will undergo rapid oxidation is frequently spoken of as its "kindling temperature." 

When making a fire in a range, we use different kinds of material; paper, kindling, wood and coal. The match is applied to the paper first to raise it to its kindling temperature. As it burns (undergoes rapid oxidation) the heat produced raises the wood to its kindling temperature and it begins to burn. The heat produced during the rapid oxidation, or burning of the wood, in turn raises the coal to its kindling temperature, and so the fire is started. The match itself is another excellent example of the use made of materials possessing different kindling temperatures. 

The small stick of wood is tipped with different materials in successive layers, the outermost one having such a low kindling temperature that the friction caused by striking the match produces sufficient heat to make it undergo rapid oxidation. In turn, each layer underneath becomes heated and begins to burn until finally the piece of wood is blazing. 

Spontaneous Combustion.-Spontaneous combustion is not infrequently given as the cause of an unexpected fire. 

Oily rags, coal in bunkers, or masses of green hay are some of the materials with which we associate such fires. These materials contain substances which are capable of undergoing slow oxidation under ordinary temperature conditions. Ordinarily, the heat resulting from a slow oxidation does not have a chance to accumulate. With a mass of oily rags, or the mow filled with green hay, or the coal in the bunker, however, there are conditions which prevent the heat from being radiated away as rapidly as it is formed.

OXYGEN 

51 The resulting rise in temperature may be very slow but in time the kindling point of the material is reached whereupon it bursts into flame. For this reason, it is never safe to allow a pile of oily rags to accumulate in an enclosed space. 

Combustible and Incombustible Substances. - Sub stances are frequently called combustible if they have a low kindling temperature and can therefore undergo rapid oxidation readily. In many cases, if the substance is a solid or a liquid, before it actually burns it is first changed to a gas. 

The wax of a candle, for example, must first be melted, then vaporized before the candle burns. The fact that the burning substance is a gas can be proved true by extinguishing the lighted candle and then relighting it by applying a match above but not in contact with the wick. The flame first appears in the air above the wick. The selection of fuels is determined in part by differences in their combustibility. Soft wood makes better kindling than does hard wood because it is more combustible. Hard wood, on the other hand, makes a more lasting fire. A combustible substance may be very valuable in many ways. It has also many dangers. Because of the readiness with which gasoline can be vaporized and burned, it must be used with great care. In many cases its storage, even in small quantities, is prohibited in dwellings. Either through carelessness or ignorance, it is often used for removing grease spots under very dangerous conditions. A poorly ventilated room permits the gasoline vapor to increase to an alarming degree. An open fire such as a stove, grate or lamp supplies the heat necessary to raise the vapor to its kindling temperature, which in the case of gasoline is low. There is a sudden burst of flame which may be even explosive because of the mixtures of the combustible gas with air forming an "explosive" mixture as it is called. Vigorous rubbing may even supply sufficient heat to cause the gasoline to vaporize

and burn, particularly if the ventilation is poor. Ether is another compound which is readily vaporized and extremely combustible, and every possible precaution should be observed in its use and storage to guard against fire. 

Non-combustible substances are those which cannot undergo rapid oxidation easily, even on heating. Their inability to burn may be due to one of two reasons. Either they are unable to combine with oxygen or they are already oxygen compounds and cannot take on any more. These substances are of great value for such purposes as fireproof construction, for safes, vaults, laboratory equipment and the like. Iron and steel, for example, are used in many ways because they will not burn or combine rapidly with oxygen, except at a temperature that can only be obtained under artificial conditions. Bricks, stone and asbestos are compounds already combined with oxygen to such an extent that even when they are heated in the presence of oxygen, they remain unchanged. Red lead and other pigments used for outside painting, if of good quality, are compounds which will not undergo even slow oxidation. 

Therefore, they are used to protect surfaces that would otherwise undergo a gradual change through constant contact with the air.

Fires and Their Requirements. Since a fire is accompanied by the chemical change of oxidation, in order to make a fire in a range it is necessary to supply not only materials that can undergo rapid oxidation easily but also an abundant supply of oxygen (air). We have already referred to the use of different kinds of materials (see rapid oxidation). By placing these materials loosely in the firebox with air spaces between the pieces and by the proper arrangement of drafts and dampers an adequate amount of oxygen is supplied. The fire is checked by lessening the supply of oxygen. Fires are extinguished either by lowering the temperature of the burning material below

OXYGEN 

53 its kindling temperature or by shutting off the supply of necessary oxygen. Sometimes both means are employed. 

Smothering a blaze with a blanket is effective because the oxygen supply (air) is cut off. Water lowers the kindling temperature and also supplies a curtain of steam, therefore the fire is put out. The method to be used should depend upon the nature of the blaze. A burning liquid, for example oil, should never be extinguished by pouring on water, for the fire is only spread over a greater area. 

There are various types of fire extinguishers which produce a gas, in some cases carbon dioxide (CO2), in others carbon tetrachloride (CC1). These gases, which are both heavy and non-combustible, settle down around the blaze serving as a curtain or blanket which cuts off the needed oxygen. In some forms of extinguishers water is also forced out through the nozzle, the fire being put out both by cutting off the oxygen and by lowering the kindling temperature (see Chapter IX-carbon dioxide fire extinguisher).

Oxidizing Agents. We have already mentioned the fact that the oxygen necessary for the oxidation of a substance can often be supplied by an oxygen-containing compound. 

Such compounds, which are commonly called oxidizing agents, are of great importance. In many cases they are made use of for the purification of water supplies. A large number of disinfectants are oxidizing agents. Potassium permanganate (KMnO4) and hydrogen peroxide have been referred to previously. The latter compound is not used now to the same extent that it was at one time. The oxygen which it can furnish is released by the help of an enzyme called an oxidase which is present in all tissues. 

For this reason the oxygen, which is the active part of the peroxide, may already have been released before the disinfectant has reached the portion of the body needing it. 

Another reason why hydrogen peroxide may be undesirable

is that the force of the released oxygen may carry the organisms deeper down into the tissue and so spread the infection. Dakin's solution contains an oxidizing agent called sodium hypochlorite (NaClO). Douches, bleaches and stain removers may also owe their effectiveness to oxidizing agents. In every case, the substance acted upon by the oxygen compound becomes changed because it has combined with the oxygen which has been supplied. In other words, it has been oxidized and has lost its undesirable characteristics. When a compound loses oxygen, it is said to undergo reduction. The oxidizing agent, then, becomes reduced when it gives up its oxygen. In other words, when an oxidation is being carried on, a reduction is taking place at the same time. Suppose we use Dakin's solution to illustrate this fact. The oxidizing agent in it, which is sodium hypochlorite (NaClO), becomes reduced to sodium chloride (NaCl) because it gives up its oxygen. 

This oxygen which has been released combines with some material, possibly harmful bacteria in a wound, which is rendered harmless because of the chemical change of oxidation which has taken place. Substances which can take oxygen away from a compound in order to combine with it are called reducing agents. The harmful bacteria, then, act as reducing agents. Later on, we shall explain oxidation and reduction in a different way. At present, however, we shall think of these changes in their narrowest sense, that is, as being concerned with the addition or removal of oxygen. 

Importance of Oxygen to the Body.-Metabolism is a term applied to the various changes, either physical or chemical, which take place throughout the body as a result of cellular activity. It is called basal metabolism when the cellular activity is uninfluenced by either food or muscular activity of any kind, that is to say, when the body is in complete repose. Of the various chemical changes

OXYGEN 

55 occurring because of cellular activity, oxidation is the one which is responsible for the production of heat for the maintenance of the body temperature and energy for carrying on both the internal work of the body, that is cellular activity in general, and external work such as lifting or moving. The materials undergoing oxidation are either the body tissues themselves or foods which have been brought to them. The substances functioning fundamentally as fuels are fats and carbohydrates, the latter reaching the body cells in the form of glucose. Both are compounds composed of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen. The percentage of oxygen in the fat molecule is, however, very much lower than it is in the glucose molecule. Fat, therefore, has a higher fuel value for it must combine with a larger amount of oxygen in order to be fully oxidized. For every gram of fat undergoing oxidation in the body, 9 calories of heat (see Chapter III) are produced. Glucose, on the other hand, yields 4 calories per gram. In each case, however, if the oxidation is complete, carbon dioxide and water are produced, both of which are eliminated as waste products. It is possible to carry on experiments with living beings in such a way as to measure the amount of carbon dioxide and water eliminated during a given time interval. 

The apparatus is called a calorimeter. There are simplified forms called respiratory calorimeters, which permit only the study of oxygen intake and carbon dioxide and water output through the lungs over short time intervals. There are also very much more elaborate pieces of apparatus, like cells or small rooms, in which the subject lives while more detailed investigations are being carried on. As the carbon dioxide and water output is a measure of the oxidation process which produced them, from the results obtained through such studies it is possible to get information about the rate of metabolism and the influence of different conditions on it. Metabolism laboratories are now being estab-

lished in hospitals to assist in diagnosing certain conditions. 

Hyperthyroidism, for example, causes a very much increased oxidation, and such a condition would be indicated by a basal metabolism above the average. Myxoedema, on the other hand, results in a lowered rate of metabolism. 

While the body cells get a small part of the necessary oxygen from compounds containing it, the bulk of it comes from the inspired air. As has already been stated, the oxygen passes through the separating cell membranes from the lung cells into the blood stream in a condition of solution. In the blood, most of it enters into a temporary union with the hemoglobin of the red corpuscles, the resulting compound being called oxyhemoglobin. Because of the slight solubility of oxygen, only a very small amount can be carried in solution. When the red corpuscles are carried by the blood stream to a cell requiring oxygen, the oxyhemoglobin, which is an unstable compound, gives up the oxygen with which it has been temporarily combined. 

It is then frequently spoken of as "reduced hemoglobin." 

On being carried back to the lungs, it can again pick up a fresh load of oxygen and the process is repeated. Hemoglobin can also combine chemically with other gases. With carbon monoxide (CO) or "coal gas," it will form carbon monoxide hemoglobin, a stable compound. By this is meant a compound that does not tend to decompose again. The poisonous effect of carbon monoxide is due to this fact. As the gas which has been breathed in enters the blood stream, it forms a permanent compound with the hemoglobin. This prevents the latter from combining with oxygen and so the oxygen carrying power of the blood is lowered. In reality, the person who had been poisoned fatally dies of asphyxiation. To treat carbon monoxide poisoning, blood transfusion is resorted to in order to supply fresh hemoglobin.

As there are many abnormal conditions which limit- the

OXYGEN 

57 body's supply of oxygen, various methods of administering pure oxygen have been attempted in the hope that the body cells can so get an amount that will be adequate for their needs. It is given most commonly by inhalation. Because of the drying effect on the tissues, the patient's lips and nose are usually oiled with vaseline. The oxygen is mixed with air and the proportion must be watched. Too large a proportion of oxygen may result in a temporary suspension of breathing or apnoea, as it is called, because of changing the normal balance of oxygen and carbon dioxide which is believed to stimulate the respiratory center. If given directly from an oxygen tank, the amount can be controlled by allowing it to bubble through water, and the funnel through which it escapes is held above the patient's face, oxygen being heavier than air. In recent years at the Rockefeller 

Hospital, an oxygen chamber has been devised for the treatment of pneumonia. This consists of a small room so constructed that the air of the entire room is enriched with oxygen. Therefore, it can be administered over long intervals and the expired carbon dioxide can be removed from the air. In this way, the proportion of oxygen can be very carefully regulated. It may vary from 40 to 60%.

Oxygen is not only essential for the respiration of all living organisms whether animal or plant; after the death f these organisms, it is equally essential for the processes decay, whereby the various complex constituents are ken down into simpler, harmless substances by processes oxidation. The natural purification of water is brought about by the oxidation of objectionable or even harmful substances, that may be present, through contact with atmospheric oxygen. To further the oxidation, the water is sprayed up into the air for several feet. The process is called aeration. Fuels cannot burn without oxygen.

Sometimes, when an unusually hot flame is needed, it is mixed with a combustible gas and burned in a specially

devised torch, like the oxyacetylene torch, which is used for cutting through metal.

SUMMARY 

Oxidation.-A chemical change in which oxygen is combined. with a substance.

Products of oxidation.-Energy product-heat-light-energy of motion. Material product-oxides, if the oxidation is complete.

Combustion.-Oxidation which is so rapid that it is accompanied by a rise in temperature and frequently by light. 

Spontaneous Combustion.-Oxidation which at first takes place slowly but in time becomes rapid. 

Kindling Temperature.-Temperature at which a substance will undergo rapid oxidation.

Oxide.-A compound composed of oxygen and one other element. 

Reduction.-A chemical change in which oxygen is removed from a substance.

Oxidizing Agent.—An oxygen compound which can readily give up some or all of its oxygen.

Reducing Agent.-A substance which can remove oxygen from a compound containing it.

Metabolism.-Physical and chemical changes in the body which are the result of the activities of living cells. 

Basal Metabolism.-Metabolism resulting from the activities of living cells when not influenced by food or muscular work.

CHAPTER V HYDROGEN 

History. The fact that hydrogen was a distinct and independent substance was established by Cavendish in 1766. Later he found that when it was burned a vapor was produced which could be condensed to form a dew-like substance. He failed, however, to interpret his results correctly. Some years later Lavoisier repeated his experiments and identified the product as water. Interpreting the results in the light of the knowledge that burning consists in a union with oxygen, the composition of water was thus established. The name "hydrogen" signifies "water producing." 

Occurrence. Unlike oxygen, hydrogen occurs in the free state to a very limited extent. Its occurrence in compounds is, however, very wide. Free hydrogen is present in the air in traces, not over 1 part in 1,500,000 parts of air. In volcanic gases its proportion varies, there being more, however, than in ordinary air. Natural gas as well as many of the manufactured gases used for fuel purposes contains a large proportion of hydrogen. Many of the compounds in which this element occurs are very familiar substances. 

References have already been made to water of which 

11 per cent by weight is composed of hydrogen. Crude petroleum and its products, such as gasoline, kerosene,

Nujol, vaseline and paraffin, consist of hydrogen-containing compounds. A large proportion of the compounds making up the tissues of the human body contain it. The hemoglobin of the blood, the fat of adipose tissue and glycogen in the liver are only a few of the many examples that might 61

be mentioned. Most foods contain hydrogen compounds. 

In milk there is milk sugar, in eggs there is albumin. and the various cereals and many vegetables contain starch. 

Wood, paper and the fabrics of which clothing and bedding are made are composed in part of hydrogen. It is present in a group of substances which have certain characteristics in common, among these being a sour or acid taste. The name "acid" is given to the entire group. Many of its members are familiar, either through their hospital use for medicinal or cleaning purposes, or because of their association with foods. Among these acids are the following: lactic, citric, acetic, tartaric, oxalic, hydrochloric, nitric and sulphuric. They are discussed in greater detail in a later chapter (see Chapter IX). 

Preparation.-Hydrogen, which is the one element comnon to all acids, can be freed from it by contact with different metals. All metals are not equally active in this ability to liberate hydrogen from the acid containing it. In a list called the electromotive series, the metallic elements, including hydrogen, are placed in the order of their relative activity when free. The list as given here includes only some Electromotive Series Sodium Potassium Magnesium Aluminium Zinc Iron

Nickel Tin Lead of the more familiar metals. All metals which precede hydrogen in it are able to displace the latter from an acid; those following hydrogen cannot do so. Zinc, iron or tin, for example, will react with an acid to form free hydrogen, whereas copper, mercury or silver will not undergo this reaction. The acid, usually hydrochloric (HCl) or sulphuric (H2SO4) must be diluted with water if it does not already contain it. While it does not take part in the reaction, it is essential to it as an aid. We can think of it as being a necessary part of the equipment. 

The metal most commonly used is zinc, commercial zinc, HYDROGEN Copper Mercury Silver

Platinum Gold

HYDROGEN 63 which contains impurities, being better than the chemically pure metal. The impurities, acting as a catalytic agent, help the reaction. No heat is needed, contact between the metal and acid being sufficient to start the reaction. The effervescence or bubbling of the mixture is an evidence of the formation of the gaseous product, hydrogen. Another product is also formed, for the metal takes the place of the hydrogen that has been freed from the acid. This product is not visible unless the liquid in which it is in solution is evaporated to dryness. It then appears as a solid. Zinc (Zn) will react with sulphuric acid (H₂SO4) to form free hydrogen (H₂) and zinc sulphate (ZnSO4).

The equation is as follows: 

Zn + H2SO4 H₂+ ZnSO4 

Iron (Fe) and hydrochloric acid (HCl) will produce free hydrogen and iron chloride (FeCl2).

Fe +2 HCl → H₂ + FeCl2 With magnesium (Mg) and acetic acid (HC₂H₂O₂), the products will be free hydrogen and magnesium acetate 

Mg (C2H3O2)2.

Mg + 2HC2H3O2 →→→ H₂+ Mg (C2H3O2)2 A comparison of the formulas for acetic acid and magnesium acetate discloses the fact that, in the case of this acid, not all of the hydrogen has been displaced by the metal. Only a part of it is capable of being freed, and for this reason the formula is written to distinguish the replaceable hydrogen, as it is called, from the other hydrogen atoms which make up the molecule. In each of these chemical changes an element and a compound have reacted to form an element and a compound, that is, a displacement (or simple replacement) action has taken place.

Those metals which can displace hydrogen from an acid are also able to displace it from water. Sodium and potas-

sium, the most active metals, can do this under ordinary temperature conditions. Many of the metals, however, require heat for the action to take place. If the water is either cold or boiling, the hydrogen is not completely displaced from it and the products of the reaction are hydrogen and a compound composed of the metal, oxygen and hydrogen, called a hydroxide. With sodium, for example, the products are hydrogen and sodium hydroxide (NaOH). 2 Na + 2 H₂O H₂+ 2 NaOH If water in the form of steam is conducted over the red-hot metal, the hydroxide, which is first produced, is further decomposed so that the final products are hydrogen and the oxide of the metal. For example, when steam is conducted over red-hot iron, a reaction takes place in which hydrogen and magnetic oxide of iron (FeO) are formed. 3 Fe + 4 H₂O 4 H₂+ Fe3O4 Certain metals, as aluminium and zinc, can react with sodium hydroxide to form hydrogen and another compound composed of sodium, oxygen and the metal used. Aluminium and sodium hydroxide will react to form hydrogen and sodium aluminate. 

2 Al + 6 NaOH 

3 H₂+ 2 Nas AlO3 

Strong soaps and washing soda behave like sodium hydroxide when they are in solution in water. For this reason they never should be used for cleaning aluminum utensils. 

Physical Properties.-Under ordinary conditions of temperature and pressure, hydrogen is a gas, colorless, tasteless and odorless, if pure. As prepared in the laboratory a slight odor is noticeable because of impurities. It can be liquified. 

It is very much lighter than air, the latter being 14.5 times as heavy. For this reason, after the receivers have been filled in the laboratory preparation of hydrogen, they should

HYDROGEN 

65 be left inverted until immediately before they are to be used for an experiment. The great difference in the weights of hydrogen and the air makes the former useful for filling balloons. Hydrogen is the lightest of all the elements and so it was the first and most logical choice as a standard for fixing atomic weights, 1 being chosen to represent its atomic weight. While it is somewhat soluble in water, it is even less so than oxygen. Therefore, it is possible to collect it by displacement (downward) of water.

Chemical Properties. Free hydrogen unites directly with only a few elements, among them being oxygen, chlorine and sulphur. Under ordinary temperature conditions it can remain in contact with free oxygen without any apparent change. If, however, the temperature is raised, the two combine to form water. As in all cases of oxidation, heat is also a product of the reaction. If the hydrogen is pure, it burns quietly with a blue, almost invisible flame which is intensely hot. If, however, hydrogen is mixed with air in varying amounts, on being raised to its kindling point, the combination with oxygen takes place more violently and is accompanied by either a shrill sound or by an explosion which may even break the container. Such mixtures of air with combustible gases are frequently called "explosive mixtures" because of the greater violence of their combination with oxygen.

Hydrogen-containing compounds, if they are capable of combustion, will produce water as one of the products. 

Kerosene, for example, which consists of compounds composed of hydrogen and carbon, will on burning form water as one of the products of its combustion. The moisture seen on the inside of the chimney when the lamp is first lighted is the water so formed, which condenses from a gas to small drops of liquid as long as the chimney remains cool. The foods upon which we depend for the heat and energy they furnish us are hydrogen-containing compounds.

As a result of their oxidation in the body, the hydrogen is converted into water and is eliminated through the kidneys, skin and lungs. Water vapor in the expired breath can be made visible by breathing against a cold window pane. The "foggy" breath, seen when we go outdoors on a cold day, is also due to the condensation of the water vapor to a liquid form.

Free hydrogen is capable of taking the oxygen necessary for its oxidation from certain oxygen-containing compounds, for example, the oxides of metals. If a stream of hydrogen is allowed to pass over hot magnetic oxide of iron (Fe3O4) the latter gives up its oxygen to the hydrogen, the products being water and free iron. In this case, the hydrogen acts as a reducing agent and the magnetic oxide of iron as the oxidizing agent (see Chapter IV). As a result of the chemical change, the hydrogen becomes oxidized and the oxide becomes reduced. It is possible to give more than one explanation of a reaction. This chemical change taking place between the hydrogen and iron oxide can be considered a displacement reaction as well as a case of oxidation and reduction. The equation is as follows: 

4 H₂+ Fe3O4 

4 H2O + 3 Fe

It is not possible to remove the oxygen from the oxide of every metal with the same ease by a reduction with hydrogen. The order in which the metals occur in the electromotive series is also an expression of the relative stability of their oxides with reference to hydrogen. The oxides of those metals up to and including aluminium can be partly reduced by means of free hydrogen. Those that follow can be completely reduced, although the reduction becomes increasingly difficult. 

Free hydrogen will also combine with free chlorine, although not under ordinary temperature conditions. The resulting compound is a gaseous product, hydrogen chlo-

HYDROGEN 

67 ride (HCI). The solution of this gas in water is called hydrochloric acid. The union between sulphur and hydrogen is brought about with difficulty, a very high temperature being necessary. Hydrogen sulphide (H₂S), a gas, is the product.

Hydrogen can be made to react with other types of compounds beside metallic oxides. Oils or liquid fats can be changed to solids through a chemical combination with it. 

The solid product which is formed still retains its noticeable fat-like characteristics, although its consistency has been changed. The process, which is called hydrogenation, is of great industrial value. Among the commercial products whose manufacture is dependent upon it is crisco, which is made from cottonseed oil.

In many cases the union of a compound with hydrogen is spoken of as a reduction, and the removal of hydrogen from a compound is called an oxidation. In other words, the use of these two terms, oxidation and reduction, is broadened to include reactions involving hydrogen as well as oxygen. In either case, they are opposite processes. To illustrate, suppose we consider the relation between wood alcohol (CH3OH) and formaldehyde (CH₂O) and the chemical change involved in changing the one into the other. 

Examination of the two formulas reveals the fact that wood alcohol has two more atoms of hydrogen than formaldehyde. 

The former can be changed into the latter by supplying oxygen, that is, by a process of oxidation. As a result, however, two atoms of hydrogen are removed from the wood alcohol molecule.

CH3OH + O(from oxidizing agent) CH2O+ H2O In this case the oxidation has really consisted in the removal of hydrogen. Formaldehyde can be changed into wood alcohol by union with hydrogen. 

CH₂O+ 2 H (from reducing agent) → CH₂OH

We have already referred to the fact that hydrogen is a good reducing agent. This change, in other words, which consists in the addition of hydrogen to a compound, can be interpreted as a reduction. 

Uses. Free hydrogen is an important constituent of many of the gaseous mixtures used for fuel purposes. Its value depends upon its combustibility and also upon the fact that it produces much heat when it burns. The luminosity of these gases, however, depends upon other constituents, for hydrogen burns with a non-luminous or blue flame. When very intense heat is needed, as in welding, a torch or blowpipe is used which permits the burning of a mixture of hydrogen and oxygen. Under carefully regulated conditions this can be done without resulting in an explosion and the heat of this flame is very intense. The so-called "lime-light" is a lump of calcium oxide (quicklime) which has been raised to such a high temperature that it has become incandescent by being heated in an oxyhydrogen flame (see Chapter XV, Calcium).

The use of hydrogen in balloons is possible because of its extreme lightness. Because of its combustibility, however, danger attends its use. Helium, another light gas, is now replacing it to a considerable extent for this reason. 

While it is somewhat heavier than hydrogen, it is so much lighter than air that it gives buoyancy to the balloon. As it does not show any tendency to enter into combination with other substances, there is no danger arising from pos、 sible explosions.

The use of hydrogen for transforming an oil into a solid fat by the process of hydrogenation has already been referred to. A number of butter substitutes and frying fats have been prepared by this process. For laboratory purposes, hydrogen is useful because of its ability to act as a reducing agent.

HYDROGEN 

69 

SUMMARY 

Oxidation.-A chemical change in which hydrogen is removed from a compound.

Reduction. A chemical change in which hydrogen is combined with a substance.

Hydrogenation.-A chemical change in which hydrogen is combined with a compound (usually applied to a fat or oil).
