CHAPTER VI WATER 

History. It is impossible to imagine a time when water was not known or made use of. In the very early days, when men first began to wonder about the nature of things around them, many believed they consisted of four "elements" as they called them. Of these, one was water, the other three being earth, air and fire. Even though the occurrence and necessity of water were long recognized, definite knowledge of its composition did not come until the eighteenth century. We have already referred to experiments carried on by Cavendish in which he obtained a dewlike substance by burning hydrogen (see Chapter V). He, however, still clung to a belief in the phlogiston theory. 

According to this theory, when any substance burned, an invisible substance called phlogiston was lost from it. The resulting product, therefore, consisted of the original material less this phlogiston. For a long time, this theory had many ardent supporters. However, with the development of methods by which quantitative results could be obtained from experiments and with the discovery of oxygen, belief in the phlogiston theory was gradually weakened. Cavendish was one of the few scientists who clung to it, and, therefore, he could not interpret rightly the product he obtained when he burned hydrogen. Later, when Lavoisier performed the same experiments, oxygen had been discovered and its connection with burning had been recognized. The product of the burning of hydrogen, which he identified as water, was therefore proved to consist of hydrogen and oxygen. 71

Composition. The proportion by weight in which hydrogen and oxygen occur in water can be found by experiment. 

By passing hydrogen, a reducing agent, over red-hot copper oxide a reaction occurs in which copper and water are produced.

H₂+ CuO H2

Cu + H₂O If the tube containing the copper oxide is weighed before and after the experiment, the difference will represent the weight of oxygen used in the formation of the water. The apparatus can be so arranged as to conduct the water vapor, which is formed, into a receiver containing fused calcium chloride which has the ability to take up water. If this receiver is weighed before and after the experiment, the difference will represent the weight of water formed. Having found the weight of oxygen and the weight of water, their difference represents the weight of hydrogen. 

Weight of tube -Weight of tube +copper oxide +copper 

Weight of oxygen

Weight of receiver + calcium chloride + water -Weight of receiver + calcium chloride. 

Weight of water 

Weight of water (composed of hydrogen + oxygen) -Weight of oxygen. 

Weight of hydrogen

Results obtained from such experiments have proved that water is composed of 1.008 parts by weight of hydrogen and 

8 parts by weight of oxygen.

Experimental proof of the proportions of these two elements by volume can also be obtained by causing free hydrogen and free oxygen to combine. If a graduated tube is filled with mercury and inverted over mercury, it is possible to introduce each gas into it separately and to measure its exact volume. The chemical combination of the two

WATER 

73 gases can then be brought about by an electric spark and the product will condense to a dew, leaving some unused gas, which can be measured and tested to show whether it is hydrogen or oxygen. Results obtained in this way have proved water to be composed of 2 volumes of hydrogen and 1 volume of oxygen. Assuming the atomic weights of hydrogen and oxygen to be, respectively, 1.008 and 16, we find the weight and volumetric relations agree. The experimental results show the proportion by weight to be 

1.008 for hydrogen and 8 for oxygen. Only one-half an atomic weight of oxygen is represented by the figure 8. As it is not possible for a fraction of an atom to enter into a chemical reaction (see Chapter III) suppose we represent the proportion by weight of oxygen by 16, its atomic weight.

The proportion by weight of hydrogen must then be represented by 2.016 in order to keep the ratio between them that experiment has found true. 1.008 8 2.016: 16 1.008 2.016 16 

In other words, 2.016 represents 2 atomic weights or 2 volumes of hydrogen and 16 represents 1 atomic weight or 1 volume of oxygen, the volumetric relation which has been found true. We, therefore, write the formula for water H₂O.

Occurrence. Water is one of the most widely distributed substances in nature. Seventy-two per cent of the earth's surface is covered with it, in places this layer of water being six miles deep. It occurs in the air in varying proportions. When present as fog, rain, snow or sleet, we are very conscious of it. It exists in the air as water vapor at all times, although often we are unaware of its presence. 

In unduly large amounts it causes the uncomfortable condition we speak of as humidity. On coming in contact with a cold surface, water pipes for example, this water vapor condenses into visible drops of water. Sixty-five per cent

of the human body consists of water. For a person weighing 150 pounds, this means that 100 pounds of his weight. are due to water. Foods vary greatly in their water content. 

In general fruits and green vegetables contain 75 per cent or over, meats 50 per cent or over, and cereals and breadstuffs less than 50 per cent.

Physical Properties.-Water when pure is a clear, tasteless and odorless liquid. Small quantities of it appear colorless, but when seen in large amounts it has a bluegreen color because of the way it refracts light. The very deep blue or green color we associate with certain bodies of water is caused also by the presence of solid matter. While water ordinarily occurs in a liquid form, it can be changed into either a solid or a gas by a change in its temperature. 

Measurement of Temperature.-Temperature, as the feeling of hotness or coldness is called, is measured by a thermometer, scaled into units called degrees (abbreviation °). There are two types of scales in common use. 

Clinical and household thermometers have the Fahrenheit scale. The Centigrade scale, on the other hand, is used for scientific measurements of temperature. Both scales are based upon the temperature condition required to change water into a solid or a gas. For the Centigrade scale O is arbitrarily chosen for the freezing point of water, 100 for its boiling point and the range in temperature between these two points is divided into 100 degrees. For the Fahrenheit thermometer 32 is chosen for the freezing point and 212 for the boiling point, in this case the range in temperature between these two points being divided into 180 degrees. In other words, exactly the same range in temperature is represented by 100 Centigrade degrees and 

180 Fahrenheit degrees. 

Therefore 100 Centigrade degrees = 180 Fahrenheit degrees. 1 180 100 

1.8 Fahrenheit degrees.

WATER 

75 

B. P. Centigrade 100 

100 Divisions 

Fahrenheit 

180 Divisions 212 F. P. 32 Remembering the difference in the placing of the freezing and the boiling points and the Fahrenheit equivalent of 

1 Centigrade degree, if we know the temperature as recorded on one scale it is possible to change it into the other. For example, suppose we wish to change 158° F. to a Centigrade reading. As the freezing point on the Fahrenheit scale is placed at 32, 158 degrees is really 126 degrees (15832) above freezing. Since 1 Centigrade degree = 1.8 

Fahrenheit degree, 126, the number of Fahrenheit degrees above freezing, divided by 1.8 will represent the number of 

Centigrade degrees above freezing on the Centigrade reading as the freezing point on this scale is 0°. 158°F = 158-32 1.8 70°C 

To change 25° Centigrade to the corresponding Fahrenheit reading, it is first necessary to multiply by 1.8 since 1 Centigrade degree is equivalent to 1.8 Fahrenheit degree. The product 45 represents the number of Fahrenheit degrees above the freezing point which is placed at 32 on this scale. 

It is necessary, therefore, to add 32 to 45 to find the corre sponding Fahrenheit reading. 

25°C = (25 x 1.8) + 32 = 77°F

Heat Capacity.-Substances vary in the amount of heat necessary to raise their temperature. Water can be subjected to more heat than most other substances without

a marked temperature change. The rock and the water at the sea shore are both exposed to the same sunshine and yet the rock very soon becomes uncomfortably hot while the water shows little or no change. This heat capacity of a substance, or specific heat as it is called, is measured by calories (see Chapter III). Because of the great heat capacity of water, parts of the country near large bodies of water show few extremes in temperature conditions. 

They are not so apt to be very hot in summer because a large amount of the heat is absorbed by the water. The cold weather in winter is moderated because of the gradual release of the heat which has been absorbed during the summer.

Pressure and the Boiling Point.-The transformation of water from a liquid to a gaseous form occurs slowly even when it is cold. As the temperature is raised, however, there is an increasingly large amount vaporized until the boiling point is reached. Then the entire surface of the liquid is in motion and the pressure of the vaporized water equals the pressure of the air upon it. The temperature does not rise beyond this point, steam and water that is boiling either rapidly or slowly being 100° C. at sea level. 

At higher altitudes, however, where the atmospheric pressure is less, the boiling point is lower. It is possible to raise the temperature at which water will boil by increasing the pressure. This is made use of in the autoclave, a piece of apparatus in which sterilization is carried on at high temperatures. In this the pressure is increased by not permitting the steam to escape. Pressure cookers work on the same principle. As there is a very great increase in volume when water is vaporized, a high pressure can be obtained easily. 

Heat of Vaporization and Fusion.-Although the temperature does not rise beyond the boiling point, a continual application of heat is necessary to keep the liquid boiling.

WATER 

77 The question naturally arises: What becomes of this heat? 

It is absorbed or stored up temporarily as the liquid is changed into a gas and is called the latent heat of vaporization. The heat capacity of water to which we have already referred is very greatly increased when there is a change in its physical form. When being changed into steam, 1 gram of water can absorb 540 calories of heat. All liquids absorb heat on evaporation, but the amount differs with the liquid. There are many important applications of this fact. The freezing which results from the spray of a local anæsthetic such as ethyl chloride is due to the absorption of an unusually large amount of heat because of its rapid evaporation. Much heat is lost from the body through the evaporation of perspiration. When steam is again condensed to a liquid the heat of vaporization is released. 

For this reason a steam burn is very much more severe than one caused by boiling water, although the actual temperatures of the two are the same.

Water is frozen by lowering the temperature or, in other words, by causing heat to be released from it. For every gram of water there is liberated, on freezing, 79 calories of heat without causing the temperature to go below the freezing point. Conversely, ice is melted by the application of heat and 79 calories of heat can be absorbed by 1 gram of ice before the temperature rises above the melting (freezing) point. This is called the latent heat of fusion. Generally, when the temperature of liquids or solids is lowered there is a corresponding contraction in volume. This holds true for water until it reaches the temperature of 4° C., at which point it is most dense. 

The metric unit of weight, 1 gram, was arbitrarily fixed as the weight of 1 cubic centimeter of water at 4° C.that being the temperature at which it is heaviest. As the temperature continues to fall below this it begins to increase in volume. At the freezing point as it changes

to a solid this expansion becomes very marked. This accounts for the bursting of water pipes, also for the fact that ice floats.

Solutions. From the standpoint of practical use one of the most important characteristics of water is its ability to dissolve substances. The dissolved material is called the solute, the dissolving material the solvent and the two together the solution. Because it will dissolve a greater variety of substances than any other liquid, water is sometimes spoken of as the universal solvent. Although we usually think of the solute as having been a solid, a solvent can also hold in solution either a liquid or a gas. Glycerine dissolved in water, or oil dissolved in gasoline are examples of liquids in solution in another liquid. Reference has already been made to the solubility of oxygen (a gas) in water (see Chapter IV). Many substances insoluble in water can be dissolved by other liquids such as alcohol, ether and gasoline. Solutions in alcohol are usually called tinctures, as tincture of iodine.

When the solute, sugar for example, is stirred into the solvent it seems to disappear as it dissolves. That it has not lost its identity, however, and that it is uniformly distributed throughout the solution are apparent because of the taste. Because of certain other characteristics of the solution, we know the dissolved particles of sugar must be very small. No matter how long it stands (without evaporating) the liquid remains clear and transparent. When the solution is filtered, the sugar particles must pass through the filter paper, for the liquid is still sweet. In the case of the solution of sugar and similar substances, the dissolved particles can also pass through an animal or parchment membrane, or undergo osmosis. For this process to occur, a membrane wall must separate two liquids. Either one or both liquids may contain substances in solution but in different amounts. Dissolved particles can go from one liquid to the other only by passing through the membrane.

WATER 

79 It is by this process that the digestion products pass from the alimentary tract into the blood stream by which they are distributed throughout the body. Sometimes the dissolved substance is made insoluble or is precipitated out of its solution. A liquid containing insoluble particles is usually called a suspension. These particles are very much larger than the dissolved particles of a solution. With milk of magnesia as an example we can see how the difference in the size of the particles affects the characteristics. The liquid is milky and opaque when shaken, but on standing the particles settle out. They cannot pass through either a filter paper or an animal or parchment membrane. 

Between a solution and a suspension is a condition which is called colloidal solution of which gelatine dissolved in water is an example. In some aspects it resembles a solution of sugar in water which, in contrast, is called a true solution. In both cases the dissolved particles are small enough to remain uniformly distributed throughout the liquid no matter how long it stands. After filtering, the filtrates still contain the dissolved substance in each case, the particles being small enough to pass through the filter paper. In the case of the colloidal solution, however, the solute cannot undergo osmosis as the particles are too large to pass through a membrane. While separate particles cannot be distinguished the solution appears transluscent instead of clear and transparent. The substances forming true solutions are called crystalloids because they are left in a crystalline form after the solvent has evaporated. The others, which are called colloids, are usually left as a jellylike or gummy mass. It was once thought that soluble solids could be classified as either crystalloids or colloids on the basis of their ability to crystallize. However, it has been found possible to crystallize some of the solids which form colloidal solutions, albumin and hemoglobin for example. 

Solubility. When a lump of a solid substance such as

alum is dissolved in a given amount of water, a longer time is required than if the alum is powdered. The amount of alum, however, that will dissolve in the water under the given conditions remains the same whether it is powdered or in a lump. In other words, its solubility is unaffected. 

By the solubility of a substance, then, is meant the amount of it that will dissolve in a definite amount of the solvent under a given condition. The condition must be specified, for temperature affects the solubility of many substances, and in the case of gases, pressure also influences it. In general the solubility of solids is increased by a rise in temperature, though there are some exceptions. In the case of calcium hydroxide, the solubility is decreased by raising the temperature. With some substances, sodium chloride for example, temperature makes little difference with the amount that can be dissolved. In the following illustrations, the figures represent the number of grams of each solid soluble in 100 cc. (grams) of water at the specified temperature:

Borax 

Alum 

Sodium chloride 

Calcium hydroxide 0° 40° 80° 100° 3 18 76 201 31 135 358 35.7 36.6 38.4 39.8 0.17 0.116 0.084 0.066 By A rise in temperature has no effect upon the solubility of a liquid unless it is one that can be easily vaporized. The amount of alcohol dissolved in water can be decreased by heating because of the vaporization of the alcohol. An increase in temperature will lessen the amount of gas in solution, since a gas always expands when heated. increasing the pressure, the amount of gas in solution will be increased. Charged waters and effervescent beverages like ginger ale contain dissolved carbon dioxide which has been put in solution under pressure. Therefore, the liquid is able to hold in solution a larger amount of it than it can under a condition of atmospheric pressure. When the

WATER 

81 זי Just rea study seal is removed from the bottle, the liquid returns to normal pressure and at once the gas in excess of what can then be held in solution begins to escape. The result is the bubbling, or effervescence, which is greatest when the seal is first removed.

Expressions of Concentration.-We use various terms to represent the amounts of solute and solvent in a solution. Some of these expressions give only a general idea of the amounts. Solutions are called "dilute," for example, if the amount of solute is very small. Others are called concentrated if there is a relatively large amount of solute in proportion to the solvent. A solution is "saturated" if it is holding all of the solute it can under the existing temperature and pressure conditions. If it is holding less than this amount, it is said to be "unsaturated.” If the solubility of a substance is increased by a rise in temperature, a saturated solution will hold a larger amount of the dissolved substance when it is hot than it will if it is cooler. In a few cases, Glauber's salt, for example, if the hot saturated solution is cooled very carefully it will continue to keep in a dissolved state the entire amount it had been holding in solution at the higher temperature, even though this is in excess of the solubility of the substance at the lower temperature. Such a solution is called "supersaturated." 

Disturbing it suddenly or dropping in a small crystal will cause a sudden crystallization of all that is in excess of its normal solubility at that temperature. So long as a solution is unsaturated when more of the solute is added it will go into solution. If it is already saturated, any additional solid will remain undissolved. If it is supersaturated, the amount of undissolved solid will be greater than the amount that is added.

There are some very definite expressions of the concentration of a solution. For example, a 10 per cent solution is one containing 10 parts by weight of the solute for every

100 parts by weight of the solution (solute + solvent). 

Since 1 cc. of water weighs 1 gram (at 4° C.) 100 grams of a 10 per cent sodium chloride solution would contain 

10 grams of sodium chloride and 90 grams (= cc.) of water. 

To make up 100 grams of a 0.7 per cent solution, we should dissolve 0.7 gram of sodium chloride in 99.3 grams (= cc.) water. Every per cent solution represents the amount by weight of the solute in 100 grams of the solution. 

Another solution representing the exact amount of solute present is called a normal solution. A liter (1000 cc.) of such a solution contains the number of grams of the solute that will bring into reaction 1 gram atomic weight (1 gram) of hydrogen, or its equivalent. A gram atomic weight of any element is the number representing its atomic weight expressed definitely in grams. Note the following examples: Element Hydrogen 

Atomic weight Gram atomic weight 1 gram Oxygen

Sodium 1 16 23 35.5 16 grams 23 grams Chlorine 

35.5 grams

By an equivalent of hydrogen is meant the number of hydrogen atoms one atom of another element can either replace or hold in combination. It does not mean equal weights. The formula for hydrochloric acid (HCl) represents 1 chlorine atom holding in combination 1 atom of hydrogen. Therefore, 1 chlorine atom is the equivalent of 1 hydrogen atom. In the formula for sulphuric acid (H₂SO), the sulphate radical (SO4) represents a group of atoms which acts as a single unit. It is regarded as the equivalent of two hydrogen atoms because it holds two of them in combination to form a molecule of sulphuric acid.

In the same way the formula for phosphoric acid (H,PO₁) represents the phosphate radical (PO4) as being the equivalent of three hydrogen atoms. A comparison of the formulas of sodium chloride (NaCl) and hydrochloric acid (HCl),

WATER sodium sulphate (Na2SO4) and sulphuric acid (H₂SO4) and sodium phosphate (Na,PO4) and phosphoric acid (H,PO4) shows that one sodium atom is the equivalent of one hydrogen atom, for in each case one or more sodium atoms is in place of a corresponding number of hydrogen atoms. HCI

NaCl H2SO4 Na2SO4 H3PO4 Na3PO4 Since a liter of a normal solution must contain enough of the dissolved material to bring into reaction one gram atomic weight of hydrogen or its equivalent how much will be required for a normal solution of sodium chloride? 

One molecular weight of sodium chloride will (NaCl) (23+ 35.5) supply one atomic weight of sodium (= 1 atomic weight of hydrogen). Therefore, 58.5 grams of sodium chloride dissolved in a liter of the solution will supply the equivalent of one gram of hydrogen. One molecular weight of sodium (Na 2SO4) will furnish two atomic weights sulphate (46 +32 +64) of sodium (= 2 atomic weights of hydrogen). One atomic weight of sodium will be furnished by one-half a molecular weight of sodium sulphate. A liter of a normal solution of this compound, therefore, must contain 71 grams (142) of the solute. Since one molecular weight of sodium phosphate (NaPO4) supplies three atomic weights of sodium (3 atomic weights of hydrogen) the equivalent of one atomic weight of hydrogen will be supplied by one-third the molecular weight (69 – '69 +31 +64 3 +64). 

A liter of its normal solution should contain, then, 54.6 grams of the solid.

The term normal is sometimes used with an entirely dif-

ferent meaning in connection with a sodium chloride solution, as when we speak of a physiological normal or a normal saline solution. In this case we mean a solution whose concentration is the same as the concentration of sodium chloride in the body fluids, especially the blood. Actually it is a solution varying in concentration from 0.7 per cent to 0.9 per cent, which is very much more dilute than the normal solution we have just described as containing 58.5 grams per liter. A normal saline solution must be used for infusions into the blood stream or the red corpuscles will be seriously damaged. If pure water is used, or a salt solution whose concentration is less than 0.7 per cent. the concentration of the blood plasma will be made lower than that of the contents of the corpuscles. Water, therefore, will pass through their surrounding walls into their interior, causing them to swell and become ruptured or laked. The process is called hemolysis. If, on the other hand, a salt solution whose concentration is greater than that of the normal saline solution is used, the density of the blood plasma is made greater than that of the contents of the corpuscles. In an effort to bring about an adjustment so that they will have equal concentrations or be "isotonic," water is withdrawn from the corpuscles and they become shrunken or crenated. This process is called plasmolysis. 

The usefulness of a strong salt solution as a gargle may be due to the plasmolysis of the bacteria present in the infected throat.

Chemical Properties.-Water is a very stable compound, being decomposed by heat to a slight extent, but only at an extremely high temperature. It does, however, enter into a number of important chemical reactions. We have already referred to the varying ability of metals to react with water to form free hydrogen and either the oxide or the hydroxide of the metal (see Chapter V). It can also combine with oxides, the resulting product being either a

WATER hydroxide or an acid, depending upon the kind of oxide. 

The oxide of a metal, as calcium oxide or quicklime (CaO), reacts with water to form calcium hydroxide or slaked lime (Ca (OH)2). CaO + H,O Ca (OH)2 The oxide of a non-metal, on the other hand, as carbon dioxide, combines with water to form carbonic acid (H2CO3).

CO2 + H2O → H2CO3 (See Chapters IX and X for more detailed discussion.)

Another very important reaction in which water is involved is called hydrolysis. As the name implies, it consists in a splitting (lysis) or decomposition of a compound into simpler compounds through a chemical reaction with water. The changes occurring in foods during digestion are due to hydrolysis. Cane sugar or sucrose (C12H22O11), for example, reacts with water, the products being glucose (C6H12O6) and fructose (C,H12O6). C12H22O11+ H₂O →C6H12O6 + C6H12O6 As both reacting substances and the products of the reaction are compounds, hydrolysis is a special type of double decomposition. It can be defined as a double decomposition in which water is one of the reacting agents. 

Water is able to enter into a loose chemical combination with certain substances to form products called hydrates. 

This union occurs as the substance crystallizes out of a solution, the water which combines with it being called water of hydration or water of crystallization. The latter term, however, is misleading, for all crystalline substances, cane sugar for example, do not contain water of hydration. 

Among the examples of hydrates are crystalline washing soda (Na2CO3.10H2O), copper sulphate (CuSO4.5H2O), calcium chloride (CaCl2.2H₂O) and gypsum or calcium

sulphate (CaSO4.2 H₂O). 

The same substance can also occur without water of hydration. In this condition it is called anhydrous. When forming the hydrate, a substance always takes up a definite amount of water. 

In the formation of washing soda crystals, one molecule of washing soda always combines with ten molecules of water. For this reason the product is sometimes called sodium carbonate decahydrate.

Na2CO3 + 10 H₂O Na2CO3.10 H₂O 

In certain cases there are quite marked differences in the characteristics of the hydrate and the anhydrous form. 

Hydrated copper sulphate is a blue, crystalline solid, whereas anhydrous copper sulphate is a gray white powder. 

For these reasons the process of taking up water of hydration is interpreted as a chemical rather than a physical change. In the formula for the product, the water is represented as a part of the molecule, but in such a way as to keep in mind the relation between the two forms and also to signify that it is in only a loose or temporary state of combination (see formulas in preceding paragraph). 

Heat will in all cases cause a hydrate to give up its water of hydration, but some are so unstable that they lose their water of hydration and with it their crystalline form on exposure to the air. They are called efflorescent substances. This accounts for the gradual change in the appearance and form of washing soda from transparent crystals to a white powder. Certain hydrates part with their water of hydration with greater difficulty, and when it has been removed the anhydrous substance may again take it up on exposure to moisture. These substances are said to deliquesce or be deliquescent. Fused or anhydrous calcium chloride is valuable as a drying agent because of its great readiness to take up moisture. Cobalt chloride is another substance which will either give up or take up

WATER water of hydration. The hydrated form is pink, the anhydrous blue. This color change is made use of as an indicator of moisture in the air, paper or cloth colored with the compound becoming pink or blue according to the changes in the water vapor content of the air. 

The use of plaster of Paris for making surgical casts depends upon the difference in the characteristics of calcium sulphate in its different forms. Gypsum is calcium sulphate, every molecule of which contains two molecules of water of hydration (CaSO4.2 H₂O). By heating it can be made to give up part or all of its water of hydration. 

Plaster of Paris (CaSO4)2.H2O) still contains a small amount but very much less than gypsum. When it is mixed with water and allowed to stand, the mass stiffens or "sets" because of the formation of gypsum crystals.

During the process it swells so that all portions of the mold in which the cast is made become filled. Heat is also liberated and therefore the cast feels warm. Should the water of hydration have been completely removed from the gypsum in the first place, although the anhydrous calcium sulphate can again combine with water, the action takes place too slowly for it to be useful for making casts. In order to make the cast impervious to moisture, it is often coated with shellac. The use of acetic acid or hydrogen peroxide makes the removal of the cast less difficult. The acid accomplishes this result by increasing the solubility of the gypsum. Through the decomposition of the hydrogen peroxide, free oxygen is formed and the force of the small bubbles of escaping gas helps to disintegrate the cast. 

Importance of Water.-Water is of such vital importance to the body that a person can go without food for a longer time than he can without it. We have already referred to the chemical change of hydrolysis for which water is necessary. This type of change occurs not only in the process of digestion but is also responsible for the

decomposition of many of the complex substances throughout the body. Water is an essential constituent of all the body fluids to provide a solvent for the other substances which they contain. Blood, for example, is the transportation system of the body. Many of the substances, food materials on the one hand, waste products on the other, are carried in solution in the water, which is therefore an important part of the blood. It is a constituent of the protoplasm of every cell. If for any reason the water content of the cell is decreased because the fluid in contact with it has an abnormally high concentration, the cells may become damaged through a withdrawal of their water content. The ingestion of a large amount of sugar causes an irritation of the mucous membrane of the stomach because of the increased concentration of its fluid content. This suggests another need for water, as a diluent to maintain the proper concentration of the body fluids. After eating a considerable amount of sugar, the thirst we feel is the body's demand for water to decrease the concentration of the stomach contents. Water also serves as a lubricant to keep surface membranes moist. Without such lubrication, a feeling of irritation or discomfort would arise. The regulation of the body temperature is dependent upon the water content. Through the process of oxidation, heat is being constantly produced throughout the body, and yet under normal conditions the temperature not only remains 98.5° 

F. but is practically uniform throughout the body. The water content helps to regulate the body temperature, partly by distributing the heat equally through the body largely by means of the blood. Eighty-eight per cent of the heat lost from the body is removed through the evaporation of water in perspiration. The amount of water present in food material while in the digestive tract, particularly the large intestine, has a very direct influence on the removal of the undigested and unabsorbed waste. Feces which are dry and

WATER hard are naturally much more difficult to evacuate and a condition of constipation results. The presence of moisture in feces prevents this condition. An unduly large amount of moisture may cause diarrhea. Certain cathartics, as 

Epsom salt (magnesium sulphate), function by causing a withdrawal of water from the surrounding tissues to supply what is needed to increase the moisture content of the feces. 

The laxative effect of agar-agar is due to the large amount of water it can absorb and so introduce into the digestive tract.

Water is constantly being lost from the body. From the skin it is removed through perspiration, the amount varying with the individual and with other conditions such as temperature and activity. Part leaves the body by way of the lungs in the expired air. Part escapes through the digestive tract in the feces. In a chronic case of diarrhea this may result in a serious loss. The output through the kidneys averages 1400 cc. daily.

Although water is one of the waste products resulting from the oxidation of either food material or body tissue, the amount lost from the body, even in normal conditions, is far in excess of the amount produced. Consequently it is necessary to make these losses good. Many people drink far too little water and as a result the well-being of their bodies is affected. Two quarts daily is a good average allowance. For the greater part of it we depend upon water itself or different beverages. Foods also supply water, the more green vegetables and fruits we include in our diet the greater being the amount contributed in this way.

Because of the belief in the great influence water has on the welfare of the body, a form of therapeutic treatment, called hydrotherapy, is based upon its use. There are many types of natural mineral waters containing in solution sufficient amounts of different substances to cause marked

tastes or odors. Those having a salty taste owe it principally to sodium chloride. Magnesium compounds cause a bitter taste. A metallic taste is due usually to iron compounds. The objectionable odor of many waters is due to sulphur compounds, in some cases hydrogen sulphide. 

Effervescence or bubbling is caused by carbon dioxide or sodium bicarbonate. Whether the beneficial effects resulting from their use can be attributed to the dissolved substances, or whether it is due to the increased intake of water and a more regular life during "the cure," may be a question.

From the household standpoint, water is of very great importance. In heating systems, it is made use of as a means of heat distribution. Its importance in the preparation of food, in cleansing processes and in carrying away household wastes scarcely needs to be mentioned. We do not always realize, however, our great dependence upon a water supply for our daily needs until that supply is materially decreased or is entirely cut off.

Industries are also very dependent upon a water supply. 

The power for carrying on their processes comes either directly or indirectly from water (see Energy, Chapter III). 

The processes themselves in many cases depend upon the use of solutions, dye baths, for example, in a dye house, or bleaching powder solutions in a bleachery.

Sources of a Water Supply.-The source of a water supply and the content of the water we use is, therefore, of very great importance to us. To trace a water supply back to what we might consider its original source suppose we start with the water vapor in the air. This in turn has been formed by the constant evaporation of the surface waters of the earth. When atmospheric conditions make it impossible for the water vapor to remain in the air, it condenses or sometimes condenses and freezes, and then returns to the earth, principally as rain. Although rain

WATER 

91 water is considered the purest of natural waters, its purity is variable. In the beginning of a storm it brings down with it oxygen, nitrogen and carbon dioxide of the air since these gases are soluble. It also carries down dust particles. 

Through later contact with roofs, more dust and dirt and possibly materials from the roof itself can be added to the water. The reservoir or cistern in which the water is sometimes caught and stored may serve to contaminate it still further. Rain water is, then, the purest form of natural water only if it is caught after the air has been well "washed" and if contamination through later contacts is reduced to a minimum. Such water is often very desirable for laundry purposes (see hard water). It can also be used for filling storage batteries if distilled water is unavailable.

After rain once reaches the ground, opportunities for taking up all manner of substances are greatly increased. 

They may either remain in suspension or become dissolved in the water. Among the insoluble materials may be clay or sand, lifeless animal and vegetable matter in varying stages of decomposition, and living plant and animal organisms. The soluble substances may consist of animal and vegetable material, nitrogen compounds resulting from its decomposition, sodium chloride, iodides, and calcium, magnesium and iron compounds.

Fitness for Use.-The undesirable nature of any of these materials will depend upon the use to be made of the water. 

If used for drinking or in the preparation of food, the presence of plant or animal material in various states of decomposition would certainly be objectionable because of the taste, odor or color imparted to the water if for no other reason. It might not be harmful, at least directly. By furnishing food or a breeding place for living plant and animal organisms, however, it might be indirectly a source of danger. All living organisms found in water are not

harmful. Fortunately the majority of them are harmless.

There are some disease-producing organisms, typhoid and cholera for example, which leave the diseased person in the excreta, and so in cases of sewage contamination there is always the possibility of their presence in water. For this reason a suspected water should be submitted to both bacteriological and chemical tests. Ammonium compounds and nitrates are formed through the decomposition of animal and vegetable material. They are, therefore, an indication of the previous presence of the latter and possibly of micro-organisms. The presence of chlorides in the water in amounts that cannot be explained by natural deposits of salt in the earth are an evidence of sewage contamination and, therefore, undesirable. Calcium and magnesium compounds may be undesirable in cases of hypertension if present in water in considerable amounts. 

A deficiency of iodides is undesirable. In certain parts of the country where they are noticeably deficient or lacking, cases of goiter are very common. A relation between the two is now recognized and foods contributing iodides or iodide salt are made use of to make up for the deficiency. 

We often speak of a water as being pure when we really mean that it is safe or wholesome. Outside the laboratory there is no pure water if we use that term in its strictest sense. By a safe or wholesome water is meant one that is not objectionable from the standpoint of clearness, color, taste and odor. It contains no disease-producing organisms and the mineral constituents are not present in unduly large amounts. How can we be sure that the water we are drinking meets these requirements? Not from its appearance, for the water which is clear, sparkling and tasteless may be more harmful than that having a decided color, taste and odor. Bacteriological and chemical tests are the only safe guarantee that a water is fit for use.

Purification. -A water which has been contaminated can

WATER 

93 

The be subjected to different methods of purification. amount of good accomplished by the purification will depend upon the method. Boiling removes nothing from the water but dissolved gases. It does, however, kill microorganisms which may have been present. The resulting flat taste is due to the loss of the gases. Distillation accomplishes more than boiling. In this process the vaporized water is conducted through a cold tube in which it is again condensed. The distilled water no longer contains any of the insoluble or soluble solids which were present previously. It does not lose any of the dissolved gases it may have held, for these will pass over with the water vapor. 

Filtering removes only undissolved material. In a small way this is done by certain types of household filters. If they are not kept clean, however, the water will be in worse condition after it has passed through the filter than it was before. On a larger scale the filtering process is carried on by allowing water to seep through filter beds which consist of layers of materials varying in the degree of fineness. As surface water percolates through the ground into underground streams, it goes through a natural process of filtration. Water coming from deep wells or an underground stream will contain less insoluble material than water taken from shallow wells, streams or lakes. In addition to these mechanical methods of purification, there are chemical methods by which harmful or objectionable substances can be changed into other substances which are harmless or not objectionable. They are for the most part processes of oxidation. In some cases atmospheric oxygen is relied upon. The process is made more efficient by increasing the surface of the water in direct contact with the air. This is accomplished by spraying it up into the air for several feet so that both in its upward journey and as it returns it comes in contact with the air. The finer the spray and the more it is blown into a mist on a windy day, the greater

will be the contact with oxygen. In some cases oxidizing agents are used. Among those in common use are ozone, chlorine and bleaching powder.

Hard Water.-When water is to be used for cleaning purposes, the presence of soluble magnesium, calcium and iron compounds is most objectionable because when soap is used in the water a sticky scum is formed before a lather can be obtained. Such water is called hard. Water coming from deep wells or underground streams is more likely to contain these substances. The chlorides and sulphates of magnesium or calcium (MgCl2, MgSO4, CaCl2, CaSO4) become dissolved in the water through its contact with the soil and rock as it filters down through the ground. 

The soluble bicarbonate of magnesium, calcium or iron (Mg(HCO3)2, Ca (HCO3)2, Fe(HCO3)2) are formed through a chemical reaction between water containing dissolved carbon dioxide and the corresponding insoluble carbonates (MgCO3, CaCO3, FeCO₂) which form a large part of the rock and stone with which the water comes in contact. CaCO3 + H2O + CO2 →→→→ Ca(HCO3)2 The presence of these substances is very annoying for several reasons. No lather will form until they have been changed into an insoluble, sticky precipitate through a chemical reaction with soap (sodium stearate NaC18H3502).

The sticky scum (calcium stearate, Ca(C18H35O2)2) is sometimes called insoluble soap or lime soap.

CaSO4 + 2 NaC18H3502 →→→ Na2SO4 + Ca(C18H35O2)2+ This precipitate is objectionable because of the difficulty with which it is removed from any surface to which it adheres, as sinks, tubs, or clothing. The large amount of soap required for making a lather is another undesirable feature. If the hardness is caused by iron bicarbonate, the latter, when changed to the insoluble, sticky iron stearate, may on standing undergo oxidation and cause a spot resem-

WATER 

95 bling rust. When water containing the soluble bicarbonates of magnesium, calcium or iron is heated, they are decomposed and the corresponding insoluble carbonates are formed. If this occurs in a teakettle or boiler, in time the insoluble material collects on the inside surface forming a scale. The thickness of the wall is gradually increased and so a greater amount of fuel must be used before the water is heated. This insoluble material will also collect on the inside of the piping which is to distribute the heated water. 

For this reason very hard water is objectionable for use in factories.

Soap affects these compounds only so long as they are in a soluble form. Any method, then, which will change them into insoluble compounds will remove the "hardness" from the water or "soften" it as it is called. We have just described the effect of heat upon the soluble bicarbonates.

Ca(HCO3)2 CaCO3 + H2O + CO₂ If water contains these compounds, it is called temporary hard water as chemical reagents are not required in order to soften it. The soluble sulphates and chlorides of magnesium and calcium cannot be changed into insoluble compounds by heating. Treatment with some chemical reagent is necessary in order to soften it. Water containing these compounds is therefore called permanent hard water. 

Washing soda (Na2CO3), borax (Na2B4O7) and household ammonia (NH4OH) are some of the softening reagents commonly used. With washing soda a reaction occurs with calcium sulphate (CaSO4), for example, in which insoluble calcium carbonate is produced.

Na2CO3 + CaSO4 Na2SO4 + CaCO3+ Soap, itself, is a softening reagent for it changes the soluble compounds which cause the hardness of water into insoluble compounds. Because of the stickiness of the

resulting precipitate and because of the cost of the soap, however, it is much more desirable to depend upon other means for softening the water and to use the soap only for the desired suds. The general result of softening hard water, whatever the means may be, is to change the soluble calcium, magnesium or iron compound into a corresponding compound which is insoluble.

SUMMARY 

Heat Capacity.-The amount of heat necessary to cause a change of 1° in the temperature.

Boiling Point. The temperature at which the pressure of the vaporized liquid equals the atmospheric pressure.

Heat of Vaporization.-The heat absorbed by water during its change from a liquid to a gaseous state without any change (rise) in the temperature.

The heat liberated when the steam from water is condensed to a liquid without causing any change (fall) in the temperature.

Heat of Fusion.-The heat absorbed by ice during its change from a solid to a liquid without any change (rise) in temperature.

The heat liberated by water when it changes from a liquid to a solid without any change (fall) in temperature. 

1 Gram. The weight of 1 cubic centimeter of water at 4° Centigrade. 

Solute.-A dissolved substance. 

Solvent. A dissolving substance.

Solution.-A liquid containing dissolved particles so small that they do not settle out and they can pass through a filter paper with the liquid. If very small they can also pass through a membrane and the solution is clear and transparent. It is a true solution. If the particles are somewhat larger they can not pass through a membrane and the solution is translucent but not transparent. It is a colloidal solution.

Suspension.-A liquid containing larger particles which are undissolved. They can neither pass through a filter paper nor a membrane. They settle out.

WATER 

97 Solubility. The weight of a solute which can be dissolved in a given weight of the solvent under a definite temperature and pressure condition. Usually expressed in figures which represent parts of the solute in 100 parts of water (when it is the solvent).

Saturated Solution. The solvent is holding in solution all the solute it normally can under the existing conditions. 

Unsaturated Solution. The solvent is holding in solution less of the solute than is possible under the existing conditions. 

Supersaturated Solution.-A solution which was saturated at a higher temperature and which continues to hold in solution the entire amount of the solvent when it is cooled. The amount in solution is greater than is normally possible at the lower temperature. % Solution.-Parts by weight of the solute in 100 parts by weight of the solution (solute + solvent). 

Normal Solution.-A solution a liter of which contains 1 gram atomic weight (1 gram) of hydrogen or its equivalent. 

Physiological Salt Solution.-A solution of salt (sodium chloride) whose concentration is the same as the concentration of the same substance in the blood. Its concentration is 

0.7%-0.9%. It is also called isotonic. A solution whose concentration is greater than this is called hypertonic. One with a lower concentration is called hypotonic. 

Hemolysis. A rupturing of red corpuscles because of the introduction into the blood of a hypotonic solution. 

Plasmolysis. A shrinking of red corpuscles because of the withdrawal of water from them due to the introduction into the blood of a hypertonic solution. 

Water of Hydration.—Water taken up in loose chemical combination when a substance crystallizes from a solution. 

Hydrate. A substance which contains water of hydration. 

Anhydrous. Without water of hydration.

Effloresce. To lose water of hydration upon exposure to the air. 

Deliquesce. To gain water of hydration on exposure to the air. 

Hydrolysis. The decomposition of a substance into simpler substances through a chemical reaction with water.

A double decomposition in which water is always one of the reacting agents.

Pure Water.-Contains no substance either in solution or suspension.

Safe or Wholesome Water.-Contains no disease organisms. 

Unobjectionable as to clearness, color, taste and odor. Mineral constituents are not in unduly large amounts. 

Hard Water-Contains soluble calcium, magnesium or iron compounds which react chemically with soap to form a sticky. scum or precipitate.

Soft Water.-Free from soluble calcium, magnesium or iron compounds.

EXPERIMENTS 

1. Solubility (a) Take 2 crystals of copper sulphate of equal size. Pulverize one, placing the powder in one test-tube and the unbroken crystal in another test-tube. Add 20 cc. of distilled water to each. Cork and shake continuously, noting the time required for each to go into solution. How does the size of the particles affect the quickness with which they are dissolved? (b) Put 10 cc. of cold water in a test-tube and add pulverized copper sulphate carefully until no more will dissolve. What is the condition of the solution? Place the tube in a beaker of water and heat to boiling. Continue the addition of powdered sulphate until no more dissolves. What is the condition of the solution? Compare the amount of solid required in the two cases.

Explain. Cool the test-tube by holding it under the running water. Note the result and explain. 

(c) Demonstration-supersaturation: 

Pulverize about 100 grams of sodium sulphate (Glauber's salt) and dissolve it in the least possible amount of water at 30-35° C. (about 25 cc.). Into 3 clean test-tubes carefully pour the resulting solution. Cork and allow them to remain standing undisturbed while they cool. What was the condition of the solution when it was made? Note the appearance of the contents of the test-tubes on cooling and explain. Shake one of the tubes and

describe what happens. Drop a crystal of sodium sulphate in one of the tubes. Note the result. Explain. 

2. Hydrates (a) Examine a small crystal of copper sulphate. Does it appear moist? Place in a test-tube and heat gently. Note the appearance of moisture at the top of the tube. Account for its formation. Continue heating and note any change in the appearance of the copper sulphate. Explain what has taken place.

When there seems to be no further change, cool and moisten with a drop of water. What is the result? Explain. (b) Moisten several pieces of filter paper with cobalt chloride solution and allow them to stand in a warm place until all apparent moisture disappears. What is the color of the paper? It is due to crystals of cobalt chloride (the hydrate). Heat carefully over the flame (avoid scorching). Note the color which is due to anhydrous cobalt chloride. Moisten one of the papers with water; breathe upon another; allow a third paper to remain exposed to the air for some time, examining it occasionally. 

What is the result in each case? Explain. (c) On clean watch glasses place a crystal of washing soda (sodium carbonate) and a piece of anhydrous calcium chloride. 

Leave both exposed to the air and examine from time to time. 

Describe what takes place and explain. 

3. Refer to Chapter III, Experiment 1, (b) 

Explain the chemical change taking place when water is added to quicklime (calcium oxide). A solution of the product (calcium hydroxide or slaked lime) in water is called limewater. A suspension of calcium hydroxide in water is called milk of lime. 

4. Demonstration-Purification 

Prepare a sample of impure water containing common types of impurities by adding to it the following: 

clay or earthy material insoluble substance sodium chloride -soluble solid potassium permanganate-soluble solid ammonium hydroxide -soluble gas (a) Distillation: 

taste - color odor Place 100 cc. of the prepared sample in a distilling flask or round bottom Florence flask. Connect with a condenser and arrange a receiving flask for the collection of the distillate.

WATER 

101 Heat the contents of the distilling flask and collect about 50 cc. of the distillate. Compare its clearness, color, taste and odor with some of the original liquid. What impurities have been removed? What impurities remain? Boil the distillate slowly until it is concentrated to one-half its volume. Does boiling remove any impurity that distillation cannot remove? Examine the liquid remaining in the distilling flask. What impurities car boiling not remove? (b) Sand filtration:

In the bottom of a large glass funnel fit a small filter paper and then fill the funnel carefully with clean sand. The paper is to keep the sand from falling into the stem of the funnel but is not to aid in the filtration. Pour 100 cc. of the prepared water through the sand and collect the filtrate. Compare with an unfiltered sample. What impurities have been removed? What impurities still remain?

Permanent hard water can be prepared by dissolving 0.11 gram anhydrous calcium chloride (0.22 gm. hydrated calcium chloride) and making the solution up to 1 liter. 

Temporary hard water can be prepared as follows: Add 

300 cc. distilled water to 44.8 cc. of a saturated solution of calcium hydroxide. Generate carbon dioxide by the action of an acid on a carbonate and allow the gas to run into the diluted calcium hydroxide solution until the precipitate of calcium carbonate which first forms re-dissolves. (a) Take 20 cc. each of soft water (distilled water), water which is permanently hard and water which is temporarily hard. 

Place each sample in a clean glass bottle or a glass cylinder and add soap solution from a known amount in a burette or a graduated cylinder. Shake after each addition of soap solution until a lather forms which lasts a minute and is the same thickness in each case. 

Note the appearance of the water and the amount of soap solution necessary in each case. Explain. (b) Boil for 5 minutes 20 cc. each of the permanent hard water and the temporary hard water. Examine. Is there any difference in the appearance? Explain. Make up to the original volume of 20 cc. by adding distilled water then make the soap solution test as in the preceding experiment. Compare the results obtained with the boiled water with those obtained with

the unboiled water and explain. Why is the water called temporarily hard? Permanently hard? (c) To 20 cc. each of the permanent hard water and the temporary hard water add a small crystal of washing soda. 

Is there any difference in the appearance? If so explain. Make the soap solution tests, compare the results with those obtained in (a) and explain.

CHAPTER VII NITROGEN History. In 1772 a Scottish chemist named Rutherford discovered the element nitrogen. A short time afterwards its presence in the air was recognized by Scheele, the Swedish chemist, whose experiments with oxygen have been referred to. Lavoisier first named it azote, meaning without life, because of the fact that it cannot support life. 

Later it was renamed nitrogen as it is one of the important constituents of niter or saltpeter (potassium nitrate KNO3). 

Occurrence.-Like oxygen, nitrogen occurs in the free state only in the air of which it makes up three-quarters of the weight and four-fifths of the volume. It is an important constituent of many compounds. Among them are complex organic compounds, called proteins, which make up a very essential part of all living tissue, both plant and animal. Simple nitrogen compounds, nitrates of potassium and sodium, are present in the soil. An exceptionally large deposit of sodium nitrate (NaNO3) occurs in Chile, and for this reason it is called Chile saltpeter. 

Preparation.-Free nitrogen can be obtained from the air by separating it from the oxygen with which it is mixed. 

In one method the air is liquefied and then more normal conditions of temperature and pressure are restored. As nitrogen returns to a gaseous state more readily than oxygen, it can be removed while the latter still remains in a liquid form. This can also be used as a method for obtaining oxygen. Another means by which nitrogen can be obtained from air is to allow moist phosphorus to remain in contact with it. By using a glass clyinder filled with air 103

and inverted over water, the relative volumes of nitrogen and oxygen can be demonstrated in this way. As the phosphorus combines with the latter element, a soluble product, phosphorus pentoxide, is formed. Water gradually rises in the cylinder to fill the space formerly occupied by the oxygen and when the action is complete the cylinder remains four-fifths full of gas. Free nitrogen can also be obtained from compounds containing it. Equal volumes of nitrogen obtained from the air and from nitrogen compounds do not have the same weight, the former being heavier. This difference in weight led to further studies which resulted in the discovery of traces of other inert gases in the air (see Chapter VIII). 

Physical Properties. Since air is four-fifths nitrogen, we know that this element is a colorless, tasteless and odorless gas. Its ability to be liquefied makes possible one method of obtaining it from air. It is slightly soluble in water. 

For this reason rain water can contain small amounts of dissolved nitrogen.

Chemical Properties. At ordinary temperatures, nitrogen is a very stable and inactive element. In spite of the large proportion present in the air there is little or no appreciable change resulting from its constant contact with a great variety of substances. Whatever change does occur is due to the effect of the oxygen, but its activity is very much decreased by dilution with an inert gas. Even at higher temperatures, nitrogen can be made to combine with only a few of the more active elements. When magnesium is burned, while the greater part of it combines with oxygen to form magnesium oxide, a small amount of magnesium nitride (Mg,N₂) may also be formed through combination with nitrogen. Union with hydrogen can be made to take place with difficulty, the product being a gas, ammonia (NH3). 

N2 + 3 H2 2 NH3

NITROGEN 

105 This is now made use of as one of the methods of making ammonia. Nitrogen forms a series of five oxides, the compounds being as follows:

N20 NO (N2O2) N2O3 NO2 (N2O4) N2O5 Nitrous oxide-nitrogen monoxide-laughing gas 

Nitric oxide-nitrogen dioxide 

Nitrogen trioxide 

Nitrogen peroxide-nitrogen tetraoxide 

Nitrogen pentoxide

Importance of Nitrogen to the Body.-Among the constituents of all living tissue, complex nitrogen compounds known as proteins occupy a very important place. The animal organism, whether lower forms of animal life or a highly specialized human being, can get the nitrogen necessary for the upkeep of these compounds from other plant or animal proteins alone. In other words, the proteins we eat in foods occupy a special place in their function, for the necessary nitrogenous tissue-building material can be furnished only by them. Plant organisms can construct their proteins out of the nitrates which they obtain from the soil. A few varieties of plants, such as the clover, possess the ability of making use of the free nitrogen of the air. Because of the presence of certain kinds of bacteria in nodules on the roots of these plants, free nitrogen can be built up into nitrates. These nitrates, together with others taken from the soil, are then built up in successive stages until the plant protein is constructed. An animal, perhaps a cow, then comes along, eats the clover and makes over the clover proteins into either beef or milk proteins. We, in turn, eat the beef or drink the milk and rebuild the proteins into others suitable for new tissue, or for the repair of worn-out tissue.

Importance of Nitrates to Agriculture.—While plant proteins can come in part from the free nitrogen of the air, the life of the plant and its ability to create these compounds depend mainly upon the presence of nitrates

in the soil. In some cases they are present in amounts sufficient for the needs of the plant. If, however, the natural supply has been drawn upon too heavily, the soil must be enriched either through the use of artificial fertilizers, which consist partly of nitrates, or through the use of manures. The latter consists of decomposing complex compounds, partly protein, and as a result of their decomposition nitrates are formed. We have already referred to the presence of nitrates in water as an evidence of plant or animal material (see Chapter VI). The natural supply of nitrates is not only very limited, but it occurs in few places in amounts large enough to be drawn upon for industrial use. Processes have been developed, however, whereby the nitrogen of the air, which is practically limitless, can be converted into nitrates. For us, the development of such a process is of great importance, for without it we are dependent upon a nitrate supply outside our own country. The recent World War has impressed upon us the necessity of having an independent means of obtaining substances which are of such vital importance in peace as well as in war times. 

Nitrogen Cycle.-Nitrogen in the form of nitrates is returned to the soil in a natural way, but the process is very slow.

Through the decay of plant and animal materials the nitrogen-containing compounds are broken down. If the material, either plant or animal, is in a lifeless form when the decomposition occurs, ammonia (NH¸) is one of the early products. If the decomposition occurs within a living animal organism, urea ((NH2)2CO) is one of the first nitrogenous decomposition products. It is, in fact, the principal nitrogenous end product resulting from the decomposition of proteins in the body. It in turn is converted into ammonia. Through oxidation processes, ammonia becomes changed to nitrites and then to nitrates, which are the final oxidation products resulting from the

NITROGEN 

107 decomposition. At this point the building-up process begins when the plant takes the nitrate and out of it constructs plant proteins. This is called the nitrogen cycle. 

It can be represented graphically as follows:

Animal Protein 

Living 1 

Animal Protein 

Lifeless Plant

Protein Urea 

Ammonia 

Nitrites Nitrates

Importance of Nitrogen Compounds to Hospitals.- 

Because of their importance in medicine there are a few nitrates which are of particular interest. One of these is nitroglycerine or glyceryl nitrate (C3H5(NO3)3). As it is also a very dangerous explosive, it must be handled and stored with great care. Heat, friction and jars are the conditions to avoid. Because it lessens muscular contraction it can sometimes be used to decrease the rigidity of the artery walls in cases of arteriosclerosis. Its chief use is in preventing attacks of angina pectoris by keeping the coronary artery relaxed. Another important nitrate is silver nitrate (AgNO3). This is used either in the form of pencils or in various solutions alone or with other substances to

decrease the activity. Argyrol is an example of the second type of solution. The usefulness of silver nitrate depends upon its ability to destroy either living or dead tissue. 

Great danger can also attend its use unless the solution is sufficiently diluted. In most states the law requires that silver nitrate solution be used in the eyes of new-born babies. While it is weak enough to produce no dangerous results, frequently a solution of sodium chloride is used afterwards as an additional precaution. 11 A number of nitrites have important medicinal uses, depending in every case upon the relaxing effect of the nitrite upon muscle fiber. Amyl nitrite (CH₁₁NO₂) is used for the same purpose as nitroglycerin. Because of its great readiness to vaporize, it can be given by inhalation and can be administered very much more quickly. It is generally sold in small pearls or ampules which can be easily crushed. An alcohol solution of ethyl nitrite (C₂H-NO₂) is called sweet spirits of niter. It increases the activity of the kidneys and sweat glands by dilating the blood vessels and so enabling a greater amount of blood to be brought to these excretory organs. 

Nitrous oxide (N₂O) is useful as an anæsthetic. Because of the fact that it sometimes produces a hysterical condition, it is also called “laughing gas." It is often used by itself in minor operations and in dental surgery. Sometimes ether or some other anæsthetic is preceded by a small amount of this gas because it is more easily taken by the patient. An overdose of laughing gas may cause a dangerous result, for the oxygen-carrying power of the blood is decreased through the union of the hemoglobin with this gas. Visible symptoms of such an effect are labored breathing and a bluish color of the face.

Ammonia (NH) is very useful because of its easy liquifaction and the fact that on its return to a gaseous state a large amount of heat is absorbed (see Heat of Vaporiza-

NITROGEN 

109 tion-Chapter VI). The manufacture of artificial ice and many refrigerating systems are dependent upon the maintenance of a cold temperature in this way. The evaporation of 1 gram of liquid ammonia can freeze 4 grams of water into ice. The liquid ammonia is driven through pipes into a coil surrounded by a brine containing calcium chloride (CaCl2). This solution can be lowered to -12° C. without freezing. As the ammonia within the coil returns to a gaseous form, it absorbs heat (330 calories per gram), and in this way the temperature of the calcium chloride solution is lowered. The very cold brine is in turn conducted through another tank in which water-filled molds are suspended, the water being frozen into cakes of ice. 

When the brine has served its purpose it is returned to the tank containing the ammonia coil, and the process is repeated. When used for refrigeration, the cold calcium chloride brine is conducted through a piping system in coldstorage rooms. As copper and brass are corroded by ammonia, iron is used for the pipes and tanks. 

When the gaseous ammonia is dissolved in water a chemical reaction occurs resulting in the formation of ammonium hydroxide.

NH3 + H2O → NHẠOH Solutions containing ammonium hydroxide are known under various names, as household ammonia and ammonia water. They are used both medicinally and as a cleaning agent. Aromatic spirits of ammonia is a solution containing ammonium hydroxide, ammonium carbonate and some volatile oil such as lemon, lavender or nutmeg. It is used principally in cases of faintness or to overcome nausea. 

Other ammonium compounds are ammonium acetate (NHC₂H₂O₂), used for increasing the activity of the kidneys and sweat glands, and ammonium chloride (NHCl), used to relieve congestion of the throat by increasing cough-

ing and by making the secretions more fluid and, therefore, more easily removed.

There are certain cases of tuberculosis of the lungs in which the patient will improve if the infected lung can rest for a time. This condition of rest can be brought about by introducing nitrogen under atmospheric pressure between the visceral and parietal layers of the pleura, thus raising the pressure so it is more nearly equal to the pressure within the lung. As a result, the lung becomes partially collapsed. 

Because nitrogen is an inert gas no chemical change is brought about. There is a gradual absorption of the gas, however, so that the lung again increases in volume until it resumes its normal size. Air can be used in place of nitrogen, but it must be sterilized. The operation must be repeated more frequently as air can be absorbed much more rapidly than nitrogen and so the resting period is shortened. 

This treatment, known as artificial pneumothorax, is also used to prevent adhesions after pleurisy. 

SUMMARY 

Ammonia.-A compound every molecule of which contains 1 nitrogen and 3 hydrogen atoms-formula NH¸. 

Ammonium.-The name given to a group of atoms (1 nitrogen and 4 hydrogen) which by themselves do not compose a molecule but must be combined with some other unit or units to form it. Ammonium radical-NH. 

Molecules of compounds containing it: 

Ammonium hydroxide NH OH 

Ammonium chloride 

Ammonium acetate

NHẠC NH,C,H,O, Ammonium carbonate (NH), CO₂

Proteins.-Complex organic (carbon-containing) compounds which contain nitrogen and which are essential to all plant and animal tissues. 

Urea. A nitrogen compound formed in the early stages of protein decomposition in animal organisms and eliminated through the kidneys and sweat glands a different substance from uric acid.

NITROGEN 

111 Nitrites. Simple nitrogen compounds formed during the decomposition of nitrogen compounds by oxidation. 

Nitrates. Simple nitrogen compounds which are the final products into which other nitrogen compounds can be oxidized. 

Nitrogen Cycle.-The continuity which exists between the nitrogen-containing proteins and the nitrogenous compounds which result from their decomposition and contribute to their creation.
