CHAPTER VIII 

AIR Composition. While air was long regarded as an element, with the discovery of oxygen and nitrogen in the eighteenth century very definite proof was obtained that it contained these two elements. A comparison of the weights of equal volumes of nitrogen obtained from the air and that obtained from nitrogen compounds disclosed the fact that the former was heavier. This led to further studies to find an explanation for the difference in weight, and as a result the presence of other inactive gases was discovered. Of these, the one present in largest amounts was called argon, meaning lazy, because of its extreme inertness. The others present in minute traces were named helium, neon, krypton and xenon. If the more variable constituents of the air are removed, nitrogen, oxygen and argon are found to be present in the following constant proportions by volume: 

Nitrogen 78% 

Oxygen 21% (approximately 1/5-see Chapter IV) 

Argon 0.94%

In addition to these gases whose proportions are constant, the air contains a number of substances, the amounts of which vary. One of these is carbon dioxide. It may come from several sources. It is one of the end products resulting from the decomposition of plant or animal material. 

It is a waste product of the oxidation processes going on in living organisms, being removed for the most part in the expired air. It is also a product resulting from the com- 112

AIR 

bustion of any carbon-containing fuel, and most if not all of them contain this element. It can readily be seen, then, why carbon dioxide is always present in the air and in varying proportions of which the following are examples: 

0.03% 3 volumes in 10,000 volumes) country air 

0.06%-0.07% (6-7 volumes in 10,000 volumes) city air 

0.5 % ( 50 volumes in 10,000 volumes) theatres or rooms occupied by a group of people

Water vapor is another compound which varies within even wider limits. It is also a waste product of the body oxidation processes, being removed largely by the lungs and sweat glands. It reaches the air, therefore, in the expired breath and by the evaporation of perspiration. It is a product of the combustion of all hydrogen-containing fuels. 

Through the constant evaporation of moisture from the soil and from all surface waters, considerable water vapor is always being added to the air. Under ordinary conditions it is present in a gaseous form. If, however, the temperature is lowered, the gaseous water vapor may be changed to a liquid either in very tiny droplets visible as a cloud, fog or mist, or in larger drops which fall as rain.

With a further fall in temperature, the drops of liquid may freeze and reach the ground in a solid form which we call sleet or hail. A very sudden chilling of the air may cause the transformation of the gaseous water directly to a solid form which we recognize as snow crystals or frost. The amount of water vapor the air is capable of holding is very variable, the following figures showing the influence that temperature conditions have upon it: 

Temperature Centigrade... 

0° 10° 15.5° 20° 30° 32.2° 13 17.1 30 34 

Grams water vapor held in 1 cubic meter of air 4.8 8.9

This represents the maximum amount under each temperature condition and is called the saturation point. Humidity is another term referring to the maximum water vapor

content, while relative humidity refers to the actual content in comparison with the maximum. While water vapor in the air is invisible under ordinary conditions, results produced by it may be very noticeable. Salt, for example, will become very moist because of the presence of impurities which have a great tendency to absorb moisture. We have already referred to the color change in cloth or paper dyed with cobalt chloride (see Water of Hydration, Chapter VI).

It is easier to make candy on days when the water vapor content of the air is considerably below the maximum. 

Clothes require a long time for drying if the air is near its saturation point.

Dust is another type of material present in very variable amounts. The particles constituting it are of various kinds. 

Earth, sand, lint, coal dust, bits of dried leaves and other lifeless vegetable and animal material form a considerable part. In addition, there may be living organisms such as pollen, yeast, mold spores and different types of bacteria. 

We may be entirely unconscious of the dust when it is in the air. However, the fact that we have to sweep and dust a room repeatedly is a direct proof of its presence. 

When a beam of light shines into a darkened room, we can actually see the minute particles suspended in the air. 

Even though we have not seen them, we are often very much aware of results produced by them. The spoiling of food is due to the presence of bacteria, yeast or mold which have contaminated it through contact with the air. To prevent this contact, food is often preserved by sealing in air-tight jars after both food and jars have been sterilized. Eggs are preserved in water glass for the same reason. The amount of dust per cubic inch of country air is estimated as 2,000 particles as compared with 30,000,000 particles in the same volume of air in an occupied room.

Two other components of air, sulphur dioxide (SO₂) and

AIR hydrogen sulphide (H₂S) are spoken of as accidental since they come from an industrial source. They are found in the air in those localities in which there are the types of industries that can contribute them.

Air a Mixture.-The fact that air was found to be composed of so many different substances made it impossible to continue thinking of it as an element. That it is believed to be a mixture rather than a compound is because of the following reasons. Certain of its components can be removed in a purely mechanical way. This we have already learned may be a characteristic of mixtures, but it is never a characteristic of compounds. When air is liquified, for example, nitrogen can be removed from it while the oxygen, carbon dioxide and water still remain in a liquid form because of the readiness with which this element returns to its natural gaseous condition. Oxygen can then be removed because it resumes a gaseous form more readily than either carbon dioxide or water. Another reason for believing air to be a mixture is that the proportion of the different components is more or less variable. The proportions by weight cannot be expressed in atomic weights or multiples of them. Therefore, no formula can be written to represent the composition of air. This would not be true. if it were a compound.

Although a mixture, it is surprising that the composition of the air is not more variable. Gases when in contact with each other tend to diffuse or mix together and winds greatly increase this tendency to mix. This makes for a greater uniformity in different places than would be possible otherwise. The balance between the production and use of oxygen keeps its proportion constant. While it is being used up in the decomposition of plant or animal material, in the combustion of fuels and during the respiration processes of living organisms it is being returned to the air as a product when plants build up carbohydrates, glu-

cose (CH12O6) for example, out of carbon dioxide and water. 6 CO, + 6 H,O C&H12O6+6 02 In the same way the amount of carbon dioxide in the air is kept in check by the balance between its production and consumption. While it is being added to the air constantly as a result of the decomposition of plant and animal material, of combustion of fuels and of respiration, the process by which carbohydrates are built up by a plant requires carbon dioxide as one of the raw materials and it is again removed from the air.

Relation to the Body.-Of the several important ways in which atmospheric oxygen is useful, the most vital is its necessity to the body for carrying on the process of oxidation whereby heat and other forms of energy are released. Although air from which the oxygen has been completely removed will not support life, a considerable quantity can be removed without causing any bad effects.

With every breath of inspired air only a portion of its oxygen content passes on through the lungs into the blood stream as a comparison of the following figures shows: 

Inspired air 

Expired air ... 

21% oxygen 

15.9% oxygen

The oxygen content can be reduced to 10% before living organisms are unable to live in it. There must be at least 

18.5% oxygen, however, to enable fuels to burn. The fact that a candle will not burn in an enclosed space does not necessarily mean that a person cannot get sufficient oxygen. 

It may serve as a warning, but does not always mean actual danger as is sometimes stated.

The nitrogen of the air is useful in two ways. It serves as a diluent for the oxygen, thereby diminishing the activity of oxidation processes which might otherwise take place too rapidly. Upon its presence we are indirectly dependent

AIR 

for the nitrogen we need to maintain the body proteins 

(see Chapters VII and XXIV). 

Although carbon dioxide is a waste product so far as the animal body is concerned, we are also dependent upon it for the carbohydrate we use as foods. As we cannot manufacture them for ourselves, we have to make use of the products the plants manufacture from the two simple compounds carbon dioxide and water. Although it can neither support combustion nor respiration, carbon dioxide is present in the air ordinarily in amounts that are too small to be a hindrance to either of these processes. Most rooms have natural means for the renewal of air, for example, invisible spaces around the edges of doors and windows. 

The length of time the room is occupied does not usually permit the accumulation of carbon dioxide to reach such a point that the oxygen content is below the minimum 10%. 

It itself is not a poison.

The body temperature is regulated in large part through the evaporation of perspiration. Fourteen and one-half grams (½ oz.) of water will by its evaporation lower the temperature of a person weighing 168 pounds 0.2° F. The ability of perspiration to be evaporated is influenced very directly by the amount of water vapor already in the air as well as by the temperature. Although the amount may actually be small, the nearer it is to the saturation point the less able will the air be to take up more. This accounts for the great discomfort we experience in muggy weather when the relative humidity is great. If, on the other hand. the air should contain an amount of water vapor much below the maximum, the effect on living organisms would be very disastrous. Under such a condition the evaporation of moisture would be excessive and the organism, a plant for example, would dry up in a short time.

The presence of dust particles in the air has an unsuspected effect upon our comfort. Without them we should

have neither clouds nor rain. In other words, they serve as points on which water vapor condenses into drops of liquid. Without these points, the condensation would take place on some other surface as a roof, an umbrella or even ourselves. It would be impossible, therefore, to protect ourselves against the wetting that would result. 

Even the living organisms in the air are of great use to us in some cases. If it were not for the help of certain types of these organisms in the decomposition of materials, the lifeless animal and vegetable compounds, which are the natural result of life processes, would accumulate and soon make the earth an unpleasant and even harmful place in which to live. Sometimes, however, the decompositions brought about by these organisms are undesired, as when food spoils and fruit molds or decays. To prevent it, we resort to canning or other methods of food preservation.

The necessity for ventilation is not due to the lack of oxygen in the air or to its increasing carbon dioxide content as was the belief at one time. The chief reason for renewing the air supply of a room is because of its increased moisture content when it is occupied by a number of people. 

The amount of water vapor in the air is added to because of the evaporation of perspiration and because of the moisture in the expired air which is saturated with it. As the air gets closer and closer to its saturation point, the feeling of discomfort becomes more and more apparent. If the air is quiet, naturally the layer nearest the person reaches the condition of saturation first. A fan, therefore, will relieve his uncomfortable feeling because it stirs the air and brings a layer with a lower water content near the skin. Thus the evaporation of perspiration is not hindered to such an extent and the regulation of the body temperature is not so quickly affected. Another reason for the increasing discomfort is the presence of other substances with perceptible odors which will also vaporize along with

AIR 

the water of the perspiration. The temperature of the air also increases. By renewing the air supply, ventilation helps to correct these unpleasant conditions. 

Since air becomes lighter as it becomes hotter, ventilation by opening windows is most effective if the window is opened both from the top and the bottom. This gives an entrance for the fresh cold air which is heavy and at the same time provides an exit for the lighter heated air which is to be replaced.

SUMMARY 

Constant Components of Air.-Nitrogen-oxygen-argon.

Variable Components of Air.-Carbon dioxide-water vapordust.

Reasons for Calling Air a Mixture.-Composition varies. Composition cannot be represented by a formula. Some of the components can be separated from each other by mechanical means.

Importance of Different Components.-Oxygen: Respiration combustion of fuels-decomposition of plant and animal substances. Nitrogen: Dilutes oxygen-indirect source of nitrogen of body. Carbon Dioxide: Source of carbon of body. 

Water Vapor: Hinders regulation of body temperature if relative humidity is high-if water content is near the maximum. Dust Particles: Points for condensation of water vapor into clouds, fog and rain. Living Organisms:

Aid decomposition of plant and animal substances-Cause spoiling of foods. 

Need for Ventilation.-Not because of lack of oxygen. Not because of increasing carbon dioxide. Due to increasing moisture content. Due to other substances vaporized from perspiration which are disagreeable but not harmful. Due to increased temperature. As Dr. C-E. A. Winslow of 

Yale says, "the question of ventilation, then, is physical, not chemical, and cutaneous, not respiratory."

CHAPTER IX ACIDS 

Composition. In the discussion of the preparation of hydrogen, we have already referred to the use of compounds which we called acids. A comparison of the formulas of some of the more familiar ones reveals the fact that the element hydrogen is common to them all. Note the following examples: 

Hydrobromic acid

HBr 

Hydrochloric acid HCI 

Hydrocyanic acid

HCN 

Hydrosulphuric acid 

Boric (boracic) acid

H₂S HBO, Nitric acid

HNO3 

Phosphoric acid 

Sulphuric acid

H3PO4 or HPO3 H₂SO 

Acetic acid HC,H,O, Carbonic acid

H₂CO3 

Citric acid

H₂C&H₂O, Lactic acid 

Oxalic acid 

Tartaric acid

HC3H503 H,C,O H,C,H,O. In each case the formula shows that the molecule of the acid is composed of hydrogen combined with another atom or group of atoms which is generally called the nonmetallic or acid radical. The hydrochloric acid molecule, for example, is composed of one hydrogen atom and one chlorine atom, the latter being considered its acid radical even though it is composed of only one atom. A molecule of sulphuric acid is composed of two hydrogen atoms and the acid radical, which in this case is called the sulphate radical (SO4). The formula for acetic acid shows that its 121

molecule is composed of hydrogen combined with a nonmetallic or acid radical (CHO) in which more hydrogen is present. The question arises, why is a portion of the hydrogen separated from the remainder in this molecule?

The same thing is true of many other acids (see list for examples). The hydrogen which is common to all members of the acid family is called replaceable hydrogen, for it is capable of being replaced by a metal. Upon this fact depends the ability of obtaining free hydrogen by treating acids with certain metals (see Chapter V). It is also one of the reasons for including hydrogen in the list of metallic elements. In the case of acetic acid, only one-fourth the total amount of hydrogen in the molecule can be replaced in this manner and in the other acids referred to a portion only of the total hydrogen shows this characteristic property. Acids may be defined, then, as compounds composed of replaceable hydrogen in combination with either a nonmetal or a non-metallic radical called the acid radical. 

Classification. The majority of the examples already given are of acids containing carbon. These are called organic acids. There is a relatively small number containing no carbon. They are called inorganic or mineral acids.

In addition to the organic acids already mentioned, other familiar examples are picric, salicylic and trichloracetic acids, and a compound called carbolic acid.

Naming. Many of their names give no indication of their composition, but in some cases the name of the acid gives considerable information. The fact that it is an acid means it must contain hydrogen. At one time oxygen was also believed to be essential to all acids, this being the reason for giving the element its name (see Chapter IV).

Later it was found that a few acids contained no oxygen, the absence of this element being indicated by the prefix "hydro" in the name (see list at beginning of chapter). In the case of the oxygen-containing mineral acids and also

ACIDS 

123 carbonic acid, the name is derived from the element which is combined with oxygen in the acid radical. The following are examples: 

HNO, nitric acid (nitrogen) 

H2CO3-carbonic acid (carbon)

In a number of cases more than one acid contains the same elements but in different proportions, as sulphuric acid (H2SO4) and sulphurous acid (H₂SO). They are distinguished from each other by the endings "ic" and "ous," the former being given to the acid having the larger proportion of oxygen and the latter to the one with the smaller proportion. 

Physical Properties.-Acids are often thought of as being liquids because the ones we are most familiar with are in this form. In most cases, however, the liquid is a solution of a compound which in the pure state may be either a gas, a liquid or a solid. Hydrochloric acid, for example, is a water solution of the gas hydrogen chloride.

Both are represented by the same formula HCl. The solution of the gas hydrogen sulphide is in the same way called hydrosulphuric acid. Pure sulphuric and nitric acids are liquids. Acetic acid is also a liquid under ordinary conditions, but when the temperature is lowered it becomes a solid, having the appearance of ice, and is therefore called glacial acetic acid. Among the more familiar solids are boric and oxalic acids. Many of the organic acids are solids when in the pure state, although they occur naturally in solution. This is true of the various fruit acids. All acids are not equally soluble, however. Boric acid dissolves to so slight an extent that usually hot water is added to a paste made of the boric acid and glycerine to make it go into solution more easily. While most acids are either colorless, if liquid, or white, if solid, picric acid is a familiar example of one having a pronounced yellow color. Some have a sharp, penetrating odor, as hydrochloric and acetic

acids. Some have a very offensive odor, as butyric acid, to which the disagreeable odor of perspiration may be due in part. Many acids are odorless. While many of the physical properties vary considerably, all acids if in solution in water have a sour taste and so the term "acid" has become synonymous with sour. We speak of acid fruits, for example. 

Chemical Properties.-One of the chemical properties which is common to all acids when in solution in water is made use of as a general test. They can cause a color change in certain organic substances. Of these indicators, as they are called, litmus is the most familiar example. It is a substance of vegetable origin which may be either blue or red. In the presence of an acid the blue becomes red and the red remains unchanged. Litmus paper, the form in which it is most frequently used, has been prepared by soaking paper in a red or blue litmus solution and then drying it. Phenolphthalein is another indicator which is either red or colorless, the red losing its color in the presence of an acid. It is more sensitive than litmus and so can be used when the amount of acid is too small to be indicated by the latter substance. Phenolphthalein is a coal-tar product in a powder form when pure. As it is insoluble in water it is used in an alcohol solution. A substance called phenolsulphonephthalein is used when testing for renal activity (see Chapter X). Methyl orange, another indicator, is red when it is in an acid solution.

There are a number of other organic substances which show color changes in the presence of an acid and are therefore useful as indicators, but the three which have been mentioned are in most general use. The color changes in flowers and leaves as they become old may be due in part to changes involving acid substances which in turn affect the vegetable pigments in much the same way that litmus is acted upon.

ACIDS 

125 The behavior of acids when in contact with metals has already been discussed (see Chapter V). The reaction which results in the production of free hydrogen and a compound composed of the metal and the acid radical and called a salt will not take place unless the acid is in solution in water. Therefore the acids which are commonly used for the preparation of hydrogen are the ones which are readily soluble in water. Nitric acid is never used, however, for the hydrogen which is freed being a good reducing agent in turn reacts with more of the nitric acid (an oxidizing agent) and so the final products are entirely different from the ones to be expected from a characteristic reaction between a metal and an acid. Metal surfaces, such as aluminum, zinc, iron and nickel, should not be cleaned with an acid because of the tendency of these metals to react with acid solutions, the result being a gradual corrosion of the surface. To clean a tarnished copper surface, however, oxalic acid is commonly used. The acid will affect the copper compounds of which the tarnish is composed, but the metal itself will not be acted upon. Acid-containing mixtures should not be allowed to stand in metal containers if they are the types of metals easily affected by acids. Of those in common use tin, if a bright, clean surface, is least acted upon. 

Neither should an enameled utensil from which the enamel is broken away be used for these purposes because of the exposed metal which is underneath. 

Another characteristic common to acids when they are in solution is their ability to react with salts of other acids, the products being a new acid and a new salt. Suppose we take as examples the reaction between an acid and either a carbonate, as calcium carbonate (CaCO3), or a bicarbonate, as sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3). Both types of carbonates are related to carbonic acid (H₂CO₂). This, however, is a very unstable compound and readily decomposes into carbon dioxide and water. Whenever an acid-con-

taining solution comes in contact with a carbonate, therefore, there is a decided effervescence, due to the escape of the gaseous carbon dioxide. When calcium carbonate and hydrochloric acid react together the products first formed are calcium chloride (CaCl) and carbonic acid, but because of the readiness with which the latter decomposes it very quickly breaks down into carbon dioxide and water. 2 HCl + CaCO3 

H2CO3 

H2CO3 + CaCl₂ H₂O + CO₂ Since marble is composed of calcium carbonate its surface would be damaged very quickly if acid were used to clean it. Sodium bicarbonate or baking soda is often used with an acid-containing solution for the sake of the carbon dioxide which is produced. The carbon dioxide fire extinguisher contains solutions of sodium bicarbonate and an acid, usually sulphuric, which are kept apart from each other until the extinguisher is inverted. A reaction then takes place at once which results in the production of sodium sulphate and carbonic acid, the latter in turn decomposing into carbon dioxide and water. The gas escapes through the nozzle, forcing much of the liquid out with it. H2SO4+2 NaHCO3 2 H₂CO3 + Na2SO4 → 2 H₂O + 2 CO2 2 H2CO3 The use of baking soda with sour milk, which contains lactic acid, depends upon the same reaction. In this case the final products are sodium lactate, water and carbon dioxide. 

The sour-milk biscuit is made light by the expansion of the gas during the baking. Sodium bicarbonate is often given as a remedy for an acid stomach. If there is an excess of hydrochloric acid, a normal component of the gastric juice present in the stomach, or if acids are produced as a result of fermentations occurring there, the amount of acid can be lessened because of the reaction with the bicarbonate. Magnesium carbonate can be used for the same purpose.

ACIDS 

127 Another very important property of acids is their ability to react with compounds called bases or hydroxides. The products formed by this reaction, which is called neutralization, are water and the salt of the acid. It will be discussed in detail in the following chapter.

Relation to Oxides.-The oxides of certain elements, as carbon, phosphorus, sulphur and nitrogen, when dissolved in water have the ability to turn blue litmus paper red. In other words, an acid has been formed through the reaction of the oxide and water. Sulphuric acid is produced when sulphur trioxide (SO) is dissolved in water. SO3 + H₂O H2SO4 

Phosphorus pentoxide and water form phosphoric acid.

P2O5 + H₂O P2O5 + 3 H2O 2 HPO3 2 H3PO4 Carbon dioxide is carried in the blood in solution as carbonic acid.

CO2 + H2O → H₂CO3 Those oxides whose water solutions show an acid reaction toward litmus are called acidic or non-metallic oxides. 

They are also called acid anhydrides, the word anhydride in this case meaning that the acid without the atoms necessary to form a water molecule (one oxygen and two hydrogen atoms) would be the corresponding oxide. If, for example, two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom could be removed from a molecule of sulphurous acid, one molecule of sulphur dioxide (SO2) would be the result, H2SO3 

H2 O SO2 Conversely, if water is added to sulphur dioxide, sulphurous acid is formed.

SO2 + H2O 

H2SO3 

Sulphur dioxide is therefore called the anhydride of sul-

phurous acid. For the same reason the anhydride of nitric acid is nitrogen pentoxide (NO). Since each molecule of this acid contains only one hydrogen atom it is necessary to think of two acid molecules in relation to one molecule of the corresponding oxide. 2 HNO3 H2 O N₂ O5 

Uses of Acids.-Acids are used medicinally, both locally and systemically. Dilute hydrochloric acid is given to supply a deficiency in the gastric juice, being given during or after a meal. Because of the action of acids on metals it should always be prepared in a glass and stirred with a glass rod. To prevent the teeth from being injured or discolored it should be well diluted and given through a glass tube. The use of sodium bicarbonate or magnesium carbonate for cases of hyper-acidity has been referred to. 

Limewater can also be used. The action will be discussed in the following chapter (see neutralization). By stimulating the salivary glands so that more saliva is secreted, a dilute acid will relieve thirst. It is usually given in the form of fruit juices, as grape juice, orangeade or lemonade.

While organic acids like citric, malic, acetic and tartaric are acid so long as they remain in the digestive tract, on reaching the body cells, as a result of the oxidation which occurs when they are ultimately made use of, they contribute to the production of carbonates. They are therefore to be considered as base-forming rather than acidforming and so may be given in certain diseases in which acids are not allowed. Carbolic acid (see Chapters XIX and XXVI), which is very corrosive, is not used internally as a medicine, but salol, a coal-tar product, breaks down in the intestines into phenol (carbolic acid) and salicylic acid. Since 40% of the salol consists of phenol, the patient must be watched, for its effect may be depressing. Because

ACIDS 

129 of its composition salol is considered an intestinal antiseptic. Carbolic acid is eliminated by the kidneys, giving the urine a characteristic dark-green color. 

For acid poisoning substances like limewater or milk of lime (calcium hydroxide), milk of magnesia (magnesium hydroxide), magnesium carbonate or sodium bicarbonate are given as antidotes. The effectiveness of the carbonates is due to a reaction which has already been discussed. For the action of the hydroxides refer to the discussion of neutralization (Chapter X). Chalk, plaster from the wall or soap suds can be given if the substances mentioned are not available. Their action is similar. For oxalic acid poisoning, a calcium compound such as milk of lime or prepared chalk (calcium carbonate) is given. Calcium oxalate, which is formed as a result of its action, is insoluble and no longer harmful. It cannot be absorbed and is eliminated in the feces. Sodium compounds must not be used as an antidote, for the resulting sodium oxalate is both soluble and poison- Carbolic acid poisoning is treated by a lavage of 

50% alcohol, after which Epsom salt (MgSO4) or Glauber's salt (Na2SO4) is given. As carbolic acid is very much more soluble in alcohol than it is in water it can be washed out of the stomach much more effectively. The salts complete the action by forming harmless sulphocarbolates through a reaction with any acid which was not removed by the alcohol. ous.

When acids are applied to the skin they cause it to become shrunken because of the water they remove from it. They may also coagulate the protein in the tissues, making them look white. The astringent action of vinegar, which is due to this effect, makes it useful in checking night sweats or for excessive sweating of the feet. If the hands are put into a weak solution of carbolic acid they become whitened and have a tingling sensation, followed by numbness, due to an effect on the nerve endings. It

is no longer customary to use wet dressings of carbolic acid on wounds because, if evaporation is prevented, the skin and underlying tissue are destroyed and gangrene may result. This process may continue without any sensation of pain because of the anesthesia of the nerve endings. 

Hypochlorous acid (HCIO) in 0.5% solution is used to disinfect wounds. As it is very unstable and in the presence of light and warmth decomposes into hydrochloric acid and oxygen it should always be kept in a cool, dark place. It is this ability to give up its oxygen that makes it useful as a disinfectant, for the oxygen when first released oxidizes any harmful material present. If there is nothing to be oxidized free oxygen is formed 

2 HCIO → 2 HCI + O2 

Picric acid, a phenol preparation, is used in some hospitals to prepare the skin for an operation, the claim being that it penetrates more than iodine and so is more effective. 

Concentrated acids are often used to destroy objectionable tissue, as warts, or tissue which has been infected by the bite of an animal. Glacial acetic, trichloracetic and nitric are the acids commonly used for this purpose. Care must be taken to protect the surrounding tissue so that only the portion to be destroyed comes in contact with the acid. 

This can be done by coating it with vaseline. 

To counteract the action of an acid, if it has been spilled upon the clothing or on the skin, the usual treatment is to apply ammonium hydroxide. If concentrated sulphuric acid has been spilled, water should be first used freely, followed by the ammonium hydroxide, as a large amount of heat is liberated when this acid reacts with a hydroxide. 

For carbolic acid, washing with 95% alcohol (for external use) is the usual treatment.

CHAPTER X  

Composition. The family name of hydroxide is given to a group of compounds because they all contain oxygen and hydrogen associated together in what is called the hydroxyl (OH) radical. A comparison of the formulas of some of the familiar members of the family shows that in every case the hydroxyl radical is combined with a metal or a radical having the characteristics of a metal. 

Sodium hydroxide 

Potassium hydroxide 

Calcium hydroxide 

Magnesium hydroxide

NaOH KOH Ca (OH)2 Mg (OH)2 

Ferrous (iron) hydroxide

Fe (OH) 2 Fe (OH)3 NH₂OH C,H,OH C₂H, (OH)3 

Ferric (iron) hydroxide 

Ammonium hydroxide 

Grain alcohol (ethyl hydroxide) 

Wood alcohol (methyl hydroxide) CH,OH 

Glycerine (glycerol or glyceryl hydroxide), etc.,

Alluminum hydroxide Al(OH)3 

Classification and Naming.-As in the case of the acids the hydroxides or bases, as they are called, containing carbon are classified as organic bases, and to one type the family name of alcohol is given. Grain alcohol, wood alcohol and also glycerine are examples of this kind of base. 

Their formulas show that the hydroxyl radical, which is common to all hydroxides, is present in each. The name of the compound does not always indicate the constituents of which it is composed. From the names of the inorganic hydroxides, which contain no carbon, it is possible to tell of what each is composed. The name sodium hydroxide, 133

for example, shows that the compound is composed of sodium combined with the hydroxyl radical. If more than one hydroxide can be formed through the union of the same kinds of units, the compounds are usually distinguished from each other by the endings "ic" and "ous" on the first part of the name. For example, there are two iron hydroxides, one whose molecule is composed of an atom of iron combined with three hydroxyl radicals called ferric hydroxide (Fe(OH)3), the other composed of one atom of iron combined with two hydroxyl radicals and called ferrous hydroxide (Fe(OH)2).

The Physical Properties.-In the pure state, hydroxides exist either as liquids or solids, although they are most commonly used in a liquid form. Lye, for example, 94% of which consists of sodium hydroxide, is a white solid. Calcium hydroxide, or slaked lime as it is more commonly called, is a white powder. Of the inorganic bases ammonium hydroxide is the only one which is naturally a liquid, being formed through a reaction between water and the gas ammonia (NH3) when the latter is dissolved. Other names for it are ammonia water and household ammonia. more familiar alcohols, wood alcohol, grain alcohol and glycerine, are liquids which dissolve so readily in water that they can be mixed with it in any proportion. The solid bases differ in their solubilities. Sodium hydroxide and potassium hydroxide are so extremely soluble that it is possible to make up very concentrated solutions. Because their solutions are very reactive the hydroxides themselves, sometimes ammonium hydroxide being included with them, are called alkalies. Limewater is a solution of calcium hydroxide. Being more soluble in cold water than it is in hot water, heating the water does not help in preparing the solution in this case. Water containing calcium hydroxide both in solution and in suspension is called milk of lime. Milk of magnesia consists of water containing both

dissolved and undissolved magnesium hydroxide. Before using either milk of lime or milk of magnesia it is necessary to shake the bottle thoroughly, as the undissolved material settles to the bottom very readily. Of the inorganic bases, ammonium hydroxide is the only one having an odor, pungent and penetrating. Because of its stimulating effect it is made use of in smelling salts. The inhalation of large amounts, which may occur if there is a leak in the piping system of an ammonia refrigeration plant, can cause harmful or even fatal results. Wood and grain alcohols have a characteristic, sweet odor. Except for a few members of the hydroxide family, however, the compounds belonging to it are odorless. All inorganic hydroxides when in solution in water have a slippery, soapy feeling and a somewhat bitter taste. 

Chemical Properties.-Like the acids, the hydroxides show certain common chemical characteristics when in solution in water. Solutions of inorganic hydroxides cause color changes to occur in the substances we have called indicators. 

Red litmus will become blue, whereas the blue will remain unchanged. Methyl orange, which is red in the presence of an acid, becomes yellow in a solution containing a base. 

Colorless phenolphthalein will become red, but the red will not be affected. Both these changes are the reverse of the effects produced by an acid-containing solution (see Chapter IX). When phenolsulphonephthalein is used to determine kidney activity, a solution containing it is injected into the patient's thigh and he is given two glasses of water to drink. During the two hours immediately following the injection any urine which is voided is collected. 

At the end of this time interval it is sometimes necessary to catheterize the patient in order to collect the full amount of urine which the kidney has excreted. The amount of phenolsulphonephthalein in it will be an indication of the activity of the kidney in removing materials carried to it

by the blood. By adding a few drops of a solution of sodium hydroxide to the combined samples of urine diluted to one liter a red color is obtained because of the indicator present. 

The depth of the color will vary with the amount of the phenolsulphonephthalein. By making a color comparison with standard tubes containing colored solutions of known concentrations it is possible to determine the per cent of the dye which has been excreted. A normal person should be able to excrete 60 to 70% of it in two hours, very rarely the amount being as low as 50%. If as low as 40% is excreted the kidney condition is considered pathological. 

Less than 20% of excreted phenolsulphonephthalein is an indication that the kidneys are functioning badly.

Another characteristic of bases is their ability when in solution to react with salts to form a new base and a new salt. Iron hydroxide which is used as an antidote for arsenic poisoning must always be fresh. If it is not on hand it can be made by adding ammonium hydroxide (NH4OH) to a solution of ferric chloride (FeCl₁), the reaction between the base and the iron salt resulting in the desired iron hydroxide (Fe(OH)¸) and a new salt, ammonium chloride (NH4Cl). 

3 NH4OH+ FeCl3 Fe(OH)3 + 3 NH4Cl Solutions of bases react with acids to form water and a salt, the process being called neutralization. The true significance of this term can be better understood if we consider the cause of the common characteristics of each of the reacting substances. The so-called acid properties, such as litmus reaction and the reaction now being discussed, since they are true not of one or two acids only but of a large group, must be due to the replaceable hydrogen which is common to them all (see Chapter IX). In the same way the common properties of the bases which are apparent when they are in solution must be due to the hydroxyl

radical which is common to them all and to a peculiar condition in which it is when the base containing it is in solution. To understand better what happens when an acid and a base react together, suppose we use the following illustration. A solution of sodium hydroxide is being added to a solution of hydrochloric acid. Each reagent when alone has a definite effect upon litmus paper, the acid causing blue litmus to become red because of the replaceable hydrogen and the base turning red litmus blue because of the hydroxyl radical. On the addition of the first few drops of the sodium hydroxide the liquid will continue to show an acid litmus reaction, however, because there is still a larger amount of the replaceable hydrogen of the acid present than there is of the hydroxyl of the base which is being introduced. For every molecule of the sodium hydroxide being added, however, a reaction takes place with an equivalent amount of hydrochloric acid, the hydrogen of the acid and the hydroxyl of the base forming a molecule of water and the metal of the base and the acid radical forming a molecule of sodium chloride. 

HCl + NaOH

HOH+NaCl The liquid will, therefore, contain water holding in solution the sodium chloride and also the unused acid, and so long as there is unused acid blue litmus will become red.

On the continued addition of the sodium hydroxide, there will come a time when the amount of its hydroxyl will equal the amount of the acid's replaceable hydrogen. At this point blue litmus will not be turned red, neither will red litmus become blue because of the equality between the units accountable for each reaction. In other words, the solution is neutral to litmus or the neutral point has been reached and the process by which this has been accomplished is called neutralization. Water is the neutral product of this reaction rather than the salt which is also

formed. That this must be so is evident when we consider what has gone into its formation. Each molecule is composed of one replaceable hydrogen atom from the acid and one hydroxyl radical from the base. In other words, each molecule represents an equality between the units which are responsible for acid characteristics and the units which are responsible for basic characteristics. Water is, therefore, a truly neutral substance. Neutralization, then, should be so defined as to place the emphasis upon this product. 

It is a reaction between an acid and a base which results in the formation of water and a salt. Some salts when in solution in water are also neutral to litmus but this is not necessarily true of them all. Certain salts like potassium bitartrate (cream of tartar), copper sulphate and aluminium chloride when in solutions show acid properties.

Other salts, such as sodium bicarbonate (baking soda), sodium carbonate (washing soda), borax and sodium stearate (soap), show basic or alkaline characteristics when in solution (see Chapters XI and XII). The use of lime water, milk of lime or milk of magnesia as an antidote for acid poisoning or to overcome hyperacidity in the stomach is dependent upon the neutralization of the acid by the base. Since sodium bicarbonate is alkaline in its reaction when in solution, its use to overcome a condition of acidity can be explained as depending on a neutralizing action as well as to the characteristic effect which carbonates and acids have on each other (see Chapter IX). 

Relation to Oxides.-That acids have a relationship to a class of oxides which are therefore called acidic or nonmetallic oxides, we have already pointed out. There is a similar relationship between the hydroxides and the oxides of the metallic elements such as sodium, potassium and calcium. If the oxides are soluble in water the solutions show a basic or alkaline reaction to litmus because of a chemical change which has taken place between the

oxide and water to form the corresponding hydroxide. For example, when sodium oxide dissolves in water, a reaction takes place by which sodium hydroxide is formed. Na2O + H₂O 2 NaOH Magnesium hydroxide is produced through a reaction between water and magnesium oxide. MgO + H2O Mg(OH)2 

That a chemical reaction takes place when water comes in contact with calcium oxide (quicklime) is very evident. 

The entire mass becomes so intensely hot through the liberation of heat that it steams and a person coming in contact with it might be badly burned. The powdery mass which remains in place of the original hard lumps of quicklime or calcium oxide is slaked lime or calcium hydroxide. 

The process by which the one is converted into the other is commonly called slaking.

CaO + H,O Ca(OH)2 The metallic oxides can also be considered the anhydrides of the corresponding hydroxides, the term being used with the same significance as when expressing the relationship between an acid and the corresponding non-metallic oxide 

(see Chapter IX). 

Uses.-Bases, as well as acids, are used to supply a deficiency in the body or to overcome a condition of acidity. 

In cases of diarrhea caused by acid fermentation, limewater or milk of magnesia is commonly used to lessen the acidity of the contents of the digestive tract, the action being one of neutralization. As the pain is increased by acids in cases of gastric ulcer, bases are sometimes ordered to be given an hour after meals, this being the time when there is the greatest amount of hydrochloric acid in the stomach.

The use of these substances as antidotes for acid poisoning has already been mentioned. In cases of poisoning by an

alkali, the antidote is an organic acid, lemon juice or vinegar being the ones most commonly used.

A condition called acidosis arises in the body through the production of unusual amounts of acid substances. Among these are three compounds, acetoacetic acid, betahydroxybutyric acid and acetone which are closely related and called acetone bodies. The name acidosis is misleading for it implies that in such a condition the blood, which is normally alkaline, becomes actually acid. This is not so; in fact it does not even become neutral except in extreme cases which are accompanied by a condition of coma. In the great effort the body exerts to counteract the acids which have been formed, alkaline substances normally present in the blood are drawn upon. These substances, of which sodium bicarbonate is an important one, are called the body's alkaline reserve. One way by which sodium bicarbonate helps to keep the blood from becoming acid is by reacting with the acid substances present, one of the products being carbonic acid which is eliminated as carbon dioxide and water.

Therefore in a case of diabetic coma, when a large amount of sodium bicarbonate has been used up, the patient's respiration becomes very rapid until only a small amount of carbon dioxide remains. It is called Kussmaul breathing. 

Acidosis can be defined as a condition in which the alkaline reserve has been lowered to such an extent that it requires replenishing. Even under normal conditions it is being used for this purpose but the base forming substances in the food are sufficient to make good the amount which has been used up. Administration of sodium bicarbonate in solution, usually intravenously or by Murphy drip is resorted to when the diet alone cannot supply an adequate amount of material to restore the normal alkalinity of the blood. If given intravenously, it must be sterilized. (See following chapter: Effect of Heat on Bicarbonates.) 

Sodium or potassium hydroxide in more concentrated

solutions is used externally to destroy tissue. When applying it, however, to a wart for example, care must be taken to protect surrounding tissue for it is very destructive. A mildly alkaline solution softens protein material and makes it more soluble. A mouth wash of sodium bicarbonate can be used to soften the coating on a patient's tongue making it more easily removed. It is a common ingredient of toothpastes because of a similar effect it has on mucus, a normal constituent of the saliva, which coats the teeth and requires more than ordinary friction to remove it completely. 

SUMMARY 

Hydroxides or Bases.-Compounds composed of the hydroxyl radical combined with either a metal or a metallic radical. 

Alkali. A very soluble hydroxide-sodium hydroxide, potassium hydroxide-ammonium hydroxide.

Alkaline Reaction or Basic Reaction.-Ability to affect indicators-i.e., litmus.

Basic or Alkaline Substance.-A substance which shows the characteristics of a base when in solution. Certain salts are classed as alkaline substances for this reason. 

A Neutral Substance.—A substance which shows neither acid nor basic characteristics when in solution.

An Acid Substance.-A substance which shows the characteristics of an acid when in solution. Certain salts are classed as acid substances for this reason.

Neutralization. The reaction between an acid and a base the products being water (the neutral substance) and a salt. 

Metallic or Basic Oxide.-An oxide whose solution (water) shows an alkaline reaction.

Basic Anhydride.-An oxide which will react with water to form the corresponding hydroxide.

Acidosis. A condition which results in a dangerous decrease in the normal alkaline constituents (the alkaline reserve) of the blood because of their use to counteract acids which have been formed in metabolism.

CHAPTER XI SALTS 

Composition. Although all salts are compounds composed of a metallic element or group of elements combined with an acid radical, the following names and formulas for some of the more familiar members of the family show that there is no one metallic unit or acid radical common to the entire group as is true of the acids and hydroxides. 

Sodium sulphate (Glauber's salt) 

Magnesium sulphate (Epsom salt) 

Sodium potassium tartrate (Rochelle salt) 

Iron (ferric) chloride 

Sodium chloride 

Potassium citrate

Na₂SO 

MgSO,

NaKC,H,O FeCl3 

Ammonium acetate 

Ethyl nitrite 

Glyceryl nitrate (nitroglycerine)

NaCl K,C,H,O, NH,C,H,O, 3 5 C.H.NO,

C3H5(NO3)3 In every case the name of the salt is derived from the metallic unit and the acid radical contained in it. For example, all salts related to sulphuric acid are called sulphates. If the sulphate radical is combined with sodium, the name is sodium sulphate; if the metallic unit is magnesium, it is called magnesium sulphate. Salts of oxygen acids whose names end in “ic” are given names ending in “ate.” If the name of the acid ends in "ous," the "ite" ending is on the name of the salt. Salts of nitric acid are called nitrates, for example sodium nitrate and glyceryl nitrate, whereas the salts of nitrous acid are called nitrites, as ethyl nitrite and sodium nitrite. Salts of the acids which contain no oxygen are given names ending in "ide," for example sodium chlor- 144

SALTS 

145 ide, a salt of hydrochloric acid and potassium cyanide (KCN), a salt of hydrocyanic acid (HCN). 

Physical Properties. Because there is no element or radical common to all salts they have no common property as the acids and bases have. In the pure state the majority of them are solid although some of the organic salts (referred to later as esters) are liquids. Many of them occur either in the crystalline form with water of hydration present or as a powder with no definite form, if water of hydration is absent (see Chapter VI). The colors vary greatly, many being white or colorless and certain kinds possessing very distinctive colors. Copper salts are green blue, iron salts are either green (ferrous salts) or red brown (ferric salts). While many have a salty taste resembling that of sodium chloride some cause a puckery sensation as in the case of copper sulphate (blue vitriol) and sodium aluminium sulphate (alum). Because of this astringent property alum is used as a styptic to check bleeding from shaving cuts or nose bleed. Potassium, sodium and ammonium salts are soluble but the solubility of those containing other kinds of metallic units varies greatly. Calcium carbonate is practically insoluble, calcium sulphate is very slightly soluble whereas calcium chloride dissolves readily. 

Osmosis and Dialysis. When two miscible liquids or solutions are placed in contact with each other there is a tendency for the molecules to mix together so that in time the entire volume of liquid is uniform in composition. This diffusion, as it is called, is due to the fact that all molecules are in constant motion. The energy expended in their movement through the liquid is spoken of as osmotic pressure. 

If the liquids are separated from each other by a membrane, diffusion can still occur provided the membrane is permeable or, in other words, if the different kinds of molecules present are capable of passing through it. As a result, the liquids on each side of the membrane, which in the begin-

ning were different in composition, will gradually become alike. This passage or diffusion of molecules through a membrane is called osmosis. Soluble substances are classified partly on the basis of their ability to undergo this change, those capable of diffusion through a membrane being called crystalloids and the others colloids. Of the crystalloids, salts form an important group. The process of separating different kinds of molecules from each other because of their different abilities to undergo osmosis is called dialysis. Membranes may be permeable to the water molecules but less so or not at all to those in solution; in this case they are called semi-permeable. The living membranes of the body, of which the walls of the alimentary tract, capillary walls, in fact all cell walls are examples, are of this type. While they are very permeable to water molecules, the molecules of the substances held in solution have greater difficulty in diffusing through them. A stream of water molecules may, therefore, travel in one direction and at the same time the dissolved crystalloids will pass through more slowly in the other direction.

We have already distinguished between hypotonic, hypertonic and isotonic sodium chloride solutions on the basis of the difference in their concentrations, comparing the amount of the salt present in each case with its amount in the blood plasma. As the osmotic pressure of a solution increases with the amount of dissolved material in it we can now make a further distinction between these solutions. An isotonic salt solution is one whose osmotic pressure is equal to that of the blood plasma, the hypertonic solution having a higher osmotic pressure and the solution which is hypotonic having a lower osmotic pressure than that of the plasma. Since sodium chloride is the salt which is present in the blood in largest amounts it is the one used in making an isotonic solution (see Chapter VI). If the concentration is below 0.

7%, its osmotic pressure is below that of the

SALTS 

147 plasma. When infused into the blood stream, therefore, the lowering of its osmotic pressure causes water molecules to pass from the plasma through the separating cell membrane into the soluble contents of the red corpuscles. 

As a result they become ruptured or hemolyzed. If, on the other hand, a solution whose concentration is higher than 0.9% is used, by raising the osmotic pressure of the blood plasma it will cause water molecules to pass into it from the blood corpuscles. In this case, they become shrunken or crenated. Either kind of change in the osmotic pressure results in their destruction. It is very necessary, therefore, that solutions to be used for infusions be made up with the utmost accuracy. Since the magnesium, calcium and potassium salts of the blood are very important although they are present in much smaller amounts, when solutions are to be used for experimental work much better results are sometimes obtained if the isotonic solution contains these salts as well. Ringer's solution is one in common use. Its composition is as follows: 

0.9 % sodium chloride 

0.026% calcium chloride 

0.03 % potassium chloride 

Chemical Properties. While there is no one test to show a physical or a chemical characteristic by which any salt can be identified, there are certain typical reactions they will undergo, the products formed depending on the reacting substances. With acids, salts will react to form a new acid and a new salt if at least one of the reacting substances is in solution. They will react with bases to form a new base and a new salt (see Chapters IX and X). Two salts can react together to form two new salts. The action taking place between sodium chloride and silver nitrate solutions is a typical example of such a reaction (see Chapters II and XV).

Hydrolysis. When a salt is dissolved in water, a chemi-

cal reaction takes place between a small amount of it and the water, the products of which are the corresponding base and acid. This chemical reaction with water, which is called hydrolysis, occurs not only with salts but also with other kinds of compounds, the chemical changes taking place during digestion being hydrolytic changes (see 

Chapters XXII, XXIII and XXIV). Hydrolysis can be defined as the chemical reaction of a compound with water which results in the formation of simpler compounds. Since both reacting substances and products are compounds, it can also be considered a double decomposition in which water is always one of the reacting agents. The following are examples of the hydrolysis of salts.

Sodium chloride + water-sodium hydroxide + hydrochloric acid NaCl + HOH → NaOH + HCl Sodium bicarbonate + water-sodium hydroxide + carbonic acid NaHCO3 + HOH 

NaCH + H2CO3 

Copper sulphate + water copper hydroxide + sulphuric acid 

CuSO4 + 2 HOH 

Cu(OH)2 + H2SO4

We have already stated that in a neutralization reaction, water is the neutral product rather than the salt which is also formed. If we consider the products resulting from the hydrolysis of some typical salts when they are dissolved in water, we can understand why all salts are not neutral substances. The solution of sodium chloride is neutral to litmus because the hydrochloric acid and the sodium hydroxide which have been formed by the hydrolysis of a small portion of it are of equal strength so far as their acid and basic characteristics are concerned. Therefore, neither one can predominate over the other. The solution of sodium bicarbonate, on the other hand, is alkaline to litmus. On its hydrolysis, sodium hydroxide and carbonic acid are formed. The sodium hydroxide being stronger as a base than the carbonic acid is as an acid, its effect predominates and, therefore, the solution shows alkaline properties. In the solution of copper sulphate, the hydrolysis

SALTS 

149 of a portion results in the formation of copper hydroxide and sulphuric acid. As the characteristic properties of the acid are more marked than are the characteristic properties of the base, they predominate and the solution is acid to litmus (see Chapter 12 for further discussion).

The bicarbonates, salts of carbonic acid (H2CO3) in which the acid's replaceable hydrogen is only partially replaced by metallic units, are decomposed when heated and the corresponding carbonate, carbon dioxide and water are produced. This property has already been referred to in accounting for the ability to soften, by boiling, water containing the bicarbonates of calcium, magnesium or iron. 

The calcium, magnesium or iron carbonate which is formed being insoluble is no longer able to affect the soap (see

Chapter VI). 

Ca(HCO3)2 CaCO3 + H2O + CO2 Sodium bicarbonate is decomposed in the same way when either the solution or the solid substance is heated. When in solution, however, a larger amount of the bicarbonate is affected. 2 NaHCO3 Na2CO3 + H2O + CO2 The escape of the carbon dioxide resulting from the decomposition accounts for the slight effervescence which is apparent when sodium bicarbonate is dissolved in hot water. 

When a solution of it is to be given intravenously, it must be sterilized and as a result some decomposition occurs. The sodium carbonate which is formed is more irritating to the tissues as its solution is more alkaline than is that of the bicarbonate.

NaHCO3 + HOH Na2CO3 + 2 HOH 

NaOH+H2CO3 2 NaOH+H2CO3 

Therefore, it does not reëstablish the alkalinity so readily and is undesirable. For this reason carbon dioxide is some-

times passed from a tank through a sterile tube into the sterilized solution, and through a reaction with the sodium carbonate and water the bicarbonate is reformed.

Na2CO3 + H2O + CO₂ →→→→→ 2 NaHCO3 Because dry sodium bicarbonate undergoes less decomposition when heated, in some hospitals the sterilization is done before the solution is made and when it is to be used it is made up with sterile water.

The reaction between acids and carbonates or bicarbonates has been explained in connection with the use of sodium bicarbonate for overcoming conditions of acidity (see Chapters IX and X). Its use as a leavening agent with some acid-containing solution is due to the same reaction, the carbon dioxide being in this case the product desired to make the dough or batter light. With sour milk it reacts with the lactic acid present, the products being sodium lactate, water and carbon dioxide. With molasses it reacts with organic acids which are present with the same typical results, the salts of the acids, water and carbon dioxide. Commercial baking powders are mixtures whose essential ingredients are baking soda (sodium bicarbonate) and another substance with acid characteristics when in solution. The acid substance may be of different kinds.

In the so-called tartrate powder it is cream of tartar (potassium bitartrate, KHC,H,O) or a mixture of this salt and tartaric acid. The products formed during the reaction of this type of powder are sodium potassium tartrate (Rochelle salt, NaKCHО), water and carbon dioxide. NaHCO3 + KHС₁НО — NаKC₁HOь + H2O + CO₂ 

Another type of baking powder contains a phosphate of either sodium or calcium as the acid substance. With monosodium phosphate (NaH,PO,) the products formed are disodium phosphate (Na,HPO4), water and carbon dioxide.

SALTS 

151 NaHCO3 + NaH2PO4 - Na₂HPO4 + H2O + CO₂ Alum baking powders contain baking soda and an aluminium salt, often sodium aluminium sulphate, which is commonly called alum. They also usually contain a phosphate. The products of the reaction are sodium sulphate (Glauber's salt), an aluminium compound (Al(OH),) and carbon dioxide. 6 NaHCO3 + Na2Al2(SO4) 44 Na2SO4 + 2 AI (OH)3 + 6 CO₂ 

Each type of baking powder forms as one of its products a salt which has a cathartic action and objections have been raised against each kind for this reason. Such an objection does not seem very valid, however, when we compare the average amount of each salt when given medicinally and the amount of the baked product it would be necessary to eat in order to equal it.

Alum (sulphate) 

Cream of tartar 28 53 The biscuit mixture must contain 9 grams of 12% baking powder and 1 pint flour and be made into 8 biscuits. Other objections have been raised against the use of alum-containing powders because of the aluminium compound which is one of the products formed. This has been a subject of great controversy and it is doubtful if it is ever settled satisfactorily. Of the three types of powders those containing either tartrates or phosphates yield products which resemble more closely the body's constituents. 

Uses. If a salt is to be used as a systemic medicine it must be soluble in water or in the secretions in the alimentary tract. They then act locally on its walls. If they are both soluble and absorbable, their action will take place

in other parts of the body to which they are carried after the dissolved molecules have diffused through the membrane walls into the blood or lymph. A number of the mineral (inorganic) salts are called saline cathartics because of their use, the following being a few of the more familiar ones. 

Glauber's salt-sodium sulphate

Na₂SO 

Sodium phosphate-disodium phosphate Na,HPO,

Rochelle salt-potassium sodium tartrate KNaC₁H¸O¸ 

Epsom salt-magnesium sulphate

MgSO Their action depends upon the fact that in the amount given they are not absorbed in the beginning, although they are soluble. Because they increase the concentration and therefore the osmotic pressure of the contents of the intestinal part of the alimentary tract, water is withdrawn from the surrounding tissues and passes into the intestine by osmosis. As a result, a watery evacuation of the intestine takes place. The dilution by the water makes it possible for some of the salt to be absorbed so that it also acts on the kidney as a diuretic. In certain types of nephritis in which elimination by the kidney is below normal, there is a retention of sodium chloride in the tissues. To equalize the concentration (osmotic pressure) which has thus been increased, water is drawn into the tissues from the blood and lymph and a condition of œdema results. When the abdominal cavity becomes filled with liquid, the condition is called ascites. A dietary treatment in such cases is the omission of table salt (sodium chloride). 

Saline cathartics are ordered because the liquid which has collected in the tissues is in turn withdrawn into the intestine and eliminated in the feces. A high blood pressure can be reduced by their action as the withdrawal of water from the blood lessens its volume. Among the saline cathartics are a number of preparations which are effervescent. Of these Seidlitz powder is a familiar example. It comes in a

SALTS 

153 powder form made up in two parts. In the blue paper is a mixture of Rochelle salt and sodium bicarbonate and the white paper contains tartaric acid. Each powder is dissolved separately in a half glass of water, not iced, and the two solutions are poured together just before being given to the patient. The effervescence is due to the escape of some of the carbon dioxide which is formed in the reaction between the bicarbonate and acid.

NaHCO3 + H₂C4H4O - NaHC4H4O6+ H2O + CO2 Obviously, the solutions should never be given to the patient separately for the carbon dioxide would then be formed in the alimentary tract producing an abnormal distention of the stomach which would be injurious if not fatal. The medicinal value of certain mineral waters is dependent in part upon the presence of saline cathartics. 

For example both Pluto water, from the French Lick springs, and Carlsbad water contain sodium and magnesium sulphate as well as other mineral salts. 

Certain salts, known as diuretics, are useful in medicine because they increase the flow of urine. We have already referred to the diuretic effect of the saline cathartics after absorption. Needless to say any diuretic must be capable of both solution and absorption. The following are some of the salts in common use, their action being to increase the activity of the kidney cells. 

Sodium acetate 

Potassium acetate

NaC₂H,O₂ KC2H3O2 

Potassium bitartrate (cream of tartar) KHC.H₁Os 

Potassium citrate 

Lithium salts 

Strontium lactate

KзC&H₂OT also a diaphoretic (increasing sweat) Sr(C3H5O3)2

Basham's mixture [ammonium acetate, NH4C₂H₂O₂, and iron acetate, Fe(C₂H₂O₂)] is a diuretic whose action depends upon its stimulating effect upon the circulation which results in more blood being brought to the kidneys.

When blood is taken from one person for transfusing into another, the coagulation which normally takes place very quickly must be prevented. This can be done by having sodium citrate present in the sterile flask in which the blood is to be received. The sodium citrate itself must be chemically pure and the 1.5% solution in which it is used must be sterilized. For every 100 cc. of blood 20 cc. of the sodium citrate solution are used. In an effort to produce the relaxation which is necessary for many surgical processes very interesting results have been obtained by using magnesium sulphate with the preliminary morphine medication. It has been found that when 1/12-3/8 of a grain of morphine is used with 1-2 cc. of a 25% solution of chemically pure magnesium sulphate instead of with water and given hypodermically, the value of the morphine is increased to such a degree that one injection will produce the same effect as 2-4 injections if given in a water solution. The magnesium sulphate apparently prolongs the effect of the morphine and does away with the nausea, vomiting and gas pains which so often follow repeated doses of the drug. One explanation offered for this action is that the salt helps to hold the morphine in contact with the nerve tissue for a longer time. Another explanation is that each chemical has an affinity for certain parts of the nervous system and thus the analgesic effect is increased. 

In some hospitals 50 cc. of a 25% magnesium sulphate solution are diluted to 300 cc. or 500 cc. with sterile water and given as a hypodermoclysis. It is begun two hours before the operation and requires half an hour. The solution of morphine in sterile water is then given as a hypodermic. Magnesium sulphate also prolongs the action of nitrous oxide to such an extent that ether is sometimes unnecessary. 

Morphine, opium, cocaine, novocaine, codeine and quinine are a few examples of drugs which belong to a group of nitrogen-containing organic bases called alkaloids. Some,

SALTS 

155 as novocaine, are artificial products. Others occur naturally, being products of vegetable organisms. For example, opium is a mixture of alkaloids derived from the poppy of which the most important is morphine. As they are soluble to a very slight degree, if at all, they are commonly used in the form of salts though the name may suggest only the alkaloid. For example, morphine sulphate or morphine hydrochloride is the form in which this alkaloid is used. Novocaine and cocaine are used in combination with hydrochloric acid (hydrochloride). Quinine sulphate is the most common preparation in which it is used. 

It is obvious that they must be used in a form that is readily soluble since many of them are given by hypodermic. 

Even if given by mouth, as in the case of quinine preparations, the principal action occurs after absorption into the blood stream. It is, therefore, equally necessary for it to be in a form which is both readily soluble and absorbable. 

Potassium permanganate is used as an antidote for alkaloid poisoning, its action being to oxidize the alkaloid to a harmless compound which can then be washed out of the stomach. The potassium permanganate should be used in a solution no stronger than 1 to 1000 and all the crystals must be thoroughly dissolved lest the stomach itself be oxidized.

Mineral salts are of very great importance in health as well as in disease. Many of the elements necessary to the various tissues of the body, either to keep them in repair or to supply new building material, must come from such compounds or from complex organic compounds. Fats and carbohydrates can supply only carbon, hydrogen and oxygen. These three elements, and in addition nitrogen and sulphur, can be furnished by the simple proteins. 

Other sources must be depended upon for the other ele ments (see Chapter II-Elements in Body). The principal salts occurring in the body are the chlorides of sodium, potassium and calcium (NaCl, KCl, CaCl2), the phosphates

of magnesium, calcium and sodium (MgHPO4, Caз (P04) 2,

Na₂HPO4, NaH,PO,), sodium carbonate (Na2CO3), sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3) and magnesium sulphate (MgSO4).

The foods contributing largely to their supply are fruits and vegetables, whole grains, milk and eggs. While it is not necessary with a well-chosen diet to add inorganic salts to our foods we do use sodium chloride to make it more appetizing. An unusually large amount of these salts is required during childhood, pregnancy and convalescence after wasting diseases because of the building of large amounts of new tissue. Therefore, at such times, the food should be chosen with special care. With the discovery of the close relation between goiter and a deficiency in iodine, it is now often made good by the use of "iodine salt," containing sodium iodide, especially in those sections in which there is a noticeable lack of iodine compounds in the soil and water.

In spite of the large amounts of acids, which are constantly being formed as a result of the normal metabolism of body tissue and of food material and which are carried in the lymph and blood before they are finally eliminated, the blood is slightly alkaline in its reaction. Only in extreme conditions does it reach a condition of neutrality. Two salts in particular, sodium bicarbonate and disodium phosphate, are mainly responsible for the maintenance of this condition of alkalinity, and because of their great ability to protect the body against acids they are called buffer salts. One way in which each helps is through a reaction with the acid, for example, sulphuric acid. Although another acid substance, carbonic acid or monosodium phosphate, is one of the products of the reaction, its characteristics are less pronounced than were those of the original acid. The following equations represent each reaction- 

(see also Chapters X and XII). 

2 NaHCO3 + H2SO4 2 Na₂HPO4 + H2SO4 Na2SO4 + H2CO3 Na2SO4 + 2 NaH₂PO

SALTS 

157 Mineral salts are essential to furnish the materials necessary to maintain the normal acidity or alkalinity of the digestive juices. It is very necessary that the proper condition be maintained, for otherwise digestive disturbances may occur. For example, gastric fermentations take place if the hydrochloric acid content of the gastric juice is below normal. As the production of the acid is believed to depend in part on a reaction between sodium chloride and carbonic acid, the necessity of sodium chloride in the body is evident. 

NaCl + H2CO3 NaHCO3 + HCI

The changes occurring during digestion are brought about through the help of enzymes. As their activity is very dependent on definite conditions of acidity or alkalinity, any alteration in it will interfere with the process of digestion. By the help of constituents supplied from mineral salts the normal reaction is maintained.

All the body fluids hold various substances in solution. 

The solubility of some of them, proteins especially, is helped by the presence of salts in small amounts. In their absence a number of these substances could not remain in solution.

We have already spoken of the osmotic pressure of solutions being due to dissolved substances in them. In a normal body an equilibrium exists between the different body fluids which are separated from each other by living membranes. Upon this depends the exchange of materials, those which are needed by the body cells on the one hand and on the other the waste products which are removed from them. 

The maintenance of the normal concentrations and osmotic pressures depends in large part upon the mineral salts present in the body fluids. If for some reason the equilibrium is upset they will help to restore a more normal condition (see oedema and saline cathartics).

Experiments have shown that salts of calcium, sodium and potassium have a direct influence on the irritability of

nerve tissue and the contractility of muscle tissue. Their presence is essential, therefore, to the proper functioning of nerves and muscles. Through the effect of calcium salts muscles contract, whereas sodium and potassium salts cause the relaxation of muscle fibers. The alternate contraction and relaxation of cardiac muscle are dependent not only upon the presence of these salts but also upon the proper relation between them.

SUMMARY 

Salts.-Compounds composed of a metal or metallic radical combined with a non-metallic or acid radical. 

Salts Whose Names End in "ate."-Are related to oxygen-containing acids whose names end in "ic." 

Salts Whose Names End in "ite."-Are related to oxygen-containing acids whose names end in "ous." 

Salts Whose Names End in "ide."-Are related to acids containing no oxygen.

Osmosis. The passage or diffusion of molecules of water or dissolved substances through a membrane.

Crystalloids. Substances whose molecules can undergo osmosis. 

Colloids. Substances whose dissolved molecules cannot undergo osmosis.

Dialysis. The separation of different substances from each other by the process of osmosis, i.e., crystalloids from colloids. 

Isotonic Solutions.-Two solutions having the same osmotic pressure.

Hypotonic Solution.-A solution whose osmotic pressure is lower than that of the one with which it is compared. 

Hypertonic Solution.-A solution whose osmotic pressure is higher than that of the one with which it is compared. 

Hydrolysis. A reaction between a compound and water which results in the formation of simpler compounds. A double decomposition in which water is one of the reacting agents. 

The decomposition of a substance into simpler substances through a chemical reaction with water.

Buffer Salts.-Mineral salts present in the blood which help to preserve its slight alkalinity through their effect upon the acid products of metabolism.
