EXPERIMENTS 1. Osmosis 

Place a dried bean in water and note the gradual change in its size and feeling. Explain. Mention some other foods in which this same change commonly occurs. 

2. Demonstration (Should Be Allowed to Stand About 24 Hours) 

(a) Scoop out the inside of a small carrot or potato (if the latter is used remove the skin from the portion which is to be in contact with the water). Fill the cavity with thin sugar syrup and fit the hole at the top with a one bore stopper (must be tight). Insert in the stopper a glass tube about 18 inches long so that it will extend about an inch into the syrup. Suspend the carrot or potato in a jar of water and arrange a support for the tube. What evidences have you of the passage of sugar into the water? Of the passage of water into the syrup? Which is taking place more rapidly? Explain what has happened.

(b) In a test-tube place 20-25 cc. of a dilute solution of copper sulphate (7 grams to 100 cc. water). By means of a pipette or medicine dropper carefully introduce into this solution 

1-2 drops of a concentrated solution of potassium ferrocyanide (20 grams to 100 cc. water). This can be done most successfully if the mouth of the pipette or medicine dropper extends into the copper sulphate solution. The surface of the drop of potassium ferrocyanide becomes coated with a precipitate of copper ferrocyanide formed by the chemical reaction between the two salts (write the reaction in words). This coating acts as a semipermeable membrane. Note the gradual swelling of the drop. 

What finally happens? Explain. This illustrates the hemolysis of red corpuscles. 

Reverse the conditions of the experiment and introduce 1-2 drops of the dilute solution of copper sulphate into the concentrated solution of potassium ferrocyanide. In this case note the gradual shrinking of the drop. Explain. This illustrates the plasmolysis of corpuscles. (From MacLeod's Chemistry for Nurses and Students of Home Economics.)

CHAPTER XII IONIZATION 

Physical Properties of Solutions. In the preceding chapter, the statement was made that the osmotic pressure of a solution becomes greater as the concentration of the dissolved substance is increased. Both the boiling point and the freezing point are affected in the same way, the former being higher than 100° C. (212° F.) and the latter lower than 0° C. (32° F). Experiments to determine the variations in these physical properties with different degrees of concentration have revealed some very interesting results. 

With the exception of acids, bases and salts the gram molecular weight (see Chapter VI) of the dissolved substance in the same volume of water in every case produces the same increase in the boiling point and in the osmotic pressure and the same decrease in the freezing point. For example, if any one of the following compounds is dissolved in 1000 cc. (1 liter) of water, the freezing point is -1.89° C., the boiling point is 100.52° C. (if the dissolved substance does not vaporize) and the osmotic pressure is equivalent to 26.6 atmospheres (250 lbs.-to the square inch). 

32 grams methyl alcohol 

46 grams ethyl alcohol 

74 grams methyl acetate 

92 grams glycerine 

342 grams cane sugar

CH₂OH C,H,OH CH,C₂H₂O₂ C₂H, (OH)3 C12H22O11 3 Since the weights mentioned are the molecular weights 161

expressed in grams, they all contain the same number of molecules. The depression of the freezing point, the elevation of the boiling point and the increase in the osmotic pressure depend, then, not on the kind of dissolved particles present in the solution but on their number. In aqueous solutions of acids, bases and salts, the influence on each of these physical properties is even greater. A solution of sodium chloride containing 58.46 grams per liter (its gram molecular weight) will freeze at -3.42° C. instead of -1.89° C. and will boil at 100.94° C. instead of 100.52° C., the depression and elevation being nearly twice that caused by the other substances. While the molar solutions of all acids, bases and salts do not show the same increase in the boiling point or decrease in the freezing point, it is true that in each case the change is greater than in other kinds of solutions. Such solutions must contain, then, a larger number of particles than would be expected from the amount of dissolved substance present.

Ionization. To reconcile the abnormal influence such substances have upon these physical properties, the molecules are pictured as undergoing a temporary dissociation or splitting into smaller particles when they are dissolved in water. Suppose we illustrate with the sodium chloride solution. The belief that a large number of the salt molecules in the solution containing 58.46 grams of sodium chloride per liter dissociate means the presence of some sodium chloride molecules and also many smaller sodium particles and chlorine particles, the total number of particles being nearly twice the number of the original molecules. Such a condition would explain the actual boiling point and freezing point the solution is known to have. 

The abnormally low freezing point of the calcium chloride brine used in making artificial ice can be accounted for in this way. That this splitting is temporary rather than

IONIZATION 

163 permanent is evident since the salt is left in its original state on the evaporation of the water. The particles of sodium and chlorine resulting from the splitting of the sodium chloride molecules must be different from particles of free sodium and free chlorine, for their characteristics are different. Free sodium cannot exist independently in water. The instant the two come in contact with each other a reaction takes place, the products of which are gaseous hydrogen and sodium hydroxide. That the salt solution contains neither of these substances is evident, for we see no effervescence; neither is the solution alkaline to litmus. Free chlorine is a greenish yellow gas with a very distinctive, irritating odor. As the sodium chloride solution is odorless and colorless we know the chlorine particles must be different from free chlorine. The difference in the particles resulting from the splitting of the dissolved sodium chloride molecules is believed to be due to the fact that each carries an electric charge equivalent to its combining ability. Each sodium particle carries one positive charge and each chlorine particle carries one negative charge. To distinguish them from free sodium and free chlorine, they are represented as Na+ and Cl. This temporary splitting of molecules of acids, bases and salts when in solution is called ionization and the positively and negatively charged particles are called ions. The metallic part of the molecule, either replaceable hydrogen or a metal, forms the positive ion and the acid radical or hydroxyl radical becomes the negative ion. The following type of equation is used to represent the ionization of a substance. 

The double arrow indicates the temporary splitting and the idea that ions recombine to form molecules at the same time molecules are ionizing so that under a given condition there is always a balance between the ionized and the unionized molecules.

NaCl Na+ + Cl- Na2SO4 Na+Na+ SO₁—— CuCl2

Cu++ + Cl− + Cl− HCI

H++ Cl- NaOH Na+ + OH- A molecule does not always ionize into an equal number of positive and negative ions, but the total number of charges carried by the positive ions always equals the total number of charges carried by the negative ions. Note the preceding examples. On ionization one molecule of sodium chloride forms one positive sodium ion and one negative chlorine ion, each of which carries one charge. A molecule of sodium sulphate ionizes into two positive sodium ions and one negative sulphate ion, but the total positive charges of the sodium ions equals the total negative charges of the sulphate ion. One positive copper ion and two negative chlorine ions result from the ionization of a molecule of copper chloride, the two positive charges carried by the copper ion equalling the total of two negative charges carried by the chlorine ions.

Degree of Ionization and Factors Influencing It.—The proportion of the dissolved molecules in an ionized condition, which is called the degree of ionization, is expressed numerically. For example, 0.92 represents the degree of ionization for a certain solution of hydrochloric acid, meaning that 92% of the dissolved molecules are there as hydrogen and chlorine ions and the remaining 8% are undissociated molecules. A number of factors influence the degree of ionization. One of these is the kind of substance in solution. All acids and bases do not ionize equally well and they are called either strong or weak accordingly (for further explanation see ionization and acid and basic char-

IONIZATION 

165 acteristics). Salts as a rule are well ionized. Another factor having a very important influence on the degree of ionization is the liquid in which the compound is dissolved. We have mentioned water as the solvent because it is the most common solvent and also because it is more favorable to ionization than any other liquid. In some, as toluene, no ionization occurs. In alcohol it is so slight the effect is not noticeable. The effect solvents have upon ionization can easily be shown by taking two small portions of copper bromide, dissolving one in water and the other in alcohol. If the water solution is sufficiently dilute, its color will be blue because of the presence of a large number of blue copper ions. The alcohol solution, on the other hand, will be brown because so much of the copper bromide is not ionized, brown being the color of copper bromide molecules. The concentration of the solution also influences the degree of ionization, the more dilute the solution is the greater being the percentage of ionized molecules as the following figures show. 

Sodium chloride 55.46 grams per liter 0.66 or 66% ionized 

Sodium chloride 29.2 grams per liter 0.74 or 74% ionized 

Sodium chloride 5.846 grams per liter 0.92 or 92% ionized

It can also be shown by a simple experiment. Using copper bromide again for the dissolved substance so long as the undissociated molecules are largely in excess of the ions the solution will be brown. This is the condition when the solution is very concentrated. When the molecules decrease in number and the ions increase the color of the solution will change from brown to brownish green to green to blue when finally the solution contains a large number of ions but very few molecules. The color change in the order given comes as the solution is made more and more dilute by the addition of water. Such an experiment is, therefore, a visible proof that ionization is affected by the

concentration of the solution. Still another factor influencing the per cent of ionized substance is the presence in the same solution of another substance whose ionization will supply more ions like either kind from the first substance. This factor, one of the most important, is called the presence of a common ion. An experiment in which copper bromide and potassium bromide are used will show the effect upon the ionization. Both substances when ionized form bromine ions, the common ion. CuBr2

KBr 

Cu+++ Br+ Br

K++ Br As both potassium bromide molecules and potassium and bromine ions are colorless any observed color change will be due to differences in the amount of ionized copper bromide molecules. Starting with a solution of copper bromide, dilute enough to be blue because of the large per cent of ions present, as more and more potassium bromide is added. to it, the color changes to green, then to greenish brown and finally to brown. In other words, the ability of the copper bromide molecules to ionize is decreased as the potassium bromide dissolves and subsequently ionizes. This is due to the common ion which it introduces into the solution.

Ionization and Double Decomposition of Acids, Bases and Salts.-While there is no one specific test which will be responded to by all acids, bases and salts, there are type reactions common to the three groups of compounds. One such reaction is the double decomposition which occurs between any two if in solution-note the following exam ples-see also Chapters IX, X and XI. 

AgNo:+NaCl 

FeCl3 NH,OH 

HCI + NaOH AgCl+

Fe(OH)3 HOH + NaNO3 + 3 NH4Cl + NaCl

IONIZATION 

167 There are three important points to be brought out in connection with these reactions. The first is that in each case the reaction takes place immediately when the solutions come in contact with each other. In the second place, the products are never formed by a combination of two metallic units or two non-metallic units. A silver unit and a sodium unit never combine, neither do the chlorine unit and nitrate radical unite. In the recombination occurring during the reaction a metallic unit combines always with a non-metallic unit, the results being silver chloride and sodium nitrate. Again, if the same two substances whose solutions react together suddenly and with a definite exchange of metallic and non-metallic units, are brought together in the solid form either no reaction is apparent or, if it does occur, it is of a very different type. It involves chemical changes that are far more deep-seated and result in products that do not have the same close relationship to the substances from which they were produced. 

Does not the assumption of the previous condition of ionization of each solution help to explain the characteristic features of a double decomposition reaction? The solution of sodium chloride, for example, contains molecules of sodium chloride and also positive sodium ions and negative chlorine ions, and the silver nitrate solution contains silver nitrate molecules, positive silver ions and negative nitrate ions. Because of the attraction particles with opposite kinds of charges have for each other when the solutions are brought together, certain kinds of ions repel each other while others are drawn together, new molecules being formed through their union. Positive sodium ions never attract other positive ions such as silver; they repel them instead. The same is true of negative chlorine and nitrate ions. The attraction is between positive silver ions and negative chlorine ions which unite to form molecules of silver chloride. Because of its insolubility this substance

remains in the form of molecules, ionization depending upon solution. Positive sodium ions and negative nitrate ions also are attracted to each other and unite to form molecules of sodium nitrate which remains in solution and therefore again forms sodium and nitrate ions. Because the dissolved substance in each solution is already in a state of ionization, the reaction can take place immediately when the two solutions are brought together as the positively and negatively charged particles capable of repelling or attracting each other are already present. 

Ionization-Conductivity and Electrolysis. Another common characteristic of acids, bases and salts when in solution is their ability to conduct an electric current and be decomposed by it. A decomposition brought about in this way is called electrolysis. The substances in which it can occur and which can conduct an electric current are called electrolytes. Suppose we attach two wires, one to the positive pole and one to the negative pole of a battery, and to the opposite end of each wire we attach a platinum strip or electrode which can be introduced into a beaker containing the substance we wish to experiment with (the electrodes must not be allowed to touch each other). By including an electric bulb in the wiring between the positive pole and the electrode at the end we can have immediate proof of the ability of each substance to conduct the current. 

Pure water shows no such ability. Neither do the substances which have only a normal effect upon the boiling and the freezing points and upon the osmotic pressure, for example sugar, alcohol, glycerine, methyl acetate, etc. If the beaker contains a solution of an acid, base or salt, however, the lamp glows because the current has been able to travel across from one electrode to the other. While this is happening, if we watch the solution carefully, we can see evidences of a chemical reaction the products of which form at either electrode. With a solution of hydro-

IONIZATION 

169 chloric acid, for example, bubbles of gas can be seen on both platinum strips. The gas collecting at the positive electrode, where the current enters the solution, is free chlorine, and at the negative electrode, at which the current passes out, free hydrogen collects and escapes. In other words, the dissolved hydrogen chloride is decomposed into hydrogen and chlorine by a process of electrolysis. A solution of copper chloride under the same conditions will show gaseous chlorine escaping at the positive electrode and a deposit of free copper on the negative electrode. With a solution of sodium chloride, gas bubbles will be seen collecting and escaping at each electrode. As in the two preceding cases the gas at the positive electrode is chlorine. At the negative electrode, in this case, free sodium is being formed, but because it is in a water medium the instant it is freed it reacts with the water producing free hydrogen and sodium hydroxide. The hydrogen, the gas seen at the negative electrode, escapes. The sodium hydroxide remains in solution and can be detected by its litmus reaction. Other examples might be cited, in every case the results being similar. Hydrogen, or the metallic part of the dissolved substance, collects at the negative plate and is either deposited, escapes in a gaseous form or undergoes another reaction if it cannot remain free under the existing conditions. The non-metallic part of the dissolved substance, either the acid radical or the hydroxyl radical, collects at the positive electrode and behaves in the same way. 

Believing that a portion of the dissolved substance is already in a state of ionization helps to explain the facts just described. Any such solution contains both molecules and positive and negative ions. The solution of hydrochloric acid, for example, contains some undissociated molecules of hydrogen chloride and also hydrogen ions and chlorine ions, this condition being dependent upon the state of solution and not upon the passage of the electric current.

When the electrodes are introduced in it and the current is turned on, each electrode becomes a charged surface, the one positive and the other negative. What is the effect upon the positive and negative ions which are already in the solution? Each kind is attracted or drawn toward the surface having the opposite kind of charge. The positive hydrogen ions travel toward the negative electrode and, as each one comes in contact with it, its charge is lost and it acquires the characteristics of an atom of hydrogen needing another atom of hydrogen to become a molecule of free hydrogen. The bubbles seen on the surface are the hydrogen molecules collecting and escaping. In the same way the negative chlorine ions are drawn over to the positively charged surface. On coming in contact with it each chlorine ion loses its negative charge, becoming first a chlorine atom which needs another chlorine atom to form a molecule of free chlorine. The bubbles seen at this electrode are molecules of free chlorine collecting and escaping.

The stream of charged ions traveling through the solution constitutes the current which is being conducted across from one electrode to the other.

A person becomes a very much better conductor of electricity when moisture is present and the skin is wet. 

Although water, if pure, is a non-conductor, that which we ordinarily use contains salts in solution in varying amounts. 

On coming in contact with the skin it has a chance to dissolve more soluble substances, notably sodium chloride which is a constant constituent of the perspiration remaining on the skin when the water evaporates. For this reason one feels a distinct shock when taking up a telephone or turning on a light with wet hands. The same reason accounts for the direction to avoid wet compresses on a patient while he is being X-rayed.

Ionization and Acid and Basic Characteristics.- -The common characteristics of acids, which we have already

IONIZATION 

171 explained as being apparent only when they are in solution, we can now say are due to the hydrogen ions which are always formed by the ionization of any acid. The common characteristics of bases, also apparent only when they are in solution, are due to the hydroxyl ions which are always formed when they ionize. The more hydrogen or hydroxyl ions the solution contains, the more pronounced will its acid or basic characteristics be. A strong acid or base, then, is one which will supply to the solution many hydrogen or hydroxyl ions because so many of its molecules can be ionized. A weak acid or base, on the other hand, supplies only a small number of hydrogen or hydroxyl ions because so few of its molecules can be ionized. A condition of acidity, alkalinity or neutrality can be thought of with reference to these two characteristic ions and the proportion between them for the same solution will contain both hydrogen ions and hydroxyl ions. This is true even of solutions of acids and bases for, while the dissolved substance furnishes only the one or the other, water molecules ionize to a very slight degree and as a result supply both hydrogen and hydroxyl ions. HOH H+ + OH- Therefore, a solution of hydrochloric acid will contain a large number of hydrogen ions and a small number of hydroxyl ions. A condition of acidity can be defined, then, as one in which there is an excess of hydrogen ions over hydroxyl ions. For the same reasons a solution of sodium hydroxide will contain a large number of hydroxyl ions and a very small number of hydrogen ions. A condition of alkalinity can be defined as one in which there is an excess of hydroxyl ions over hydrogen ions or, as it is also expressed, hydrogen ions are in very much smaller amounts than hydroxyl ions. A condition of neutrality results when hydrogen ions and hydroxyl ions are equal in amount.

Neutralization is a process, therefore, in which solutions of an acid and a base are brought together in such amounts as to cause an equality between the hydrogen and hydroxyl ions present in the resulting solution. Water is a truly neutral product because its molecule is formed by the union of one hydrogen ion and one hydroxyl ion. H++ OH- → HOH The acidity or alkalinity of solutions of certain salts can now be explained more fully. In a solution of sodium carbonate we have said that some of the salt undergoes hydrolysis, forming sodium hydroxide and carbonic acid. 

Both compounds ionize, the one producing hydrox ions and the other producing hydrogen ions. However, sodium hydroxide is a strong base and carbonic acid is a weak acid and, therefore, the hydroxyl ions are in excess of the hydrogen ions (or hydrogen ions are much less in amount than hydroxyl ions). The result is a condition of alkalinity. 

In a solution of aluminium sulphate by the hydrolysis of some of the salt, aluminium hydroxide, a weak base, and sulphuric acid, a strong acid, are produced. Therefore, the solution will contain hydrogen ions in excess of hydroxyl ions, its reaction being acid.

The acidity, alkalinity or neutrality of the body fluids is often referred to as the hydrogen ion concentration. 

Since in every case it results from the balance between the hydrogen and hydroxyl ions, the function of the buffer salts in the blood is to keep down the hydrogen ion concentration so that it is less than the hydroxyl ion concentration (slight alkalinity). A partial explanation of the action of sodium bicarbonate has already been given. By interpreting the action from the standpoint of ionization it is now possible to show two ways by which it helps. 

Through the reaction of the sodium bicarbonate with a stronger acid, as sulphuric acid, a new acid, carbonic acid,

IONIZATION 

173 and a new salt are formed. As the carbonic acid is a weak acid a very small amount of it will ionize. Therefore, the number of hydrogen ions present will be greatly reduced. 

On ionization both sodium bicarbonate and carbonic acid form the same negative ion.

NaHCO3 

H2CO3 

Na++HCO3- H+ + HCO3¯ Therefore, sodium bicarbonate is useful in another way, for because of its presence in the blood and the common ion which it supplies, the ionization of the carbonic acid is checked. By this action the hydrogen ion concentration is reduced still more. The other buffer salt, disodium phosphate, functions in the same ways. Therefore, a decrease in the amounts of these two substances below a point which cannot be made good through the food supply will lessen the body's ability to control the hydrogen ion concentration of the blood.

SUMMARY 

Ionization. The temporary splitting of dissolved molecules of acids, bases and salts into smaller particles which carry either positive or negative charges equivalent in each case to the combining ability of the particle.

Positive Ions. The particles carrying positive charges-always hydrogen ions and metallic ions.

Negative Ions. The particles carrying negative charges-always acid radical ions and hydroxyl ions. 

Degree of Ionization. The proportion (per cent) of the dissolved substance that is ionized.

Electrolysis. The decomposition of a substance that has been brought about by electricity.

Acids. Compounds which yield hydrogen ions when ionized. 

Strong Acids.-Acids which ionize readily, i.e., have a high degree of ionization. Therefore, their solutions will contain a large number of hydrogen ions.

Weak Acids.-Acids which do not ionize readily-i.e, have a low

degree of ionization. Therefore, their solutions will contain. a small number of hydrogen ions.

Bases. Compounds which yield hydroxyl ions when ionized. 

Strong Bases.-Bases which ionize readily-have a high degree of ionization. Therefore, their solutions will contain a large number of hydroxyl ions. Weak Bases.-Bases which do not ionize readily. Their solutions will contain a small number of hydroxyl ions. 

Acidity. Hydrogen ions in excess of hydroxyl ions. H+) OH-. 

Neutrality.-Hydrogen ions equal to hydroxyl ions. H+ OH-. 

Alkalinity. Hydrogen ions less than hydroxyl ions. H+ ( OH−. 

Neutralization.-A reaction in which an acid and a base are brought together in such amounts that the hydrogen and hydroxyl ions are equal in amount.

CHAPTER XIII THE HALOGENS 

Some General Characteristics. Fluorine, chlorine, bromine and iodine belong to a group of elements named halogens, meaning salt formers, because they all form compounds with sodium which resemble sodium chloride. We have already suggested the grouping together of compounds because of common properties, for example the acids and the bases. In the halogen family we have an illustration of a similar classification of elements for the same reason. 

In this particular case the relation between the members of the family and a gradation in their properties following their atomic weights is very marked. Fluorine, which has the smallest atomic weight (19), is a gaseous element. In fact it retains this form under a wide range of temperature conditions, being liquified and then solidified with great difficulty. Chlorine, whose atomic weight is 35.46, is also gaseous under ordinary conditions, but is liquified and solidified much more readily than is fluorine. While bromine, atomic weight 79.9, is ordinarily a liquid it vaporizes so readily that even on exposure to the air considerable gas will be formed. Iodine, having the highest atomic weight (126.9), is a solid which changes to a vapor on heating.

Ordinarily when a solid is heated it first liquifies and then vaporizes, but in some cases, as with iodine, a gas is formed directly from the solid. This is called sublimation. With the increase in atomic weight the colors also become more pronounced (see table in summary). The solubility in water, however, decreases. The chemical properties show the same resemblances, with gradations. For example, they 175

are all so active as elements that they occur naturally only in combination with other elements. Fluorine, one of the most active of all the elements, is freed from its compounds with great difficulty. Chlorine, bromine and iodine can be obtained more easily, the readiness increasing in each case with the rise in atomic weight. All four elements combine with hydrogen to form compounds which are named, respectively hydrogen fluoride, hydrogen chloride, hydrogen bromide and hydrogen iodide. Fluorine, the most active of the four, forms the most stable compound, while the compound with iodine is the least stable. These hydrogen compounds are soluble in water, the resulting solutions having acidic properties. The halogens themselves are therefore classified as non-metals. Other similarities in properties might be mentioned, but the few examples we have chosen are sufficient to show the close relation existing between the members of the family. 

Chlorine and Its Compounds.-Chlorine is the most important member of the halogen family. It occurs in combination with sodium and potassium principally and to a less degree with magnesium and calcium. Of the 3.6% of solid matter in sea water 2.8% consists of sodium chloride.

In the earth there are also deposits of chlorides which in certain localities form layers of great thickness. In the body it occurs largely as sodium chloride, an essential constituent of body fluids, and in the gastric juice as hydrochloric acid.

We have already referred to the liberation of free chlorine in the decomposition of any chloride by the electric current (see Chapter XII). This is the principal commercial method of manufacture. In the laboratory it is easily obtained by a reaction between hydrochloric acid and an oxidizing agent. Manganese dioxide (MnO2) or potassium permanganate (KMnO,) are the ones commonly used, with the latter no heat being needed. The products of the

THE HALOGENS 

177 reaction are the chlorides of manganese and of potassium, water and free chlorine. 

2 KMnO4 + 16 HCl 

2 KCl + 2 MnCl2 + 8 H2O + 5 Cl₂

Being very soluble in water it cannot be collected over water. The method of collection is by upward displacement of air; in other words, the receiving flask is left in an upright position. As the gas is nearly two and one-half times as heavy as air, on entering the flask it sinks to the bottom and forces the air upward. Because of its color it is not difficult to tell when the flask has become filled. 

The odor of free chlorine is penetrating and irritating to the mucous membranes, causing them to increase their secretions. Inhaled in large amounts it is nauseating and even poisonous. In fact it was one of the first "poison gases" to be used in the World War. Inhaling alcohol or ammonia lessens the irritation of the mucous membranes. 

Care must be used, however, with ammonia as it also produces harmful results if inhaled in too great quantities. 

Sodium bicarbonate is also given to counteract the hydrochloric acid which is said to be increased by the chlorine. 

Following this, milk or albumin is given to protect the mucous membrane of the stomach against the irritating effect of the gas. Mixed with air in small and carefully controlled amounts, the gas is thought to have a beneficial effect on colds, and in recent years a chlorine treatment has been developed. The idea that it might be of use in this way came from the observation that employees in chlorine plants were remarkably free from colds.

Chlorine water, a water solution of the gas, contains not only dissolved chlorine but also hydrochloric and hypochlorous acids (HCl and HCIO), products of a reaction. between the water and the chlorine.

H2O + Cl₂ 

HCI + HCIO

When the solution is first made only a portion of the chlorine is changed chemically because of the tendency of the two acids to react together to form the original substances. Hypochlorous acid is very unstable, however, and it gradually decomposes into hydrochloric acid and oxygen. 

The action is hastened by light, so that eventually the solution contains only hydrochloric acid. For this reason chlorine water can be kept for only a short time even when it is stored in brown bottles. The oxygen which is released from the hypochlorous acid escapes as free oxygen unless some substance is present which can be oxidized by it while it is still in its nascent (most active) state. 

2 HCIO 2 HCl + O2 

The principal uses of chlorine for bleaching, stain removal and disinfection are dependent upon the action of this hypochlorous acid. It can easily be proved that in the absence of moisture chlorine has no bleaching effect by placing samples of the same colored materials in two jars of chlorine, in one of which no moisture is present and in the other the sample is wet. In the latter case only is the color removed because the condition present makes the formation of hypochlorous acid possible. The oxygen released by it oxidizes the dye to a colorless compound. 

In the manufacture of linen and cotton cloth the natural yellow or brown color of the fibers is removed by bleaching, free chlorine or chlorine compounds being the oxidizing agents most frequently used. They cannot be used with silk and wool, however, for these fibers are disintegrated very quickly by their action. Certain food materials, for example flour, are sometimes bleached by means of chlorine.

Because of its action on micro-organisms and upon protein material and its decomposition products chlorine is useful as a disinfectant. A 0.3% solution will destroy even the spores of bacteria in about three hours. It also removes

THE HALOGENS 

179 disagreeable odors. A number of cities, among them being 

New York, Buffalo, Baltimore, Chicago and Philadelphia, use either free chlorine or chlorine compounds for the purification of their water supplies. Free chlorine can be obtained in small, steel cylinders in which it is kept under pressure in a liquid form and from which it is used. The chief advantage in using liquid chlorine for large volumes of water is that it is easy to control the chlorine concentration by means of a gas pressure gauge. 0.5-1 part per 

1,000,000 parts of water is usually adequate for the purification.

For smaller volumes of water or for use as a hospital disinfectant a chlorine compound is more satisfactory. 

Chloride of lime or bleaching powder, as it is commonly called, is often used. This compound is the calcium salt of both hydrochloric and hypochlorous acid, its molecule being composed of an atom of calcium holding in combination one acid radical from each of the two acids (CaCICIO). 

For this reason it is difficult to give it a chemical name which expresses its exact composition. When bleaching powder is added to water a reaction takes place in which hypochlorous acid is formed. Therefore, its action is similar to that of free chlorine.

CaCICIO + H₂O 

CaOHCl + HCIO (basic calcium chloride) 

On exposure to the air a reaction between the bleaching powder, carbon dioxide and water (carbonic acid) will occur in which hypochlorous acid is formed. 

2 CaCICIO + H2CO3 

CaCl2 + CaCO3 + 2HCIO Many gaseous substances having objectionable odors can be oxidized by the hypochlorous acid formed in this way.

Bleaching powder is useful, therefore, in purifying the air as well for the disinfection of urine, stools or a water supply. 

Through a reaction between chloride of lime and sodium

carbonate sodium hypochlorite, a salt of hypochlorous acid is formed. Javelle water, a solution containing this salt, has long been known. It is useful for bleaching and removing stains, for cleansing medicine droppers and douche nozzles and even as a hand disinfectant. Because of excessive amounts of alkaline substances in it, however, it is unreliable as a disinfectant for wounds. The action of lime soda, a preparation consisting of dry chloride of lime and sodium carbonate, as a hand disinfectant, may also be due to this reaction.

CaCICIO + NaCO → CaCO3 + NaCl + NaClO Dakin's solution is essentially a solution containing 

0.45-0.5% sodium hypochlorite made neutral usually with boric acid. The action of the salt is similar to that of the acid, the oxygen which is released being responsible for the action. Because of its instability and the necessity for its frequent renewal, methods have been devised by which wounds can undergo a constant irrigation.

NaCIO- NaCl + 0 (which disinfects by oxidation) Zonite is a stabilized sodium hypochlorite solution. It has been treated in such a way as to make its effect more lasting and also to increase its keeping qualities. 

Other chlorine compounds useful as disinfectants are chloramine-T and dichloramine-T, both of which can be used in higher concentrations than the hypochlorite (see 

Chapter XXVI). Potassium chlorate, another chlorine compound acting as an oxidizing agent, is useful as a mouth wash and a gargle. Two other common compounds, chloroform CHCl,) and chloral hydrate (CC1,CHO.H₂O), are useful as depressants, the former as an anæsthetic, the latter to produce sleep or to prevent convulsions. Among the chlorides having medicinal uses are mercurous chloride (calomel HgCl), a cathartic, and mercuric chloride (HgCl₂), a disinfectant.

THE HALOGENS 

181 

Bromine and Its Compounds.—Sodium and magnesium bromide are associated with the chlorides, being found in the upper layers of the deposits in the earth. They are also present in water. Methods for freeing the element from its compounds are similar to those used for chlorine. 

It can be obtained either by the electrolysis of a bromide or by the reaction between hydrobromic acid and an oxidizing agent. Either process is used industrially, but for laboratory purposes the latter is more practical. As hydrogen bromide is less stable than hydrogen chloride it is more convenient to form it during the reaction by using a mixture of sodium bromide and sulphuric acid. Manganese dioxide is the oxidizing agent most commonly used. 2 NaBr+3H2SO4 + MnO2-2NaHSO4 + MnSO4+2H2O+ Br₂ We have already referred to the color and the physical state of bromine. It has a very offensive odor to which it owes its name, which means stench. The fumes are irritating to the mucous membranes as are those of chlorine. 

The liquid causes a very severe and painful burn because of its destructive effect on tissue. The skin should be washed at once with large amounts of water. Alcohol is also good because of its solvent power, but such a burn should have medical treatment. Because of its destructive action it is very occasionally used to cauterize infected wounds.

In medicine, however, bromine finds its most important uses in the form of the bromides of potassium, sodium, strontium or ammonium. Their action depends upon their depressing effect upon the nervous system, for by lessening the activity of the brain they produce a quieting effect. As potassium salts make breathing slow and shallow and ammonium salts increase both respiration and the pulse, a better effect is obtained by giving either sodium bromide or by using a combination of them. One such mixture,

called triple bromides, consists of sodium bromide, potassium bromide and ammonium bromide.

Iodine and Its Compounds.-Iodides are present in sea water in very small amounts. They are present in larger amounts in certain sea weeds, kelp for example, which have absorbed them from the water. This was the original source from which free iodine was obtained. Iodides may also be present in other water. The element also occurs naturally as sodium iodate (NaIO) in combination with Chile saltpeter (NaNO3). In the body it is present in the white corpuscles and in the thyroid gland. It is a very essential constituent of the latter, the compound in which it is present being named iodothyrin. A deficiency in the iodine content of the thyroid results in a condition of goiter. 

Iodine can be prepared from an iodide by using sulphuric acid and manganese dioxide. The reaction is analagous to that involved in the preparation of free bromine. 2 NaI +3 H2SO4 + MnO2- 2 NaHSO4 + MnSO4 + 2 H2O + I2 

As free chlorine can displace iodine from an iodide it can also be prepared by treating sodium iodide with free chlorine. 2 NaI + Cl2 2 NaCl + I2 This was the old industrial way of obtaining it, the sodium iodide being extracted from the ash of kelp. Industrially it is now made principally from sodium iodate by a reaction with sodium sulphite (Na₂SO) and sodium bisulphite (NaHSO3). 

2NaIO3 + 3 Na₂SO3 + 2 NaHSO, 5 Na2SO4 + H2O + I2 Iodine is only slightly soluble in water, although its solubility is increased by the presence of potassium iodide in the solution. Such a solution is commonly used as a laboratory reagent. Tincture of iodine is an alcohol solution containing 7% iodine and 5% potassium iodide. This is used

THE HALOGENS 

183 as a disinfectant. Another tincture, Churchill's, which contains 16.5% iodine, is used as a counter-irritant. Colorless iodine is an alcohol solution made up with sodium thiosulphate (Na2S2O3) and ammonia. Through a reaction between the iodine and the thio-sulphate a colorless iodine compound, sodium iodide is formed. 2 Na2S2O3 + I2 Na2S406 + 2 NaI (sodium tetrathionate)

This is not so strong as the ordinary tincture and for this reason is not used in hospitals. Iodine is used as a skin disinfectant when preparing the skin for an operation, and also for surface wounds where it has free access to any infected tissue. It should not be used in too concentrated a solution, the skin should be dry and the painted surface should not be bandaged. Otherwise it may blister the surface or be absorbed into the deeper tissues and cause their destruction. Repeated applications should not be made because of too much irritation or the possibility of iodine poisoning due to its absorption. Some seem more susceptible to its effect than others. Iodine is sometimes taken with suicidal intent. Starch paste is an antidote when iodine has been swallowed. Several explanations are given for its action. According to one, the starch paste combines with the iodine, the compound formed being called starch iodide. The mucous membranes should also be protected by giving albumin, milk or some other protecting drink.

Among the iodine compounds used as disinfectants are potassium mercuric iodide and iodoform (see Chapter 

XXVI for discussion). Reference has already been made to the use of sodium iodide for supplying a possible iodine deficiency in the diet or in water (Chapter XI). An extract of sheep thyroid, called iodothyrin, is also used for the same purpose. Iodides of sodium, potassium and ammonium and also hydriodic acid are given in tertiary syphilis and in

arterial diseases such as aneurism, arteriosclerosis and other conditions of high blood pressure. 

Tests for Iodine-Bromine Chlorine.-Free iodine, even in traces, can be recognized by shaking the liquid with a few drops of carbon disulphide. This not only dissolves iodine more readily than other liquids, but the solution is a violet color. With starch paste free iodine causes the characteristic deep blue to black color due to the formation of starch iodide. Free bromine can be detected by shaking the solution with a few drops of carbon disulphide, which is a better solvent for it than any other liquid. The color of this solution is red. Free chlorine has the ability to displace either bromine or iodine from its compounds. 

Therefore, by adding either potassium bromide or potassium iodide to the solution, either free bromine or free iodine will be formed if the solution originally contained free chlorine. 2 NaBr + Cl2 2 NaI + Cl2 

2 NaCl + Br₂ 2 NaCl + I2 The free bromine or iodine can be detected by the methods just described.

Fluorine and Its Compounds.-The compounds of fluor ine occur less widely and have fewer uses than those of the other halogens. In nature it is found combined with calcium as fluorspar (calcium fluoride, CaF2). While it occurs in the body in traces only, it is an important constituent of teeth and bones. Hydrogen fluoride, either in a gaseous form or in solution as hydrofluoric acid (HF), is used to etch glass. The latter is first coated with paraffin or some protective paint and where the marking is desired this coating is removed by means of a sharp needle. It is then exposed to the hydrogen fluoride, which reacts chemically with the silicon compounds of which the glass is composed. As a result the surface becomes opaque. This process is used for marking the scales on thermometers and measur-

THE HALOGENS 

185 ing graduates. Great care must be observed when using hydrogen fluoride, for it can produce dangerous or fatal results if inhaled or because of burns from the solution.

N. B.-Arrow denotes activity of element or stability of compound decreasing in direction of head.

Sublimation. The change from a solid to a gaseous state-and from the gaseous to a solid state (without passing through a liquid state).

Nascent. The condition of an element when its atom has just been freed from a compound containing it and before it has again entered into combination with other kinds of atoms to form a new compound or with another atom of its own kind to form a molecule of the free element.

QUESTIONS 

1. Compare the form and color of the free halogens. How can the differences be accounted for? 

2. Mention three properties of chlorine which made it valuable as a poison gas. 

3. Of what does chlorine water consist when it is first made?

What does it contain after it has been standing some time? 

4. What is bleaching powder? To what is its odor due? 

Account for its action when used: (a) by exposing it to the air to remove objectionable odors caused by gases present; (b) by adding it to urine for its disinfection. 

5. Javelle water is the filtered solution of a mixture of washing

soda (sodium carbonate) and bleaching powder. What does it contain that makes it useful for removing stains? 

Explain its formation. 

6. What is the compound present in Dakin's solution that is active in the work accomplished by it? Explain the change taking place both in this compound and the substance being disinfected. 

7. How could you detect the presence of free chlorine which has not been used up when water has been purified by chlorination? 

8. Upon what does the usefulness of potassium chlorate as a gargle depend? 

9. Liquid bromine is occasionally used as a caustic. Why must it be used with very great care? 

10. Mention some bromine compounds which are useful medicinally? 

11. Why is iodine believed to be a very necessary constituent of the body? Name two compounds used to supply a deficiency in this element. 

12. When painting a portion of the skin with iodine mention two precautions to be taken to prevent absorption of the iodine into the underlying tissues. 

13. Sodium thiosulphate will remove the brown stain resulting from the application of iodine. Explain its action. 

14. Account for the blue stain which appears when tincture of iodine is spilled on a starched apron. 

15. Suppose the labels have been lost from bottles of potassium bromide and potassium iodide. Suggest a way by which you could distinguish between them. 

16. Explain the use of starch paste for removing an iodine stain from the skin. 

17. How can the markings on a thermometer be made?

EXPERIMENTS 1. Formation of Iodine 

Mix together some potassium iodide and manganese dioxide, place in an evaporating dish and arrange on a wire gauze on a tripod or ring stand so that it can be heated. Directly over the evaporating dish arrange a beaker containing water so that any material leaving the dish will collect on the bottom of the beaker. Add concentrated sulphuric acid to the contents of the

THE HALOGENS 

187 evaporating dish and heat gently. Is a gas produced? What is its color? Is a deposit formed on the bottom of the beaker? 

What is it? How was the iodine formed? What happened to it after it was formed? What is this called? 

2. Solubility of Iodine 

Test the solubility of iodine crystals in each of the following, noting the color of the solution in each case. Ether-alcoholcarbon disulphide. Which of these makes a distinctive test if only a trace of iodine is present? 

3. Iodine and Starch Paste 

To a thin starch paste solution add 1 drop of iodine. What is the result? The color is explained as being due to starch iodide. 

How has it been formed? This is used as a characteristic test for iodine. 

4. Solubility of Bromine

Add a few drops of carbon disulphide to a solution of bromine in water and shake. What is the result? How does the color of the bromine solution in carbon disulphide compare with the color of the bromine solution in water. Would it be easy to detect a trace of free bromine by this method? 

5. Comparative Activity of Chlorine, Bromine and Iodine (a) Dissolve a small crystal of potassium bromide in a little water in a test-tube. Add a little carbon disulphide. Is there any change in the color of the carbon disulphide? Explain. Add a little chlorine water to the test-tube and shake. What is the result? Explain. (b) Dissolve a small crystal of potassium iodide in a little water in a test-tube. Divide the solution in two portions. To one add some starch paste solution and to the other add some carbon disulphide. Is there any color change in either case? 

Explain. To each add some chlorine water and shake. What is the result? Explain. (c) Repeat experiment (b) using bromine water in place of chlorine water. Explain your results.

The more active halogen can displace from their compounds the halogens that are less active when in the free state. Arrange the halogens in the order of their activity (when free) and give reasons for your arrangement.

CHAPTER XIV  

Occurrence. Because of its wide occurrence in the free state, sulphur has been known from very early times. 

Deposits of it are found in volcanic regions, one of the largest being in Sicily. Most of that in commercial use in this country is obtained from mines in Louisiana, where it is taken from extensive deposits 900 feet below the surface. Louisiana sulphur is over 99% pure. As the mines' annual output, 250,000 tons, does not represent their maximum yield, the sulphur mountains near Yellowstone Park, where there is another large deposit, remain as yet an unworked source. Sulphur compounds also occur in deposits in the ground, in water and to a very much less extent as gases in the air. The two gases, sulphur dioxide (SO2) and hydrogen sulphide (H₂S) are products of volcanic activity and so are present in the air in these regions. 

The deposits of free sulphur also found there are believed to be caused by the interaction of these gases. 2 H2S + SO2 — 2 H₂O + 3 S + The air of factory districts may be contaminated by either or both of these gases as they are by-products of many industrial processes. The presence of hydrogen sulphide in solution in the waters of sulphur springs causes the disagreeable odor and taste which are characteristic of them. Mineral ores contain metals combined with sulphur, among the more common ones being lead sulphide (PbS), mercuric sulphide (HgS) and two iron sulphides, ferrous sulphide (FeS) and ferric sulphide (Fe₂S¸), the latter called fool's gold because 188

it resembles gold in appearance. Garlic owes its characteristic odor and taste to allyl sulphide ((CH,CHCH2)2S), an organic compound (containing carbon). In combination with metals and oxygen sulphur occurs commonly in compounds called sulphates (salts of sulphuric acid). Among the more familiar ones are sodium sulphate, magnesium sulphate and calcium sulphate to which we have already re ferred (see Chapter XI). Gypsum (CaSO4.2 H₂O) the form in which calcium sulphate is usually found, is sometimes well crystallized and colored with impurities. It is then called alabaster. Magnesium sulphate was named

Epsom salt because of its presence in the water of a spring in Epsom, England. It also occurs in solid deposits in salt beds. Sulphur is a very essential constituent of nitrogencontaining organic compounds called proteins (see Chapter XXIV). Plant and animal tissues, therefore, contain it in varying amounts, egg and asparagus being examples of foods with a high sulphur content. The peculiar odor noticeable in urine after asparagus has been eaten is due to a decomposition product containing the sulphur, called methyl mercaptan (CH,SH), which is eliminated by the kidneys. 

Physical Properties of Free Sulphur.-In the previous discussions of elements, the existence of each as a gas, liquid and solid has been noted although under ordinary conditions of temperature and pressure it occurs in only one of the three states. In sulphur we find an example of an element which has more than one solid and liquid form. 

When the same substance exists in two or more forms of the same physical state, i.e. solid, liquid, etc., the term allotropic has been given to the different forms. Sulphur, for example, has several allotropic forms. There are two distinct types of crystalline sulphur. When the crystallization has taken place at ordinary temperatures compact, rhombic crystals result whereas if it occurs at higher tem-

peratures more slender, needle-shaped crystals, called monoclinic, will be formed. Both forms are soluble in carbon disulphide but on the evaporation of the liquid only the rhombic crystals will appear. Monoclinic crystals will, on standing, be converted into the other variety, as the temperature is lowered. Therefore sulphur, as it exists under natural conditions, consists mainly of rhombic crystals although they are generally imperfect because of the compact mass. Roll sulphur or brimstone, as it is commonly called, is sulphur which has been melted to separate it from the earthy materials with which it is found. The melted sulphur is then run into cylindrical molds to harden. The resulting mass is largely composed of rhombic crystals although inside the roll some needle-shaped crystals may be formed for here the crystallization has taken place at a higher temperature. Very finely divided particles of rhombic sulphur are formed by adding an acid to a solution containing a polysulphide, a compound which has a high percentage of this element. The precipitated free sulphur is white and because of the milky appearance it gives the solution has been called milk of sulphur or sulphur lac. 

This precipitate, thoroughly washed to remove all traces of the substances with which it was associated, is the usual medicinal form in which sulphur is used. Being so very finely divided it can undergo chemical reactions more readily and is therefore more effective. If sulphur, which has been heated to the boiling point, is suddenly chilled by running it into cold water it forms an elastic, dough-like mass which is called plastic sulphur. On standing it loses its plasticity and becomes hard. A large portion, consisting of rhombic sulphur, can be dissolved in carbon disulphide but an insoluble residue will remain consisting of particles having no definite form. It is called amorphous sulphur (meaning without form). Flowers of sulphur, which is formed when the element sublimes (see Chapter XIII) consists

largely of small rhombic crystals mixed with particles of amorphous sulphur. When melted, sulphur, first becomes a thin, straw-colored liquid, but as the temperature is raised, the color deepens and it becomes more and more gummy and viscid. These are two distinct liquid forms. The ability of sulphur to vaporize has been suggested by speaking of flowers of sulphur as sublimed sulphur.

Sulphur is practically odorless. The so-called sulphur odor, which is suffocating and choking, is due to sulphur dioxide which is formed when sulphur burns. The most common form of sulphur is yellow although the color varies according to the form. All forms are insoluble in water. 

Crystalline sulphur is soluble in chloroform, ether, benzene, turpentine and fatty oils as well as in carbon disulphide. 

Chemical Properties.-Sulphur closely resembles oxygen in some of its chemical properties for it combines with the metallic elements, including hydrogen, to form compounds called sulphides which are analogous to the corresponding oxides. As in the combination with oxygen heat is liberated during the union although it may be necessary to heat the two elements to start the action. For example, if iron filings and powdered sulphur are heated together, when the action begins to take place the entire mass glows even if heat is no longer applied. The product is ferrous sulphide. FeS Fe + S 

With carbon, sulphur will combine to form carbon disulphide, the compound which has already been mentioned as its solvent. Bromine, iodine, phosphorus, rubber and fats are also soluble in carbon disulphide. It is useful not only as a solvent but also as an insecticide and for the extermination of rats and mice. It must be used with great care for it is both very volatile and very inflammable. 

Sulphur also combines with some of the non-metallic elements. With chlorine it forms a compound called sui

phur monochloride (SC) which is used in vulcanizing rubber. When sulphur is burned under ordinary conditions it forms sulphur dioxide. It can also be made to unite with a larger proportion of oxygen to form sulphur trioxide (SO). Both oxides have been referred to as the anhydrides of sulphurous acid (H₂SO) and sulphuric acid (H2SO4) (see Chapter IX). If sulphur is moist it will undergo a slow oxidation at an ordinary temperature, the product being sulphuric acid. 2S+ 2 H2O + 3 02 → 2 H₂SO Because of this action flowers of sulphur is used in vineyards to destroy fungus growths. The trace of sulphuric acid formed by the slow oxidation is sufficient to produce the desired result without being a source of harm. 

Uses. The old-fashioned mixture of sulphur, molasses and cream of tartar owes its beneficial effect to the laxative properties of its ingredients. Compound licorice powder, another laxative preparation, contains 8% sulphur mixed with senna and licorice. The action of the sulphur is probably due to the formation of sulphides which are later eliminated in the breath and feces, their presence causing a very objectionable odor in both cases. Sulphur is also applied externally as a disinfectant because it can destroy parasites and check bacterial growth. In the pure form it is used for scabies and certain skin diseases. 

It is also used in the form of ointments mixed with iodine, lanolin or benzoin and lard. Ichthyol, a sulphur-containing compound obtained from deposits of fossil fishes composed of about 10% sulphur, is also used both externally and internally as an antiseptic. The use of sulphur candles will be discussed in the following paragraph. 

Oxides of Sulphur and Their Related Acids and Salts.- 

Sulphur burns with a pale blue flame and the resulting gas, sulphur dioxide, has an irritating, choking odor which

is commonly but incorrectly called the odor of sulphur. The solution of this gas in water is called sulphurous acid. S+ O2 

H2O + SO2

SO₂ 

H2SO3 

Sulphurous acid is a very unstable compound and becomes oxidized to sulphuric acid either slowly on contact with atmospheric oxygen or more rapidly by removing oxygen from some compound able to give it up. To this reducing property which it possesses are due many of the important uses of sulphur dioxide and indirectly of sulphur (as the sulphur candle) from which the oxide has been produced.

It is one of the oldest disinfectants, having been used since 1771. Formerly its use was more extensive than it is now for it is no longer believed to be useful as a germicide. 

It is still valuable, however, for the extermination of insect pests, vermin, rats and mice, which may be the carriers of micro-organisms by acting as intermediary hosts. All cracks and keyholes must be carefully stuffed before a room is fumigated in order to prevent the gas from penetrating to other parts of the house. In the presence of moisture, sulphur dioxide also acts as a bleaching agent. Because of this effect, curtains, rugs, clothing or other fabrics which are exposed to the gas during the fumigation may be injured. Fruits or vegetables which are to be dried are often "sulphured"; in other words, they are exposed to sulphur dioxide fumes. Because of the bleaching effect the natural discoloration which comes when sliced fruit is exposed to the air is prevented. In open air drying sulphur also serves as a protection against insects and for this reason a practice which might be considered objectionable is allowed. Another explanation of the bleaching and disinfecting action of sulphur dioxide is that it combines directly with the dye or the objectionable substance, the resulting product being either colorless or harmless.

The transformation of sulphur dioxide into sulphur trioxide takes place very slowly unless a catalytic agent is present. In the presence of both a contact agent and water the final product will be sulphuric acid, the commercial manufacture of which depends upon these reactions. Concentrated sulphuric acid is a heavy, oily liquid, sometimes called oil of vitriol, which is very corrosive in its action. 

It readily takes up water, forming a loose chemical compound called a hydrate (H₂SO4. H₂O). When the concentrated acid has been spilled on the skin the immediate effect is to cause a shrinking of the tissues beneath because of the withdrawal of their moisture. There is no feeling of pain. Unless the acid is very thoroughly removed by rinsing and neutralization, a destruction of the tissue will follow. Concentrated sulphuric acid is occasionally used as a caustic because of this effect. Even though no moisture is present because of the avidity with which the acid seeks water to form the hydrate, it will cause the removal of hydrogen and oxygen from compounds containing these elements. This accounts for the charred spots which are formed when the acid is spilled on paper or a wood surface. 

Both consist of cellulose, a compound containing carbon, hydrogen and oxygen. Only carbon is left when the hydrogen and oxygen have been removed by the acid. A great amount of heat is liberated when the hydrate is formed, therefore, when concentrated sulphuric acid is diluted with water there will be a marked rise in temperature. Because of this, the acid should always be added very slowly to the water instead of in the reverse order. If the water is added to the acid, because of the high temperature some of it will be changed to steam and in attempting to escape from the surrounding liquid it will spatter. In addition to the usual antidotes for acid poisoning, such as milk of lime or magnesia, eggs, milk or oils should also be given to protect the mucous membrane against its corrosive action. A number

of the important salts of sulphuric acid have already been discussed (see Chapter XI, also XV). 

Sulphides. Hydrogen sulphide, a gas with an extremely disagreeable odor like that of rotten eggs, is one of the products of the decomposition of sulphur-containing organic compounds, for example proteins. When the decomposition takes place in a limited supply of air the production of this gas is more noticeable. This accounts for its presence in the gases produced in cases of intestinal putrefaction.

The similarity of its odor to that of rotten eggs is due to the fact that they contain compounds rich in sulphur and the condition under which their spoiling occurs, i.e., within the shell, is very favorable to the formation of hydrogen. sulphide. Hydrogen sulphide is a constituent of most gaseous fuels, therefore, it is apt to be present in the air in traces because of slight leaks. The tarnishing of silver, which consists of a coating of silver sulphide rather than of silver oxide as is commonly supposed, is due to a reaction with it. 

H2S2 Ag Ag₂S + H2

When in solution in water, hydrogen sulphide shows faintly acidic properties. Therefore, the solution is called hydrosulphuric acid and the metallic sulphides are considered its salts. These sulphides have a number of uses. Some, principally barium sulphide and sodium sulphide are the basis of depilatories. The lime sulphur spray which is used as as insecticide and fungicide for fruit trees consists of polysulphides of calcium (CaS, and CaS). It is made by boiling together sulphur and slaked lime.

Organic Sulphur Compounds.-Two organic compounds containing sulphur which are used medicinally are sulphonal and trional. Sulphonal is a depressant. Its chemical name is diethylsulphondimethylmethane. Trional, which is a sedative and hypnotic, is diethylsulphonmethylethylmethane.

SUMMARY 

Allotropic Forms.-Different forms of solid, liquid or gas in which the same substance can exist.

QUESTIONS 

1. Distinguish between the following: 

Brimstone flowers of sulphur; milk of sulphur-plastic sulphur. 

2. Odors are sometimes likened to the smell of sulphur. Comment on the comparison. 

3. Mention a use for sulphur candles. Give an explanation of the action that makes it possible. 

4. Account for the shriveled appearance of the skin noticeable when concentrated sulphuric acid has been spilled on it. 

5. If concentrated sulphuric acid must be diluted, what method should be used? Explain. 

6. Of what does the tarnish on silver consist? How can it be accounted for? 

7. Why do eggs which have spoiled in the shell have such an objectionable odor? 

8. Rubber bands contain sulphur. Why should they not be left in a silver tray?

EXPERIMENTS 

1. Rhombic Crystals

Pulverize a small amount of roll sulphur and dissolve in the least possible amount of carbon disulphide (10 cc.). If any undissolved sulphur remains, filter through dry filter paper into a small beaker. As the solvent is very volatile precautions must be taken to prevent its evaporation during the filtration. This can be done by placing a watch glass over the funnel and by adjusting the latter so the end of the stem is very near the bottom of the beaker. After collecting the filtrate fit a filter paper closely over the top of the beaker to retard its evaporation. The more slowly the carbon disulphide evaporates, the larger and more perfeet the sulphur crystals will be. Note their shape.
