3. Action of a Sulphide on Silver 

Place a drop of hydrogen sulphide solution on a bright silver coin. Explain the result. (Hydrogen sulphide solution can be prepared by adding dilute acid to lumps of ferrous sulphide and arranging the apparatus so that the gas can be collected in water.)

CHAPTER XV  

Aluminium. Although aluminium does not occur naturally in the free state it ranks next to oxygen and silicon in abundance because of its wide occurrence in compounds. 

Eight per cent of the materials constituting the compounds of the rocks and soil consist of it, principally in the form of aluminium silicates and oxides. Among the former type of compound are such substances as mica (isinglass), asbestos, clay, tale and Fuller's earth. The oxides are extremely hard and for this reason two of the cheaper forms, corundum and emery, are used for grinding and cutting. In some cases the oxide occurs in a well-defined crystalline form colored by traces of other substances. We know these crystals as sapphires, rubies, oriental amethysts and the topaz. They can also be made artificially. The synthetic gems, as they are called, lack the microscopic flaws or bubbles possessed by the natural crystals. The white sapphire, for example, is the synthetic uncolored crystal of aluminium oxide. For a long time the element could only be obtained from its compounds by methods which were difficult and costly but a young American chemist, Charles M. Hall, discovered a way of freeing it from the oxide which proved both practical and cheap.

A number of the properties of aluminium underlie the uses made of it. It has a light gray metallic luster which is little dimmed by tarnish, although a thin film of oxide will form on its surface when in the presence of moisture. 

The appearance of this film, however, differs very little from 198

the appearance of the metal itself. For this reason, the finely divided metal suspended in oil is used as a paint for protecting other metallic surfaces, such as iron pipes. 

Its use in cameras, opera glasses and other instruments depends upon its lightness combined with a strength superior to nearly all other metals except steel. It is an excellent conductor of electricity and heat. Because of its ability to conduct the latter so readily as well as because of its lightness and comparative cheapness it is now used widely for cooking utensils. As the entire utensil becomes evenly heated, food is less likely to scorch during cooking than when it is prepared in utensils made of other materials which become very hot on the bottom while the sides remain cooler. Boiling water has no perceptible action upon the metal but if sodium chloride is present in the water there are very decided evidences of corrosion, especially when acetic acid or oxygen is present. As an aluminium compound is a result of the reaction there are very grave doubts in the minds of many as to the suitability of aluminium as a material for cooking utensils. The food is in contact with oxygen especially when it is being cooked in an open kettle and the larger and more shallow the saucepan the greater is the opportunity for this contact. In many cases acetic acid or some other vegetable or fruit acid is present and in the cooking of vegetables the absence of sodium chloride is the exception rather than the rule. The effect of alkaline solutions on aluminium is referred to in connection with the formation of hydrogen and also in the discussion of cleaning processes (see Chapters V and XXV). The ability of this metal when free to take the place of others in compounds is made use of in the cleaning of silver in aluminum pans (see Chapter XXV).

A number of aluminium compounds are important because of the uses made of them. Aluminium sulphate forms

double salts, called alums, with sodium sulphate, potassium sulphate or ammonium sulphate. 

Ammonium aluminium sulphate or ammonium alum (NH.),Al2(SO.). 

Potassium aluminium sulphate or potassium alum K,Al:(SO.). 

Sodium aluminium sulphate or soda alum Na,Al:(SO.).

Potassium alum, which can be purified very easily, is used because of its astringent action to contract mucous membranes or to check bleeding. The use of sodium aluminium sulphate in baking powders has already been discussed (see 

Chapter XI). Aluminium acetate is used as an antiseptic and astringent because of the hardening effect it has on the protein constituents of the cells. It is used in a 0.3% -1% solution as a vaginal douche and for wet dressings in 0.3%- 

5% solutions. When aluminium hydroxide is precipitated from a solution containing a soluble aluminium salt it is formed as a jelly-like precipitate which settles slowly. If the liquid also contains very fine insoluble particles in suspension they are carried down by this precipitate and it becomes clear. During the purification of water sometimes an aluminium salt is added to it because of this action. When aluminium sulphate is dissolved small amounts of it are hydrolized and as a result aluminium hydroxide is formed with sulphuric acid as the other product. Water is usually sufficiently alkaline, however, to cause the acid to be changed into the corresponding salt.

Al2(SO4)3 + 6 HOH - 2 Al(OH)3 + 3 H2SO4 

Arsenic. The highly colored sulphides of arsenic, which occur in nature, have long been known, having been used as pigments in Aristotle's time. The element also occurs widely in ores composed of the metallic sulphides, one very common one being known as arsenopyrite (FeAsS). Occasionally its occurs free and more rarely in combination with oxygen as arsenic trioxide (As2O3). The pure element is obtained either by reducing the oxide with carbon or by

heating arsenopyrite. In the former method the arsenic vaporizes when it is freed because of the temperature at which the reduction occurs. On cooling, it again solidifies without first becoming changed to a liquid. 2 As2O3 + 3C AS4 + 3CO2 

On heating arsenopyrite, ferrous sulphide and free arsenic are formed. 4 FeAsS AS4+4 FeS When formed in large quantities arsenic is a gray crystalline solid having a metallic luster. If small amounts of arsenic vapor become cooled rapidly a yellow crystalline solid is formed which resembles flowers of sulphur. When cooled more slowly it forms a black-brown solid deposit referred to as an arsenic mirror. Both forms in time change to the gray solid which is the most permanent of the three. 

When in combination, arsenic exhibits either metallic or non-metallic properties for it combines with a number of different elements, some of which are metals while others are non-metals. With the halogens, which are themselves non-metals, it combines to form such compounds as arsenic chloride (AsCl). It does not, however, form sulphates, carbonates or nitrates. 

Arsine (ASH), a compound composed of arsenic and hydrogen, is interesting and important as its formation and behavior underlie the method commonly used for the detection of arsenic when its presence is suspected. This compound is formed through the union of the two elements composing it. It is a very poisonous gas having the odor of garlic and burning with a blue-white flame to form arsenic trioxide and water. 2 AsH3 + 3 02 AS2O3 + 3 H₂O If this gas is heated before it burns, or if the oxidation is interfered with by holding a cold surface against the flame,

free arsenic will be deposited on it as a brown-black mirror. 

In performing the Marsh test, as it is called, a hydrogen generator (see Chapter V) is set up. In it are placed granulated zinc and hydrochloric acid necessary for the production of hydrogen together with a solution containing the suspected material. Assuming it to be arsenic trioxide, the following equations represent the reactions which occur:

AS2O3 + 12 H (nascent hydrogen) 2 AsH3 + 3 O2 2 AS203 2 AsH3 + 3 H₂O (arsine)

AS2O3 + 3 H₂O AS4+302 (arsenic mirror)

The appearance of a brown-black coating on the porcelain surface held against the arsine flame is an evidence that the material tested contained arsenic.

The combination with oxygen can occur in two different proportions, the products being arsenic trioxide (As₂O₂) and arsenic pentoxide (As2O5). They are both non-metallic oxides and the anhydrides of corresponding acids, arsenious acid (H,AsO,) and arsenic acid (H,AsO4). Arsenious acid can be formed by dissolving the oxide in water but it cannot be obtained in the pure form from this solution. By different methods of preparation arsenic acid can be produced. Two familiar salts of arsenious acid are Scheele's green and Paris green. The former is copper hydrogen arsenite (CuHASO,) and the latter is a mixed salt composed of copper arsenite and copper acetate (Cu3(AsO3)2 • 

Cu (C2H3O2)2). Both are used as insecticides. Lead arsenate (Pb (AsO4)2), a salt of arsenic acid is one of the active ingredients of the lime sulphur spray. 

Arsenic is one of the oldest drugs used in medicine. It is used in the form of arsenic trioxide but it is referred to frequently as arsenic or white arsenic as well as arsenious oxide. Fowler's solution, which is a familiar preparation

of arsenic, contains arsenious oxide, potassium bicarbonate and oil of lavender. An ordinary dose contains 1/20 of a grain of arsenious oxide. The medicinal use of arsenic depends upon its ability to increase the hemoglobin content of the blood by increasing the red corpuscles. It also decreases oxidation processes so that the body has a greater chance to store up a reserve and a gain in weight results. 

The drug is eliminated principally through the kidneys but more slowly than it is given, therefore, it is in a sense a cumulative poison. Although the patient's tolerance to it may be raised through repeated use, chronic arsenic poisoning is common. The antidote for arsenic is freshly prepared ferric hydroxide. It reacts with the arsenic forming an insoluble compound which should be removed from the stomach. Ferric hydroxide can be prepared by adding magnesium hydroxide to a solution of iron alum. 2 KFe (SO4)2+3 Mg(OH)2 →→→K₂SO4 +3 MgSO4 + 2 Fe(OH)3↓ 

Two other recent arsenic preparations, used especially as specifics for syphilis, are Salvarsan and Neosalvarsan. The former is often called 606 because it was the six hundred and sixth arsenic preparation worked out by Ehrlich. Its chemical name is arsphenamine or paradiamidodioxyar senobenzol hydrochloride (HCINH₂OHCH¸As: AsС¸H₂OH- 

NH₂HCI). Neosalvarsan is known as 914.

Bismuth. In mineral regions bismuth occurs usually in the free state and also as bismuth sulphide (Bi₂S) and bismuth oxide (Bi₂O). To obtain the pure element the ore containing it in an uncombined state is heated and as a result the bismuth melts and can be drawn off. It is also produced from the sulphide and oxide but the process is more complicated for in this case it must be freed from compounds.

Bismuth is a brittle, crystalline solid with a silvery, metallic luster in which there is a pronounced pinkish tinge. It

can be melted and fused with certain other metals, among them being lead and tin. A uniform mixture, called an alloy, results. Its melting point is lower than is that of any of the metals composing it. Because of the low melting point of bismuth alloys, they are made use of for automatic sprinklers, fire curtains and doors. Plugs in a sprinkling system are sealed with it and in case of a fire the alloy becomes melted as the temperature begins to rise. The water pressure forces the plugs out and the fire is extinguished.

At ordinary temperatures bismuth does not combine with oxygen but on heating, a series of oxides can be formed of which bismuth trioxide (Bi₂O) is the most familiar. As the metal is less active than hydrogen and is below it in the electromotive series (see Chapter V) it does not react with acids to form free hydrogen and a salt. With hot concentrated sulphuric acid or nitric acid, however, it will react, one product of the reaction being the corresponding salt. 

Other familiar bismuth salts are bismuth chloride and bismuth carbonate. In water or a dilute acid these salts hydrolize to form the acid and another salt which contains hydroxyl radicals as well as the acid radical. They are called basic salts for this reason. Bismuth nitrate, for example, forms basic bismuth nitrate and nitric acid.

Bi(NO3)3 + 2 HOH Bi(OH)2NO3 + 2 HNO3 These basic salts are insoluble and lose the atoms constituting a molecule of water. In this form the term "sub" or "oxy" is applied to them. Basic bismuth nitrate becomes bismuth subnitrate or bismuth oxynitrate (BiONO3). Bi(OH)2NO - BiONO3 + H₂O Bismuth subcarbonate and bismuth subnitrate are two common bismuth compounds which are used medicinally. As dusting powders they sooth an irritated skin. Internally they have both an astringent and antiseptic effect and are

used largely for gastric and intestinal disorders. As they form a coating on the inside surface of the alimentary tract they are apt to prove constipating for they prevent the usual secretion of the fluids from it. They also prevent 

X-rays from penetrating further than is desired and so quantities of a bismuth preparation are given before an X-ray photograph of the digestive tract is taken. The salt is changed to bismuth sulphide when in the intestine and the feces are black as a result. In order to mark off the feces resulting from certain kinds of diets used in feeding experiments, before the experimental study is made and again after it is completed, bismuth is given to the subject. If the data to be obtained includes a study of the feces it is possible in this way to determine what portions have resulted from the foods used during the experiment. 

Calcium.-Because of the readiness with which calcium reacts it does not occur naturally in the free state. It is widely distributed, however, in a number of familiar compounds, among them being gypsum (calcium sulphate), limestone and marble (calcium carbonate) and fluor spar (calcium fluoride). To obtain free calcium it must be released from one of its compounds, calcium chloride being ordinarily used for this purpose. When it is melted it can be decomposed into chlorine and calcium by a process of electrolysis. 

Calcium is a silvery white metal which is a little harder than lead. It reacts readily with water to form free hydrogen and calcium hydroxide.

Ca + 2 HOH →Ca(OH)2 + H2 Its behavior is similar to that of free sodium under the same conditions, but as the action is less violent and the calcium sinks to the bottom it is safer to use. When heated, calcium combines readily with a number of the non-metallic elements. With sulphur, calcium sulphide is formed. This

compound is the basis of many of the depilatory preparations, which are sold as powders and applied in the form of a paste, because of the readiness with which it undergoes hydrolysis when water is added to it. Hydrogen sulphide, which is one of the products, accounts for the characteristic, disagreeable odor these preparations have when they are being used. The other product, calcium hydroxide, is a mild alkaline compound which can dissolve hair without injuring the skin.

CaS+ 2 HOH → H2S + Ca(OH)2 With nitrogen, a compound called calcium nitride (Ca,N₂) is formed. It also combines with oxygen to form calcium oxide (CaO). When calcium is heated in the air, instead of in an atmosphere of pure nitrogen or pure oxygen, both calcium nitride and calcium oxide are produced. 3 Ca + N2 2 Ca + O2 Ca3N2 2 CaO Calcium oxide is obtained industrially by heating calcium carbonate in the form of limestone. It decomposes in a manner characteristic of carbonates, the products being carbon dioxide and calcium oxide.

CaCO3 CaO + CO, This oxide, known as quicklime, has many important uses. 

If it is heated intensely hot by means of an oxyhydrogen flame it gives an intense white light frequently spoken of as a calcium or limelight. On the addition of water or more slowly through contact with atmospheric moisture, quicklime becomes changed to slaked lime or calcium hydroxide. CaO + HOH - Ca(OH)2 

The product is called either water-slaked or air-slaked, according to the condition under which the change occurs. 

In the former case the reaction takes place very rapidly and so much energy in the form of heat is released that

there is a noticeable rise in temperature. The heat is sufficient to cause any combustible material present to burn. 

Quicklime is sometimes used to consume or dispose of organic material for this reason. Basic oxides, of which calcium oxide is an example, can combine with acidic oxides to form the salt which would be formed through the reaction of the corresponding base and acid. For example, calcium oxide can combine with carbon dioxide to form calcium carbonate.

CaO + CO, CaCO3 Soda lime, which consists partly of calcium oxide, is used to absorb or remove carbon dioxide from air passed through it because of this reaction. By determining the weight of the soda lime before and after it has been used in this way it is possible to determine the carbon dioxide content of the air which has gone through. This is again referred to in connection with metabolism experiments carried on with a respiration calorimeter (see Chapter XVI). 

The basic reaction of calcium hydroxide has already been referred to (see Chapter X). A water solution is usually called limewater. Milk of lime consists of water which contains calcium hydroxide in solution and insoluble particles in suspension. The use of both limewater and milk of lime as antidotes for acid poisoning has already been referred to. The former is sometimes added to milk to prevent the formation of large curds of casein during gastric digestion. Limewater and linseed oil form a mixture called carron oil which is used as an application for burns. If carbon dioxide comes in contact with calcium hydroxide a reaction takes place of which calcium carbonate and water are products.

Ca(OH)2+ CO2 CaCO3 + H2O By using a clear solution of limewater this reaction is made use of as a test for carbon dioxide, for the solution

becomes cloudy, due to the formation of the insoluble calcium carbonate. For the same reason limewater will deteriorate if the bottle is left unstoppered. The hardening of plaster and mortar, both of which contain slaked lime. as an ingredient, is due to the gradual reaction taking place between it and atmospheric carbon dioxide.

Calcium chloride is another useful calcium salt. As a solid it is obtainable both in the anhydrous and the hydrated form (see Chapter VI). The former takes up water very readily, first as water of crystallization and finally in sufficient amounts to become dissolved in it. A number of its uses depend upon this property. For laboratory purposes it is a good drying agent because of the readiness with which it absorbs water. It has also been tried out as a means of laying the dust on roads. Because of its very low freezing point a brine of calcium chloride is used in refrigeration plants (see Chapter VII). Calcium carbonate, which occurs naturally in a number of impure forms, as in eggshells, oyster shells, coral and pearls, can be obtained as a pure compound by precipitating it from a solution of calcium chloride through a reaction with sodium carbonate. CaCl2 + Na2CO3 CaCO3+2 NaCl This precipitate is frequently called precipitated chalk and if very finely divided it is used for tooth pastes and tooth powders. Blackboard chalk or crayon consists of calcium sulphate. This compound, both in the form of gypsum and plaster of Paris, has already been referred to (see Chapter VI).

The use of the term lime in connection with calcium compounds may create confusion, for it is applied to compounds which are similar only in their calcium content. As has already been pointed out, an oxide, a hydroxide and a carbonate are spoken of, respectively, as quicklime, slaked

lime and limestone. The hardness of water is frequently attributed to the presence of lime salts. These may be either calcium bicarbonate, calcium sulphate or calcium chloride. In discussing the importance of calcium to the body again the term lime salts is frequently used. Reference has already been made to the necessity of calcium compounds for the clotting ability of the blood. In order to increase it, as in the case of hemorrhages, calcium chloride or calcium lactate is administered. Calcium phosphate makes up about 80% of the inorganic constituents of bone. 

The importance of milk for infants and growing children depends in part upon the calcium compounds which it contains. The contraction and irritability of muscle cells is influenced by the calcium compounds present. 

Copper. In the ores which contain copper this element is found both in a free state and in combination, principally with sulphur or oxygen. The methods for obtaining the pure metal must, therefore, include processes for freeing the element from its compounds as well as processes for separating the free copper from the other substances with which it is mixed. The blister copper which is obtained is 98% pure. As many of the uses made of it demand a pure metal, the blister copper is purified by electrolysis.

In spite of the toughness and strength of copper it is soft enough to roll into sheets or draw out into wire. Many of the forms in which it is used very commonly, as wire, sheeting, nails and rivets, depend upon these properties. Its color is so characteristic that the term "copper color" is used. It is an excellent conductor of both heat and electricity. 

Formerly it was used to a great extent for cooking utensils. 

Large equipment for institutions is still made of it. It is frequently lined with a plating of tin, however, to lessen the chance of the formation of soluble and poisonous copper salts by the action of the foods on the metal. Copper forms

a number of familiar alloys, among them being the following:

Brass Bronze 

63-73% copper, 27-37% zinc 

70-95% copper, 1-25% zinc, 1-18% tin 

Gun metal 

90% copper, 10% tin 

Gold coin .. 

10% copper, 90% gold 

Nickel coin 

75% copper, 25% nickel 

Silver coin 

10% copper, 90% silver

Because it is less active than hydrogen, when in a free state copper does not react with dilute acids or alkalies to form free hydrogen. With acetic acid a reaction will take place if oxygen is present and basic copper acetate or verdigris is formed as a result (see Chapter XXI). As concentrated nitric acid and hot sulphuric acid can act as oxidizing agents a reaction with copper can occur in which the copper salt of the acid is formed and either sulphur dioxide or oxides of nitrogen. Through the action of moist air a copper surface becomes tarnished. The coating may consist of oxides, cuprous oxide (Cu₂O) or cupric oxide (CuO) or basic copper carbonate (CuOHCO3). The latter compound, which is green in color, is the result of the action of atmospheric carbon dioxide as well as moisture and oxygen. The removal of this tarnish is described in connection with cleaning processes (see Chapter XXV). Basic copper chloride (3 Cu(OH)2. CuCl) is formed when copper comes in contact with sea water (sodium chloride) and air (oxygen). This accounts for the green coating which sometimes appears on copper fittings on boats. 

Copper combines with other elements in two different proportions. One series of compounds in which it has a valence of one is given the name cuprous. Cuprous oxide is the most important compound of this kind. It is referred to as the product formed from Fehling's solution or Benedict-Fehling's solution when it is acted on by a reducing agent, for example glucose (see Chapter XXIII).

To another series of compounds in which copper has

a valence of two the name cupric is given. The most familiar example of a compound of this type is copper sulphate (CuSO4), which is more commonly known as blue vitriol or blue stone. It is used as an insecticide and also for the purification of water to destroy certain kinds of alge which give it the taste of fish or cucumbers. Because of the small amounts in which it is used, 0.1-0.25 parts to 

1,000,000 parts of water, it is not effective for typhoid or dysentery bacilli. As copper sulphate can precipitate proteins from solutions containing them it will have an astringent action on tissues and can also be used to destroy them. 

Its use for granulated eyelids and for canker sores is due to this effect. Fehling's solution contains copper sulphate and sodium hydroxide, which under normal conditions react together to form a thick blue-white precipitate of cupric hydroxide. This compound, which is the oxidizing agent for which Fehling's solution is used, is kept in solution by the presence of a third ingredient, sodium potassium tartrate. As this compound is capable of being oxidized, the solution must be kept in two parts until it is to be used, when it is mixed together in equal proportions. Solution 1 contains copper sulphate and solution 2 contains the sodium hydroxide and the tartrate. Benedict-Fehling's solution is a modification which contains a citrate in place of the tartrate and sodium carbonate in place of the sodium hydroxide. As the citrate is incapable of oxidation under the existing conditions all three ingredients can be kept in one solution. 

Iron.-Iron ores, in which the metal is present chiefly as a sulphide, oxide or carbonate and in smaller amounts in combination with other elements, are very abundant. In fact there are few rocks and soils which do not contain this element, at least in traces. It is also an essential constituent of chlorophyll, the green coloring in plants, whose presence is necessary to their life processes. Hemoglobin, an important conjugated protein in red corpuscles, contains

iron in the compound hematin, which is combined with. amino acids to form the protein. This element is most essential to the oxygen-carrying power of the hemoglobin, for when oxyhemoglobin is formed the combination occurs between oxygen and the iron in the protein molecule. When iron is released from the compounds present in its ores it is not obtained in a pure state, as many of the other metals are, but it still contains varying amounts of carbon and other substances. The difference in the proportion of carbon, its relation to the iron and the methods by which the metal is obtained result in different varieties of iron, the most important being cast iron, wrought iron and steel.

Each has many important uses. Galvanized iron consists of an iron foundation which is coated with zinc. 

When ordinary iron is exposed to the air in the presence of moisture a deposit of ferric oxide (Fe2O3) begins to form slowly. When taking care of any iron surface, therefore, the important thing to consider is its protection from the conditions which make the rust possible. This is usually accomplished by paints or blacking (see Chapters IV and 

XVI). Because of the readiness with which the metal reacts with acids (see Chapters V and IX) the latter should not be used when cleaning it. When the enameled or granite coating of cooking utensils becomes broken and the iron foundation is exposed, they should no longer be used for acid-containing mixtures for the same reason. 

Iron combines with other elements in different proportions to form two series of compounds. In one, whose members are called ferrous compounds, it has a valence of two, whereas in the ferric compounds it has a valence of three. 

The following examples show the difference between the two: 

Ferrous hydroxide Fe(OH)2 

Ferrous oxide 

Ferrous chloride

Ferric hydroxide Fe(OH), Ferric oxide FeO Fe2O3 FeCl, Ferric chloride FeCl3 

Ferrous sulphate FeSO

Ferric sulphate Fe2(SO4)3

It can remain combined with the same unit, but be changed from the ferrous to the ferric state of combination by a process of oxidation. A process of reduction, on the other hand, will cause the iron in a ferric compound to be changed to iron in a ferrous compound. Solutions of ferrous salts are green when they are freshly prepared, but on standing they gradually become yellow because of a change into the corresponding ferric salts.

There are about twenty-four official preparations of iron listed in the pharmacopoeia. Many of them are metallic salts, as iron carbonate in Blaud's pills and ferric chloride in the tincture of iron, or they are organic preparations which contain not only the iron compound but also some protein constituents, for example beef, wine and iron preparations. Because of the astringent action of the iron compounds they are sometimes used locally to check bleeding. 

This action, which is due to the precipitation of proteins from their solution, also accounts for the puckery sensation noticed when medicinal iron is taken. The constipating effect of Blaud's pills and other iron preparations is caused by this astringent action on the mucous membranes lining the digestive tract, which checks the secretions normally produced from them. As these iron compounds blacken the enamel of the teeth and also silver, care should be used in the preparation of the dose and the way it is given to a patient. It should be well diluted, stirred with a glass rod and given through a glass tube. As iron compounds combine with tannic acid to form iron tannate, a constituent of many inks, tea or medicines containing tannin should never be given with those containing iron. 

Sodium bicarbonate and an iron salt, as ferric chloride, must not be given at the same time because of the formation of an insoluble iron compound, ferric carbonate, through a reaction between them. (For the removal of rust spots and stains caused by medicines containing iron see Chapter XXV.)

We normally depend upon foods for the proper maintenance of the iron content of the body. Meats, eggs, greens and raisins are especially valuable as sources of this element. Milk is deficient in it, but an infant starts life with enough stored iron to carry it through the first nine months. By that time the child's diet has been well supplemented with foods which contribute iron, for example eggs and spinach. When the body is getting an insufficient supply of iron or for some reason cannot make use of the amount which is supplied, an anæmic condition results as fewer red corpuscles are made or their iron content is deficient. The vitality is lowered because of the lowered oxygencarrying power of the blood, which naturally affects the functioning of all the tissues. The tonic effect of medicinal iron may be due directly to the increase in available iron or it may be beneficial because it makes the body better able to use the iron which is supplied through the foods in organic compounds. 

Lead. Very ancient lead articles have been found showing that its use was known in early times. It occurs naturally as lead sulphide, gold and silver as well as copper, arsenic and bismuth often being associated with it in the ore. The lead which is first obtained from the ore still contains the other metals and is called hard lead because of the greater firmness which they give it. Pure lead is softer than any of the other heavy metals. To give it greater firmness and strength it is frequently made into an alloy. Solder, composed of lead and tin, is perhaps the most familiar of these alloys. Because of its lower melting point it can be applied to a lead or a tin surface without any danger of either being melted during the soldering process. When freshly cut, lead has a silvery luster, but it very quickly becomes dull gray because of the formation of a superficial film of lead suboxide (Pb₂O). When soft water containing dissolved oxygen remains in contact with

lead a reaction takes place in which a soluble compound, lead hydroxide, is formed. The presence of the magnesium, calcium or iron salts contained in hard water prevents this reaction as they themselves react with the lead to form insoluble lead salts. These coat the inside of the pipe and thus prevent any further contact between the lead and water. Lead piping for water supplies was at one time more commonly used than it is to-day. Because all soluble lead compounds are poisonous it should never be used for water which is soft or has a low degree of hardness. Acids, even when in very dilute solutions, react readily with lead and, with the exception of hydrochloric and sulphuric acids, soluble salts are formed as a result. As lead sulphate is the most insoluble of all the lead compounds, workmen in factories producing or handling lead in any form frequently add sulphuric acid to their drinking water. The danger of lead poisoning is lessened in this way, though not entirely overcome, for it can be absorbed through the broken skin or through food which has become contaminated with it by handling.

A number of lead compounds are familiar through the use made of them. Red lead is an oxide (Pb304) which is used commonly as the first coat of paint on iron surfaces. 

White lead or basic lead carbonate ((PbCO3)2. Pb(OH)2) is used as a pigment in white paint or forms the body of other paints whose colors are due to other pigments. 

Because of their astringent action lead compounds are used to heal wounds and ulcers. They precipitate the soluble protein content of the blood and tissue cells and so check bleeding and contract the tissues. They are used in solution, as an ointment or in a plaster. The lead compound most commonly used is lead acetate, otherwise known as sugar of lead. Another lead compound which has come into prominence within the past year is known as lead tetraethyl. It is used in gasoline to overcome the tendency of

engines to "knock." Lead compounds are called cumulative poisons because they are eliminated from the body much more slowly than they are absorbed. Therefore, the hazard for workmen in the lead industries is very great. 

Manganese. The metal manganese occurs principally in the form of manganese dioxide, a black solid. The metal, when free, is a hard solid with a gray luster resembling iron. Its chief importance is in the alloys which it forms with iron and which are used in the various arts. 

Although there are not many uses made of free manganese, a number of its compounds are very important. Reference has been made to the use of manganese dioxide as a catalytic agent in the preparation of oxygen (see Chapter IV). It can also act as an oxidizing agent. Another manganese compound which is used more commonly as an oxidizing agent is potassium permanganate. Its use in removing stains, as an irrigant and gargle depends upon this action (see Chapters IV and XXV). It is also used as an antidote in cases of poisoning by alkaloids, for example morphine. The drug is rendered harmless because of its oxidation. Among the other manganese compounds used medicinally are manganese sulphate (MnSO₁), manganese hypophosphate and manganese dioxide. 

Mercury. Mercury occurs in the form of mercuric sulphide (HgS). Because the element can be vaporized and recondensed it is readily obtained from its ore after the latter is decomposed. It was originally called quicksilver because it is a liquid with a silvery color. A number of the physical properties this metal possesses underlie some common uses made of it. The fact that it has a very low freezing point and high boiling point and that a change in temperature causes considerable expansion makes it useful in thermometers. It forms alloys with a number of different metals, two very familiar ones being with silver and with tin. These alloys in which mercury occurs as

one of the metals are usually called amalgams, for example the amalgam fillings used by dentists. The backing on mirrors is sometimes an amalgam containing tin. 

Mercury forms two series of compounds which are called either mercurous or mercuric according to whether the valence of the metal is one or two. The two compounds it forms with chlorine are examples. 

Mercurous chloride or calomel-HgCl

Mercuric chloride, bichloride of mercury or corrosive sublimate-HgCl2 There are many preparations containing mercury used medicinally. As it is one of the few drugs which can be absorbed through the skin it is sometimes used locally in the form of an ointment. This may contain mercury, mercuric oleate, mercuric oxide, mercuric nitrate or mercuric ammonium chloride. The last is a complex compound formed through a reaction between mercuric chloride and a solution containing ammonia. 

HgCl2 + 2 NH, HgNH₂Cl + NH4Cl 

Because it is a white, insoluble substance it is sometimes called infusible white precipitate. Mercury compounds are also given as cathartics, mercurous chloride or calomel being one very commonly used. It should be kept in a dark bottle, for light causes it to be changed slowly into the poisonous mercuric chloride. 2 HgCl →→→→ HgCl2 + Hg 

Patients are sometimes cautioned not to use acid foods when calomel is being taken because it is believed that the mercurous compound can be changed over into a mercuric compound. The latter being more soluble is more readily absorbed and may cause symptoms of chronic mercury poisoning such as excessive salivation or sore gums. To lessen the chance of the hydrochloric acid of the gastric

juice bringing about a similar change sodium bicarbonate is frequently given with the calomel. The sodium chloride which is formed by its reaction with the hydrochloric acid cannot affect the mercury compound.

HCl + NaHCO3 NaCl + H2O + CO2 The use of mercury compounds as a specific for syphilis is probably due to their antiseptic action. The use of mercuric chloride as a disinfectant is discussed in Chapter XXVI. 

Radium.-Radium is one of a small number of rare elements which possess a peculiar property called radio activity. Uranium was the first one of the group whose activity in this respect was discovered. Then it was found that pitch blende, which is essentially uranium oxide, possessed a greater radio activity than the pure salts of uranium. 

It was believed that this might be due to the presence of another unknown element. Through the untiring efforts of Monsieur and Madame Curie a very minute amount of a new element was isolated in the form of a chloride and the name radium given to the element. Later Madame

Curie isolated the element. Both the metal and its salts possess some very interesting characteristics. Radiations from the salts cause glass and quartz to disintegrate slowly, char paper and convert oxygen into ozone. A photographic plate wrapped in black paper and placed near the salt will be affected as if it were exposed to the light even if no light is present. If some metallic object, as a coin, is placed on the plate, its image will be photographed. The radiations causing these results are believed to be electrically charged particles which are being shot off from the radium atoms as they disintegrate. This decomposition is entirely different from that of a chemical compound, for it occurs without any assistance and cannot be stopped. During the disintegration of radium and its intermediate products much energy is released which has been estimated as being 132

calories per gram of radium per hour. The chemical and physiological effects produced by the radium rays have been made use of in the hope of finding a cure for cancer. 

While cures have been effected in many cases it is a dangerous treatment to use. Many scientists have lost their lives through burns which they have suffered during their investigations. 

Silver. Silver is found free in small amounts but it occurs to a greater extent in the form of silver sulphide in ores which also contain lead and copper. It is soft enough to be readily hammered into sheets or drawn into wires. These properties, together with the high luster which can be given it, make it a very useful metal for ornamental purposes. For coins and table silver, called sterling, it is alloyed with copper to give it greater firmness. The spots which appear when a silver spoon is allowed to stand in vinegar are due to the action of the acetic acid on the copper, verdigris being a product of the reaction (see Chapter XXI). The tarnish (silver sulphide) which appears on silver has already been referred to (see Chapter IV). 

The compounds which silver forms with the halogens are important because of their sensitiveness to light, especially when an organic substance is present. Plates and films which are to be used for photography are prepared with a coating of gelatine which contains silver bromide. On the exposure of the film the silver salt is affected in varying degrees according to the intensity of the light striking it. The effect becomes visible only when the film is developed. It is then called a negative because the light and dark portions are exactly the reverse of the printed picture or of the original object which was photographed. Silver nitrate is the principal silver salt used medicinally. It was named lunar caustic by the early alchemists because of the fact that their symbol for silver was the moon. "Caustic" suggests the corrosive action which this compound has. It is

used in the solid form, molded into pencils, to destroy granulated tissue which forms during the healing of a wound. 

It is also used in solution. To prevent gonorrheal infection a 1-2% solution is dropped into the eyes of new-born babies.

Organic preparations containing silver, of which argyrol is a familiar example, are less irritating because of the protein which is present. Twenty-eight states in this country have laws either requiring or recommending the use of silver nitrate or one of its preparations as argyrol, or protargol, in new-born babies' eyes to prevent blindness. These preparations are astringent and antiseptic in their action and so are used locally on mucous membranes, for example in cases of conjunctivitis. Because water is apt to contain chlorides, distilled water should be used when making up a solution of silver nitrate. Otherwise, a reaction will take place and insoluble silver chloride will be formed, causing a milky appearance in the solution. For the same reason when a solution of silver nitrate is to be used as an irrigant it must be put into a container which has been thoroughly rinsed, for it may previously have contained a salt solution. The chloride content of urine is frequently determined by using a solution of silver nitrate of known concentration and making the calculation from the volume necessary to react with the chloride present in the sample used for the experiment.

Zinc.-Although the occurrence of zinc is local it is found in several different compounds, among them being the sulphide, the oxide, the carbonate and silicate. Commercial zinc, when it is freed from its ores, still contains impurities. 

It has a silvery color and is crystalline. This accounts for the characteristic appearance of sheet zinc. The readiness with which acids act upon it makes it necessary to prevent them from coming in contact with the metal (see Chapters V and IX). Alkalies also react with it. Therefore, a zinc surface should not be cleaned with washing soda or a strong alkaline soap (see Chapters V and X).

Zinc compounds are used medicinally because of their astringent action and also as disinfectants. In concentrated form they destroy tissue. Zinc chloride, for example, is an ingredient in a number of so-called "cancer cures." Zinc oxide is used principally in the form of an ointment. Zinc stearate is used either as an ointment or as a dusting powder. Zinc sulphate and zinc acetate are used in solutions.

CHAPTER XVI  

Occurrence.-Because of its wide occurrence and also because of the many useful ways both it and its compounds serve us, carbon is one of the most important of the elements. It is found in a great variety of compounds, many of which occur naturally while others are artificial products. 

The carbonates of calcium, magnesium and iron form a considerable portion of the rock materials of the earth. 

Proteins, fats and carbohydrates (see Chapters XXII, 

XXIII and XXIV) are important constituents of plant and animal organisms. Both crude petroleum and natural gas are mixtures consisting wholly or in part of carbon compounds. These are only a few of the many examples which might be mentioned (see Chapters XVII, XVIII, XIX, XX, XXI for discussions of important carbon compounds).

In a relatively free state the element occurs in coal, the proportion depending on the type. It also occurs in natural deposits in two forms, the diamond and graphite, so widely different in physical characteristics it is difficult to believe that they are the same substance. That both are carbon has been proved by the formation of carbon dioxide as the only product when each is heated at a high temperature.

Forms.-Carbon, like sulphur, can exist in several allotropic forms. In the case of graphite and diamonds it is crystalline. Diamond crystals, which are colorless octohedrons, are formed naturally far below the earth's surface under great heat and pressure. Within recent years small imperfect crystals have also been made artificially. Deposits of carbon in the form of graphite also occur in the earth. 

These crystals are small, black, hexagonal plates. In addi- 223

tion to the two crystalline forms, carbon occurs in an amorphous state known as charcoal, the various kinds being distinguished by the material from which each is made. 

Wood, soft coal, mineral oils and bones or refuse animal material are heated in a very limited supply of air, as a result of which an extensive decomposition takes place and many products are formed. In each case a large part of the original carbon content remains as free carbon and is called wood charcoal, coke, lamp black (soot) or bone black (animal charcoal). Free carbon, as it exists in coal, is also of the amorphous variety.

Properties and Uses.-Upon some of the physical properties of carbon, as it occurs in diamonds and graphite, the uses of these two forms depend. The former, possessing great hardness, is used for cutting and polishing glass, for bearings in watches and very delicate instruments such as balances, and for boring through rock. Only the smaller, imperfect crystals or fragments are used for these purposes. 

Graphite, which is soft and greasy and adheres to any surface which it coats, is used as a lubricant in the parts of machinery which become so heated that ordinary lubricating oils and greases (carbon compounds) cannot be used without rapid decomposition and charring. The "leads" of pencils consist of graphite mixed with clay in varying proportions, the more graphite there is the softer being the pencil. The fact that it leaves a black mark when drawn across a surface to which it adheres makes this use possible and the name graphite, from a Greek word meaning "write," was given to it for this reason. All forms of carbon are insoluble in all ordinary solvents. They also show no tendency to react with any substance with which they come in contact under ordinary temperature conditions. For these reasons lamp black is a valuable ingredient of indelible and printer's inks and of black varnishes. Graphite is useful in stove polishes as a protection against the action of moisture and oxygen not only because the iron is cov-

ered but also because the coating itself is not affected unless the temperature is raised. It is then burned off, forming carbon dioxide. This accounts for the necessity of renewing the polish from time to time. The ends of fence posts which are to be in contact with the soil are often charred because the carbon thus formed prevents the remainder of the wood from being rotted.

Another property of charcoal is its ability to cause other kinds of substances to adhere to its surface. This is called adsorption. Because of this ability, which is most marked in animal charcoal and the very fine porous varieties obtained from cocoanut shells and the pits of fruits like the peach, charcoal is used for removing gases from the air and coloring material or impurities either in solution or suspension from liquids. The old-fashioned custom of placing a piece of charcoal in an ice box or refrigerator as a purifier is due to this ability to adsorb gaseous substances having an odor. The following figures represent the extent to which it can remove hydrogen sulphide and ammonia. 

90 vols. ammonia adsorbed by 1 vol. charcoal 

55 vols. hydrogen sulphide adsorbed by 1 vol. charcoal 

During the war quantities of cocoanut shell and peach-pit charcoal were used in gas masks to take "poison gases" out of the air, as it was inhaled through the mask. The use of charcoal in filters depends upon the same principle. In this case, however, the substances, either in solution or suspension, are removed from a liquid. As the adsorbtive ability of the charcoal is limited, at intervals it must be removed from the filter and thoroughly cleaned by heating to redness. In this way the substances which have been adsorbed will be decomposed into gaseous products and driven out. If this is not done, instead of improving the condition of the water, the contamination will be increased in its passage through the filter. Charcoal tablets have been used in digestive disturbances. Here again their usefulness

is probably due to their ability to adsorb the gaseous products which result from gastric fermentations. 

The most important chemical property of carbon is its ability to unite with free oxygen or to remove it from an oxidizing agent. Although the element is very inert at ordinary temperatures, its oxidation can be brought about easily if the temperature is raised. Coke and wood charcoal are useful as fuels because of their readiness to burn and release large amounts of heat. The relative fuel value of the different kinds of coal depends upon the carbon content. The greater the proportion of free carbon the more desirable is the coal, not only because of its energy value but also because of the amount of soot, objectionable gaseous products and unburnable ash. When anthracite coal has been unobtainable, as in the winter of 1925-1926, the difference in atmospheric conditions due to the products of combustion of the other forms of coal has been most marked. Their presence in the air has also had far-reaching results so far as the health of the community has been concerned. The following table shows the difference in the fuel value of different types of coal and the relation it bears in each case to the composition, especially the carbon and hydrogen content. Fuels Wood... Charcoal. Peat.... 

Brown coal. Bituminous. Coke.. Anthracite.. Composition excluding ash and moisture

Carbon Dioxide-Its Occurrence and Formation.-The occurrence of carbon dioxide in the air and in water has already been referred to (Chapters VI and VIII). In the body, where it is a waste product resulting from the oxidation of body tissues or food materials brought to them, carbon dioxide has to be carried by the blood until it is finally eliminated. It is present partly in solution in the blood plasma as carbonic acid, partly as sodium bicarbonate, a salt of this acid, and partly in combination with the protein constituents of the blood. Outside the body it is formed by a corresponding process during the burning of different fuels. It is also a product resulting from the decay of the carbon-containing compounds in plant and animal organisms. During the yeast fermentation of starch and sugar-containing mixtures both carbon dioxide and grain alcohol are products (see Chapters XIX and XXIII).

It is always formed when an acid comes in contact with a carbonate (see Chapters IX and XI). This is made the basis of a test for the detection of carbonates, for example, to show the presence of baking soda in milk or the bicarbonates of calcium, magnesium or iron in water. As it is in solution in very small amounts the liquid must first be completely evaporated and the residue burned to an ash. 

The carbonate, if present, will be in this ash and will cause it to effervesce when an acid is added. Baking powders, used for the carbon dioxide they can produce, contain baking soda, a carbonate and some acid constituent which may be a tartrate, a phosphate or an aluminium compound (see Chapter XI). Another reaction resulting in the formation of carbon dioxide occurs when either bicarbonate or carbonates are heated. Upon this reaction the ability to soften water containing bicarbonates depends (see Chapter VI). It also makes necessary the care to be observed in the use of sodium bicarbonate which has been sterilized 

(see Chapter XI).

Properties and Uses.-Because all gases expand on heating the presence of one in a dough or batter will act as a leaven during the baking. Carbon dioxide is especially desirable in that it is colorless, tasteless and odorless and it has no objectionable effect upon either the food or the person who has eaten it. The gas can be liquefied and solidified. Solid carbon dioxide, a white solid, returns directly to a gaseous condition, absorbing much heat in the process. It is now used to a considerable extent in packing ice cream so that it can be taken home by the purchaser.

This "dry ice," as it is called, keeps the ice cream hard for several hours. It has the advantage of being not bulky and leaving nothing to be thrown away when its work is accomplished. Because of the solubility of carbon dioxide in water the blood is able to carry a large amount of the gas in solution in the plasma. At 0° C. 100 cc. of water can hold in solution 171.30 cc. of the gas whereas at 20° C. the amount of dissolved gas decreases to 87.80 cc. Increasing the pressure so increases the volume of gas held in solution that the pressure in a bottle of siphon soda is 140 pounds per square inch. In ginger ale bottles it is 90 pounds and in club soda it is 105 pounds. On opening these bottles the gas escaping from solution rushes out with such force that there is danger of the bottle bursting if the glass is defective. Because carbon dioxide is heavier than air, does not burn and permits nothing else to burn it is valuable as a fire extinguisher (see Chapter IX). As it escapes from the nozzle of the container in which it is being formed it settles down around the fire, shutting out the oxygen needed for the burning and being itself neither combustible nor a supporter of combustion the fire becomes smothered.

The hydroxides of sodium, potassium, calcium and magnesium are able to react chemically with carbon dioxide, the product being the corresponding carbonate. With cal-

cium hydroxide (limewater Ca(OH)2), for example, calcium carbonate and water are formed.

Ca(OH)2 + CO2 → CaCO3 + H2O This is made the basis of a test for the detection of carbon dioxide. Limewater is a clear, colorless liquid. As calcium carbonate is practically insoluble the presence of even traces of it will cause the limewater to become milky in appearance. It is therefore possible to detect the presence of very small amounts of carbon dioxide in this way. If expired air is conducted through containers holding any of these hydroxides the carbon dioxide will be kept in the container because of this same reaction. The difference in the weight of the container, before and after the air has been passed through, represents the weight of the carbon dioxide expired during a given time interval. Part of the data obtained in metabolism experiments, in which a calorimeter is used, is found in this way.

If water containing carbon dioxide in solution comes in contact with a carbonate the corresponding bicarbonate is formed. This reaction accounts for the presence of the soluble bicarbonates of calcium, magnesium or iron in hard water. As water holding carbon dioxide in solution trickles through the soil it comes in contact with the insoluble carbonates of calcium, magnesium or iron and as a result the corresponding bicarbonate is formed..

CaCO3 + H2O + CO2 →→→ Ca(HCO3)2 The Carbon Cycle.-We have already referred to an interesting and important relation between plant and animal organisms which is called the nitrogen cycle (see Chapter VII). There is also a close relation and interdependence of the two types of organisms from the standpoint of carbon. Plants take in carbon dioxide, an animal waste product, and by combining it with water build up more complex carbon compounds called carbohydrates containing a higher

percentage of carbon. At the same time they restore to the air free oxygen upon which all living organisms are dependent for the maintenance of life. Their ability to construct carbohydrates from water and carbon dioxide depends upon the presence of a vegetable pigment called chlorophyll. 

Sunlight is also necessary to supply the large amount of energy which is stored up in the carbohydrate. The animal then eats the carbohydrate and on its final oxidation the stored energy is released and carbon dioxide and water are again produced as waste products. 6 CO, + 6 H,O C6H12O6+6 02 Carbohydrate

C6H12O6+6 O₂ energy being stored 6 CO₂+6 H₂O energy being released This carbon cycle can be represented by the following diagram: CO₂+H2O and Released energy

Carbon Monoxide-Its Occurrence and Formation.— 

Carbon monoxide, a constituent of "coal gas," is a product of the incomplete combustion of the carbon content of fuels. 

In mines it occurs as choke damp and is a source of great

danger both because of its poisonous effect and because of its combustibility. The gaseous product escaping from the exhaust of an automobile or gasoline engine of any type also contains this gas in large amounts. For this reason the engine should not be allowed to run when the car is standing in a poorly ventilated place. Neither should one stand near the exhaust for more than a moment. Carbon monoxide is also produced during the burning of coal in a limited supply of air. When the drafts are closed or a fresh supply of cold fuel has been put on the fire the conditions are especially good for its formation and escape.

In the bottom of the fire box, where a small supply of air is accessible, the carbon of the coal burns to carbon dioxide. 

As this gas rises through the red-hot coal in which other carbon needs oxygen for its oxidation it loses part of the oxygen it holds in combination and carbon monoxide is formed. C + O2 CO2 + C CO2 2 CO Ordinarily, as this carbon monoxide passes on to the top of the fire box, it will burn and a flickering blue flame is seen as a result. If, however, the oxygen supply is limited there is no chance for it to undergo further oxidation. 

Unless the dampers are open it may escape into the room instead of finding an outlet through the chimney. Another condition favoring the formation of carbon monoxide occurs during the burning of gas in the air chamber of the burner. 

For this reason when the gas strikes back into the air chamber it should be turned out instantly and relighted.

Properties.-Carbon monoxide is a colorless and practically odorless gas. The odor thought to be typical of it is due to other gaseous products which are formed at the same time. It burns with a pale blue flame, the final product being carbon dioxide. Its poisonous effect is due to its ability to combine with the hemoglobin of the red cor-

puscles to form carbon monoxide hemoglobin, a very stable compound. The oxygen-carrying power of the blood, which is due in great part to the temporary combination of this gas and hemoglobin as oxyhemoglobin, is thereby lessened. 

The inhalation of 10 cc. of carbon monoxide for every kilogram a person weighs is sufficient to cause the loss of one-third of the hemoglobin, a fatal result. For a person weighing 154 pounds (approximately 70 kilos) this would amount to 70 cc. of carbon monoxide. Death from carbon monoxide poisoning is really due to asphyxiation. Methods of treating this type of poisoning depend either on the stimulation of the respiration to increase the oxygen intake and the elimination of carbon monoxide which has not yet passed beyond the lung cells, or on the introduction of new hemoglobin into the blood. Artificial respiration, the use of pure oxygen or carbon dioxide, the latter to stimulate the respiratory center, try to correct the condition by affecting the respiration. Blood transfusions introduce new hemoglobin to take the place of that which has been put out of commission. The use of iron-containing remedies attempts to supply to the body material necessary to its >wn manufacture of hemoglobin. In serious cases, however, its action would probably be too slow.

SUMMARY 

Allotropic Forms of Carbon.

-Crystalline forms-diamond and graphite. Amorphous form-charcoal. 

Adsorption. The ability a substance has to cause molecules of other substances to attach themselves to its surface.

EXPERIMENTS 

1. Ability of Charcoal to Remove a Gas or a Solid from a 

Mixture Containing It (a) Half fill a small bottle or flask (250 cc.) with water and to it add sufficient ammonium hydroxide to give it a perceptible but not overpowering odor. To this add some powdered animal charcoal and shake thoroughly. Filter and compare the odor of the filtrate with that of the original mixture (before the addition of the charcoal). Result? Explain. (b) Repeat the above experiment but in place of the ammonium hydroxide add to the water a small amount of litmus solution or dye to color it. Compare with the original mixture the

filtrate obtained after shaking the colored solution with animal charcoal. Explain the result. 

2. Formation of Carbon Dioxide from Baking Powder

Fit a test-tube with a one hole stopper through which passes a short glass tube with a right angle bend. By means of a rubber connector attach to this glass tube another tube with a right angle bend, one arm being long enough to extend down into a second test-tube containing filtered limewater. In the first test-tube place about 1 teaspoon of baking powder. Add a small amount of water and put in the stopper immediately. 

What evidence do you see that a gas is produced? What is it? 

Explain your proof. 

3. Effect of Heat upon Sodium Bicarbonate (a) Dissolve some sodium bicarbonate in water and place in a test-tube fitted up as in the preceding experiment. Heat the solution and watch for evidences of the production of carbon dioxide. Explain what has happened. (b) Repeat the preceding experiment using dry sodium bicarbonate instead of a solution containing it. Is there any evidence of the production of carbon dioxide? Compare your results with those obtained in the preceding experiment and explain. 

4. Solubility of Carbon Dioxide 

Blow through a glass tube into some distilled water for several minutes. Add a few drops of clear limewater to it. What is the result? What have you proved to be in your expired breath? 

What happened to it when it entered the water?

CHAPTER XVII HYDROCARBONS 

Organic Compounds. As a result of one of the first efforts to classify different kinds of compounds, they were called animal, vegetable or mineral according to their natural occurrence. Later it was recognized that many compounds which were identical or very similar came from both animal and vegetable sources. It was believed that their occurrence in both kinds of organisms was not a coincidence but was due to some vital force and that their origin depended on the life processes of the plant or the animal. 

The term organic was therefore used to designate such compounds. In 1828 a German chemist named Wöhler produced in an entirely artificial way the compound urea which had previously been recognized as a waste product made by animal organisms. As a result of other experiments it was found possible to make more and more compounds whose formation had been believed to be dependent on the functions of living organisms. The original meaning of organic was therefore no longer correct. As carbon had been found to be common to all these compounds it was decided to use the term organic as a group name for carbon-containing compounds. Those in which the element is not present were either called inorganic or retained the name mineral. 

Included with these latter, however, we generally find simple compounds of carbon in an oxidized condition, for example its two oxides, carbon dioxide and carbon monoxide, and the carbonates.

It is very desirable to put the carbon compounds into a group by themselves. As there are over 200,000 of them 235

such a grouping simplifies the classification of chemical substances. From the standpoint of their composition and characteristics they are quite different from other compounds. Although they contain relatively few elements the proportion in which these are combined has endless variations. For example, the following familiar compounds are composed of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen but in very different proportions by weight. 

Formaldehyde CH,O 

Grain alcohol C,H,OH

Ether 

Acetic acid (C₂H₂)₂O C₂H₂O₂ There are also many instances of compounds not only containing the same elements in the same proportions by weight, but the actual number of atoms composing the molecule is different in each case. The following are examples: 

Formaldehyde CH,O 

Acetic acid

C₂H₂O₂ 

Lactic acid Glucose

C.H₂O C&H12O6 There are even cases in which two or more compounds are not only composed of the same elements in the same proportion by weight but the actual number of atoms in the molecule is the same. Three sugars, glucose, fructose and galactose are composed of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen, each of the three molecules containing 6 carbon atoms, 12 hydrogen atoms and 6 oxygen atoms. Therefore, the formula C6H12O6 represents them all. That these sugars are actually different compounds is evident from the fact that certain of their physical and chemical characteristics are different. The explanation of the difference is that it must depend upon the arrangement of the atoms in each molecule. Such compounds are called isomers. To illustrate the effect the arrangement of the atoms would have on the resulting combination imagine the integers 5, 7 and 9.

HYDROCARBONS 

237 Out of them the following very different numbers can be formed: 579 975 - 759 957 795 -597 The relation between carbon compounds, either those belonging to the same family or corresponding members of different families is very close. Such relations will be pointed out in the discussions of important organic compounds (see hydrocarbons—alcohols—ethers—aldehydesacids). The chemical reactions which these compounds undergo result in changes which are far more profound than those occurring in inorganic compounds. On heating, the majority of them char, an evidence of the carbon, and finally become completely decomposed to gaseous products. 

Some organic compounds will dissolve in water to form a solution which is either transparent, for example, sugar, or translucent, as in the case of starch paste. Many of them, as fats, paraffin or rubber, will dissolve only in liquids like alcohol, ether, acetone, etc., which are themselves carbon compounds and are therefore called organic solvents. 

As the group of organic compounds is so very large it has been found convenient to subdivide them into smaller groups. We shall suggest only the main divisions of the classification which is based upon the composition of the compounds. Those composed only of carbon and hydrogen are grouped together in a family called hydrocarbons. All other organic compounds containing one or more other elements in addition to carbon and hydrogen are so closely related to them that they are thought of as hydrocarbon derivatives. If they contain oxygen they are called oxygen derivatives. Wood alcohol (CH,OH), for example, is an oxygen derivative of methane (CH4). Halogen derivatives contain chlorine, bromine or iodine, chloroform (CHCl₂) and iodoform (CHI) also being derivatives of methane.

Other derivatives contain nitrogen or sulphur, usually in addition to oxygen. Allyl sulphide ((C,H,)2S), to which

the odor of garlic is due, is a sulphur derivative of propylene (CH). The alkaloids, such as morphine and strychnine, are complex nitrogen derivatives.

Hydrocarbons-Their Composition.-The interpreta tion of the composition of hydrocarbons is based on the belief that one carbon atom is capable of holding in combination four hydrogen atoms. This combining ability of an atom, whatever the kind may be, is called its valence. 

Carbon, in other words, is believed to have a valence of four, often represented CIV. One atom of hydrogen, on the other hand, can hold in combination only one other atom, therefore it has a valence of one. Another belief underlying the interpretation of the structure of hydrocarbon molecules is that a carbon atom can satisfy any or all of its four valences by combining with other carbon atoms as well as by combination with atoms of hydrogen. 

In many hydrocarbons each of the carbon atoms of which the molecules are composed holds in combination four separate atoms of hydrogen or carbon; that is to say its combining ability is fully made use of or its four valences are completely satisfied. Such compounds are called saturated. 

The entire group of saturated hydrocarbons is called the paraffin family because the familiar substance known by this name consists of complex members belonging to it. 

Included in the group are compounds having from one to sixty carbon atoms in their molecules. The following list contains the names and formulas of a few of the simple members: Methane Ethane 

CH4-the only hydrocarbon having only one carbon atom C2H6 Propane

C3H8 Butane C4H10 

Pentane, etc.

C5H12 

Decane, etc. C10H22

HYDROCARBONS 

239 On comparing the formulas of any two successive members of the family, for example methane and ethane or ethane and propane, it will be noted that there is always a definite increase of one carbon and two hydrogen atoms. 

This close relation between its members holds true not only for the paraffin family, but also for many other groups of organic compounds. Such a group of related compounds which vary from member to member by one carbon and two hydrogen atoms is called a homologous series. 

Up to this point compounds have been represented by formulas which show only the kind and number of atoms in their molecules. For inorganic compounds and for simple carbon compounds like methane this molecular formula, as it is called, gives all necessary information. It is often desirable, however, to know not only the number and kind of atoms which are present, but also their arrangement or relation to each other. A formula which attempts to picture this arrangement so as to show the valence of each atom and by what other atom it is satisfied is called a graphic formula. Lines, referred to as bonds, are used to represent the valences and the symbols at the opposite ends of each represent the atoms which are held in combination because of each combining ability. On comparing the two formulas for ethane it is evident how much more is shown by the graphic, for only in it does it appear that carbon has a valence of four. This formula also shows that three of the four valences are satisfied by combination with hydrogen atoms and that the fourth valence holds in combination the other carbon atom.

C2H6 (ethane) H-Ċ-Ċ – H H H The graphic formula for propane shows that in this mole-

cule three carbon atoms are linked together by single bonds to form a straight carbon chain. This combination between the carbon atoms leaves the middle one with two valences to be satisfied and each of the end ones with three valences, all of which hold hydrogen atoms in combination. The molecular formula, on the other hand, gives no idea of this relation between the different atoms.

HHH C3H8 (propane) H 

C-H H H It is possible to show two different relations existing between the carbon and hydrogen atoms of which the molecule of butane (C4H10) is composed.

HHH H-C HHH C-H normal butane

d. H H-C-H H-C – H isobutane In one the carbon atoms form a straight chain, whereas in the other they form a branched chain; in both cases the remaining valences are satisfied by hydrogen atoms. There is experimental proof that two hydrocarbon molecules composed of four carbon and ten hydrogen atoms do occur.

That they are not the same is evident from the fact that their characteristics are different. The difference in the position of the atoms is shown by the graphic formulas. 

There are also many hydrocarbons whose carbon atoms are not holding in combination as many other atoms as they

HYDROCARBONS 

241 can and for this reason they are called unsaturated hydrocarbons. Ethylene (C₂H) and acetylene (C₂H₂) are two of the most familiar examples. On comparing their formulas with that of ethane (C₂H), the corresponding saturated hydrocarbon, the following points will be noted: 

1st. All three hydrocarbons contain the same number of carbon atoms. 

2nd. In ethane, a saturated hydrocarbon, 6 atoms of hydrogen are held in combination by the 2 carbon atoms. 

3rd. In ethylene, only 4 atoms of hydrogen are held in combination by the 2 carbon atoms, 2 less than the maximum number. 

4th. In acetylene, only 2 atoms of hydrogen are held in combination by the 2 carbon atoms, 4 less than the maximum number.

The fact that the four valences of each carbon atom are not satisfied by four separate atoms, as in ethane, is expressed in the graphic formulas by using more than one bond to represent the union between the carbon atoms. H H 

Ethylene H- 

C-H 

Acetylene H-CC-H 

Instead of representing a stronger union, as might be thought, the additional bond or bonds shows greater weakness; for the carbon atoms can hold each other in combination by making use of only one of the valences of each. 

The additional valences are used only temporarily because there is no other atom to be held in combination. Whenever the hydrocarbon is given a chance to react with another substance the second or third bond that has been in temporary use is called upon to hold in combination the new atoms which have been supplied.

In the examples already chosen the carbon atoms, if more than one is present, form a carbon chain whose ends are not connected. Compounds of this type are called open chain organic compounds. There are other hydrocarbons in whose molecules the carbon atoms are believed to be associated together in a closed chain. Benzene (C,H) is the simplest and also one of the most familiar of these closed chain compounds. Its graphic formula represents a valence of four for each carbon atom, the double bonds indicating that in this compound we have another example of unsaturation.

H-C C-H 

C6H6 Benzene

H-C -H Occurrence.-Hydrocarbons are formed during the natural or artificial decomposition of more complex organic compounds. In marshy regions vegetation, partly or completely covered with water, is undergoing decomposition, and among the products formed is methane or marsh gas, bubbles of which are seen escaping from the water. The same gas is formed underground and escapes from the seams of coal mines. In these surroundings it is called fire damp and is often the cause of the explosions which are so apt to occur. Fermentations and putrefactive changes which occur within the digestive tracts of animals result in the formation of methane as one of the products, the amount sometimes being as high as 50%. Within the ground animal and vegetable material, much of which was the remains of the life of prehistoric ages, has gradually undergone

HYDROCARBONS 

243 extensive changes, the products of which are mixtures consisting wholly or in part of hydrocarbons. Two of these we know as crude petroleum and natural gas. The predominating compound in the latter is methane. Crude petroleum consists of a mixture of liquid hydrocarbons holding in solution others which are gaseous or solids. 

Because they vaporize at different temperatures, it is possible to separate the petroleum into a number of products. 

While each of these products is also a mixture it contains a smaller number of hydrocarbons than were in the original. 

The following are among the products obtained by the distillation: Petroleum product Boiling point Hydrocarbons content
