On chilling the lubricating oils solid flakes of paraffin (C22H46—C28H58) separate out. Vaseline (C22H46-C23H48) is obtained when lubricating oils are distilled in a vacuum. 

Nujol and similar mineral oil preparations used as laxatives consist of mixtures of hydrocarbons which have been very highly refined to remove all traces of the compounds whose odors and tastes are very distinctive and unpleasant. 

In the artificial decomposition of soft coal by heating in a limited supply of air, hydrocarbons are also formed. 

The gaseous product, which is one kind of artificial gas used as a fuel, consists largely of methane and hydrogen with smaller amounts of carbon monoxide and other hydrocarbons. The luminosity of the gas is due to the products mentioned last. Coal tar, which is another product of

the decomposition, contains a number of the closed chain hydrocarbons, among them being benzene (CH), toluene (C&H CH3), naphthalene (C₁oHs) and anthracene ((C&H₁CH)2). 

Properties and Uses.-Certain of the physical properties of the hydrocarbons have a bearing on their uses or the precautions to be observed in handling them. The few which are gaseous under ordinary conditions, methane, ethane, ethylene and acetylene, are found in the gaseous fuels. Ethylene is useful as an anesthetic, and the fact that it is a gas makes it easy to administer. A large number of the hydrocarbons are naturally liquids, but they become gaseous if the temperature is raised. The readiness with which this occurs, however, differs. Because gasoline vaporizes very much more readily than kerosene it is much more dangerous, for the combustion in either case takes place only after the vapor is formed. Those which are naturally solids melt and finally vaporize. Paraffin, for example, if heated to a sufficiently high temperature produces a vapor which may become ignited unless proper precautions are observed. In the case of naphthalene we have an example of a solid which is transformed to a liquid and returns to the solid form when it is cooled. The hydrocarbons are all insoluble in water but soluble in other liquids. Acetylene is very soluble in an organic solvent called acetone, particularly when it is under pressure. This makes it possible to store or ship large volumes of the gas in a comparatively small space. When the stop cock of the cylinder is turned the acetylene escapes in a gaseous form. The inability to remove a vaseline stain by sponging with water is due to its insolubility. Carbon tetrachloride, benzine or gasoline, however, will take it out because hydrocarbons are soluble in these liquids. Certain of the liquid hydrocarbon mixtures are themselves excellent solvents for other substances. Gasoline, benzine and naphtha are com-

HYDROCARBONS 

245 monly used for removing grease spots. We have already referred to the dangers attending their use (see Chapter IV). 

Kerosene is useful for cleaning porcelain tubs and sinks because it dissolves the sticky precipitate which is formed when soap is used with hard water. The greasiness of a number of the hydrocarbons and their ability to adhere to a surface make them useful as lubricants. Because they are organic compounds, however, when the machinery in which they are used becomes heated the grease gradually undergoes decomposition and much of the carbon becomes free. 

At certain intervals, therefore, the grease or oil has to be removed. The use of Nujol and other mineral oil preparations as a laxative depends in part upon the lubricating effect within the digestive tract. The food mass acquires a slippery consistency which makes it more readily moved along by the peristaltic action of the muscles. As the oil is undigested and unabsorbed its action is wholly within the digestive tract.

One of the most important properties of hydrocarbons is their ability to burn and upon it depends the use of many hydrocarbon mixtures as fuels to furnish heat, light or energy to move machines. If the oxidation is complete, carbon dioxide and water will be formed as final products. 

With many of the hydrocarbons, either the more complex paraffins or those which are unsaturated, there is a tendency, however, for a decomposition resulting in the formation of some free carbon to occur before the oxidation is complete. The presence of this free carbon in the burning gas gives it luminosity. If the flame comes in contact with any surface the carbon is deposited on it as soot; otherwise it is eventually oxidized to carbon dioxide. A blue or nonluminous flame, which contains no free carbon particles, will deposit no soot. If a gaseous or liquid fuel is to be used for heating purposes, the blue flame is desirable both because of the absence of soot and because it is hotter.

Therefore, the burners in which such fuels as kerosene or acetylene are to be used have been devised to permit it to be completely oxidized before the free carbon has a chance to become deposited. On the other hand fuels which consist largely of methane, hydrogen and carbon monoxide, all of which burn with blue flames, must be enriched with some other substances from which carbon can be formed if they are to be used for illuminating purposes. The suspension over the flame of a cone or mantle composed of compounds which become incandescent when hot will also produce luminosity. The familiar Welsbach mantle is a device of this type.

Coal-tar Products.-The mixture of compounds in coaltar are most important industrially, for from them a great variety of other substances, called coal-tar products, can be made. Among them are compounds whose odors or tastes are similar to those of compounds occurring naturally in flowers or fruits; therefore they are used for perfumes and flavoring extracts. Many compounds used as drugs are derived from the same source. Crude phenol, more commonly called carbolic acid (C,H,OH) is one of the constituents of coal-tar. From it the purified phenol and also methylphenol or cresol (C,H,CH₂OH) are obtained. Among the other manufactured products are a number which are used to relieve pain or headache of which antipyrine (C₁₁H₁₂N₂O), phenacetin (C&H₂OC₂H₂NHC,H,O), and aspirin or acetyl salicylic acid (CHOC₂H₂OCOOH) are examples. Although aspirin is supposed to be the least dangerous, they are all depressants and should not be used indiscriminately. Both antipyrine and phenacetine have an antiseptic effect. As the latter can combine with the hemoglobin of the red corpuscles to form metahemoglobin, the lips and nails of the patient to whom it has been given may appear blue, for the oxygen-carrying power of the blood is lowered. 

Salicylic acid (CH₂OHCOOH) and its salts are used medi- 6 4 3 11

HYDROCARBONS 247 cinally for their antiseptic action, some of them being administered intravenously as well as through the mouth. 

Sodium salicylate (C.H4OHCOONa), methyl salicylate or artificial wintergreen oil (CH4OHCOOCH,) and phenyl salicylate or salol (C&H4OHCOOCH¿) are a few of the more familiar salts. During digestion in the intestinal part of the digestive tract salol is hydrolized into phenol and salicylic acid, both of which act as intestinal antiseptics and on absorption salicylic acid can act as a urinary antiseptic. Aniline (CH¿NH₂), another coal-tar product, is the basis of many dyes of which methylene blue is an example. It is not only used for staining bacteriological slides, but it also can be used as a specific in malaria and as a urinary antiseptic. 

From it another drug, acetanilid (C,H,NHCH,CO), can be made. By affecting the heat-regulating mechanism it lowers the body temperature. It is also the basis of many headache remedies. Being a very dangerous depressant they must be used with great care. The poisonous effect of many of these drugs is said to be lessened if they are given with sodium bicarbonate. This may be due to the maintenance of an alkaline condition in the body fluids. 

Resorcin (CHOH), an antiseptic used in lotions for the skin, is made from phenol. Picric acid or trinitrophenol (CH₂(NO2)3OH) is another phenol derivative. In the laboratory it is used to precipitate proteins and alkaloids from solutions containing them. It is also used as a dressing for burns because of its antiseptic action and its ability to contract the skin. Another very dangerous explosive, trinitrotoluol or T. N. T., as it is more commonly called, is made from toluol. It is a noteworthy fact that the perfection of the production of peace-time products makes more efficient the production of those used for less peaceful purposes.

Certain of the hydrocarbons in coal-tar have individual uses of their own when pure. Benzene (C&H), whose com-

position has already been referred to, is useful as a solvent. 

A very similar name, benzine, has been given to a mixture. of saturated hydrocarbons obtained from crude petroleum. 

Although they are hydrocarbons in each case, their compositions are quite different. Toluol, or toluene, is used as a preservative. Urine can be kept some time before a laboratory examination is made, if it is covered with a layer of this compound. Naphthalene has slight antiseptic properties. We buy it as tar balls, sometimes called camphor balls, and use it to kill insects such as moths. In the body it is sometimes used in cases of abscesses of the lungs and to check the growth of bacteria in the intestine. 

It is also a vermifuge, being used to expel tapeworms. It will act as a deodorant if sprinkled on the gauze dressing of a discharging wound.

SUMMARY Organic Compounds.-Original meaning: Compounds whose formation depends on the life processes of plants or animals. 

Present meaning: Carbon-containing compounds—they usually contain hydrogen. Many of them also contain oxygen. 

Sometimes nitrogen, sulphur or a halogen is present. 

Isomers. Two or more compounds whose molecules are alike in the kind of atoms and the number of each present. 

Valence. The combining ability of an atom-which is expressed by a number representing the number of hydrogen atoms or their equivalent one atom of the element in question can hold in combination.

Saturated Hydrocarbons.-Compounds each of whose carbon atoms is holding in combination four other atoms, either of hydrogen or carbon. Compounds in whose molecules the combining abilities of the atoms are made use of to the fullest extent.

Unsaturated Hydrocarbons.-Compounds whose carbon atoms are holding in combination fewer hydrogen atoms than the maximum number.

Homologous Series.-A group of chemically related compounds which vary from member to member by one carbon atom and two hydrogen atoms.

HYDROCARBONS 

249 Molecular Formula.-A group of symbols with numbers which show the kind of atom and number of each composing the molecule in question.

Graphic Formula.-Represents not only the kind and number of atoms but also the relation they bear to each other in the molecule. In it the valence of each atom and how it is satisfied are expressed.

5. Distinguish between benzine and benzene. 

6. Intestinal gas usually contains methane. How can it be accounted for?

CHAPTER XVIII HALOGEN DERIVATIVES 

Composition. In the preceding chapter reference was made to a group of compounds called halogen derivatives containing iodine, bromine or chlorine as well as carbon and hydrogen.

The close relation between such compounds and the corresponding hydrocarbons is seen more clearly if we consider a few specific examples. Methyl chloride (CH,Cl), methylene chloride (CH2Cl2), chloroform (CHCl3) and carbon tetrachloride (CC1) are all considered derivatives of methane (CH4). A comparison of the formulas of the halogen compounds with that of methane shows the following facts: 

1st. That each molecule contains 1 carbon atom. 

2nd. That in the case of the halogen compounds either 

1, 2, 3, or 4 valences of the carbon are satisfied by halogen atoms rather than by a corresponding number of hydrogen atoms. A similar relation holds true for all halogen derivatives. Therefore, they are defined as being compounds whose carbon atoms hold in combination one or more halogen atoms in place of the corresponding number of hydrogen atoms in the related hydrocarbon. Among the more important ones are the following:

251 Naming. While in many cases the names of organic compounds give little or no information about their composition, as in the case of chloroform and methane, names can be made to show the atoms or groups of atoms (radicals) of which the molecules are composed. In methyl chloride and ethyl chloride the part common to the corresponding hydrocarbon is the group consisting of the carbon and hydrogen atoms, and in each case this radical is given a name suggesting the relation.

Radical Hydrocarbon Methyl

CH3 Methane

CH. Ethyl

C2H5 Ethane

C2H6 Propyl

C3H7 Propane

C3H8 Butyl CH, Butane C4H10 etc. 

To the organic radicals related to the paraffins the general name alkyl is given. Each is composed of the atoms of the corresponding hydrocarbon molecule minus one atom of hydrogen and in order to become a molecule it must hold in combination another atom or radical to satisfy the remaining valence. Each alkyl radical is named from its related hydrocarbon, but in place of the "ane" ending in the name of the latter the ending "yl" is substituted.

These radicals enter into the composition of organic compounds other than halogen derivatives.

In the case of methylene chloride (CH2Cl₂) we find another grouping of carbon and hydrogen atoms in which two of the valences of the carbon are satisfied by halogen atoms. Ethylene chloride (C2H4Cl₂) is another example.

Although it is a saturated compound it is given a name which suggests a relation to the unsaturated hydrocarbon ethylene (C2H4). In both cases the number of carbon and hydrogen atoms is the same and the hydrocarbon can be converted into the halogen compound by a chemical com-

bination with free chlorine. This reaction which results in only one product is called an addition reaction and it can occur only with unsaturated compounds. 

Formation. In the discussion of hydrocarbons their combustibility was the only chemical property mentioned.

They can also react with free chlorine, bromine or iodine. 

The result with an unsaturated hydrocarbon has just been referred to. With the members of the paraffin family of hydrocarbons the reaction with the halogens, which is one of the very few chemical changes they can undergo, takes a different course. As they are saturated compounds to begin with, in order to become combined with the carbon atoms the halogen atoms must substitute or take the place of an equivalent number of hydrogen atoms. This type of chemical change is called a substitution reaction. Two. products result from it, a compound consisting of carbon. hydrogen and the halogen and a compound composed of the halogen and the hydrogen which had to give up its position in the hydrocarbon. The following equations represent the stages through which methane will go during a substitution reaction with chlorine.

CH4 + Cl₂ CH CI+ Cl2 

CH2Cl2 + Cl2 

CHCl3 + Cl2 

With bromine a similar reaction 

CH.Cl + HCI 

CH2Cl2 + HCl 

CHCl3 + HCI 

CCl4 + HCl occurs more slowly, the products corresponding to those formed with chlorine. 

With free iodine the reaction is too slow to show visible results.

There are more practical methods by which the halogen compounds are obtained. Iodoform, which is the most important of the iodine derivatives, can be readily prepared from grain alcohol (ethyl alcohol-C₂H₂OH), free iodine and sodium hydroxide, the final product of the reaction being iodoform (CHI) and sodium formate (NaHCO₂).

HALOGEN DERIVATIVES 

253 Chloroform is obtained by a similar reaction between grain alcohol and bleaching powder, the latter yielding calcium hydroxide and chlorine, which react with the alcohol. In both cases an intermediate product is formed. In the chloroform preparation the intermediate product is trichloracetaldehyde, more commonly called chloral (CC1¸CHO)— 

(see Chapter XX). 

CH3CH₂OH + 4 I2 — CI3CHO + 5 HI (triiodoacetaldehyde) or CH3CH2OH + 4 Cl₂ CC1,CHO+ 5 HCl (trichloracetaldehyde) + NaHCO, CICHO+ NaOH - CHI3 2 CCA,CHO + Ca(OH), (iodoform) (sodium formate)

CHCl3 + Ca(HCO2)2 (chloroform) (calcium formate)

Properties and Uses.-With the exception of iodoform, which is a yellow, crystalline solid, the more simple halogen derivatives are liquids which evaporate very readily. For those used as anæsthetics this is a very important characteristic. The evaporation of ethyl chloride occurs at so low a temperature that the warmth of the hand is sufficient to drive it in a fine spray from its special container. When it strikes the skin so much heat is absorbed because of its very rapid evaporation that it freezes the surface tissue. 

Therefore, it can be used as a local anesthetic. It is also used as a general anæsthetic, although it is given only in the beginning because it weakens the heart action and does not produce complete muscular relaxation. Chloroform, as well as the less familiar compounds, ethyl bromide and methyl chloride, are also used to produce anæsthesia. 

Somnoform is a mixture consisting of the following: 

Ethyl chloride 65%, ethyl bromide 5%, methyl chloride 30%

As chloroform irritates the skin, vaseline should be applied to the patient's nose and mouth before it is administered.

Because of its ability to relax muscles it may be given as an inhalation to stop convulsions. Chloroform liniment, which is composed of soap liniment and 30% chloroform, is applied locally to relieve pain. Because of its antiseptic action it is sometimes given as chloroform water to check fermentation or gas formation.

Many of the halogen derivatives have an overpowering, sweet odor. In the case of iodoform it is very penetrating and unmistakable even in minute traces. Because of the ease with which it can be identified by its odor it is possible to test for the presence of acetone in the urine by using reagents which will cause the production of iodoform. The same test is sometimes used to detect the presence of grain alcohol, since iodoform can also be formed from it. 

Among the commonly used organic solvents are chloroform and carbon tetrachloride. The former is very desirable for removing spots from delicate materials because of its rapid evaporation. As neither compound is combustible this is an added point in their favor. The familiar cleansing fluid, Carbona, is essentially carbon tetrachloride. It is much safer to use than the liquid hydrocarbon mixtures, such as gasoline and benzine, which are very combustible.

The fact that carbon tetrachloride does not burn, together with certain other characteristics it possesses, makes it valuable as a fire extinguisher. It vaporizes readily and the gas which is formed is heavier than the air. When it is sprayed from an extinguisher, as the Pyrene, a heavy incombustible gaseous blanket settles down around the fire shutting off the necessary oxygen supplied by the air.

Although chloroform does not burn, it can unite with oxygen very slowly and one of the resulting products is carbonyl chloride (COCl2) or phosgene. The presence in chloroform of this compound which was one of the "poison gases" used in the World War is very undesirable if it is to

HALOGEN DERIVATIVES 

255 be used as an anæsthetic. Even in traces its effect might be harmful, especially if the vitality of the person being anæsthetized is below normal. As hydrochloric acid is also a product of the reaction, if a sample of the cholorform is tested with silver nitrate, even a slight milky appearance will be a proof that some oxidation has taken place. If the following precautions are observed in its storage, the chance for the oxidation will be lessened. It should be stored in amber-colored bottles, for light helps the change to occur.

The bottles should be kept filled, as increasing air space increases the contact with oxygen. The presence of a very small amount of grain alcohol in the chloroform prevents the oxidation from taking place so readily.

Iodoform, either alone or in a paste with paraffin oil and bismuth subnitrate, is used as an antiseptic. The efficiency of its action, at least when used alone, is not now believed to be so great as was at one time supposed. Whatever effect it has is believed to be due to the release of free iodine which is the actual antiseptic (see Chapter XXVI).

SUMMARY 

Halogen Derivative.-A compound whose carbon atoms hold in combination one or more halogen atoms (chlorine, bromine or iodine) in place of a corresponding number of hydrogen atoms.

Alkyl Radical.-A group of carbon and hydrogen atoms which needs only one more atom of hydrogen to become a molecule of a saturated hydrocarbon. Instead of the hydrogen atom it can hold in combination some other monovalent atom or radical. The radical bears the name of the hydrocarbon with the ending "yl" instead of "ane."

Substitution Reaction.-A reaction between two substances in which the entrance of a new atom or radical in a molecule is at the expense of another which had been there previously. 

A chemical change in which there is a substitution of one kind of unit for another in the compound undergoing the change.

Addition Reaction.-A reaction between two substances resulting in the formation of only one product. A chemical change in which new atoms can be added to a molecule because it was unsaturated in the beginning. 

QUESTIONS

How can it be detected? 

8. Why can the presence of acetone in the urine be easily detected by causing it to be converted into iodoform? 

Explain what this compound is. 

9. On a Carbona label is a picture of a lighted match held over the mouth of the bottle. Explain its significance.

CHAPTER XIX  

Alcohols Their Composition.-A group of oxygen derivatives of hydrocarbons called alcohols is in reality a family of organic hydroxides, for they all contain one or more hydroxyl radicals in place of an equivalent number of hydrogen atoms present in the corresponding hydrocarbon. Examples of different alcohols are given in the following list:

Those whose molecules contain only one hydroxyl radical are called monohydric alcohols. The first four mentioned in the list are saturated compounds like the paraffins to which they are related and they belong to another homologous series. The graphic formulas for the 257

first two members differ from those for the corresponding methane and ethane in that the hydroxyl radical is held in combination by one of the four valences of a carbon atom. Two of its valences are satisfied by hydrogen atoms and the fourth by either hydrogen (in methane) or another carbon atom (in ethane). This association of one carbon atom, two hydrogen atoms and the hydroxyl radical (-CH2OH) is called a primary alcohol radical. H-C он

On writing a graphic formula for propyl alcohol (Ç₂H₂OH) we find two possible arrangements for its atoms. In one, the hydroxyl radical is attached to a carbon atom at the end of the carbon chain, this position always resulting in a primary alcohol radical. In the other, the hydroxyl radical is attached to an inside carbon atom whose other three valences are satisfied by one hydrogen atom and two carbon atoms. This forms a group of atoms called the secondary alcohol radical (—CHOH).

Two propyl alcohols are known, some of whose properties are different, although their molecules are composed of the same kind and number of atoms. This is another example of isomerism (see Chapter XVII). In more complex alcohols there is another possible position for the hydroxyl radical, for it can be attached to a carbon atom whose other three valences are satisfied by carbon atoms forming a

tertiary alcohol radical (-COH). Because of the position of the hydroxyl radical which affects certain of its characteristics, alcohols are sometimes designated as primary, secondary or tertiary. Allyl alcohol (C,H,OH) is an example of an unsaturated monohydric alcohol.

HHH 

COH 

Allyl alcohol

Its graphic formula makes the following facts apparent: 

1st. Its unsaturation-represented by the double bond. 

2nd. That it is a primary alcohol.

Naming of Alcohols.-The saturated, monohydric alcohols are named according to the alkyl radical which with the hydroxyl radical forms each molecule. For example, the name ethyl alcohol (or ethyl hydroxide) suggests the ethyl radical (C₂H). Glycerol, or glycerine (C,H(OH),) is an example of a saturated alcohol containing three hydroxyl radicals. It is a trihydric alcohol. Its graphic formula represents the fact that each hydroxyl radical is attached to a different carbon atom so that the molecule consists of two primary and one secondary alcohol group. HHH H-C C C OH OH OH 

- H Glycerol 

Because of its taste it was named from a Greek word meaning “sweet.” The name glyceryl alcohol suggests the presence of the glyceryl radical (C3H5). For many of the more complex alcohols the general ending "ol" is used on a name having some peculiar significance. Cholesterol, the name meaning "solid bile," is of interest as it is one of the essential constituents of body cells and as a waste product it

occurs in the bile, which is the only body fluid in which it is soluble. Certain types of gall stones are made up wholly or in part of this compound. Phenol (CH₂OH) is the simplest of the alcohols which are related to the closed chain hydrocarbons, in this case benzene (CH). It is commonly but incorrectly spoken of as carbolic acid because of its extremely corrosive action. Other examples of alcohols of this type are cresol, a constituent of lysol, naphthol, menthol and thymol.

Manufacture. We have already referred to phenol, cresol and naphthol as coal-tar products (see Chapter XVII). 

Grain alcohol, the technical name for ethyl alcohol, suggests the source from which it is most commonly obtained. 

Starch from grains of various kinds and also sugars in fruit juices will finally yield ethyl alcohol and carbon dioxide as the result of a fermentation depending partly or entirely on the presence of yeast. The chemical changes will be discussed in a later chapter (see Chapter XXIII). As a result of the fermentation a solution containing not more than 12% alcohol can be obtained. To increase the amount, alcohol must either be added, as in fortified wines like port, or the liquid must be distilled. As the alcohol boils at 

78° C. it will vaporize more quickly than the water and on condensation the distillate will contain more and more alcohol as the process of distillation is repeated. In distilled liquors like whisky, rum and brandy the alcohol content is increased to 50%. Absolute alcohol, from which the water is almost entirely removed, 0.4% being left, can be made only by adding some substance like quicklime. Its effect, coupled with that of the distillation, makes it possible to obtain nearly 100% alcohol.

When wood is heated in a very limited supply of air, methyl alcohol is one of the resulting products. For this reason it is known technically as wood alcohol. The process, which is called destructive distillation, is applied to both

bituminous coal and wood and analogous products are formed from each. 

Type of product 

1. Solid... Coal Coke.. 2. Thick liquid... Coal-tar. 

3. Thin liquid.... "Ammoniacal liquor" contains ammonium compounds. 

4. Gases..

Combustible-an artificial fuel gas..

Charcoal Wood-tar Wood con- "Pyroligneous acid" tains acetic acid, acetone, methyl alcohol 

Combustible-used as fuel to carry on this industry 

By subjecting the pyroligneous acid to processes of purification, the acetic acid and acetone are removed. Technical wood alcohol usually contains traces of acetone, however. 

Denatured alcohol is grain alcohol to which other liquids have been added to make it unfit for internal use. The substances used, among them being benzine and wood alcohol, depend upon the use to be made of the mixture. For medicated alcohols no substance must be put in which would be injurious when it is applied externally. If it is to be used as a fuel, there must be nothing which would interfere with its combustibility. Methyl alcohol would be very undesirable if it were used for rubbing a patient because of the danger of absorption.

Glycerol is a product of the soap-making industry. When fat and a solution of lye, the latter being principally sodium hydroxide, are brought into contact with each other a reaction takes place the products of which are soap and glycerol (see Chapter XXII). The commercial value of the latter was not realized in the early days of the industry, but it has now become a very important product. 

Properties and Uses.-The simple alcohols are liquids which vaporize easily. This property accounts for the cooling effect of an alcohol sponge bath, for as the alcohol evaporates heat is absorbed and the body temperature is lowered. A number of them, notably methyl alcohol and

ethyl alcohol, have very characteristic, somewhat sweetish odors. Serious or fatal results can be produced by the inhalation of gaseous methyl alcohol or by its repeated external application as well as by its internal use.

Most of the alcohols are soluble in water and mix with it in all proportions. Cholesterol is an example of one which is insoluble. Its presence as an essential constituent of living cells is probably accountable for their ability to remain intact even though they exist in a liquid medium. 

For example, under ordinary conditions there is no tendency for blood corpuscles to be destroyed by being dissolved by the water solution in which they are suspended and carried. 

The hemolizing effect of anaesthetics such as ether or chloroform is probably due in part to the fact that they will dissolve the cholesterol, and so it is no longer able to protect the corpuscles against the dissolving action of the water. A number of the alcohols have the ability to absorb and retain water, a property which is called hygroscopic. Because of this it is very difficult to remove water completely from ethyl alcohol. For the same reason glycerine (glycerol) is used in many tooth pastes to keep them moist. Its ability to keep the skin soft and moist is due to the same property. 

Methyl and ethyl alcohol are useful because of their ability to dissolve other substances. The former alcohol has many industrial uses as a solvent for such substances as gums, resins and shellac. Fruit flavors, perfumes and many substances used medicinally or for toilet purposes, as iodine and benzoin, are put up in alcohol because of their solubility in it. Adhesive plaster can be removed more readily if it is first soaked in alcohol because the gum is then dissolved.

A number of alcohols owe their uses to their disinfectant properties. Thymol is an intestinal antiseptic which is given for hookworm. The cresol content of lysol makes the latter effective. Slides and needles which are being used for bacteriological work are washed in ethyl alcohol. The

protein constituents of all living cells are in a semi-fluid condition, but through the action of these compounds the condition is so changed that the life of the cell is no longer possible. Any micro-organisms present will therefore be killed if they come in contact with the disinfectant. Because of the extremely corrosive effect phenol has on tissues, its use must be attended with great care (see Chapter IX). 

The astringent effect of alcohol is probably due to the same action, but as the protein constituents of the skin are very much more resistant it serves to harden the superficial tissues without destroying them. 

One of the most important chemical changes alcohols can undergo is oxidation. Rapid oxidation can occur so readily that methyl alcohol and ethyl alcohol are useful as fuels. They burn with a very hot, colorless flame which produces no soot, the products being carbon dioxide and water. 2 CH3OH + 3 02 2 C2H5OH + 6 02 > 2CO,+4 H,0 > 4CO,+6 HO 

Sterno, a so-called solid alcohol, consists of these alcohols in which so much solid material has been dissolved that the entire mass solidifies. When heat is applied some of the alcohol vaporizes and burns and, as the substances in solution are themselves combustible, the contents of the can will in time be completely used up. After using sterno, the cover should be put back loosely until the can becomes cool. 

If it is made air-tight at once the pressure of the alcohol which continues to be vaporized while the contents of the can are still hot will blow the cover off. In the body, alcohols also serve as a source of energy and will on complete oxidation yield the same products, carbon dioxide and water. 

Glycerol, which is a product the body cells obtain from fat, undergoes this chemical change when fat is functioning as a fuel (see Chapter XXII). Ethyl alcohol also undergoes the same change. The immediate feeling of increased

warmth, however, is followed by a lowering of the body temperature. This is due to the larger amount of blood brought to the surface, an evidence being the flushed skin and the consequent loss of heat from it. 

Alcohols are also able to undergo slow oxidation. The condition under which it can occur and the products formed depend upon the kind of alcohol. We have already referred to a difference in them due to the position of the hydroxyl radical. Primary alcohols such as methyl alcohol and ethyl alcohol, can be oxidized so readily that they can make use of atmospheric oxygen if the condition is favorable. They can also take it from oxygen compounds which give up this element easily. Such compounds are called mild oxidizing agents. In either case the products resulting from the oxidation are first a compound called an aldehyde (see 

Chapter XX) and then an acid (see Chapter XXI), both of which contain the same number of carbon atoms as the original alcohol. For example, methyl alcohol will yield formaldehyde (HCHO) and formic acid (HCOOH). From ethyl alcohol, acetaldehyde (CH,CHO) and acetic acid (CH3COOH) will be formed. The manufacture of formaldehyde from wood alcohol and also of vinegar (acetic acid) from a solution containing ethyl alcohol depends upon this property. In the latter case the action is favored by the presence of bacteria which are in the mass we call "mother of vinegar."

HCH2OH + oxygen (methyl alcohol) 

HCHO+ oxygen

CH,CH₂OH + oxygen (ethyl alcohol) 

CH,CHO + oxygen

HCHO+ H₂O (formaldehyde) > HCOOH (formic acid) 

CH,CHO + HO (acetaldehyde)

CH3COOH 

(acetic acid)

Under the same conditions, that is by using atmospheric

oxygen or a mild oxidizing agent, an alcohol containing a secondary alcohol radical will be converted into a compound called a ketone (see Chapter XX) which contains the same number of carbon atoms. At this point the oxidation will stop unless the conditions are made more favorable. The further oxidation will result in the splitting of the ketone into two products, each a carbon compound. The behavior of secondary propyl alcohol illustrates this type of oxidation. The product first formed is acetone, a ketone. It is in turn changed into acetaldehyde and carbonic acid. 

CH3CHOHCH3 + oxygen (secondary propyl alcohol) 

CH3COCH3 + oxygen →CH,COCH3 + H2O (acetone)

CH3CHO + H,CO3 (acetaldehyde + carbonic acid)

Tertiary alcohols require a very favorable condition for oxidation, as it results in an immediate splitting of the compound into products each of which contains some of the original carbon of the alcohol. The behavior of compounds containing primary and secondary alcohol radicals is of particular importance as it is the underlying reason for the formation of the intermediate oxidation products of fats and carbohydrates when they are being utilized in the body (see Chapters XXII and XXIII). 

Another important property of alcohol is their ability to react with acids. In this reaction, which is due to the hydroxyl radical of the alcohol, this compound behaves like a base and the resulting products are water and a salt. 

These salts, formed from organic hydroxides like alcohols, are often called esters (see Chapter XXII). Ethyl alcohol will react with hydrochloric acid, forming water and a salt which in this case is ethyl chloride. This compound, which we have already discussed and classified as a halogen derivative, can also be considered an ester because of its close relation to an alcohol and acid.

C2H5OH + HCl HOH + C₂H5Cl In a reaction between glycerol and nitric acid, glyceryl nitrate and water will be formed. The former of the two products is known technically as nitroglycerine. It is a very dangerous explosive. In a 1% solution it is used as a cardiac sedative.

C3H5(OH)3 + 3 HNO3 3 HO CH5(NO3)3 (glyceryl nitrate) 

Methyl alcohol and salicylic acid produce water and methyl salicylate (synthetic wintergreen). Because of this characteristic reaction alcohols, especially ethyl alcohol, have great industrial value, for from them many other useful conpounds can be manufactured.

Ether-Its Composition and Relation to Other Com pounds. The compound known familiarly as ether is more correctly named ethyl ether ((C2H5)2O). Its formula discloses the fact that it is an organic oxide, the oxygen atom holding in combination two ethyl radicals. "Ether" is really a family name for a group of compounds of similar composition of which the following are examples:

Formula (CH3)20 or C₂H₂O Name 

Methyl ether. 

Methyl ethyl ether. 

Ethyl ether.. etc. 

Note the homologous series

CH,C2H5O or C3H8O (C₂Hs)2O or C4H10O 

Related hydrocarbon Methane 

Ethane and methane Ethane 

In each case the compound bears the name of the alkyl radicals combined with the oxygen. If they are alike the compound is called a simple ether; if they are different it is a mixed ether. Ethyl ether is the most important member of the family. It is another oxygen derivative related to the hydrocarbon ethane. In this case the oxygen is not in a hydroxyl group. It occupies the position of

two hydrogens in two ethane molecules, whereas in ethyl alcohol, which is also an oxygen derivative of ethane, one hydroxyl radical occupies the position of one hydrogen atom of the corresponding ethane molecule. xx - C-

Preparation.-Ethyl ether is made by boiling together ethyl alcohol and concentrated sulphuric acid. Because of the acid used it is sometimes called sulphuric ether. It usually contains 4% ethyl alcohol and a little water. The most simple way of explaining the chemical change is to consider the acid a dehydrating agent. That is to say that because of its presence alcohol molecules lose either hydrogen atoms or hydroxyl radicals which combine to form water and the two fragments of the alcohol molecules combine to form the molecule of ether. For this reason ethyl ether is sometimes called the anhydride of ethyl alcohol just as an inorganic oxide, as calcium oxide, is referred to as the anhydride of the corresponding hydroxide. As the sulphuric acid remains sulphuric acid after the change has taken place it is not represented in the equation. → HOH + (C2H5)2O C2H5OH + C2H5 | OH Theoretically the reaction could continue indefinitely if

alcohol were supplied to the mixture. However, the dehydrating action of the acid becomes less as it is diluted by the water formed. Because of the heat, varying amounts of free carbon will be formed by the charring of the alcohol, an organic compound. This carbon in turn causes the decomposition of some sulphuric acid, the products being water, carbon dioxide and sulphur dioxide. As this reduces the amount of sulphuric acid it must be renewed occasionally. A relatively small amount of acid, however, can convert a large amount of alcohol into ether. From these same compounds, ethyl alcohol and concentrated sulphuric acid, an entirely different product, ethylene (C₂H₁) can be made. The condition determining the product to be obtained is the proportion of the materials used. With alcohol in excess it will be ether. If an excess of sulphuric acid is present, ethylene will be formed. In this latter preparation the acid also serves as a dehydrating agent, but both hydrogen atom and hydroxyl radical are removed from the same molecule of alcohol.

C2H5OH → C₂H₁ + HOH This is an excellent example of the great difference conditions make in reactions in which organic compounds are involved. 

Properties and Uses.-Underlying the uses of ether and the precautions to be observed in its handling and storage are a number of important properties. The one to be most emphasized is its extreme combustibility. Very closely linked with this is the readiness with which it vaporizes, for it is the gaseous ether which burns and the gas when mixed with air forms a very explosive mixture. Therefore, ether should never be kept or used near heat. Some go so far as to say that a flame should never be nearer than ten feet. Because of the great rapidity with which it vaporizes it produces a frozen condition when sprayed on

the skin and so can be used as a local anesthetic. This ability to vaporize also makes possible its use as a general anæsthetic, for only gaseous substances can be given by inhalation. Ether has a sweet odor which is sickening and overpowering, especially to one who has been under its influence. It is a very good solvent for grease and gums and because of its rapid evaporation it is more desirable than some other solvents when removing spots from delicate fabrics. It should be used in a well-ventilated place, however, for otherwise the fumes may become overpowering, especially if considerable time is required for the removal of the spot. Ether can be used for the removal of adhesive plaster, although it may be less desirable than alcohol because of the patient's dislike of the odor. A cool place should be chosen for the storage of ether. The tin container is often bulged at the ends because of the evaporation of some of the liquid. If too much gas is formed, the pressure inside the can may be increased enough to blow out the cork or, if the can is sealed, to cause it to burst.

SUMMARY 

Alcohols. Organic hydroxides containing one or more hydroxyl radicals in place of the equivalent number of hydrogen atoms in the corresponding hydrocarbon.

Monohydric Alcohol. An alcohol containing one hydroxyl radical.

Dihydric Alcohol.-An alcohol containing two hydroxyl radicals. 

Trihydric .Alcohol.-An alcohol containing three hydroxyl radicals.

Polyhydric Alcohol.-An alcohol containing a number of hydroxyl radicals.

Primary Alcohol.-An alcohol in which the hydroxyl radical is held in combination by a carbon atom which is also combined with two hydrogen atoms as well as one carbon atom. 

Secondary Alcohol.-An alcohol in which the hydroxyl radical is held in combination by a carbon atom which is also combined with one hydrogen atom and two carbon atoms. 

Tertiary Alcohol.-An alcohol in which the hydroxyl radical is

held in combination by a carbon atom which is combined with three other carbon atoms. 

Denatured Alcohol.-Grain alcohol (ethyl alcohol) to which other substances have been added which make it unfit for internal use.

Medicated Alcohol.-A denatured alcohol which can be applied to the body externally. The compounds which have been added to the ethyl alcohol are not harmful to the skin. 

Ethers.—Organic oxides compounds composed of oxygen and two alkyl radicals. 

Mixed Ethers.-Ethers in which the oxygen holds in combination two different alkyl radicals.

Simple Ethers.-Ethers in which the oxygen holds in combination two alkyl radicals of the same kind.

CHAPTER XX  

Composition. In the preceding chapter reference was made to compounds called aldehydes and ketones which are formed during the oxidation of alcohols. Because of the common carbonyl radical (-C=O) which occurs in them all these compounds can be classified together in one family. The following list includes some of its more familiar members:

Formaldehyde... HCHO Acetaldehyde CH3CHO Propion aldehyde C₂H,CHO

Formula hydro- Formula carbon

CH,COC₂H, Butyl alcohol

C9H16CO 

When the carbon atom of the carbonyl radical is the end atom of the carbon chain two of its valences remain to be satisfied, one by hydrogen and the other by another carbon atom. This is the position of the radical in all aldehydes and therefore the association of a carbon, a hydrogen and an oxygen atom is commonly called the aldehyde radical (-CHO). If, however, the oxygen atom of the carbonyl radical is held in combination by an inside carbon atom the other two valences of the latter are satisfied by carbon atoms. Ketones contain the radical in this position, and 272

co it is frequently called the ketone radical (—CO) when no hydrogen is associated with it. To illustrate the difference in the position compare the graphic formulas of the two derivatives of propane both of which contain the carbonyl radical. 

CHO-represents either an aldehyde or ketone.

H H H 

H-C- C-CO Propion aldehyde-note the oxygen atom is attached to the end carbon atom. H H H-C-C · C - H dimethyl ketone or acetone-note the oxygen atom is attached to an inside carbon atom.

Naming. The names of the ketones are more descriptive of their composition than are those of the aldehydes. 

For example, methyl ethyl ketone suggests at once the two alkyl radicals which are combined with the carbonyl radical. Dimethyl ketone, which is the simplest one of these compounds, is referred to more commonly by its industrial name, acetone. Each aldehyde is named from the acid into which it can be oxidized. The simplest compound of this type is called formaldehyde because on oxidation it is changed to formic acid. The aldehyde which can be oxidized into acetic acid is named acetaldehyde. 

Formation. Whenever a primary alcohol undergoes slow oxidation the first product formed is an aldehyde. For example, methyl alcohol is oxidized into formaldehyde and ethyl alcohol into acetaldehyde.

HCH2OH + oxygen (methyl alcohol)

CH,CH₂OH + oxygen (ethyl alcohol)

HCHO+ H₂O (formaldehyde) 

CH3CHO + H2O (acetaldehyde)

In every case the change brought about is the removal of two hydrogen atoms from the alcohol molecule. Such a reaction is considered an oxidation, however, for this term is broadened to include changes in which either oxygen is combined with another substance or hydrogen is removed from it (see Chapters IV and V). The name aldehyde has arisen from the fact that aldehydes are formed from alcohols by a dehydrogenation. In other words, an aldehyde is an alcohol dehydrogenated. This was shortened to aldehyde. The industrial manufacture of both formaldehyde and acetaldehyde is based upon this action. The consequent dependence of these two compounds on their corresponding alcohols makes for two very important ways in which the latter are necessary to manufacturing industries. Because of the ease with which methyl alcohol can be converted into formaldehyde and the presence of the latter can be detected, this reaction also underlies a method of testing for the presence of methyl alcohol in solutions which may contain it, for example bootleg liquor. If a coil of copper wire is heated and plunged into the suspected solution, this process being repeated many times, methyl alcohol will be converted into formaldehyde. The action is explained as follows. 

When the wire is heated its surface becomes tarnished due to the formation of a film of copper oxide. When the hot, tarnished wire comes in contact with the alcohol, the copper oxide acts as an oxidizing agent and the wire becomes bright because on losing the oxygen metallic copper is again formed. This oxygen, together with two hydrogen atoms from the alcohol, form water, and the other product is formaldehyde. 

HCH2OH + CuO HCHO+ H2O + Cu 

If concentrated hydrochloric acid containing a trace of iron and some milk are added to this solution, the presence of formaldehyde in it will be detected by a purple color when the mixture is heated.

Secondary alcohols will also produce ketones when they are oxidized. For example, if acetone were to be made by the oxidation of an alcohol it would be necessary to use secondary propyl alcohol (CH₂CHOHCH¸). Primary propyl alcohol (CH3CH₂CH₂OH) would yield an aldehyde by the same process.

H- H H H 1 Ċ — Ċ-Ċ—H + oxygen H OH H 

Secondary propyl alcohol

H-C C C 

H 0 H 

Acetone or dimethyl ketone

There are other more practical ways of manufacturing this compound, however. When fat is oxidized in the body normally, carbon dioxide and water are formed as end products. 

If, for some reason, the fat metabolism is disturbed, the oxidation is not completed, but it stops with the production of betahydroxybutyric acid, acetoacetic acid and acetone. 

These three compounds, which are called "acetone bodies," are often the cause of a condition of acidosis (see Chapter XXII).

While acrylic aldehyde (C₂H,CHO) might be formed by the oxidation of allyl alcohol (C₂H,CH₂OH) it is produced more frequently by the overheating of glycerol or of fat (see Chapter XXII). As a result of the heating the glycerol loses hydrogen and oxygen atoms and the products formed are water and acrylic aldehyde or acrolein.

C3H5(OH)3 C,H,CHO +2H,O 

The irritating, pungent odor often noticed when candles are first snuffed may be due to this compound. When fat, which is being used for frying, is heated until it smokes the irritation of one's eyes, nose or throat is caused by the acrolein which is formed. 

Properties and Uses.-Aldehydes and ketones are for the most part liquids which vaporize readily. Formaldehyde

is an exception, for it occurs naturally as a gas. The liquid, which is purchased under its name, is a 40% solution of the gas. In general, the odor of the aldehydes is irritating and unpleasant. Benzalaldehyde, however, has an odor and taste resembling those of a natural oil in bitter almonds.

Its use in flavoring extracts depends upon the similarity. 

Ketones, on the other hand, have a sweet, rather agreeable odor. In a condition of acidosis due to the acetone bodies, acetone may be eliminated in sufficient amounts to impart its characteristic odor to the urine or the breath. In the latter case the condition is spoken of as "acetone breath."

Both aldehydes and ketones are soluble in water. Formaldehyde is soluble to such an extent that it is possible to have a 40% solution. The solubility of acetone makes it possible for it to be carried in solution in the blood and to be eliminated in solution in the urine. Acetone is one of the important solvents for other substances. The gaseous hydrocarbon, acetylene, is very soluble in it, especially when it is under pressure. It is stored and shipped in this condition in small steel cylinders. Fats and resins are easily dissolved in acetone. For this reason it is an ingredient of many varnish removers.

The use of formaldehyde for fumigating rooms or clothing or as a preservative for biological specimens depends upon its action as a germicide and disinfectant. Its effect is due to the change in the condition of the protein constituents of whatever comes in contact with it. Living organisms, as bacteria, are killed because their protein constituents are made insoluble. For the same reason the preserved specimen is hardened. Some people are especially susceptible to the action of formaldehyde and the constant handling of specimens preserved in it may produce an intense irritation of the skin unless proper precautions are observed.

An excellent way to protect clothing against moths is to hang it in an air-tight closet in which an open bottle of

formaldehyde has been placed. Sufficient gaseous formaldehyde will escape from the bottle to kill any moths or larvæ which may be present. 

Formaldehyde combines chemically with ammonia to form a compound called urotropine or hexamethyleneamine. 

This is a urinary antiseptic which is given by mouth. On reaching the kidneys after its absorption, it will be decomposed in the urine which is being excreted, especially if it is acid in reaction. For this reason it is commonly given with sodium dihydrogen phosphate, the latter serving to increase the acidity of the urine. As a result of the decomposition of the urotropine, formaldehyde is again formed and it acts as a disinfectant during the passage of the urine through the ureters and into the bladder.

An aldehyde has the ability to combine several of its molecules into one molecule having the same percentage composition but whose molecular weight is a multiple of that of the original aldehyde. Such a compound is called a polymer. From several formaldehyde molecules, possibly three, a solid substance called paraform is produced. This compound, known most commonly in the form of candles, is used for fumigation. When the candle is burned the paraform is decomposed into gaseous formaldehyde. 

3 HCHO (HCHO), (HCHO), 3 HCHO

In the same way molecules of acetaldehyde will combine to form a polymer called para-acetaldehyde or paraldehyde. 

3 CH3CHO → (CH3CHO)3 This compound is used as a hypnotic to produce sleep and to lessen muscular contractions in epilepsy and delirium tremens. Because of its disagreeable taste it is usually given in milk or a syrup. Both paraformaldehyde and para-acetaldehyde can readily be decomposed into the orig-

inal compound from which each was formed. When in the polymerized form neither one retains the characteristics which are typical of aldehydes. It is believed, therefore, that the aldehyde molecules combine through their oxygen atoms. As all compounds whose units are known to be joined through oxygen atoms are less stable than are those in which the union has taken place in some other way, this would account for the readiness with which the polymer is decomposed. It would also account for the loss of the aldehyde characteristics since the oxygen atom of each molecule is used in the combination. The union of three formaldehyde molecules through their oxygen atoms to form paraformaldehyde is represented as follows: H- C = 0 + C-H H- Aldehydes can also be combined into polymers whose characteristics are not entirely like those just described. While the percentage composition is the same and the molecular weight of the polymer is a multiple of that of the aldehyde in some cases polymers are formed which cannot be decomposed into the original aldehyde. This type of polymer also retains the characteristics of an aldehyde. These differences are believed to be due to the union of the molecules through carbon atoms rather than through oxygen atoms. 

It is thought that the building up of complex compounds like fats and carbohydrates in living organisms depends upon a combination of this latter type. From two very simple compounds, carbon dioxide and water, which are

taken in by a plant, it ultimately manufactures a sugar (C6H12O6). The reaction is frequently expressed as follows: ✈ C6H12O6 + 6 O₂ 

6 CO, + 6 H,O

It is quite probable, however, that the plant cells first convert the carbon dioxide and water into formaldehyde and then polymerize it into the sugar. 6 CO, +6 H,O 

6 HCHO > 6 HCHO +6 O,

C6H12O6 There is experimental proof that such a conversion is possible, for a product resembling sugar has been made artificially from a solution of formaldehyde. 

Because of its ability to react with chlorine or iodine and sodium hydroxide, acetone, is useful in the manufacture of chloroform and iodoform. This reaction is also the basis of a method for the detection of acetone in urine. By treating the specimen with reagents supplying the iodine and base, iodoform will be produced. The very characteristic odor of the product makes its presence easily observed. 

In the manufacture of iodoform and chloroform an intermediate product is formed which is either tri-iodo-acetaldehyde or trichloracetaldehyde.

CH,CHO acetaldehyde CCI,CHO trichloracetaldehyde 

Trichloracetaldehyde, more familiarly known as chloral, is an oily, colorless liquid. When water is added to it a crystalline substance, called chloral hydrate (CCl₂CHO.H₂O) is formed which is used as a hypnotic and depressant. It is not only a habit-forming drug, but it can produce very dangerous results if given to a person whose heart action is weak.

The behavior of aldehydes and ketones during oxidation was referred to in the discussion of alcohols (see Chapter 

XIX). Aldehydes can be oxidized so readily that they can either make use of atmospheric oxygen or take this element from mild oxidizing agents. The product which

is formed is the acid to which the aldehyde owes its name. 

It contains all the atoms of the aldehyde molecule together with an additional oxygen atom. In other words, the molecule is not split as a result of the oxidation. 

HCHO+ oxygen

CH3CHO+ oxygen HCOOH (formic acid)

CH3COOH (acetic acid)

Molecules of ketones split when they undergo oxidation and two products are formed each of which contains a part of the original carbon in the ketone. Because of the splitting, the oxidation is brought about with greater difficulty and the reagent which can accomplish it is called a strong oxidizing agent.

CH3COCH3 + oxygen 

CH,CHO + HCO3 (acetaldehyde)

This difference in the behavior of compounds containing _C_O) radicals is of aldehyde (CHO) and ketone (-C=O) radicals is of great importance for it underlies the explanation of the products formed during the use of foods or body constituents as fuels (see Chapters XXII, XXIII, XXIV).

Polymer. A complex molecule formed through the union of several simple molecules of the same kind. Its percentage composition is like that of the simple molecule. Its molecular weight is a multiple of that of the simple molecule. 

Polymerize. The act of combining to form a polymer.

CHAPTER XXI ORGANIC ACIDS 

Composition. The replaceable hydrogen, which is common to all acids, is in the case of organic acids in a radical consisting of a carbon atom, an oxygen atom and a hydroxyl radical, the group being called the carboxyl radical (-COOH). All organic acids contain at least one of these radicals, but many contain two or even more. Since the hydrogen, which becomes the hydrogen ion when the acid is in solution, comes from the carboxyl radical, acids are called monobasic, dibasic, etc., according to the number of these radicals in their molecules. In addition to the carboxyl radical which is always present many organic acids also contain some other characteristic radical. They can be subdivided into families according to the kinds of radicals present. Hydroxy acids, of which lactic acid (CH,CHOHCOOH) is an example, contain the hydroxyl radical as the group name very definitely states. The amino acids, which will be discussed in connection with proteins (see Chapter XXIV) contain the amino radical which consists of a nitrogen atom and two hydrogen atoms (—NH₂). 

The list on page 284 includes some of the more important organic acids.

Relation to Other Compounds. All acids containing only one carboxyl radical form a very complete group to which the family name of fatty acid has been given. This name is peculiarly significant since many of its members are closely related to fats and are formed from them during digestion (see Chapter XXII). With the exception of the 1 283

These characteristics, which will be referred to in the discussion of the properties and uses of organic acids, become more pronounced as the compounds become more complex. Each member of the fatty acid family can be considered in relation to a corresponding alcohol and hydrocarbon. For example, formic acid has already been mentioned as an oxidation product of methyl alcohol (see Chapters XIX and XX). Methane is the hydro carbon to which it is related. On referring to the organic acids listed as examples, it will be noted that in each case the corresponding alcohol and hydrocarbon contain the same number of carbon atoms as does the related acid. A comparison of the formulas for acrylic acid (C₂H¸COOH)

ORGANIC ACIDS 

285 and propionic acid (C₂H,COOH) shows that the difference between them is in the number of hydrogen atoms present in the molecule. Since in the acrylic acid molecule the carbon atoms are not holding in combination their full quota of hydrogen atoms the acid is said to be unsaturated.

Propionic acid, on the other hand, is a saturated fatty acid for its molecule contains the largest number of hydrogen atoms which can be held in combination. Oleic acid, linoleic acid and linolenic acid are unsaturated fatty acids which are similar to stearic acid, a saturated fatty acid, so far as their carbon content is concerned. They differ in their hydrogen content, the more hydrogen atoms the molecule lacks the greater being its degree of unsaturation. For example, both oleic acid (C₁7H3COOH) and linolenic acid (C17H2COOH) are unsaturated, but the latter is more so than the former. Of the unsaturated fatty acids, oleic acid is the most important because of its wide occurrence in food and body fats. Those organic acids which contain more than one characteristic radical can be considered derivatives of a corresponding acid containing only carboxyl radicals as the common unit. Their names often suggest such a relationship. Lactic acid, for example, is called hydroxypropionic acid and the amino acid, alanine, is called amino propionic acid. A comparison of the formulas shows at once the significance of each name.

H H OH H H OH H- Hddd_o H-C-C ddd-o H OH 

Hydroxy propionic acid

H HN2 Amino propionic acid Occurrence. Many organic acids occur commonly in animal or vegetable organisms as the free acid or as its salt. 

The inflammation resulting from the sting of an ant, wasp or nettle is due to formic acid which has been injected

beneath the skin. The name formic, meaning ant, was given to the acid because of this association. Animal secretions, such as perspiration, consist in part of acids, among them being acetic acid, butyric acid and valeric acid. The two latter compounds are responsible for its disagreeable odor.

The weakening and rotting of fabrics through continued contact with perspiration is due to the effect of the acids on the fibers. The fatty acids occur most widely in combination as organic salts some of which we know as fats and waxes. Others are in part responsible for the odors and flavors of fruits and perfumes of flowers (see Chapter 

XXII). Acetic acid is present in fruit juices which have been undergoing decomposition through the help of yeasts and bacteria. The fruit sugar is first changed to ethyl alcohol and carbon dioxide by a yeast fermentation. The ethyl alcohol in turn becomes oxidized to acetic acid if bacteria capable of helping this change are present. These chemical reactions account for the changing taste in sweet cider as it becomes old. Lactic acid is most commonly associated with sour milk. It is also responsible for the characteristic sour taste of sauerkraut. In an animal it is a product of the metabolism of fats and carbohydrates when these compounds are being used as a source of heat and other forms of energy (see Chapters XXII and XXIII). 

While the names of some organic acids link them specifically with a certain fruit or vegetable, generally the same acid occurs more widely. Oxalic acid is present not only in the sorrel from which it derives its name but also in rhubarb and tomatoes. Citric acid which we usually associate only with lemons and the other citrus fruits is present in practically all sour fruits and berries as well as many vegetables. 

Milk, too, contains calcium citrate. Tartaric acid and its salts occurs in many fruits and vegetables although it is most abundant in grapes. Malic acid, meaning apple, occurs

ORGANIC ACIDS 

287 not only in this fruit but is a common constituent of fruits and vegetables.

Formation. In general organic acids can be produced by the oxidation of a suitable alcohol. In some instances, as in the case of acetic acid, the alcohol can be formed so readily that this is a practical method for making the acid. 

Vinegars are made by the oxidation of a solution containing ethyl alcohol. By having bacteria present which furnish enzymes helpful to the reaction, the process can be completed in a relatively short time. The necessary oxygen is supplied by the air.

CH,CH₂OH + oxygen 

CH3CHO + oxygen

CH,CHO CH3COOH 

Much industrial acetic acid is made by the purification of the liquid product called pyroligenous acid which is obtained in the destructive distillation of wood (see Chapter XIX). 

By purification the acetic acid is removed from the methyl alcohol and acetone which it also contains. 

Another general method for obtaining an organic acid is by a reaction in which its salt or ester undergoes hydrolysis (see Chapter VI). Many of the fatty acids, as stearic acid, palmitic acid and oleic acid, are obtained by the hydrolysis of fat (see Chapter XXII). If we assume fat to be glyceryl stearate, the products resulting from this reaction will be glycerol and stearic acid. 

C3H5 (C17H35COO)3 + 3 HOH —— C3H5(OH)3 + 3 C17H35COOH

In many cases neither of the two general methods mentioned is practical because of the cost of the proper alcohol or ester or because of the inability to obtain it. Therefore, for certain acids there are industrial methods each of which is peculiar to a particular acid. Tartaric acid or its salt potassium hydrogen tartrate, for example, is isolated from grape juice in which it is normally present in solution. During the alcoholic fermentation of the juice the tartrate be-

comes insoluble and is deposited in lumps of crude argol. 

These lumps are removed and purified, the solid ultimately obtained being pure potassium hydrogen tartrate (cream of tartar) or the corresponding acid, tartaric acid, into which it is converted by a chemical reaction with another acid. 

In a similar way citric acid can be isolated from lemon juice which is said to contain 6% free acid. Carbohydrates, such as sugar or starch, will also produce citric acid as the result of a fermentation induced by certain types of mold. 

Starch and sugars other than milk-sugar will undergo a lactic acid fermentation. With the proper types of bacteria present lactic acid can then be converted into butyric acid.

The peculiar flavor of Limburger cheese is due to a butyric acid fermentation which has occurred. 

Properties and Uses.-A large number of organic acids are soluble in water. They either occur naturally in solution or when used they are put in solution. The fruit acids occur in the juice. Lactic acid is in solution in sour milk. When oxalic acid is used for removing ink spots or for cleaning copper surfaces, it is dissolved in water. Except for the simple fatty acids the members of this family become decreasingly soluble and take on the solubility characteristics of fat as their molecular weights increase. In other words, they are soluble in such solvents as ether, chloroform, acetone and gasoline. The simple saturated fatty acids and the unsaturated fatty acids are oily looking liquids.

Lactic acid is also a liquid. Many of the acids are in a solid form when pure. The more complex fatty acids are greasy and have the consistency of fats. Oxalic acid and the other fruit and vegetable acids are crystalline solids when pure. Many are odorless but a few have a very characteristic odor. In the case of acetic acid and to a less degree with lactic acid it has a sharp, pungent smell and sour taste. The disagreeable odor and taste of rancid butter is due to butyric acid.

Because of the replaceable hydrogen which they all con-

ORGANIC ACIDS 

289 tain and which becomes the hydrogen ion when the acid is in solution, these compounds exhibit properties which are also characteristic of the mineral acids. They turn blue litmus red. They react with bases to form water and a salt which is called an ester if the base is an alcohol. 

With sodium hydroxide, salicylic acid reacts to form water and sodium salicylate whereas with methyl alcohol it produces water and methyl salicylate.

C&H OHCOOH + NaOH C&H4OHCOOH + CH3OH HOH + CH4OHCOONa HOH + C&H4OHCOOCH, Because of this reaction with bases it is possible to measure quantitatively the total acidity of a fruit juice or of sour milk. By taking a known volume of the liquid containing the acid and adding to it a solution of sodium hydroxide of known concentration until the neutral point is reached, the acidity of the solution can be determined from the volume of the base required for the neutralization. A normal solution is one, every liter of which contains the amount of dissolved substance which will bring into a reaction 1 gram of hydrogen or its equivalent. Equal volumes of different solutions of the same concentration will, therefore, be equal to each other and 1 cc. of a 1/10 normal solution of sodium hydroxide reacts with the same volume of a 1/10 normal solution of any acid. Suppose the acidity of a fruit juice were measured in terms of citric acid: 

1 gram mol. wt. of citric acid (192 grams) would bring into a reaction 3 grams of hydrogen. therefore 1/3 gram mol. wt. citric acid (64 grams) would bring into a reaction 1 gram of hydrogen. therefore 1 liter of N. solution of citric acid contains 64 gms. acid. 

1 liter of N/10 solution of citric acid contains 6.4 gms. acid. 

1 cc. of N/10 solution of citric acid contains 0.0064 gms. acid.

As each cubic centimeter of N/10 sodium hydroxide reacts with an equivalent volume of citric acid, multiplying the

volume of base required by its citric acid equivalent will give the weight of citric acid in the volume of fruit juice used in the experiment. If the weight of the latter is also known the percentage of acid can be determined.

Organic acids react with carbonates to form the salt of the organic acid, carbon dioxide and water. There are a number of familiar ways in which this reaction is made use of. When baking soda is used with sour milk the lactic acid in the milk is responsible for the reaction which takes place.

NaHCO3 + HC3H5O3 (sodium (lactic bicarbonate) acid)

NaC3H5O3 + CO2 + H2O (sodium lactate) The effervescence taking place when the two solutions of a Seidlitz powder are combined is due to a reaction between sodium bicarbonate (baking soda) and tartaric acid.

Effervescent saline cathartics, such as magnesium citrate, or sodium phosphate, contain one of the salts mentioned and also sodium bicarbonate and either citric, or tartaric acid, or a mixture of the two.

Because of the ability of acids to react with metallic oxides, oxalic acid is used to clean copper. The tarnish which consists in part of copper oxide adheres so closely to the surface that it can be removed only by being dissolved. 

Through a reaction between the oxide and the acid a soluble salt, copper oxalate, is formed which can be washed away. CuO+H2C2O4 → CuC2O4 + H2O This acid is also useful for removing stains due to ink or iron rust. In this case also a soluble compound is formed by a reaction between the acid and the insoluble substance causing the stain. Assuming iron rust to be ferric hydroxide, the reaction would be as follows: 2 Fe(OH)3 + 3 H2C2O4 → Fe(C2O4)3 + 6 HOH (ferric oxalate)

ORGANIC ACIDS 

291 With a bright copper surface acetic acid undergoes a reaction which is peculiar to it. At the point where the surface of the acid touches the copper a blue-green deposit is formed but the portion of the copper below the surface is not affected. Oxygen, as well as the acid, is involved in the reaction and the blue-green deposit which is formed is basic copper acetate (CuOHC₂H₂O₂), more commonly known as verdigris. As this is a poisonous substance it is unwise to use a copper utensil for cooking or storing any food mixture containing vinegar.

A number of organic acids are useful because of their antiseptic properties. Benzoic acid is used both externally on the skin and internally as an intestinal and urinary antiseptic. The specific action of salicylic acid in rheumatism may also be due to its effect on infections in the joints. 

Externally it is applied to the skin to soften portions which have become very much thickened and hardened, as corns and callouses. As a result they can be removed more readily.

A number of organic acids or their salts can be precipitated from their solution by the addition of a soluble calcium salt. The use of calcium compounds as an antidote for oxalic acid poisoning depends upon the formation of insoluble calcium oxalate. In this way the oxalic acid is prevented from being absorbed. Blood which is to be used for experimental work can be prevented from coagulating by allowing it to come in contact with an oxalate the instant it is withdrawn from the blood vessels. The calcium compounds which are essential to its clotting are precipitated by the oxalate and the resulting calcium oxalate is no longer able to help in the formation of the clot. The presence of a citrate will also prevent clotting but not by the formation of a precipitate as calcium citrate is precipitated out only on heating. In this case the new combination in which the calcium occurs does not help the clotting even though the compound is still soluble. To prevent the clotting of blood

which is to be used for transfusion, an oxalate cannot be used because of its poisonous effect. In this case sodium citrate is used.

SUMMARY 

Organic Acids. Are acids containing one or more carboxyl radicals.

Carboxyl Radical.-A group consisting of 1 carbon atom, 1 oxygen atom and 1 hydroxyl radical (-COOH). 

Hydroxy Acid.-An acid containing both carboxyl radicals (one or more) and hydroxyl radicals (one or more).

Amino Acid.-An acid containing both carboxyl radicals (one or more) and amino radicals (one or more).

Fatty Acids. A group of organic acids containing 1 carboxyl radical as the group common to them all. 

Saturated Fatty Acid.-A fatty acid whose carbon atoms are holding in combination the largest possible number of hydrogen atoms.

Unsaturated Fatty Acid.-A fatty acid whose carbon atoms are not holding in combination the maximum number of hydrogen atoms.

CHAPTER XXII  Composition and Relation to Other Compounds.-The units entering into the composition of esters can be considered as having been derived from organic bases called alcohols on the one hand and on the other from some acid. 

For this reason they are classified as organic salts or esters since they are analogous to the compounds formed through the reaction of an acid and any inorganic base. They are usually so named as to suggest the related base and acid. 

Note the example on page 295.

Occurrence. Many esters occur naturally as constituents of either animal or plant tissues. The odors of flowers and the odors and flavors of fruits are due in part to their presence. In the essential oil of the fruit or blossom are a number of compounds possessing fragrance and a pleasing flavor. In addition to esters the mixture may contain complex hydrocarbons of which limonene is an example, alcohols such as menthol and aldehydes like benzaldehyde. The following list includes a few examples of esters and the familiar taste or odor which they cause: Ester 

Methyl salicylate 

Menthyl acetate 

Amyl acetate 

Ethyl butyrate 

Odor or flavor wintergreen peppermint pear pineapple 

It is interesting to note that butyric acid, which is most offensive because of its taste and odor forms an ester which is prized for its pleasing flavor and aroma.
