the more complex alcohols belonging to the ethyl alcohol family. Bayberry wax and beeswax are examples. They both consist largely of a compound whose chemical name, myrical palmitate, suggests its relation to myrical alcohol and palmitic acid. Lanolin, which is used as the basis of ointments is another very familiar example. It forms the waxy coating in sheep wool.

Of all the naturally occurring esters, the fats and oils are perhaps the most important. In every case they are related to fatty acids and the alcohol, glycerol (CH¿(OH)3).

Therefore, a chemical name would include the name of the glyceryl radical (C,H,) and the name of the fatty acid with which it is combined, as glyceryl stearate, glyceryl oleate, etc. The abbreviated names stearin and olein suggest only the related fatty acid. It is not possible to represent correctly a naturally-occurring fat by one name, however, for they are all mixtures made up of these glyceryl esters of fatty acids in differing proportions. Palmitin, stearin and olein are present in them all. In cocoanut oil and in butter there is also a large proportion of esters of some of the simpler fatty acids such as butyric acid, capric acid, caproic acid and caprylic acid (see Chapter XXI). The presence in butter of the glyceryl ester of butyric acid is responsible for the characteristic taste of this fat which makes it preferable to other fats.

Formation. The close relation between an ester and its corresponding alcohol and acid makes it possible to form it by a reaction which is in reality a neutralization since it involves an acid and an organic base. The formation of methyl salicylate from methyl alcohol and salicylic acid has already been referred to (see Chapter XXI). If ethyl alcohol and acetic acid are heated together in the presence of concentrated sulphuric acid, through the help of the latter a new compound is formed having a distinctive, sweet, fruity odor quite different from that of any of the com-

pounds used. It is due to ethyl acetate, the ester formed in this case. In the body, fats are formed through a similar reaction between glycerol and fatty acids. The following equations represent the changes taking place with some of the typical fatty acids.

C3H5(OH)3 + 3 C3H,COOH-C3H5(C3H7COO); + 3 HOH (glycerol) (butyric acid)

C3H5(OH)3 + 3 C15H3COOH (palmitic acid) 

C3H5(OH)3 + 3 C17H35COOH (stearic acid)

C3H5(OH)3 + 3 C17H33COOH (oleic acid) (glyceryl butyrate) →C3H5(С15H31COO)3 + 3 HOH (glyceryl palmitate) →C3H5(C17H35COO)3 + 3 HOH (glyceryl stearate) →C3H5 (C17H33COO)3 + 3 HOH (glyceryl oleate)

The reacting compounds glycerol and the fatty acids may be the original digestion products of food fats which are recombined into fat during the process of absorption. They may also come from starches and sugars which are in excess of the body's energy requirement and which it transforms into fat for storage. The excess carbohydrate is in part converted into glycerol and in part into fatty acids. These compounds are in turn combined and the resulting fat is stored as inert or adipose tissue (see Chapter XXIII). 

Classification. The glyceryl esters of fatty acids or glycerides, as they are also called, are classified as saturated or unsaturated according to the kinds of fatty acids entering into their composition. For example, olein is an unsaturated fatty acid. Stearin, palmitin and butyrin, on the other hand, are saturated because of the saturation of the cor responding fatty acids (see Chapter XXI). The unsatu rated glycerides are liquid. The consistency of those which are saturated parallels their molecular weight. The simplest ones are liquid but as their molecular weights increase they become firmer. Butyrin is a liquid whereas stearin is a solid resembling paraffin. Therefore, the consistency of the naturally occurring fats will be influenced by the proportion of the different glycerides contained in them. Vege-

table oils, such as olive, cottonseed, etc., are liquid because of the high percentage of unsaturated glycerides they contain. A large proportion of the simple saturated glycerides like butyrin and caproin will cause the fat containing them to be softer than it would be were the glycerides the more complex ones like palmitin and stearin. Although butter and cocoanut oil are solid at ordinary room temperature, their consistency is soft and a very slight rise in temperature will cause each to melt. With the exception of codliver oil most animal fats are solid and for this reason they are usually referred to as fats, for example bacon fat and beef fat. Vegetable fats, on the other hand, are commonly called oils as most of them are liquid at ordinary room temperature. This term is even applied to the fat obtained from cocoanuts in spite of the fact that under ordinary conditions it is solid.

Oils are sometimes differentiated as fixed or non-volatile oils and volatile oils. Such a distinction is based upon the ability of the substance to be vaporized without being decomposed. True fats and oils cannot be vaporized. For this reason a spot of oil on a tablecloth or garment will not disappear on standing as a water spot will. It must be removed in some other way. The so-called volatile oils are the essential oils previously referred to which consist not of glyceryl esters of fatty acids but of a mixture of compounds such as hydrocarbons, alcohols, aldehydes or esters of alcohols other than glycerol. The term oil is used in this case because of certain physical characteristics. The greasy feeling of one's fingers noticeable when removing the skin of an orange is due to the essential oil. Flavoring extracts which contain these substances must be used in larger amounts, if they are added to a food mixture before it is cooked, to allow for loss by evaporation. A mustard plaster should be made with water which is luke warm rather than hot for the latter will cause the vaporization of so much of

the essential oil that the efficiency of the plaster will be decreased. The skin will be less likely to be blistered if a mixture of mustard and lard is used for the presence of the latter lessens the amount of oil vaporized at any given time. 

A glyceride molecule contains three fatty acid radicals. 

In every example already mentioned, as butyrin, olein, etc., these radicals are alike and the compound is called a simple glyceride for this reason. There are molecules, however, which contain two or even three different fatty acid radicals. 

If so, they are called mixed glycerides, stearo-palmito-olein being an example. Its name suggests at once that a molecule each of stearic acid, palmitic acid and oleic acid has reacted with a molecule of glycerol to form it. Naturallyoccurring fats are mixtures made up of both simple and mixed glycerides. 

Properties and Uses.-Because of the insolubility of fats and oils in water their presence in a water medium can be readily seen by the appearance of fat globules or a greasy layer on top of the water. Butter can be washed to remove the salt present in it without any loss of fat because of the insolubility of the latter. Grease spots cannot be removed by sponging or washing with water for the same reason. Although fats and oils are not soluble in water they can be dissolved in other liquids. The ones in common use are gasoline, benzine, benzene, carbon tetrachloride, ether and chloroform. One reason for the appropriateness of the name fatty acid is that the majority of these compounds are also soluble in these solvents. Certain other compounds, notably cholesterol, a complex alcohol (see

Chapter XIX) and lecithin, a compound related to glycerol, fatty acids and a phosphorus compound and sometimes called a phosphorized fat, have the same solubility characteristics. They are classified together as lipins because of this similarity in their solubility and not because of any similarity in composition. Vitamine A, one of the com-

pounds essential to normal growth and metabolism, about whose composition little is known as yet, is like fat in its solubility and so it is frequently called fat-soluble A. The determination of the fat content of food or body tissue depends upon its extraction or solution by means of some solvent, ether or gasoline being most commonly used.

Tables giving the results obtained are usually headed "ether-extractable material." The figures represent not only the true fat but also any other lipin which may have been present such as cholesterol and lecithin, which are essential constituents of plant and animal tissues. Another important use made of fat solvents is for the removal of grease and oil in cleaning processes.

Another physical change which fats and oils can undergo is called emulsification. As in the case of solution, the fat is distributed through a liquid medium but the results are different in the two cases. The solution is clear and transparent because the fat globules are too small to be visible even when in large masses whereas the emulsion is cloudy or milky in appearance, the globules being not so small and therefore visible in masses but not individually. Because of this difference in the size of the fat particles they do not settle out of the solution so long as the solvent does not evaporate. On the contrary, they do settle out from the medium in which the fat is emulsified. The thicker the medium is the longer the fat or oil remains distributed through it. Mayonnaise, an emulsion of oil in egg, is much more permanent than is French dressing, which is an emulsion of oil in vinegar or lemon juice. In meat stock the fat remains separate from the liquid and on top of it whereas in gravy the presence of the thickening used creates a medium in which the fat is emulsified. Milk is a natural emulsion in which the milk fat is distributed through a solution containing a number of compounds, the proteins, casein, lact-albumin and lact-globulin being the ones which make

it a good emulsifying medium. The cleansing action of soap and water is due in part to the fact that in the process of washing or scrubbing the grease becomes broken up mechanically into smaller particles which are in turn emulsified in the soap solution. When in this condition they can be removed easily from the article being washed. Of the medicinal emulsions, cod-liver oil preparations are the most familiar.

The unsaturated glycerides can be converted into saturated compounds by a chemical change known as hydrogenation. In it a chemical reaction takes place between the glyceride and hydrogen and a more saturated compound is formed. For example, the glyceride of linolenic acid is converted first into one related to linoleic acid, then into one related to oleic acid, olein, and finally into stearin. 

C3H5(C17H29COO)3 + 3 H₂ 

C3H5(C17H31COO)3 + 3 H₂ 

C3H5(C17H33COO)3 + 3 H₂ 

C3H5 (C17H31COO); C3H5(C17H33COO)3 C3H5(C17H35COO)3

This process has become of great industrial importance for it has made available for use fats and oils which at one time were considered useless. Fish oils, for example, lose their objectionable odor as a result of the change. The manufacture of Crisco from cottonseed oil depends upon hydrogenation. From an oil which is liquid because of the large proportion of olein present a hard product is formed because of the conversion of the olein into stearin.

Another chemical change of great importance is the saponification of fats and oils. In it a reaction with either sodium hydroxide or potassium hydroxide takes place, the products of which are glycerol and soap, the sodium or potassium salt of the related fatty acids. The following equation represents the reaction if we call the fat stearin. 

C3H5 (C17H35COO)3 + 3 NaOH 3 NaC17H35COO+C3H5(OH)3

If sodium hydroxide is used, the resulting sodium salt is called hard soap. If the potassium salt is formed because

of the use of potassium hydroxide, it is called soft soap. 

As soap is ordinarily made at home a solution of lye, which is about 95% sodium hydroxide, is beaten into the melted. fat until the mixture becomes thick and creamy. This results in the emulsification of the fat. As saponification takes place gradually this "cold process soap," as it is called, cannot be used until after it has been allowed to stand for some time. When ready for use it contains not only the soap but also the other product glycerol and any excess of lye or fat which was not saponified. Another process, called the "hot process," is used industrially. In it the lye and fat are boiled together so that the saponification takes place more quickly. When it is completed the soap is separated from the glycerol and any excess of the reagents which may be present by the addition of sodium chloride. As soap is insoluble in a solution containing this compound it becomes precipitated and the other compounds which remain in solution can be drained off. To the soap additional ingredients can then be added to adapt it to the purpose for which it is to be used. Colors and perfumes are often used for toilet soaps. For scouring purposes some insoluble compound like powdered pumice-stone is put in. Washing soda, borax, or naphtha are a few of the substances added to increase the cleansing action of soap used for general cleaning and laundry purposes.

If fat is heated, it will react with water to form glycerol and fatty acids, the compounds from which it was made. 

Even though no water is added the small amount naturally present is sufficient to permit some hydrolysis to occur. 

The presence of this moisture can be proved by heating fat or oil in a test-tube and noting the beads or film of moisture which collect at the top. The sputtering of fat which occurs as it is being heated is due to water. When a moist mass of food is put into fat to be fried the liquid appears to boil. When any liquid boils it is being vaporized rapidly. As fat cannot undergo this change the decided

movement is not due to its vaporization but rather to the evaporation and escape of the water present. Fat which has been heated a number of times shows a more decided litmus reaction than fresh fat although the latter usually shows a very slight acid reaction. The presence of free fatty acid formed by the hydrolysis of the fat is responsible for it. The longer it is heated the more extensive is the hydrolysis and so there is a pronounced change in the odor and taste of the fat as well as an increasing effect on litmus. 

Glycerol, one of the products of the hydrolysis, undergoes a further decomposition during the heating as a result of which the aldehyde, acrolein, is formed (see Chapter XX). 

C3H5(C17H35COO)3 + 3 HOH (stearin) (water)

C3H5(OH)3 (glycerol) →C3H5(OH)3 + 3 C17H35COOH (glycerol) (stearic acid) 

C,H,CHO + 2 H,O (acrolein) (water)

Some fats will be decomposed at a very much lower temperature than others. For this reason butter is much less desirable for frying than certain other fats such as the vegetable oils or Crisco. Hydrolysis of fats will occur even without the application of heat. It is partly responsible for the changed taste and odor of fats when they become rancid.

Because butter contains butyrin, a glyceride whose hydrolysis yields butyric acid, it is especially disagreeable when rancid.

As hydrolysis is the chemical change which occurs during digestion, the digestion products of fats and oils are glycerol and the related fatty acids. It is helped by the enzyme lipase which occurs in both gastric and pancreatic juice.

Food fats which are already in an emulsified condition may undergo considerable digestion when in the stomach. The bulk of fat digestion occurs, however, in the small intestine. 

During absorption a recombination of glycerol and fatty acids occurs so that it travels in the lymph and blood in the form of fat.

In olden times fats and oils were widely used as fuels and

tallow candles are still used to some extent. On being burned the fat is oxidized to carbon dioxide and water but before complete oxidation occurs much free carbon is formed. Its presence causes the luminosity of the candle flame and the deposit of soot if any object comes in contact with it. When fat serves as a fuel in the body the same chemical change of oxidation occurs. 2 C3H5 (C17H35COO)3 + 163 02 114 CO₂+ 110 H₂O It is probable that the fat is first hydrolized to glycerol and fatty acids and that these products are in turn oxidized. 

While the end products are carbon dioxide and water, in both cases many intermediate products are formed. Those resulting from the oxidation of glycerol are such as would be expected from a compound containing both primary and secondary alcohol radicals (see Chapter XIX). The following formulas represent them in the probable order of their production.

From glycerol there is first formed the corresponding aldehyde, glyceric aldehyde, by the oxidation of a primary alcohol radical. By a reduction of its primary alcohol radical and an oxidation of the secondary alcohol radical pyruvic aldehyde is then formed. This compound may undergo either one of two changes. It may be directly oxidized to the corresponding acid by a change which affects only the aldehyde radical or it may be converted to lactic acid because of both a reduction and an oxidation, the former change occurring in the ketone radical and the latter in the aldehyde radical. If lactic acid is formed it will then be changed to pyruvic acid by the oxidation of the secondary alcohol radical. On further oxidation, the pyruvic acid molecule is split because of the ketone radical, carbon dioxide and acetaldehyde being formed. The latter is changed to acetic acid which on further oxidation forms carbonic acid and formic acid due to another splitting of the molecule. Formic acid is finally oxidized to carbonic acid. 

The ultimate oxidation products of glycerol are therefore carbon dioxide and water. The same series of products result from the oxidation of glucose. By a reversion of the process either glycerol or glucose can be formed from any one of the four first mentioned, glyceric aldehyde, pyruvic aldehyde, lactic acid or pyruvic acid (see Chapter XXIII).

During the oxidation of fatty acids another series of products is produced, the final ones again being carbon dioxide and water if the oxidation is complete. It is believed that in every case the point of attack in the molecule is the second carbon atom from the carboxyl radical. To designate this the prefix beta, represented as B, has been attached to the names of the products resulting from the oxidation. 

For example, if we assume caproic acid to undergo this change, the products formed will be represented by the formulas on page 306.

It will be noted that the changes occurring throughout this series of oxidations are those characteristic of a sec-

If fat metabolism is disturbed in any way either because of dietary conditions, such as low carbohydrate or salt free diets, or because of some pathological condition, as in diabetes, fatty acid oxidation does not go through to completion. The process stops with the production of 

B-hydroxy butyric acid and acetoacetic acid in varying amounts, the latter compound being in part converted into

acetone. These three compounds, called the "acetone bodies," represent the end products which the body must then eliminate. To protect itself against them the alkaline material of the body tissues and fluids is drawn on for the conversion of the acids into salts. A condition of acidosis then arises when the alkaline substances are lost more rapidly than they can be supplied to the body. 

Unsaturated glycerides can undergo a slow oxidation outside the body. Oils, like linseed oil, which contain a high percentage of the more unsaturated glycerides are very useful because of their ability to undergo this change. The product resulting from the oxidation is a thick gummy substance. Mixed with ground cork and spread on a fabric backing it is made into a floor covering, such as linoleum. 

If this gummy substance is spread in a thin film it becomes hard. Because of this behavior, linseed oil is called a drying oil. It helps paint to dry because the pigment becomes fixed in the hardened film formed by the oxidation. 

Oils containing olein also undergo slow oxidation but less readily than those containing the more unsaturated glycerides. A decomposition will gradually take place because of it, however, and the products formed have a disagreeable taste and odor. Oils become rancid more readily than fats for there is a greater possibility of the formation of decomposition products by either oxidation or hydrolysis because of the larger proportion of unsaturated glycerides. Among these products are free fatty acids. By adding N/10 sodium hydroxide to an alcohol solution containing a known volume of the oil the degree of the rancidity can be determined from the amount of the sodium hydroxide required to neutralize it.

SUMMARY 

Ester.-A compound related to an alcohol and an acid. As an alcohol is an organic base an ester is an organic salt.

Wax.-An ester related to a complex monohydric alcohol and a fatty acid.

Fat.-An ester related to glycerol and a fatty acid. It is some. times called the glyceryl ester of a fatty acid or a glyceride. 

Simple Glyceride.-A glyceride in which all three fatty acid radicals are alike.

Mixed Glyceride.-A glyceride in which two or more of the fatty acid radicals are unlike.

Unsaturated Fat.-A glyceride containing the radicals of unsaturated fatty acids.

Saturated Fat.-A glyceride containing the radicals of saturated fatty acids.

Oil.-A term applied to a mixture of glycerides which is liquid at ordinary temperatures. It contains a large proportion of unsaturated glycerides or of simple saturated glycerides.

Fat.-A term applied to a mixture of glycerides which is either a hard or a soft solid at ordinary temperature. It contains a large proportion of the more complex saturated glycerides. 

Fixed Oils.-Are true fats or glycerides. They are not vaporized.

Volatile or Essential Oils.—A mixture of compounds consisting of esters (not glycerides), aldehydes, alcohols or hydrocarbons. They can be vaporized. 

Emulsion. A suspension of finely divided fat globules in a liquid medium. The mixture is milky in appearance and in time the fat may separate from the emulsifying medium. 

Hydrogenation.-A chemical reaction between an unsaturated glyceride and hydrogen in which the glyceride becomes more and more saturated.

Hydrolysis. A chemical reaction with water as a result of which glycerol and fatty acids are formed from the glyceride. 

Saponification.-A chemical reaction between a glyceride and either sodium hydroxide or potassium hydroxide which results in the formation of glycerol and the sodium or potassium salts of the fatty acids.

Hard Soap. The sodium salt of the fatty acids related to fats. 

Soft Soap. The potassium salt of the fatty acids related to fats.

QUESTIONS 

1. Give an example of an ester and explain why it can be considered a salt. 

2. Give an example of a wax. Why is it classified as an ester? 

3. To what compounds does the term "essential oil" refer?

Give three examples. Why is the term oil applied to these compounds? 

4. If a flavoring extract is to be used in a food requiring cooking, why should it be added when the preparation is finished if it is possible? 

5. Give the chemical name for a mixed glyceride and for a simple glyceride which show the relation of each to its corresponding alcohol and acid. 

6. Olein is called an unsaturated glyceride whereas stearin is saturated. Explain the difference. 

7. Can a chemical name be given to a naturally-occurring fat? 

Give a reason for your answer. 

8. Why is the term "oil" used in some cases and the term "fat" in others when referring to these substances (question 

7)? Give an example of each. 

9. What reason can be given for the fact that butter melts much more quickly than suet although both are solid if the temperature is comparatively low? 

10. Compare the consistency of olive oil and suet and account for the difference. 

11. What characteristic of fat causes its presence in meat broth to be so apparent? 

12. How is the fat content of food or body tissue determined? 

What other substances might also be represented in the results obtained? What family name is used for these compounds? 

13. Upon what characteristic of fat does each of the following depend? (a) The removal of a grease spot with gasoline; (b) the removal of a grease spot with soap and water. 

14. Give an example of an emulsified fat which is used medicinally-one which is used as a food. Describe the condition of fat when in an emulsion. 

15. What chemical change occurs in fat when it undergoes digestion? What are the products? 

16. In the body, fat is formed by a process of neutralization. 

What is the other product? What compounds react together to form them? From what may these reacting compounds have come? 

17. When fat is used as a fuel in the body what chemical change does it undergo? What are the final products? 

18. About 10% of food fat is believed to be converted into glu-

cose before it is finally used as a fuel. How can this transformation be accounted for? 

19. Name the acetone bodies. Account for their formation as incomplete oxidation products of fat. 

20. To what is the disagreeable odor of overheated fat due? 

Account for its formation. 

21. Why is butter very undesirable to use for frying purposes? 

22. What is soap? From what is it made? What is the process called? 

23. Why does soap not form a lather readily in sea water? 

24. Is a "hot process" soap or a "cold process" soap more desirable as a toilet soap? Give a reason for your answer. 

25. Crisco is a hydrogenated fat. Explain what is meant. 

26. Why is linseed oil called a "drying oil"? Why is it useful in painting? 

27. Does a fat or an oil become rancid more readily? Give a reason for your answer. 

28. How could the rancidity of a fat be determined? 

29. Why is butter more disagreeable than many other fats when it becomes rancid?

EXPERIMENTS 

1. Solubility of Fats

Set up a series of test-tubes containing 10 cc. of the following liquids: Water-gasoline-benzine-carbon tetrachloride-ether -chloroform. To each add 1 drop of cottonseed oil. Shake and note whether the liquid remains clear and whether or not the oil remains visible as a layer on top of it. 

2. Emulsification of Fats

Set up a series of test-tubes containing 10 cc. of the following: 

Water-solution of washing soda-solution of soap-vinegarwhite of egg-solution of starch paste. To each add 2 cc. of cottonseed oil. Shake vigorously and note whether the liquid becomes clear or milky. Which form the best emulsions? How can it be accounted for in each case? 

3. Digestion of Fat 

To 20 cc. of milk add a little powdered blue litmus. Divide in two portions and to one add a little artificial pancreatic juice (pancreatin dissolved in a dilute solution of sodium carbonate),

If the two mixtures are not the same shade of blue make them so by adding a solution of sodium carbonate. Place both tubes in water which is at body temperature and from time to time watch for any change in color and odor. In which tube does it occur? 

To what is it due? Explain the change which has taken place. 

4.. Effect of Heat on Fat

Shake a small portion of fresh butter and of some other fresh fat in alcohol and test with litmus paper. Note the result in each case. Heat another portion of each fat until it smokesnote the odor in each case. To what is it due? Explain the change which has taken place. When the fat has cooled add some alcohol-stir thoroughly and test with litmus paper. Compare the result with that first obtained. If any difference is noted account for it. 

5. Saponification of Fat

Melt 15 grams of fat (equal weights of suet and cottonseed oil) in a large casserole or evaporating dish and add 50 cc. of strong sodium hydroxide (1:1). Boil, adding water when necessary until there is no further evidence of fat. Its presence can be seen by the appearance of fat globules on the surface of the mixture. 

As the saponification occurs the mixture will become thick and creamy. When it is complete add small amount (10 cc.) of a saturated solution of sodium chloride. The soap becomes insoluble and on cooling will form a layer on top. Remove and rinse thoroughly with water. When the salt is completely removed it will again form a creamy mixture on being heated with a very small amount of water. Then pour it into a mold to harden. 

Explain the change which has taken place. What other product was formed? What became of it? 

6. Action of Soap on Water 

(a) Dissolve a small amount of your soap in distilled water and shake. Does it form a lather? (b) Add a small amount of a solution of your soap to a dilute solution of calcium chloride and shake. Note that a precipitate forms instead of a lather. What is it? Continue adding soap solution until a lather will form. How does the amount required compare with that used in the preceding part of the experiment? 

Waters which are hard contain in solution salts of iron, magnesium or calcium. Why are they objectionable for cleaning processes?

7. Fatty Acids 

(a) Formation from soap:

Dissolve a piece of soap in water and to the hot solution add dilute sulphuric acid gradually until there is no further formation of a precipitate. This consists of the fatty acids which have been formed by the hydrolysis of the soap. What is the other product? 

What was the purpose of the sulphuric acid? Filter and rinse the fatty acids thoroughly to remove all traces of sulphuric acid. 

How can you be sure of its complete removal? (b) Characteristics of fatty acids. 

Describe the appearance, feeling, odor and taste. Are they soluble in water, alcohol, gasoline and ether? 

What is the effect on litmus?

To a small portion add a few cc. of a solution of sodium hydroxide and stir very thoroughly. Add some distilled water and shake thoroughly. Account for the result. 

What fat characteristics have you observed? 

What acid characteristics have you observed?

CHAPTER XXIII CARBOHYDRATES 

Composition. The name carbohydrate suggests the composition of the different members of the family to which it is given. As in the case of the other compounds previously discussed they contain carbon, hydrogen and oxygen. 

Unlike these compounds, however, the hydrogen and oxygen are in the proportion of 2 atoms to 1 atom as in water. 

Hydrate is used as the second part of the name to suggest this proportion rather than the idea that water is present as water of crystallization. Glucose, for example, should be represented by the formula CH12O6 and not Ce(H2O).. 

In sucrose or cane sugar (C12H22O11), another familiar carbohydrate, the same proportion occurs. Although the formula for starch ((C8H1005)n) represents the composition less accurately, the proportion between the hydrogen and oxygen is also apparent here. There are a few exceptions to this similarity in composition. 

For example, there is a carbohydrate, rhamnose (C&H12O5) in which the characteristic proportion between the hydrogen and oxygen does not occur. There are also a number of compounds which do not belong to the family of carbohydrates, although they do contain carbon, hydrogen and oxygen and the two latter elements are in the proportion of 2 atoms to 1 atom. This is true of the following familiar compounds: 

Formaldehyde CH₂O or HCHO 

Acetic acid 

Lactic acid

C₂H₂O₂ or CH,COOH 

C,H,O,or CH,CHOHCOOH Because of these exceptions and because it is not the num- 313

ber of each kind of atom but their relation to each other which determines whether or not the compound has carbohydrate characteristics, it is better to describe their composition more accurately. In every case carbohydrates are compounds called simple sugars or monosaccharides, such as glucose, or they are more complex compounds like sucrose and starch which are built from the simple sugars. The most important simple sugars are closely related to polyhydric alcohols having either five or six hydroxyl radicals as arabinol (C,H,(OH),) and sorbitol (C,H,(OH)) (see

Chapter XIX). In the simple sugar there is either an aldehyde radical in place of one primary alcohol radical or a ketone radical in place of one secondary alcohol radical. 

The aldehyde derivative of the polyhydric alcohols, of which glucose, galactose and arabinose are examples, are called aldose sugars or aldoses. Fructose, which contains a ketone radical, is called a ketose. The same molecular formula, C6H12O6, represents the composition of the three sugars glucose, fructose and galactose. The graphic formula shows whether the sugar is an aldose or a ketose. In those given below for glucose and for fructose note the aldehyde radical (CHO) represented in the formula for the former and the ketone radical (—C—) in the formula for the latter. 11 HHH HH H H-C OH OH OH OH OH H H H H Glucose

C H Fructose

OH OH OH OH OH Classification.

The simplest group of carbohydrates is

CARBOHYDRATES 

315 the one previously referred to as simple sugars or monosaccharides. It consists of all carbohydrates which are incapable of hydrolysis. The three most important members, glucose, fructose and galactose, are called hexoses because each contains six oxygen atoms. There are also other simple sugars containing less than six oxygen atoms as well as some containing a larger number. The pentoses, of which arabinose (CH10Os) and rhamnose or methyl pentose (C6H12O5) are examples, are the only other kind having any biological importance. These simple sugars are the units or building stones from which all the other carbohydrates are formed. Those which are formed through the union of two simple sugar molecules are called double sugars or disaccharides. The most important members of this group, sucrose, maltose and lactose have the same molecular formula C12H22O11. Still another group of carbohydrates is given the name of polysaccharide because the members belonging to it have been formed through the union of a larger number of simple sugar molecules. In representing them by the molecular formula (C&H1005) the number of simple sugar molecules entering into the formation of the polysaccharide is stated indefinitely by a letter. One reason for this is that there is still much uncertainty about the composition. If it were known exactly it would not be possible to use one formula for the entire group since different numbers of simple sugar molecules unite to form the different polysaccharides. Cellulose, starch, dextrin and glycogen are composed of glucose molecules. Pectin and agar-agar have been formed from galactose. Arabinose has entered into the composition of vegetable gums like gum-arabic. To make it possible for the simple sugar molecules to unite to form either a double sugar or a polysaccharide they must lose either hydrogen atoms or hydroxyl radicals. These combine to form water which is another product of the reaction. The simple sugar molecules can be

reformed by restoring the lost hydrogen atom and hydroxyl radical through a process of hydrolysis. The classification of carbohydrates can therefore be said to be based on their behavior during hydrolysis. 

1. Simple sugars or monosaccharides cannot undergo hydrolysis. 

2. Double sugars or disaccharides produce 2 simple sugar molecules as a result of hydrolysis. 

3. Polysaccharides produce many simple sugar molecules as a result of hydrolysis.

Occurrence.-Carbohydrates occur most widely in plant organisms. A few, however, are peculiar to animals. Lactose or milk sugar is manufactured by the mammary glands and is present in milk. As human milk contains a much larger proportion than cow's milk, formulas for modified milks call for the addition of carbohydrates in some form, dextri-maltose and lactose being used commonly. In this way the sugar content can be made to resemble the amount present in human milk. Glycogen is another carbohydrate manufactured by the animal body and stored in the liver and muscles. It is sometimes called animal starch, not because it resembles this carbohydrate in its characteristics but because it plays the same part for the animal that starch does for the plant. Each is the form in which a reserve supply can be stored. 

Sucrose is commonly called cane sugar but this name is misleading for it occurs not only in the sugar cane but also in the sugar maple, beet and other fruits and vegetables.

Its most important commercial sources, however, are the first three mentioned. The processes of manufacturing sugar include the extraction of the juice or sap which contains the dissolved sugar, the purification of this solution to remove other substances and finally the concentration of the solution by boiling and the crystallization of the sugar from the resulting syrup. Both brown sugar and granulated sugar are the same so far as the carbohydrate is

CARBOHYDRATES 

317 concerned. The brown color and characteristic pleasing flavor of the former are due to the presence of foreign substances which have not been completely removed. Because of the perfection of the manufacturing processes, molasses now contains practically no sucrose. The sugar present in it is glucose. As maple sap does not undergo processes of purification the syrup and sugar which are finally obtained still contain other substances which give the flavor for which these products are prized so highly. Maltose or malt sugar is present in sprouting grains. Its formation from the starch stored in the seed will be referred to later (see hydrolysis). Malted foods such as Grape Nuts and malted milk, also contain this sugar. Glucose is commonly called grape sugar because of its occurrence in considerable amounts in this fruit. It is also present in other fruits and in vegetables being frequently accompanied by fructose or fruit-sugar. Honey also contains a mixture of these two sugars. In the body carbohydrates are distributed by the blood in the form of glucose except in the portal circulation in which fructose and galactose are also present. The amount present at any given moment is kept low and uniform by a control which will be referred to later. 

Starch, the form in which a reserve supply of carbohydrate is stored in plants, is found principally in roots, tubers and seeds. Sometimes the fleshy part of a fruit, as the apple and banana, contain it in varying proportions. 

There is an interesting relation between the starch and sugar content of a fruit or vegetable which varies with its maturity. In many cases the younger it is the higher is its sugar content and at the same time there is little or no starch present. As it ripens there is a decrease in sugar with a corresponding increase in starch. Green peas and corn are more desirable when they are young because of the larger amount of sugar which they contain. As they grow older they lose their sweet taste and cause a sensation

of greater fullness when eaten. With bananas and apples on the other hand, the green fruit contains starch which decreases as the sugar increases when the fruit ripens. The framework of the plant consists of cellulose which becomes fibrous or woody as the plant grows older. Pectin, a carbohydrate which makes it possible for fruit juices to jelly is found in many fruits and certain vegetables, for example, the carrot.

Formation.-Reference has already been made to the ability of plants to take simple compounds for the building of others which are much more complex (see Chapters VII and XVI). For the manufacture of carbohydrates they. take carbon dioxide and water which they synthesize into glucose with free oxygen as a secondary product. It is believed that formaldehyde may be an early product of the synthesis and that it is in turn polymerized into glucose 

(see Chapter XX). Sunlight and the presence of chloro phyll, the green pigment in plants, are necessary for this process of photosynthesis (building by light) as it is called. 

Much energy is absorbed and stored up in the carbohydrate as potential energy. Later it is released when the carbohydrate is utilized as a fuel either in the body in the form of glucose or in the stove as wood (cellulose). If more glucose is manufactured than is necessary to meet the needs of the plant it is converted into starch by the combination of many glucose molecules.

NC6H12O6 > (CHıoO)n + NH,0 Animals must depend upon other living organisms, either plant or animal, for the materials from which they manufacture carbohydrates. The glucose molecules from which glycogen is built may come directly from food carbohydrates. Although the same equation as that given for the formation of starch is used to represent its synthesis it must be remembered that the letter N used in the gen-

CARBOHYDRATES 

319 eral formula for polysaccharides represents a different number in each case. Although starch and glycogen molecules are both composed of glucose molecules the number of the latter is not the same in the two cases. Glucose can be formed from fat. Reference has already been made to the fact that the oxidation products formed from glycerol are the same as those formed from glucose (see Chapter XXII). If the oxidation has not progressed beyond the stage at which pyruvic acid has been formed either it or the products preceding it, lactic acid, pyruvic aldehyde or glyceric aldehyde, can be converted into carbohydrates instead of undergoing further oxidation. Protein, too, can be changed into carbohydrate (see Chapter XXIV). In a condition of either pathological or experimental diabetes there is direct proof that the body is manufacturing glucose from some compound other than carbohydrates. Although the polysaccharides and sugars in the diet must be restricted because of the body's inability to utilize these compounds in a condition of diabetes, glucose may continue to be elimininated in the urine in amounts that cannot be accounted for from the food eaten. Experiments have shown that approximately 10% of food fat and 58% of food protein can contribute to carbohydrate formation. Therefore, these compounds as well as carbohydrates themselves must be considered when calculating a diabetic diet. 

Properties and Uses.-The simple sugars and double sugars dissolve in water to form solutions which are transparent and capable of dialysis. Certain polysaccharides, as the vegetable gums, pectin, dextrin and starch paste form a collodial solution which is transluscent and opalescent in appearance and cannot be dialized. Raw starch and cellulose are both insoluble in water. Were it not for the insolubility of cellulose which makes up the cell wall framework of plants, the latter would rot very much more quickly than they do because of contact with moisture. Those

polysaccharides which form collodial solutions in water are insoluble in alcohol. This characteristic is made use of in determining the fitness of fruit juices for jelly making. The pectin on which the jellying depends is normally present in the juice obtained from the fruit after cooking. It is made visible by the addition of alcohol to a sample of it for it is then precipitated. The amount of jelly-like precipitate obtained can be used as a measure of the pectin content.

When a colloidal solution evaporates a non-crystalline, gummy solid is left. This is characteristic of the polysaccharides, a familiar example being starch paste. As it dries it becomes sticky and thick but there are no crystals. The sugars on the other hand are crystalline in structure. The readiness with which they crystallize and the size of the crystals differ with the sugar. Sucrose crystallizes the most quickly and forms the largest crystals. The formation of its crystals is very desirable for certain products such as rock candy. Strings are suspended in a heavy syrup and allowed to stand undisturbed. Because of the tendency of the crystals to deposit on any surface touching the syrup, they gradually form on the string and increase in size. Sometimes methods are employed to prevent the formation of sucrose crystals as they would cause the product to be coarse and grainy in texture. For example, when making frosting or candy, conditions are provided which will enable much of the sucrose to be converted into the two simple sugars, glucose and fructose, of which it is composed. As these crystallize very much more slowly forming finer crystals, the product will be smooth and velvety (see hydrolysis of carbohydrates).

The characteristic of sweetness as well as the solubility and ability to crystallize make a dividing line between the polysaccharides on one hand and the disaccharides and monosaccharides on the other. The two latter groups are

CARBOHYDRATES 

321 spoken of as sugars as their members possess sweetness in varying degrees. Sucrose is the sweetest of them all. In many cases it is used for this quality rather than for any food value it has. Therefore, care must be taken not to use it in a way that would make possible its change into glucose and fructose which are less sweet (see hydrolysis). The coal-tar product saccharin (CHCOSO₂NH) is often confused with sucrose or saccharose as the latter is also called. 

The two are very different substances both from the standpoint of their composition and from their function. Saccharin is a nitrogen-containing compound which cannot be used by the body either as building material or as a source of energy. Its usefulness depends upon the fact that in very minute quantities it will sweeten any mixture to which it is added. Therefore, various foods can be made more palatable for patients who are not allowed much carbohydrate. Sucrose on the other hand is a carbohydrate.

It not only increases the palatability of foods because of its sweetness but it also increases their energy value. The fact that the sweetness of lactose and maltose are barely perceptible is important. If it were more perceptible, milk would be too cloying to be satisfying as a beverage. It would also be impossible to increase the carbohydrate content of cow's milk by using dextri-maltose or lactose when making up modified milk. Lactose is sometimes added to foods in considerable quantities during their preparation in order to increase the caloric value of the diet. This could not be done if it had a pronounced taste. 

The light coming from a luminous body or a luminous flame is due to ether waves which ordinarily vibrate in all directions. It is possible, however, to cause them to travel in only one plane by making them pass through a Nichol prism. Such light is said to be polarized. Solutions of certain substances have the ability of diverting polarized light from its course by bending the rays to the right or the left.

All water-soluble carbohydrates possess this characteristic of optical activity as it is called. With the exception of fructose they are dextro-rotatory since the rays of light are bent to the right when they travel through each solution. 

Glucose has been called dextrose for this reason. Fructose was also named levulose because it is levo-rotatory, bending the rays to the left. By means of an instrument called the polariscope it is possible to measure the degree polarized light is affected by the solution through which it is passed. 

This is made the basis of a quantitative determination of the dissolved substance present. The glucose content of urine can be measured in this way after the specimen has been treated with some substance, such as basic lead acetate, which will precipitate out any other compounds which might also possess optical activity.

Three of the polysaccharides, starch, glycogen and one of the dextrins called erythrodextrin, form a colored product on the addition of a few drops of an iodine solution. 

According to one interpretation a combination occurs between the iodine and the polysaccharide which results in the formation of blue starch iodide, red dextrin iodide or red glycogen iodide. Because of the readiness with which each product can be detected even when it is present in traces iodine is commonly used as a test for starch. The blue spot appearing when iodine is dropped on linen or cotton garments or sheets is due to the starch present as sizing or as a result of their being laundered. The use of boiled starch as an antidote for iodine poisoning also depends upon this reaction. By staining sections of tissue with iodine the presence of glycogen in them can be detected during their microscopic examination. Experimentally iodine can be used to show the gradual change which occurs when starch undergoes hydrolysis (see hydrolysis). 

Another important reaction which all the sugars except sucrose can undergo is with a solution containing in it a dissolved metallic hydroxide or a metallic oxide. The fol-

The dissolved metallic hydroxide or oxide acts as a mild oxidizing agent and carbohydrates can be readily oxidized by them if they contain either a free aldehyde or a free ketone radical. Not only the three simple sugars glucose, fructose and galactose respond to these reagents but also two double sugars maltose and lactose. CARBOHYDRATES 

325 Many uses are made of these reactions. The presence of sugar in urine can be detected by heating a sample of the specimen with Benedict-Fehling's solution. A precipitate will form whose yellow or brick-red color can be readily seen when it settles out. Under certain conditions, as in the latter stages of pregnancy or shortly after parturition, this sugar may be lactose. To determine whether it is glucose or not other tests must be made, one commonly used being a fermentation by yeast (see fermentation).

If uric acid is present in sufficient amounts in the sample being tested it may also cause the formation of a precipitate which will give a cloudy, turbid appearance. This may prove confusing and misleading. This precipitate is colorless, however, whereas the one due to glucose is yellow or red. Blood sugar determinations can be made with the same reagent, the tests being so adapted as to make the result quantitative as well as qualitative. The fact that polysaccharides or sucrose have undergone chemical changes can be proved by testing them with one of these reagents, for the sugars which can be formed from them belong to the group of reducing sugars.

On hydrolysis all carbohydrates will produce as end products the simple sugar molecules of which they are composed. When this point is reached no further hydrolysis can take place since the simple sugars themselves are incapable of undergoing this change. As has already been stated each double sugar molecule yields two simple sugar molecules. 

The changes in each case are represented as follows:

The end product formed during the hydrolysis of sucrose is sometimes called invert sugar and the process inversion.

These names are significant because of the optical activity of the sugars. Sucrose is dextro-rotatory. Glucose also

bends rays of polarized light to the right but fructose, which has greater optical activity than glucose, bends them to the left. Therefore, the effect of a solution containing glucose and fructose in equal amounts will be the reverse of the effect of a solution containing sucrose. In other words the sucrose has been inverted. 

On complete hydrolysis polysaccharides will break down into the many simple sugar molecules of which each is composed. The change takes place in stages, however, and a number of intermediate products are formed. Starch, for example, is first hydrolized to a somewhat simpler compound called soluble starch, which belongs to the polysaccharide family. On further hydrolysis dextrin and maltose are formed. The dextrin becomes changed to maltose and finally the maltose is converted into glucose. In the following series of equations the change from starch to soluble starch is omitted. 

3 (C6H1005)N + NHOH (starch) 

2 (C6H1005)N+NHOH (dextrin)

C12H22O11+ HOH (maltose) (C6H1005)N + NC12H22011 (dextrin) (maltose)

NC12H22011 (maltose)

C6H12O6+ C6H1206 (glucose) (glucose)

The polysaccharide glycogen can also undergo hydrolysis with maltose an intermediate product and glucose the final product. Although cellulose cannot undergo hydrolysis under conditions in the body, it is possible to bring it about experimentally. The end product is glucose. Because the body cannot hydrolize cellulose this compound which is both insoluble and incapable of dialysis cannot pass from the digestive system into the blood to be distributed to body cells as a fuel. It is useful to the body, however, as it contributes bulk to the contents of the alimentary tract and so helps to stimulate peristalsis. This is one reason why a diet in which there is considerable cellulose helps to prevent a condition of constipation.

CARBOHYDRATES 

327 The conditions favorable to the hydrolysis of carbohydrates are similar to those mentioned for fats. Long continued contact with moisture makes the change take place although it occurs very slowly. If starch paste is kept for some time it can be proved that small amounts of starch are being changed. A positive test with Benedict 

Fehling's solution can be obtained due to the maltose and possibly glucose which are products of the hydrolysis. The reaction can be made to take place with greater rapidity if heat is applied. There is enough natural moisture in bread or flour to enable a slight hydrolysis to occur when making toast or browning flour. If it has been very thoroughly heated, there may be a noticeable difference in the thickening ability of the browned flour, for dextrin, the principal product of the hydrolysis in this case, does not swell when water is added to it as starch does. The length of time bread is heated when making toast is much less than when flour is browned. While some dextrin is formed in this case it is for the most part a surface change and there is still a relatively large amount of starch present. The desirability of toast is due to its dryness and the consequent necessity for more thorough mastication rather than to the fact that it has been hydrolized to any extent. If an acid is present in the carbohydrate mixture being heated, the chances for hydrolysis are even better. For this reason a substance like cream of tartar, which behaves like an acid when in solution, is added to a sugar syrup when it is cooked. This permits a larger amount of the sucrose to be changed to glucose and fructose. Such a change is desirable if the product is to be used for candy or icing for the crystals of the simple sugar products form more slowly and are much finer than those of sucrose. Sucrose which is to be used to sweeten a fruit sauce should not be added before the fruit is cooked especially if it contains much acid as in the case of sour apples or plums. As glucose and fructose

are less sweet than sucrose, a greater amount of the latter would need to be used to produce the desired result. There are also other objections to this method, for example, the greater danger of burning and the darkening of the sauce. When cornstarch is used to thicken an acid-containing liquid unless the acid is added when the cooking is nearly completed the mixture is thinner than would naturally be expected. This is due to the hydrolysis of some of the starch into products which cannot thicken the liquid. The unexpected failures when making the lemon filling for a pie can thus be accounted for. The manufacture of commercial glucose and corn syrup depends upon hydrolysis. The starch from corn is generally used and the reaction is aided by an acid which is removed afterwards by processes of purification. The product consists of a mixture of glucose, maltose and dextrin.

Within the body and elsewhere carbohydrates undergo hydrolytic changes aided by enzymes. Each enzyme bears a name which suggests the carbohydrate whose hydrolysis it helps. The following table includes these enzymes, the carbohydrates which can be hydrolized and the products which are formed.

The bulk of the carbohydrate eaten is in the form of starch

CARBOHYDRATES 

329 and sucrose and to a less extent lactose. Before these can pass from the digestive system into the blood stream they must be changed into simple sugars which are readily dialyzed. The presence in the digestive juices of the enzymes previously mentioned makes possible the necessary hydrolysis. Another example of a hydrolytic change occurring in the body is the conversion of liver and muscle glycogen into glucose, the change being aided by the enzyme glycogenase. It is also under the control of substances called autacoids which are supplied by the internal secretions of the adrenal glands and the pancreas. From the former is sent a hormone called adrenalin which stimulates the formation of glucose from glycogen. The pancreas, on the other hand, sends a chalone which acts as a check on the hydrolysis in case a larger amount of glucose is being formed and turned into the blood than the body cells can utilize. A condition called hyperglycemia arises when the blood sugar content is increased above the usual amount. 

Normally, the kidneys do not excrete glucose but when the blood sugar content becomes too great the kidney cells become permeable to it and a condition of glycosuria results. A condition called hypoglycemia occurs when the blood sugar content is below normal.

Carbohydrates reach the body cells in the form of glucose. 

Here oxidation reactions take place which result in the release of energy and the formation of carbon dioxide and water as end products. As has already been stated the intermediate products correspond to those resulting from the oxidation of glycerol. Probably the glucose molecule is first split into two simple molecules which may be either glyceric aldehyde or pyruvic aldehyde. If the former product is formed, it is converted into pyruvic aldehyde and the remainder of the oxidation follows the course already described for glycerol (see Chapter XXII).

An enzyme called an oxidase which aids oxidizing reactions is a constituent of body cells. The pancreas also manufactures a substance which we now call insulin whose presence in a body cell is essential to its ability to utilize glucose as a fuel. If anything interferes with the formation of this substance carbohydrate metabolism becomes affected and the kidneys are forced to excrete the unused glucose. It is now possible to obtain insulin from the pancreas of animals. By giving it in measured amounts called "units" the patient's tolerance for carbohydrates is raised so that a less restricted diet can be used. Since the proportion of carbohydrate affects the metabolism of fat the use of insulin is a great advantage. It makes it possible to keep the carbohydrate content of a diabetic diet higher than would be possible otherwise. Conditions of acidosis due to the formation of Beta-hydroxybutyric acid and acetoacetic acid are therefore less likely to occur. Sometimes a condition called "sugar shock" is caused by too large a dose of insulin. In this case the body has been made able to oxidize such a large amount of glucose that its own reserve is withdrawn too suddenly. Sugar is then fed to the patient to readjust the condition.

Although both fats and carbohydrates are fuel foods they are not equally useful in this respect. Fats can release 

9 calories of heat per gram whereas the same weight of

CARBOHYDRATES 

331 carbohydrate can release only 4 calories. A comparison of the formulas of stearin (CH, (C17H35COO)3) and glucose (C&H12O6) shows at once the reason for this difference in the caloric value. Although both contain oxygen there is a much greater proportion present in glucose. In other words, it is already oxidized to a greater extent than fat is and so there is less chance for it to combine with additional oxygen. As it is not so complex a compound as fat the series of products formed during its oxidation are very much fewer and the process can therefore be completed in a much shorter time. This accounts for the fact that carbohydrates are a quicker source of energy than fats. For this reason sugar is sometimes used when there is a sudden demand for an unusual amount of energy.

The major part of the oxidation of carbohydrates occurs in the muscle cells. If they do not need the entire amount of glucose which is being brought to them by the blood, to a limited extent they can convert the excess into glycogen and store it. Any further excess of glucose is converted into fat and stored as adipose tissue. Both glycerol and fatty acids which are necessary to the formation of fat can be made from glucose. In time of need these two reserves, glycogen and adipose tissue, can be drawn on to supply the body's energy requirements.

Carbohydrates can undergo a number of different types of changes which are referred to by the general term fermentation. This word, meaning "to boil," was applied originally only to those changes which resulted in gas production. In a larger sense it can be used with reference to changes brought about by the presence of bacteria, yeasts or molds. The formation of carbon dioxide and ethyl alcohol by a yeast fermentation has already been referred to 

(see Chapter XIX). The simple sugars, glucose, fructose and galactose, are decomposed into these two compounds together with smaller amounts of glycerol, succinic acid

and a mixture of propyl alcohol and amyl alcohol called fusel oil. The enzyme, zymase, which is manufactured by the yeast cell is responsible for the change. The equation accounts only for the production of the carbon dioxide and ethyl alcohol as they are the principal products. C6H12O6 aided by zymase 2 CO₂+ 2 C2H5OH The small amounts of fusel oil which are formed cause a very unpleasant, sharp taste and may produce poisonous effects. During the aging process the compounds of which it consists undergo changes which result in the formation of compounds having desirable odors and flavors. Aged liquors are more valuable than those which are raw for this reason.

Since yeast cells can also manufacture sucrase and maltase, solutions containing either sucrose or maltose can undergo a yeast fermentation. The preliminary step in each case is a hydrolysis and the resulting simple sugars are then decomposed by the process previously described. 

Yeast cells do not make the enzyme lactase. Lactose, therefore, cannot produce carbon dioxide and alcohol unless this enzyme, which is necessary for the preliminary hydrolysis, can be introduced in some other way. C12H22O11+ HOH

In order to produce ethyl alcohol and carbon dioxide from a starch-containing mixture the enzyme amylase, which aids the hydrolysis of the starch into maltose, must be supplied from some other source than the yeast since this organism does not manufacture it. In the manufacture of alcohol an extract obtained from sprouting grains is used as the enzyme is present in larger amounts than usual in seeds when they are germinating. The leavening of bread dough depends upon a yeast fermentation. The changes tak-

As flour is the ground wheat seed it naturally contains small amounts of amylase or diastase as it is also called. The hydrolysis of the starch of the flour is, therefore, possible. The maltose which is formed can undergo further hydrolysis because of the enzyme maltase which the yeast can supply. From the end product, glucose, both carbon dioxide and ethyl alcohol will be formed through the help of the enzyme zymase also supplied by the yeast. The fact that lactose may be present in urine has already been referred to. If a urine specimen shows a positive reaction with Benedict-Fehling's solution, it is sometimes necessary to find out if it is caused by the lactose or if glucose is also present. This can be shown by adding yeast to a sample of the specimen and watching for the production of carbon dioxide. If only lactose is present no yeast fermentation will occur. The appearance of bubbles of a gas, which on being tested with limewater proves to be carbon dioxide, is an evidence that glucose was present in the urine. 

Another important and familiar fermentation to which carbohydrates are subject is the lactic acid fermentation.

Although it is commonly associated with milk carbohydrates

other than milk sugar can be changed into lactic acid. if the proper types of bacteria are present. If the carbohydrate is not a simple sugar, the first change to occur is a hydrolysis. The simple sugar products are then converted into lactic acid. It is quite possible that intermediate products may be formed which resemble those produced during the formation of lactic acid as a product of incomplete oxidation in the body. Assuming lactose to be the carbohydrate, the following equations represent the changes involved: (C12H22O11+ HOH 

Lactose + Water (C6H12O6 Glucose 2 C3H6O3 

Lactic acid

C6H12O6+ C6H12O6 

Glucose + Galactose

The natural souring of milk is due to this change. In the preparation of lactic acid milk, pure cultures of the desired type of bacteria are used to inoculate the milk and the resulting fermentation is carefully controlled to obtain definite proportions of the acid. In the preparation of sauerkraut, dill pickles and pickled string beans, the vegetable is covered with a salt brine and put under pressure. 

The vegetable juices, which contain the dissolved carbohydrates, are drawn out and the sugars present undergo a lactic acid fermentation.

Carbohydrates will undergo fermentation in the body. 

When a condition of hypoacidity occurs in the stomach a gastric fermentation takes place to which sucrose and glucose are especially susceptible and lactose very much less so. 

As a result, acids, gases and in some cases alcohol are produced. These products are often the cause of great discomfort. B. coli communis, a type of bacteria normally present in the large intestine, can cause fermentation to occur in either proteins or carbohydrates. If both kinds of compounds are present, the latter are more apt to be affected. As lactic acid, the product of the fermentation

CARBOHYDRATES 

335 is less objectionable than the products resulting from the protein putrefaction, the use of carbohydrate foods in larger amounts is one means by which the decomposition of the proteins is prevented (see Chapter XXIV). Because of its acidity lactic acid also checks the protein putrefaction which cannot occur in an acid medium. For the same reason lactic acid milks are used. They also serve to change the intestinal flora by introducing different types of bacteria. 

SUMMARY 

Carbohydrate.-A compound composed of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen, the last two elements being in the proportion of 

2 atoms of hydrogen and 1 atom of oxygen as in water.

A compound which is a simple sugar or which on hydrolysis will produce simple sugars. A compound which is either the aldehyde or the ketone derivative of a polyhydric alcohol.

Aldose Sugar-A simple sugar which contains an aldehyde radical. 

Ketose Sugar.-A simple sugar which contains a ketone radical. 

Simple Sugar or Monosaccharide.-A carbohydrate which cannot undergo hydrolysis. 

Hexose. A simple sugar having six oxygen atoms. 

Pentose. A simple sugar having five oxygen atoms. 

Double Sugar or Disaccharide.-A carbohydrate which has been formed by the union of two simple sugar molecules. A carbohydrate which will yield two simple sugar molecules when it undergoes hydrolysis.

Polysaccharides.-Carbohydrates which have been formed by the union of a large number of simple sugar molecules.

Reducing Sugars.-Those sugars which can be oxidized by a mild oxidizing agent such as Fehling's solution. They are: glucose, fructose, galactose, maltose, lactose. 

Autacoid.-Substances manufactured by different glands and turned directly into the blood. Carried by it to other cells. they either stimulate or retard the activities being carried on. Stimulating autacoids hormones. Retarding auta chalones. coids: 

Hyperglycemia.-A condition in which the blood sugar content is above normal.

Hypoglycemia.-A condition in which the blood sugar content is below normal. 

Fermentation.-A term applied to chemical changes which are aided by bacteria, yeasts or molds.

Glycosuria.-A condition in which glucose is present in the urine.
