EXPERIMENTS 1. Detection of Reducing Sugars 

Place in each of three test-tubes 5 cc. of Benedict-Fehling's solution. To one add a few drops of a sucrose solution, to the second the same amount of lactose solution and to the third glucose solution and heat each to boiling. Which sugars produce a yellow or brick red precipitate? These are reducing sugars. Which one is not a reducing sugar? 

2. Detection of Starch 

Add a drop of iodine solution to a small amount of starch paste. Note the result. It is due to starch iodide. Warm the solution. Result? Should the starch test be applied to a cold or a hot solution? 

3. Hydrolysis of Carbohydrate-Aided by Acids 

(a) Dissolve about 1/2 teaspoon of sucrose in 50 cc. of water and add either a few drops of dilute hydrochloric acid or a very small pinch of cream of tartar. Boil 10-15 minutes. Neutralize with a solution of sodium carbonate. When the acid is completely neutralized there will be no further effervescence. To 

3 cc. of the neutralized solution add 5 cc. of Benedict-Fehling's solution and heat to boiling. Result? Compare it with the result previously obtained with sucrose and explain. (b) To about 25 cc. of thin starch paste add 1 cc. of dilute hydrochloric acid and boil. From time to time test small portions with a drop of iodine. Result? Dextrin forms a red compound with iodine. Have you any evidence that dextrin is present? What? Continue boiling until the solution shows no color change with iodine. When this point is reached neutralize with a solution of sodium carbonate and test a small portion of the solution with Benedict-Fehling's solution? Result. To what may it be due? 

4. Hydrolysis of Carbohydrate-Aided by Enzymes 

To 50 cc. of starch paste thin enough to pour add 5-10 cc. of saliva. Stir thoroughly. Place 5 cc. of the mixture in each of 

10 test-tubes. Place in a beaker of water and keep as near body temperature (37° C.) as possible. At the end of each of the following time intervals remove two tubes. With the contents of one try the iodine test. Test the contents of the other with Benedict-Fehling's solution. Record the result in each case. 

What evidences have you obtained that the starch is undergoing

CARBOHYDRATES 

339 hydrolysis. What products might be formed? (Time intervals: 1 min.-5 min.-10 min.-15 min.-30 min.) 

5. Yeast Fermentation 

Mix some yeast about the size of a pea with enough glucose solution to fill a fermentation tube. Repeat, using solutions of sucrose and lactose. Place the tubes in a warm place and examine from time to time for gas bubbles. The production of carbon dioxide is an evidence that yeast fermentation has occurred. In which tube does it occur most readily? In which tube does no fermentation occur?

CHAPTER XXIV PROTEINS 

Composition.-Another group of compounds of biological importance has been given the name protein, meaning "to take first place," because they are our only source of available nitrogen and sulphur. In addition to the elements carbon, hydrogen and oxygen, which are present in fats and carbohydrates as well, all proteins contain nitrogen and most of them also contain sulphur. In a number, phosphorus or iron is present in addition to the elements previously mentioned. The proportion of these elements of course varies in the different kinds of proteins. For nitrogen the variation is approximately 15-18%, the average usually being represented as 16%. The determination of the protein content of different materials, foods for example, is based on the assumption of this as the average proportion of nitrogen. The actual weight of this element present in a known amount of the food in question can be found by causing it to be changed into ammonia and then determining the amount of this compound by titration. If 16% represents the proportion of nitrogen present in the protein, the entire amount of protein or 100% is 6.25 times as much.

Therefore, by multiplying the weight of nitrogen found for the sample experimented with by 6.25 the weight of protein present in the sample will be found. Since the weight of the sample is known the proportion can then be determined in terms of percent. In tables giving the percentage composition of different materials the column containing the figures for protein is usually headed nitrogen multiplied by 6.25. 340

PROTEINS 

341 Amino acids, which were mentioned as one type of organic acid (see Chapter XXI), are called the building stones of proteins. Two characteristic radicals, the amino radical (NH2) and the carboxyl radical (COOH) are common to these acids. The latter, which is present in all organic acids, is responsible for the typical acid characteristics which they possess. Because of the amino radical through which they are related to ammonia they also have basic characteristics. Therefore, they can combine with either acids or bases. They can also combine with each other, one molecule playing the part of a base and another molecule acting as an acid. The following equation represents the combination of two molecules of glycine, the simplest amino acid related to proteins. CH,NH(H)COOH (glycine) +

CH₂NH2CO(OH) 

(glycine)

HOH + 

CH₂NHCOOH CH2NH2CO 

(glycyl glycine)

When a large number of amino acids have been combined the complex molecule analagous to glycyl glycine is a protein. Because water is also a product of the reaction, proteins are called the anhydrides of amino acids. At least eighteen different amino acids which are related to proteins have been recognized. The following list includes a few examples of the more common ones. 

Amino acid 

Glycine or amino-acetic acid

Alanine or amino-propionic acid 

Phenylalanine or 6-phenyl a-amino propionic acid 

Tyrosine or 6-hydroxyphenyl a-amino propionic acid 

Tryptophane or 6-indol a-amino proprionic acid

Formula 

CH₂NH2COOH CH.CHNH2COOH C&H&CH₂CHNH2COOH C&H.OHCH₂CHNH2COOH C&H&NCH₂CHNH2COOH 

Cysteine or 6-thio a-amino propionic acid CH₂SHCHNH2COOH Although the composition of protein molecules is not

known with sufficient accuracy to make it possible to represent many of them by formulas, the results from quantitative studies have shown their molecular weights to be very large, many of them averaging 16,000. From studies of casein it has been estimated that its molecule must be composed of over one hundred amino acid molecules of which there are at least fifteen different kinds. Therefore, it can readily be seen that protein formulas would be too complicated to use commonly even though it were possible to write them.

Comparison of Proteins and Carbohydrates.-Proteins resemble carbohydrates in certain respects. Both contain carbon, hydrogen and oxygen. Both are anhydrides of simpler molecules which will again be formed when the carbohydrate or the protein undergoes hydrolysis. There are also a number of important differences. The additional elements, nitrogen, sulphur, phosphorus and iron contained in proteins make it possible for these compounds to supply the body with building material as well as energy. Even though proteins are compared with a complex carbohydrate like starch, the number of simple molecules of which the latter is composed is relatively small when contrasted with the number contained in a protein. In the case of starch, too, these simple molecules are all of the same kind. No carbohydrate contains more than three or four different kinds of simple sugars in its molecule, whereas not only the number of amino acid molecules making up a protein is large but there are many kinds present. Still another difference between carbohydrates and proteins is, that in the case of the former, the simple sugars, of which the more complex carbohydrates are the anhydrides, are themselves carbohydrates. Amino acids, on the other hand, are not proteins. 

Classification.-Because of the large number of amino acids which can enter into the composition of proteins there

PROTEINS 

343 are many different combinations possible and, therefore, a large variety of proteins. The entire family is subdivided. into three main groups. All proteins which are composed only of amino acid molecules are gathered together into a group called simple proteins. Since they are composed only of amino acids, on hydrolysis they will yield only amino acids or other compounds resulting from their decomposition. There are many simple proteins which differ considerably in their composition and characteristics. These are again divided into smaller groups, the classification being based mainly upon a difference in their solubility (see 

Summary). To another of the three main groups of proteins the name conjugated protein is given, the idea suggested by the word conjugated being that these compounds have some other kind of molecule joined with the amino acids. In other words, conjugated proteins are composed of amino acids combined with some other kind of compound. Therefore, on hydrolysis they will produce both amino acids and the other compound. There are a number of different compounds which can be combined with amino acids to form conjugated proteins. This group, therefore, is subdivided into smaller groups on this basis. When either simple or conjugated proteins undergo hydrolysis, a number of intermediate products which are still proteins but less complex than the original ones will be formed before the amino acids are produced. These form the third main division which is called the group of derived proteins. The significance of the word derived, meaning "to come from,” is that these compounds have come from other proteins as a result of hydrolysis. The group is again separated into two divisions according to the extent of the hydrolysis. The products resulting from a very slight hydrolysis are called primary derived proteins. Those in which more profound changes have occurred because the hydrolysis has been more extensive are called secondary derived proteins, the peptids

being the simplest products of hydrolysis which still show protein characteristics. A more detailed classification and common examples of the different kinds of proteins will be found in the summary.

Occurrence.-Proteins are found in nature only in living organisms or as products of their life activities. While it has been found possible to manufacture artificially polypeptids composed of a number of amino acid molecules, the manufacture of the very complex protein molecules is as yet an unsolved problem. These compounds are the essential constituents of all tissues, both plant and animal. In many fruits and vegetables, however, the proportion is so small as compared with the carbohydrate constituents that they contribute little to the protein content of the diet. They can be freely used, therefore, in diets which must be low in protein. 

Peas and beans, however, have a large enough amount of protein present to be classified as protein foods. As animal tissues contain a larger proportion such foods as meat and fish are used essentially for the protein content. Eggs, cheese and milk are also valuable protein foods.

Formation. The ability of plants to make use of very simple substances for the construction of proteins has already been referred to (see Chapter VII). Carbon dioxide and water contribute carbon, hydrogen and oxygen. 

Nitrates, ammonium compounds and free nitrogen supply nitrogen. Sulphates and phosphates furnish sulphur and phosphorus. From these different compounds the plant cells gather together the necessary atoms and reassemble them into plant proteins. With the increased specialization developed by animals they have lost the ability to make use of such simple compounds. For their protein materials they depend upon what has already been built up by some other plant or animal organism so that there is of necessity a very close relation and interdependence between the two types of life. For example, the clover plant takes in the

PROTEINS 

345 simple substances previously mentioned which it builds into clover proteins. The cow comes along, eats the clover and, after the digestion of its proteins into amino acids and the absorption of the latter, rearranges them into beef proteins or milk proteins. We eat the beef or drink the milk and the process is repeated. The amino acid digestion products of these proteins are absorbed and distributed by the blood to the body cells needing building material. Here they are rebuilt into the particular kind of protein which is required.

The animal body can manufacture certain amino acids from other material than that furnished by protein. Pyruvic acid, for example, an intermediate oxidation product of both glucose and the glycerol part of fat, will combine with ammonia (NH,) the product being ultimately the amino acid, alanine. However, proteins are to a certain extent essential to the amino acids which can be built in this way for they are the only source of the necessary ammonia. Because of the ability of the body to utilize fats and carbohydrates for the construction of proteins, these compounds are called protein sparers. Carbohydrates are more efficient in this respect than fats for the glycerol part of the latter represents a comparatively small part of its molecules. There is another way in which the term proteinsparing is commonly used. Proteins can be used as fuel foods, and if the supply of fat and carbohydrate is inadequate, they must function in this way in order to meet the energy requirement of the body. Carbohydrates and fats, therefore, prevent proteins from being used in this way, or, in other words, they spare them for their more important work as tissue builders. 

The amino acids cysteine, lysine, and tryptophane must be supplied as such from other proteins for the body cannot use fats or carbohydrates as a source of material for their construction. Although tyrosine can be built by the body,

the amount is entirely inadequate to its needs. Therefore, food proteins must also supply it. If any one of these four amino acids is absent from a protein or the amount is deficient, the protein in question is unable to keep up the normal repair of body tissue, or to supply material for the new tissues being built during growth or pregnancy. Feeding experiments in which isolated and purified proteins from different kinds of food were used produced very marked results in the rate of growth of the animal undergoing the experiment and its ability to carry on the process of reproduction. It was found that by supplying from some other protein the amino acids which were deficient or lacking, the rate of growth or process of reproduction again became normal. Proteins have been classified according to their ability to function as tissue builders. 

All proteins, whose amino acid content is such that it can not only provide material for the maintenance and repair of the body tissues but also for new tissues during growth and reproduction, are called complete proteins. Partially complete protein can supply material for repair but not for growth and reproduction. Proteins are incomplete if they can neither keep the tissues in repair nor build new tissues. 

Casein is an example of a complete protein. As its cysteine content is low, however, milk should be used freely. Gelatine is an example of an incomplete protein. Ordinarily, a food material contains a number of different proteins so that the deficiencies of one are made good by an abundance in another. The proper balance is helped still further by the fact that our diet usually consists of a mixture of different kinds of foods.

Properties and Uses.-The solubility of proteins often has a very great influence on the methods used in dealing with mixtures containing them. As the classification suggests, this characteristic shows many variations. Some, as egg albumin, lactalbumin and serum albumin, are readily

PROTEINS 

347 soluble in cold water. Others of which serum globulin and muscle globulin or myosin are examples are soluble only if a very small amount of salt is present in the water. The fact that the blood carries both serum albumin and serum globulin in solution is due to the presence of sodium chloride which is also one of its normal constituents. If, when poaching eggs, they were put first into cold or lukewarm water the egg white would have an opportunity to dissolve and become distributed through the water. Later, on becoming cooked, it would be in many small pieces and a very unattractive looking product would result. Gliadin and glutenin, two proteins in wheat to which the name gluten is given, are insoluble in water. The gluten can be removed from wheat flour by washing out the starch. If it were soluble, this method would be impossible. Most proteins are insoluble in hot water even though they may be soluble if it is cold. Gelatine, however, is an exception for in cold water it only swells and to dissolve it boiling water must be used. The proteins which make up the external protective tissues, as the skin, nails and hair, are characterized by their marked insolubility. This quality makes it possible for them to be in long continued contact with water without any danger of loss from solution. Because of their very large molecules, the water-soluble proteins form colloidal solutions, two characteristics of which are translucence and an inability to dialyze. Therefore, the blood proteins are unable to pass through the kidney cell walls and become excreted in the urine unless a diseased condition causes the cell walls to become permeable to them. In this case, a condition known as albuminuria arises in which albumin appears in the urine. 

Proteins can be precipitated from solutions containing them by the addition of different reagents. By saturating the solution with ammonium sulphate any soluble protein except peptones can be made insoluble although it is

unchanged chemically. If sodium chloride is used in the same way, globulins will be made insoluble but other proteins, if present, will remain in solution. Magnesium sulphate, which has the same effect as ammonium sulphate, is sometimes used to detect the presence of albumin in urine. If a sample of the specimen is saturated with it the protein will become visible because of its precipitation. 

Concentrated mineral acids, as sulphuric, hydrochloric and nitric acids, will also cause a soluble protein to become insoluble. In the beginning, no chemical change occurs but gradually the precipitated protein becomes soluble again, due to a chemical reaction which is more apparent if the temperature is raised. In the Babcock test for milk fat the condition of the milk proteins is changed by the addition of sulphuric acid. As a result they no longer make a good emulsifying medium for the fat and it rises to the surface of the mixture. A very common test for the detection of albumin in urine is the Heller ring test. In this test concentrated nitric acid is added very carefully to a sample of the specimen so that there will be two distinct layers, one of acid, the other of urine. At the point of contact a white fluffy ring becomes visible if albumin is present due to its precipitation by the acid.

The precipitation caused by the addition of a silver nitrate solution to a protein solution is due to a chemical change. The new insoluble compound, which can be called silver proteinate, is formed because the protein behaves like an acid in this reaction. When silver nitrate is used as a caustic the destruction of the treated tissue is due to the precipitation of the protein which is normally present in the protoplasm in a state of semi-solution. Mercuric chloride (bichloride of mercury), which has the same effect upon dissolved proteins, is used because of this action. Its effect as a disinfectant is due to the fact that the soluble protein constituents of micro-organisms are changed to

PROTEINS 

349 insoluble substances which make it impossible for the life of the organism to continue. The use of white of egg as an antidote for poisoning caused by bichloride of mercury and other salts of heavy metals, as lead, iron, copper and silver is due to the same reaction. Through the combination of the egg proteins with the metallic salt an insoluble compound is formed. The absorption and distribution of the bichloride of mercury is thus retarded but not completely prevented, for there is a tendency for the precipitate to redissolve if there is an excess of either of the compounds which formed it. Therefore, the administration of the antidote should be quickly followed by some other method to remove the contents of the stomach. A number of acids will react with the proteins due to the ability of the latter to behave like bases, the complex salts formed as products being insoluble compounds. Among the acids commonly used to precipitate proteins from their solutions because of this effect are tungstic acid, phosphomolybdic acid, picric acid and tannic acid. Before making certain blood tests the protein constituents whose presence would interfere with the results are often precipitated by the addition of tungstic acid or picric acid. In the leather industry tannic acid is used in the treatment of hides to change the proteins present. As a result the leather has a very different texture and feeling. It is claimed that the use of milk in tea renders the latter less harmful because its tannic acid is precipitated by the milk proteins.

Through the addition of a number of reagents to proteins colored products are formed which make it possible to detect the presence of the protein. These tests are not always entirely conclusive, however, as in many cases the result is due to a characteristic radical which other compounds as well as proteins contain. A knowledge of the character or source of the material tested sometimes makes it possible to rule out the chance of the presence of any

substance other than protein. When nitric acid is added to proteins the chemical change which follows its immediate precipitation, if in a soluble form, will result in the production of a yellow compound if the protein contains phenylalanine, tyrosine or tryptophane. If a base is added, the yellow color is deepened to an orange. This is called the xanthoproteic test from a Greek word meaning "yellow."

The yellow stain on the skin caused by nitric acid is due to this reaction. If the acid is concentrated, the tissues will be destroyed. It is sometimes used for this purpose as in the removal of warts. 

Another reagent used to produce color reaction is one called Millon's reagent which contains mercury salts in solution. Therefore, if it is added to a dissolved protein, its immediate effect will be to form a precipitate. On heating, the precipitate becomes red. As only proteins containing tyrosine will respond to this test, gelatine, which lacks this amino acid, shows no change in color. 

The biuret reagent which contains potassium hydroxide and copper sulphate causes a lavender color with practically all proteins except proteoses and peptones. With the latter the color is pink. This reagent can be made use of, therefore, to detect the presence of proteoses and peptones during a digestion experiment. The response to this test is due not to the presence of any particular kind of amino acid but rather to the way they have been joined together to form the protein. Because a substance called biuret, formed from urea, also responds to the test, the name was given both to the test and the reagent used in it. 

The classification of proteins suggests the products which are formed during hydrolysis. The kind of primary derived protein formed in the early stages of hydrolysis depends upon the condition by which it is brought about. By the application of heat alone a product called a coagulated protein is formed. Unless a very high temperature is used, as superheated steam, and for a long continued time, the

PROTEINS 

351 hydrolysis will not continue beyond this point. The change taking place during the cooking of protein foods results in the production of coagulated proteins. The temperature at which a coagulation giving the best texture and consistency to the product takes place is relatively low although it varies to some extent with the kind of protein and with the condition present. If the medium is slightly acid or if salt (sodium chloride) is present, it occurs very much more readily than it does if the medium is neutral or slightly alkaline. As the blood is slightly alkaline this may account for the fact that blood proteins coagulate at a somewhat higher temperature than do other kinds. In all cases, however, the temperature is considerably below the boiling point. This accounts for the necessity of controlling the temperature carefully and of using methods for moderating it, such as a double boiler, when cooking foods whose protein content is relatively great. The addition of a small amount of salt to the water in which a poached egg is cooked makes it possible to obtain a more attractive product because the egg is coagulated or "set" before it has a chance to spread out through the water. Acid added to the water in which fish is boiled increases its firmness and whiteness for the same reason. The uniform consistency of a custard or a boiled salad dressing depends upon the coagulation of the egg to a point at which it is jelly-like so that it remains uniformly distributed throughout the liquid with which it is mixed. If overcooked it becomes hard rather than jelly-like and it then separates from the liquid. A curdled custard is the result. This principle of coagulation is made use of in a test for the detection of albumin in urine. The specimen is slightly acidified and then carefully heated. A turbidity due to coagulation is an evidence of the presence of protein. If the specimen is concentrated, it should be diluted for otherwise phosphates may become precipitated as a result of the treatment causing a turbidity which is misleading. When alcohol is added to a

solution containing a protein the immediate effect is to precipitate out the protein unaltered so far as its chemical composition is concerned. As this precipitated protein continues to stand in contact with the alcohol it gradually becomes hydrolized to a coagulated protein. Both the precipitated and the coagulated protein are white, insoluble solids but in the latter case the mass has a firmer consistency. The use of alcohol, either as a preservative for biological specimens or as a disinfectant to clean instruments and glass slides used for bacteriological work, is dependent upon this effect upon proteins. The change from a soluble form, which is the normal condition of proteins in tissues or living cells, to an insoluble form makes impossible the life of any micro-organism present. 

Proteans, another kind of primary derived proteins, which are also insoluble, are formed through the action of acids or enzymes. When milk curdles because it has become sour the proteins which are normally present in a semi-dissolved state have been changed to an insoluble protean. Another example is the change which takes place in the soluble fibrinogen of the blood which changes to insoluble threads of fibrin forming the meshwork of the clot. 

As a result of the longer action of acids or alkalies, soluble primary derived proteins, called metaproteins, are produced. They may be formed artificially or occur as one of the early digestion products of proteins. An acid metaprotein is formed if the change occurs in an acid medium, as the gastric juice. In the small intestine whose contents are slightly alkaline, alkali metaprotein will be formed. Either raw or cooked (coagulated) proteins can be converted into metaproteins. 

Whatever the primary derived protein may be, as the hydrolysis is continued secondary derived proteins will be formed. The conditions favorable to a more extensive change are a high temperature for a long period of time, the action of an acid or of enzymes. It is believed that some

PROTEINS 

353 amino acids begin to be split away from the protein molecule at the stage when proteoses are formed. Still others are formed with peptones and with peptids. When the peptids are hydrolized amino acids are the only products. 

The enzymes chiefly concerned with protein hydrolysis are rennin, pepsin, erepsin and trypsin. Rennin helps to bring about a change in the casein of milk in which it is hydrolized to a soluble metaprotein called paracasein. This in turn combines with calcium compounds in the milk to form insoluble calcium paracaseinate. The distribution of this compound through the milk causes it to form a solid, jelly-like mass. Junket tablets consist of this enzyme, rennin, which has been obtained from the stomach of the calf. Through the help of the pepsin of the gastric juice either raw or cooked proteins can be hydrolized to proteoses and peptones with acid metaprotein as an intermediate product because the change is taking place in an acid medium. Erepsin, an enzyme supplied in the intestinal juice, is able to complete the work started by the pepsin. By its help proteoses or peptones can be changed into peptids and finally into amino acids. Trytsin, an enzyme in the pancreatic juice, can do all or any part of the work that requires the combined efforts of pepsin and erepsin. As this hydrolysis occurs in an alkaline medium, alkali metaproteins are one of the first products to be formed. The following equations represent in a general way the changes taking place during hydrolysis: or conjugated proteins,

Simple proteins + Water 

Primary derived proteins + Water Proteoses + Water Peptones + Water Peptids + Water 

Primary derived proteins 

Proteoses + amino acids 

Peptones + amino acids Peptids + amino acids Amino acids Fermentation changes occur in proteins through the help

of different types of bacteria. The production of desirable flavors in cheese and meat depend upon such reactions but if they proceed too far the food becomes unfit for use. 

Protein fermentation, or putrefaction as it is more commonly called, also occurs within the body in the large intestine where a condition of alkalinity and the presence of bacteria, among them being B. coli communis, favor it. 

As a result, undigested proteins undergo changes similar to those taking place during digestion, and the amino acid products together with any unabsorbed digestion products undergo further decomposition. A variety of products is formed among them being gases, such as methane, hydrogen sulphide, carbon dioxide and methyl mercaptan, which cause a condition of flatulence. There are a number of compounds whose effect is toxic although if they are formed in small amounts their action is not immediately recognized. 

Ptomaines, one type of toxic compound related to ammonia, produces very marked cases of poisoning if present in sufficient amounts. From the amino acids phenylalanine, tyrosine and tryptophane, phenol, cresol, skatole and indole are produced. To protect itself against these substances the body combines them with sulphuric acid and they are eliminated in the urine. The amount of indican (potassium indoxyl sulphate) present is used as a measure of protein putrefaction. It can be detected very easily by treating the specimen with a reagent which will oxidize the indican to indigo-blue or less frequently to indigo-red. The depth of the color varies with the amount of indican present. As an alkaline medium favors protein putrefaction, one way to restrict it is to cause a condition of acidity. 

This can be accomplished by the use of lactic acid milk or by increasing the carbohydrate content of the diet. As the organism B. coli communis can bring about fermentation in either carbohydrates or proteins but prefers the former, protein putrefaction will be prevented by the use

PROTEINS 

355 of carbohydrates not only because of the lactic acid which is produced but also because the action of the bacteria is diverted from the protein to the carbohydrate. As the chances for putrefaction are better the longer material remains within the large intestine, a method which corrects constipation will also reduce the putrefaction. 

When the amino acids have been absorbed through the cell walls of the digestive system into the blood stream to be carried to the body cells needing them, a number of different kinds of changes are possible. Primarily they are tissue builders and if they function in this way the proper combinations of amino acids are made to form the particular protein needed. In this way those tissues which are being worn out are constantly replaced. Large amounts of material are needed for hair and nails, for the skin cells which are worn out, for red corpuscles and to a less degree for other body tissues. For growing children and also in pregnancy even larger amounts are needed for the construction of entirely new tissue. If the protein intake is in excess of the demand for building material, it must be disposed of in some other way for there is little tendency for the body to store up these compounds except in the conditions previously mentioned or after there has been a serious loss of protein from the body. Another condition which may cause the amino acids to be decomposed instead of built up into proteins is the fact that the cells receiving them may have a greater need for fuel than for building material. Protein is a very expensive fuel, however, and the work of the kidneys is greatly increased for they must eliminate the greater part of the nitrogenous waste products. If the amino acids are decomposed, the first change they undergo is a process called deaminization in which the amino radical is split away with enough hydrogen to form ammonia. The fragment which is left is then a nitrogenfree acid. Assuming alanine to be the amino acid, the prod-

ucts resulting from its deaminization are ammonia and pyruvic acid. Alanine 

Ammonia + Pyruvic acid

Since ammonia would have a toxic effect if it were allowed to accumulate, the body protects itself against it by combining it with carbonic acid. This action really serves two purposes for it is at the same time a means of keeping down the hydrogen ion concentration of the body fluids. The ammonium carbonate which is formed as a result is decomposed into water and urea, the latter one of the principal nitrogenous end-products of protein metabolism eliminated in large part in the urine. 

2 NH3+H2CO3 (NH4)2CO3 (NH4)2CO3 (ammonium carbonate) → 2 H₂O+ (NH2)2CO (urea) Uric acid, another nitrogenous waste product, is frequently confused with urea. All proteins contribute to the formation of the latter through the ammonia resulting from the deaminization of amino acids. Uric acid is a waste product resulting from the metabolism of only one type of proteins, the nucleoproteins which contain nucleic acid. 

Purine bases in the nucleic acid part of the protein or free purine bases form uric acid when they are oxidized. Examples of purine bases are adenine, guanine and caffeine, theophylline and theobromine which are closely related compounds present in coffee, tea and cocoa. If other acids are formed as a result of metabolic changes, as sulphuric acid and phosphoric acid, ammonia is used to combine with them to form ammonium salts as a means of protection against an increasing hydrogen ion concentration. If amino acids are synthesized in the body from products derived from glucose or fats, as pyruvic acid, ammonia is also required.

Thus a product resulting from the decomposition of one amino acid may be used for the construction of another.

PROTEINS 

357 If proteins, or rather amino acids, serve as fuels, the portion undergoing oxidation is the nitrogen-free acid which is left after the acid has been deaminized. Assuming the amino acid to be alanine, pyruvic acid is the compound which is oxidized (see Chapter XXII and XXIII). The fact that protein can contribute to the formation of glucose has already been referred to (see Chapter XXIII). Pyruvic acid, the decomposition product which is common to both amino acids and glucose, is the bridge between the two, that is to say, the compound which can be built up into glucose. As pyruvic acid represents approximately 58% of the amino acid molecule this proportion of protein is assumed to be converted into glucose. There is little evidence that protein can be converted into fat. Possibly glycerol can be built from it since pyruvic acid is a common bond between the two. Whether or not protein directly contributes to the formation of fat, it is indirectly a source of it, for the glucose into which it can be converted can in turn be changed into fat.

SUMMARY 

Amino Acids are organic acids which always contain one or more amino radicals (NH2) as well as the carboxyl radical (COOH) which is common to all organic acids. 

Proteins are compounds always containing carbon, hydrogen, oxygen and nitrogen, usually sulphur and sometimes phosphorus or iron. They are compounds formed through the union of many amino acids. They are called the anhydrides of amino acids. 

Classification of Proteins

I. Simple Proteins.-Proteins which yield only a-amino acids or their derivatives on hydrolysis. 

1. Albumins: Soluble in pure water and coagulable by heat. 

Examples: egg albumin, lactalbumin (milk), serum albumin (blood), leucosin (wheat), legumelin (peas). 

2. Globulins: Insoluble in pure water but soluble in neutral salt solutions. Examples: muscle globulin, serum globulin (blood), edestin (wheat, hemp, and other seeds), legumin (peas and beans), tuberin (potato),

amandin (almond), excelsin (Brazil nut), arachin and conarachin (peanut), egg globulin. 

3. Glutelins. Insoluble in all neutral solvents but readily soluble in very dilute acids and alkalies. Example: glutenin (wheat). 

4. Alcohol Soluble Proteins: Soluble in relatively strong alcohol (70-80%) but insoluble in water, absolute alcohol and other neutral solvents. Examples: gliadin (wheat), zein (corn). 

5. Albuminoids: Pronounced insolubility in all neutral solvents. Present in skeletal structures of animals and in external protective tissues. Examples: collagen 

(skin, bone, connective tissue), elastin (ligament), keratin (horn, hoof, hair). 

6. Histones: Soluble in water and insoluble in very dilute ammonia and in the absence of ammonium salts insoluble even in an excess of ammonia, yield precipitates with solutions of other proteins and a coagulum on heating which is easily soluble in very dilute acids. 

On hydrolysis they yield several amino acids among which the basic ones predominate. Examples: globin (hemoglobin of blood), thymus histone. 

7. Protamines (the simplest natural proteins): Soluble in water and not coagulable by heat. Possess strong basic properties and on hydrolysis yield a few amino acids among which the basic amino acids greatly predominate. They are of no importance as food. 

II. Conjugated Proteins.-Proteins which yield both amino acids. 

and some other compound on hydrolysis. 

1. Nucleoproteins: Compounds of one or more protein molecules with nucleic acid. Examples: thymo-nucleic acid (thymus gland), tritico-nucleic acid (wheat germ). 

2. Glycoproteins: Compounds of the protein molecule with a substance or substances containing a carbohydrate group other than a nucleic acid. Examples: mucins (body secretions, saliva, etc.). 

3. Phosphoproteins: Compounds of the protein molecule with some, as yet undefined, phosphorus-containing substance other than a nucleic acid or lecithin. 

Examples: caseinogen (milk), ovovitellin (egg yolk).

PROTEINS 

359 

4. Hemoglobins: Compounds of the protein molecule with hematin or some similar substance. Examples: hemoglobin (blood). 

5. Lecithoproteins: Compounds of the protein molecule with lecithins or some similar substances. Example: egg yolk. 

1. Derived Proteins. 

1. Primary Derived Proteins: Derivatives of the protein molecule apparently formed through hydrolytic changes which involve only slight alterations of the protein molecule. a. Proteans: Insoluble products which apparently result from the incipient action of water, very dilute acids, or enzymes. Examples: casein (curdled milk), fibrin (coagulated blood). b. Metaproteins: Products of the further action of acids and alkalies whereby the molecule is sufficiently altered to form proteins soluble in very weak acids and alkalies but insoluble in neutral solvents. 

Examples: acid metaprotein or acid albuminate, alkali metaprotein or alkali albuminate. 

c. Coagulated Proteins: Insoluble products resulting from the action of heat or alcohol. Example: cooked egg albumen. 

2. Secondary Protein Derivatives: Products of the further hydrolytic cleavage of the protein molecule. a. Proteoses: Soluble in water, not coagulable by heat, precipitated by saturating their solutions with ammonium sulphate or zinc sulphate. b. Peptones: Soluble in water, not coagulable by heat and not precipitated by saturating their solutions with ammonium sulphate or zinc sulphate. They represent a further state of cleavage than the proteoses. r. Peptids: Definitely characterized combinations of two or more amino acids. They represent a further state of cleavage than the peptones. Dipeptid-a peptid molecule containing two amino acids; similarly, tripeptid, 3 amino acids; tetrapeptid, 4 amino acids; pentapeptid, 5 amino acids; polypeptid, several a-amino acids.

Complete Proteins.-Proteins which are able to keep body tissues in repair and to supply material for the development of new tissue during growth or reproduction. They have an adequate amount of cysteine, lysine, tyrosine and tryptophan. 

Partially Complete Proteins.-Proteins which are unable to supply material for new tissues although they are able to provide for the maintenance and repair of worn out tissue. 

Incomplete Proteins.-Proteins which can neither provide material for the repair of worn out tissue nor for the construction of new tissue.

EXPERIMENTS 

1. Test for Nitrogen

Heat a small amount of dried egg albumen in a dry test-tube and note the odor. Have you ever smelled it before? When?

Add a small lump of quicklime or some soda lime and heat again. 

What odor can you now detect? Hold a moist piece of pink litmus over the mouth of the test-tube. Result? To what might it be due? Prove it by holding another strip over the mouth of a bottle of ammonium hydroxide. Why can your results be considered an evidence of the presence of nitrogen in the protein? 

2. Color Reactions (a) Xanthoproteic test: 

Add a few drops of concentrated nitric acid to a solution of white of egg. What is the result? Explain. Heat and describe the change which has taken place. How does it differ from the

first effect produced by the acid? Cool and neutralize with ammonium hydroxide.. Note the result. (b) Biuret test: 

To 10 cc. of a solution of potassium hydroxide add a solution of copper sulphate drop by drop until you obtain a clear, blue solution. This is the biuret reagent.

To a few cc. of a solution of white of egg add a small amount of the reagent. Compare the color with that of the reagent. 

To a few cc. of a solution of Witte's peptone add a small amount of biuret reagent. Result? Compare it with that obtained with the solution of egg white. 

3. Precipitation Tests 

(a) By sodium chloride:

Add solid sodium chloride to 10 cc. of a solution of white of egg until no more will dissolve. Result? To what is it due?

Explain the effect on the protein. (b) By mercuric chloride: 

To a few cc. of a concentrated solution of white of egg add a few drops of mercuric chloride. Result? To what is it due?

Compare the effect produced on the protein in this case with that in the preceding experiment. 

4. Formation of Coagulated Proteins 

(a) By heat:

Fill a test-tube to the depth of two inches with raw white of egg and place a thermometer in the test-tube. Suspend the testtube in a beaker of water so that the egg white is completely surrounded by water. Heat gradually and note the temperature at which the egg is coagulated. What change has taken place? 

What product is formed? 

Repeat the experiment using white of egg to which a drop of dilute acetic acid has been added. What difference does the acid make? 

(b) By alcohol: 

Add about 1 cc. of ethyl alcohol to 5 cc. of a solution of white of egg. Result? What has happened to the protein? Put a cork in the test-tube and allow it to stand for several hours. Is there any change in the appearance? How is the protein affected after continued contact with the alcohol?

PROTEINS 

363 

5. Digestion of Proteins 

In each of three test-tubes place equal amounts of hard boiled white of egg which has been cut into small pieces. To the first add 5 cc. of artificial gastric juice (a solution of pepsin acidified with hydrochloric acid). To the second test-tube add 5 cc. of artificial pancreatic juice (a solution of pancreatin made alkaline with sodium carbonate). To the third add 5 cc. of distilled water. Place the test-tubes in a beaker of water and keep at body temperature for some time. Is there any change in the appearance of the pieces of egg? Is there any evidence that they are becoming dissolved? Filter and divide each filtrate in two portions. Heat one portion. Is there any evidence of coagulation? Try the biuret test with another portion. Result? How is it accounted for? Mention all the evidences of digestion you have observed.

CHAPTER XXV  Factors to Consider in the Choice of a Method.-The objective of all cleaning processes is to change the location of dirt, which has been described as "matter out of place." 

The choice of the method to be used is guided by many factors. The material from which the dirt is to be removed may be a surface of wood, porcelain, enamel or metal, or it may be a fabric. If it is the latter, the character of the fiber will frequently necessitate modifications in its treatment, silks and wools requiring very much more careful handling than cottons and linens. The color of the fabric as well as the character of the fiber must be considered, for many methods, which can be used successfully on white materials, prove disastrous on those which are dyed. The dirt may be distributed over the entire object or it may be confined to a small spot. A method which would be appropriate if only a small area needed to be cleaned might prove very laborious and unsuitable if applied to a larger surface. The character of the dirt as well as the amount will make a great difference in the method used for its removal. Loose dry particles, like sand or dust, which do not adhere to a surface, can be brushed or shaken off or removed more completely with a vacuum cleaner. Certain kinds of dirt can be dissolved out by using an appropriate solvent. Sugar syrup, for example can be washed off with water. A fat solvent will remove a grease spot. Sometimes the objectionable substance is insoluble but it can be changed to a soluble compound by treating it with some reagent which will react with it. Oxalic acid will remove the tarnish on a 364

copper boiler or an iron rust spot from many fabrics because a soluble compound is formed which can be washed or rinsed off. In many cases, if a chemical reagent is used, the color is removed and so the unwanted substance is no longer apparent even though it may still be present. As many complications may occur because both the dirt itself and the material in which it is found must be considered, there is no small wonder that the results are so often unsuccessful.

Cleansing Action of Soap Solutions.-The most uni versal way of cleaning depends upon the use of soap and water. The dirt to be removed ordinarily consists largely of dust or soot particles coated with more or less fatty material. At one time it was supposed that the cleaning action was due to sodium hydroxide which was formed as a product of the hydrolysis of the soap.

NaC17H35COO+ HOH - NaOH + C17H35COOH (soap) (stearic acid)

Through the reaction of the greasy dirt with this sodium hydroxide, the insoluble fat was thought to be changed into soluble soap and so it could be easily removed. When the soot particles lost their greasy coating they, too, could be rinsed out. Although this seemed to be a perfectly plausible explanation it was found that a solution of sodium hydroxide, whose concentration is the same as that formed in a soap solution through the hydrolysis of the latter, has no cleansing effect. Two reasons are now given for the efficiency of soap and water for cleaning purposes. When soap is rubbed on the article or the latter is scrubbed in water containing dissolved soap, any grease present is broken up into fine particles of fat which become emulsified. As soap forms a colloidal solution it makes an especially good emulsifying medium. Therefore, the particles of grease remain emulsified in it and are removed in this condition. The soot particles are removed by a process of adsorption (see

´ 

Chapter XVI). The fact that in the solution the soap is present as colloidal particles increases the tendency to the cohesion between them and the fine particles of soot. In many cases of adsorption the carbon removes the colloidal material from the solution because the former is present in much the larger amounts. While the property of adsorption is also responsible for the removal of soot (carbon) particles by a soap solution, in this case the latter, being present in larger amounts, carries off the soot.

The behavior of soap in hard water has already been described (see Chapter VI). When the sticky insoluble precipitate, consisting of the calcium, magnesium and iron salts of the fatty acids, adheres to a porcelain or enameled surface it can be removed with kerosene which dissolves it. 

If this precipitate becomes attached to clothing which is being washed, it is not so easy to remove. Ferrous stearate (Fe(C17H35COO)2), which is formed if water contains ferrous bicarbonate (Fe(HCO3)2), is the most objectionable form for on exposure to the air it becomes oxidized to ferric hydroxide (Fe(OH)3) or ferric oxide (Fe2O3) both of which are red compounds. The unexpected appearance of rust spots can sometimes be accounted for in this way. Three compounds which are commonly used to soften hard water 

(see Chapter VI) are washing-soda (Na₂CO), borax 

(Na2B4O7) and household ammonia (NH4OH). It is often claimed that their usefulness, especially in the case of the borax and household ammonia, is due to a bleaching action.

It is true that clothes may look whiter and cleaner if these compounds are used but it is not because they have been bleached. So long as the sticky precipitate due to the hard water has a chance to form and becomes entangled in the clothing which is being washed it is very difficult to remove it completely no matter how thoroughly they are rinsed. 

They will, therefore, have a gray or yellowish appearance. soluble salts which cause the hardness and change them to 

Because the borax or household ammonia can react with the

insoluble salts, the latter cannot longer form the sticky precipitate. Washing-soda and borax also increase the ability of the solution to emulsify fat and oils and for this reason the process of cleaning is more thorough. Many laundry soaps, soap flakes and washing powders contain either washing-soda or borax in addition to the soap. 

Bluings. With constant use clothing often acquires a yellowish tinge even when the washing is done carefully and thoroughly. For this reason bluing is added to the water used for the final rinsing. As blue is the complement of yellow, the latter color is counteracted and as a result the clothes appear white although no bleaching action has taken place. Three compounds ordinarily used for the purpose are indigo, ultramarine and Prussian blue or ferric ferrocyanide (Fe₁[Fe(CN)6]). Indigo, which was the compound used most commonly at one time has now been almost entirely replaced by the other two. Prussian blue comes usually in a liquid form and ultramarine in balls or cubes. All three compounds are insoluble in water but the particles are so fine that when present in it the effect produced is that of a blue solution. If clothing is allowed to stand for any length of time in the bluing water, however, the small insoluble particles settle out in the folds causing a streaked appearance. Prussian blue is the least desirable of the three compounds for it can react with sodium hydroxide to produce ferric hydroxide, a red, jelly-like precipitate, which will adhere to the fabric. Fe(Fe(CN) 6)3 + 12 NaOH - 4 Fe(OH)3 + + 3 Na4Fe(CN). 

When soap, washing soda or borax is dissolved in water a slight hydrolysis takes place, the action being greater if the water is hot. One product of this hydrolysis is in every case sodium hydroxide.

Na2CO3 + 2 HOH Na2B4O7+2 HOH 

2 NaOH+H2CO3 2 NaOH + H₂B407 Therefore, unless the clothing is very thoroughly rinsed

before it is blued the presence of even small amounts of any one of these three compounds will give the Prussian blue something to react with. The ferric hydroxide which is produced is another cause of an unexpected iron rust spot. In commercial laundries aniline dyes are sometimes used for bluings and the material actually becomes dyed. 

Certain types of these dyes require an acid bath in order to set them. One reason for the rapid appearance of holes in articles sent to the laundry may be the use of oxalic acid for this purpose. In some hospital laundries a preparation called "snow-blue" is used. One ounce of this is boiled in one gallon of water and then diluted to proportions of one to five. For 500 gallons of water three-quarters of an ounce of this solution is used. 

Stain Removal.-There are three important principles to remember when removing a stain. 

1st-Remove the stain as quickly as possible after it has been formed as an old stain is very much more resistant to treatment. 

2nd-Try the simplest and least harmful method first.

If it proves unsuccessful gradually try other methods whose effects are more pronounced. 

3rd-If possible try out the method on a sample or some portion of the surface which is not visible. In this way unexpected bad results may sometimes be avoided.

The materials which are used for removing stains can be divided roughly into three groups: absorbents, solvents and reagents which cause chemical reactions to occur. 

A number of materials are commonly used because of their ability to absorb or soak up the substance causing a stain. Grease spots are removed by placing blotting paper or some other soft finished paper above and below the spotted material. If a hot iron is then passed over the paper, the grease is more readily absorbed because it has been melted. If powders, such as Fuller's earth (alu-

minium silicate) and French chalk (magnesium silicate) used either dry or in a paste, are allowed to cover a spot for some time and then are brushed off it may be found that the stain has been lessened or removed completely. A very common method used for a stain when it is first made on table linen is to sprinkle it with salt. Although it may not remove the stain it will keep it from spreading because it absorbs the moisture. Absorbents of which only a few examples have been mentioned, are useful when a solvent or a chemical reagent cannot be used for fear of injuring the article being cleaned. nove Water is the most common of the liquids use stains by dissolving them. Sometimes it is more desirable to have it cold, as when washing out blood stains or mucus, both of which consist largely of proteins. Many stains, however, will be removed more readily if the water is hot. 

According to some directions the water is to be poured from a height. The advantage of this method is in the greater force with which the water strikes the spot which serves as a mechanical aid. Many stains which are insoluble in water can be dissolved by many of the organic solvents such as gasoline, benzene and car etrachloride. 

Fats and oils, mineral oils, vaselines and lucating greases are the stains which are most commonly removed by their use. Thethod is frequently spoken of "dry cleaning" although a liquid is used. Because of its rápid evaporation, however, the material does not remain wet very long. The best ways of handling and storing these ids have already been discussed with reference to their properties 

(see Chapter XVII and XVIII). Alcohol is useful as a solvent for removing many medicine stains. 

Some of the chemical reagents used to take out stains do so by appearing to dissolve them. The substance which is dissolved, however, is a soluble compound formed through a reaction between the reagent and the insoluble substance

causing the stain. Many acids are used in this way, among them being citric acid, most commonly in the form of lemon juice, lactic acid in sour milk, acetic acid in vinegar, hydrochloric acid and oxalic acid. If pure oxalic acid is not on hand rhubarb stalks or begonia leaves, which contain oxalic acid and their salts can be boiled in a small amount of water and the filtered liquid used. If the stalks of rhubarb are highly colored, it is better to remove the skin. When the acid solution is dilute the stained material can remain in contact with it for a longer time. If it is concentrated, it must be applied more carefully and rinsed at frequent intervals, the process being repeated as often as seems advisable. Borax or ammonium hydroxide should be added to the last rinsing water to neutralize any trace of acid which may possibly be left. When removing an iron rust spot with oxalic acid the insoluble ferric hydroxide is changed to soluble ferric oxalate. 2 Fe(OH)3 + 3 H2C2O4 →Fe2(C2O4)3 + 6 HOH A few salts whose solutions behave like acids are sometimes used, for example, cream of tartar (potassium hydrogen tartrate) and salts of lemon. The name of the latter compound is very misleading for one would naturally expect it to be a salt of citric acid. It is, however, a salt of oxalic acid, its chemical name being potassium tetraoxalate (K₂H₂(C₂O4)2). Sodium thiosulphate, more commonly called sodium hyposulphite or hypo, can be used to take out iodine stains because of the formation of a colorless, soluble iodine compound. Its action has been discussed with reference to the preparation of colorless iodine (see 

Chapter XIII). 

A number of reagents which are commonly used as stain removers owe their effectiveness to their ability to oxidize the material causing the stain. As a result a colorless compound is produced. Among the familiar reagents used in

this way are hydrogen peroxide, a solution of bleaching powder, Javelle water and potassium permanganate. The behavior of the first three of these reagents has already been discussed (see Chapters IV and XIII). When potassium permanganate is used, although the original stain is removed or rendered colorless because it has been oxidized, a reduction product, manganese dioxide (MnO2), is formed from the reagent. This is a brown, insoluble solid which can be removed by using a solution of oxalic acid or a solution of sodium bisulphite (NaHSO,). The alternate use of potassium permanganate and sodium bisulphite removes many stains which are resistant to other reagents. They have the added advantage of being not harmful and so can be used on the skin or on silks and wools. The chlorinecontaining reagents are especially injurious to silks and wools for their protein constituents, as well as the stain, are acted on by the hypochlorous acid or its salts. As a result the fibers become very much weakened. 

While it is impossible to give directions for the removal of a large variety of stains or to mention many methods which might be used, a description of a few of the more familiar ones will be included. In many cases the examples chosen illustrate the fact that one method may depend upon the application of a number of different principles. 

Balsam of Peru.-Stains caused by balsam of Peru, a brown sticky liquid which is insoluble in water, can be dissolved out with alcohol. They can also be removed by soaking in kerosene after which the oily stain can be washed out.

Blood. The stained material should be soaked for some time in cold or lukewarm water (never hot water) to which household ammonia has been added in the proportion of one ounce to every gallon of water. For materials which cannot be soaked, as mattresses, blankets or flannel, cover the stains with a paste made of raw starch and cold

water. When it has dried, brush it off and repeat the process until the stain disappears.

Chocolate and Cocoa.-These stains contain a mixture of fat, starch, sugar, milk and coloring matter. They can ordinarily be removed in the usual laundry processes. The stain can also be sprinkled with borax and cold water and then rinsed with boiling water. 

Coffee. Coffee stains, if fresh, can be removed by boiling water poured from a height on the material which has been stretched over a bowl. They can also be removed with soap and water. If the soap is alkaline, it may first change the brown color of the stain to a bright yellow but the substances present will be dissolved. If soap and water fail to remove the stain completely, a solution of potassium permanganate can be used to complete the process. 

Silk or woolen materials spotted with coffee should be sponged carefully with cold or lukewarm water. If the coffee contained cream, it may be necessary afterwards to remove the grease spots with a fat solvent, as carbon tetrachloride, chloroform, ether, gasoline or benzine (caution! all but the first two are inflammable). Sometimes the stain can be removed from a light silk by laying it between clean, damp cloths and pressing with a hot iron. 

Dust. Cover the stains with a paste made of egg yolk and alcohol, let it dry and then scrape it off. If necessary remove the egg with a cloth moistened in warm water. 

Fruit. As many fruit stains are set by alkalies it is wiser not to use soap for their removal. For a fresh fruit or berry stain as soon as possible stretch the material over a large bowl and from a height of three or four feet pour boiling water on it. If the stain is not completely removed, lemon juice and sunlight may take out the remainder of the spots. 

An old stain, which has become blue or gray, and will not be removed by hot water, may be made more soluble if an acid is first added. Stains from cooked fruits should be treated in the same way.

Grease.-Axle grease and the lubricating grease from the hinges of wheel-chairs can be removed by covering the spot with lard to soften it and then washing with lukewarm water and soap.

Indelible Pencil.-Soak the marked spots in alcohol, then wash in soap and water. If they do not come out, Javelle water can be used if the material is undyed cotton or linen. 

Prepare Javelle water by dissolving 1 pound of washingsoda in 1 quart of water and adding 1/4 pound of bleaching powder (CaCICIO). Stir thoroughly and filter. The solution will keep longer if stored in a dark bottle. When using Javelle water stretch the material over a bowl filled with hot water and apply the solution with a medicine dropper or glass rod. Rinse thoroughly and repeat the process if necessary. A very dilute solution of tartaric acid or oxalic acid used before the final rinsing will react with any trace of the sodium hypochlorite contained in the 

Javelle water. Precaution should be taken, however, to remove it thoroughly by rinsing, for if left in the material the continued action of either the sodium hypochlorite or the acid will weaken the fibers.

Inks. The composition of inks varies so greatly that a method which has proved successful for one may be ineffective for another. There are two kinds of indelible inks, those which contain a silver compound and aniline black inks. From the directions on the ink bottle one can tell what the type of ink is. The inks containing silver compounds must be heated, either by placing the marking in the sun or by ironing it. This precipitates the silver so that the mark becomes visible. To remove an ink spot of this type soak it in a solution of sodium thiosulphate or treat with Javelle water. After the stain is removed rinse in water containing ammonium hydroxide to remove the silver chloride produced in the reaction. The directions for using aniline black inks state that the article must not be ironed until it has been washed. This type of ink cannot

as a rule be removed after it is dry. Ordinary writing inks usually contain an iron salt of tannic acid, an aniline dye or finely divided carbon. If the ink spots are treated before they have dried, ordinary laundering may remove them.

No chemical reagent will take out carbon ink. By dissolving the binder which holds it in suspension carbon tetrachloride may help to remove it. When the spot has dried the loosened carbon particles can then be brushed out. Solutions of oxalic acid or salts of lemon will take out the ink if it contains an iron salt. The spot can be soaked in lemon juice or sour milk if milder treatment is necessary because of the fabric. The ink eradicators consisting of two parts usually contain in the first bottle a solution of bleaching powder and in the second a solution of an acid such as tartaric acid. By using these alternately the spot becomes bleached. Red ink spots can sometimes be removed with alcohol to which a few drops of nitric acid have been added. 

If they contain carmin or eosin as ingredients they can be removed with a chlorine compound unless the spot is on a silk or woolen material. 

Iodine. If iodine is spilled on unstarched material a brown stain results. When treated before it has a chance to dry it may come out if washed in cold water. Another method is to soak in cold water and then dry in a warm place. As iodine tends to vaporize when heated it may be possible to remove it in this way. For small articles the spots can sometimes be removed by boiling in a starch solution to form the soluble starch iodide which can then be rinsed out. If the material cannot be wet with water, an iodine spot can be removed by sponging or soaking in alcohol. An iodine stain may be blue instead of brown because the spotted material contained starch. This stain, as well as the one which is brown, can be removed by soaking in ammonium hydroxide or sponging with it if the material must be treated more carefully. When a spot

is removed by sponging with a reagent, if a clean piece of absorbent cloth or blotting paper is placed beneath it, it absorbs the moisture and so prevents the spot from spreading. If it is sponged on the wrong side of the material the stain can be removed more readily for the substances causing it are not drawn through the fibers to the absorbent beneath. Another method is to dip the stained portion in a dilute solution of sodium thiosulphate and rinse thoroughly. 

Medicines. Many medicines contain alcohol-soluble ingredients and can therefore be removed by using it as a solvent. Medicines which contain iron compounds, as a tincture of iron chloride and Basham's mixture, can be taken out with an acid, for example oxalic acid or citric acid.

Mildew. Certain species of molds, which can grow on cloth or other fabrics if they are damp, form spots called mildew. If fresh, these stains can sometimes be removed with soap and water. If they resist this treatment, they may be taken out by soaking them over night in sour milk and then placing the material in the sun without rinsing it. 

Lemon juice can be used in the same way. Old mildew stains will probably require Javelle water. 

Paint.—Fresh paint can be removed with soap and water if the material can be washed. Otherwise, use turpentine, benzine or gasoline to dissolve the linseed oil medium and brush out the loosened pigment. This method can also be used for spots which have become dried. 

Rust.-Acids are generally used for the removal of these spots, the kind and the strength of the solution depending on the delicacy of the material. If white cotton or linen, the stained portion may be stretched over a bowl of hot water and concentrated hydrochloric acid, which has been diluted with an equal volume of water, applied drop by drop until the stain becomes bright yellow. Then rinse thoroughly. It may be necessary to repeat the process. To

neutralize any acid left after the stain has been removed, add a little household ammonia or borax to the final rinsing water. Oxalic acid may be used in place of the hydrochloric acid, either as a solution or in crystals which are moistened after they are spread on the spot. If the article is small it can be boiled in a solution of cream of tartar (KHCHOε) in the proportion of 4 teaspoons of the former to 1 pint of water. In some cases the spot can be removed by sprinkling it with salt, adding lemon juice and leaving it in the sun. Scorch. Silk and woolen materials are more easily damaged by scorching than cotton and linen for their fibers become very much weakened. If the garment is white and can be wet with soap and water and then laid in the sun, the color may be removed. Hydrogen peroxide can be used in place of soap and water, if these cannot be used. If cotton and linen materials are only slightly scorched the spots may come out when they are laundered the next time. 

Silver Nitrate.-Silver nitrate stains can be removed from bed linen and towels by brushing with a solution which contains 45 grains of the mercuric chloride (HgCl2) and 45 grains of ammonium chloride (NH4Cl) with enough distilled water to make one fluid ounce. As this solution is very poisonous great care must be exercised when using it. A solution of sodium thiocyanate will also react with silver compounds to form a complex soluble compound. For this reason it can be used to remove silver nitrate stains. Potassium cyanide also dissolves silver compounds by forming a complex soluble compound. Its use as a stain remover is not recommended, however, for it is a deadly poison. A 10% solution is sometimes used for fabrics and a 0.5-1% solution for removing stains from the skin. When applied to the latter it causes redness and inflammation and if the skin is broken, enough may be absorbed to cause symptoms of poisoning. Argyrol, a preparation containing silver oxide combined with proteins,

causes a brown stain which can be removed with salt and ammonia when it is fresh. 

Tea. Fresh tea stains can often be removed either by washing in water or by soaking in a borax solution and then rinsing thoroughly in boiling water. If they do not respond to this treatment, boil the stained article in a strong soap solution. Another method is to cover with lemon juice and place it on the grass in the sun. For very old stains potassium permanganate may be used. 

Varnish. A varnish stain can be removed by wetting with alcohol and turpentine, letting it stand and repeating the process until the stain disappears.

Vaseline. When fresh, vaseline stains can be removed by the use of the ordinary fat solvents. If the stain is old the spot may be sponged with turpentine. It is commonly supposed that these stains cannot be removed if the material has previously been laundered. While they are very much more resistent, by first soaking and then thoroughly sponging them with turpentine they can often be removed. 

Metal Cleaning.-There are two factors to consider when selecting material for a polishing powder: it must be harder than the coating to be removed and it must be so fine it leaves no visible marks. An abrasive must not be too hard, however, if it is to be used for cleaning a soft metal since some of the latter will be removed with the tarnish or corroded coating. Infusorial earth (the skeleton remains of very small organisms called infusoria) is the most common material of which scouring pastes and powders are composed. It consists principally of silicon dioxide (SiO2). 

Because it is both hard and very fine grained it is desirable for this purpose. It is also called electro-silicon or tripoli. Powdered silica, a cheaper form of silicon dioxide, is coarser than infusorial earth and should not be used for polishing gold or silver. Pumice-stone, another material commonly used as a polisher, is the lava produced during a

volcanic eruption. It consists of silicates. It is ground into a powder and used in a mixture with either water or oil. 

Ground sandstone, composed of other silicon compounds, is ground and pressed into bricks which are sold as Bath bricks or Bristol bricks. They are used principally for scouring steel. Ferric oxide (Fe2O3), commonly called jeweler's rouge, is mixed with grease and sold under the name of Putz paste. 

It is used either dry or mixed with water, alcohol or oil. 

Whiting or finely powdered chalk (CaCO3) is mixed with household ammonia and used commonly for cleaning. The simplest way of cleaning silver is to place it either in an aluminum pan or on an aluminum cover in a granite pan, cover it with water containing a small amount of washing soda and boil. Aluminium replaces the silver in the tarnished coating and the resulting aluminium compound being soluble in an alkaline solution is easily removed. This leaves an untarnished surface. The method is not so good for plated silver as it is for sterling for the plating is pure silver and will, therefore, be removed much more quickly.

It is desirable to keep an aluminum pan especially for the purpose for it is quickly spoiled for any other use because of the action of the alkaline solution on it. Soaps or washing powders which are strongly alkaline and washing-soda should not be used for cleaning aluminum for the same reason. A reaction takes place between the metal and sodium hydroxide, which the soap or washing-powder solution contains because of its hydrolysis, and sodium aluminate and hydrogen are formed. Very fine steel wool and a neutral soap or polishing powder containing no alkali are the best materials to use.

QUESTIONS
