CHAPTER XXVI DISINFECTANTS 

Disinfectants vs. Antiseptics.-The word "disinfectant" should be used to denote a substance which destroys pathogenic organisms. In other words, it is practically synonymous with "germicide." An "antiseptic," however, inhibits the growth of micro-organisms without necessarily killing them. The substances which are used in the hospital to prevent wound infection are often spoken of as antiseptics although most of them are both antiseptic and germicidal in their action. They may be classified under two main headings: 

1st-Inorganic substances, examples of which are chlorine and its compounds, salts of the heavy metals, as zinc sulphate, bichloride of mercury, silver nitrate and other silver compounds, hydrogen peroxide and potassium permanganate. 

2nd-Organic substances, examples of which are phenol, cresol and other coal-tar derivatives.

Conditions Which Influence Their Activity.-Many of these substances are unstable and in order to get the best results it is necessary to know how they act. The temperature condition has been found to influence their activity as it does the rate of ordinary chemical reactions. From experimental results obtained when B. paratyphosus was the organism tested, a rise of 10° C. was found to increase the velocity of disinfection by metallic salts from 2-4 times. 

When phenol was used it was increased 8 times. The nature of the medium is another condition having a marked influence on the activity of the disinfectant. They are most 380

DISINFECTANTS 

381 active in distilled water or salt solution whereas their activity is reduced by the presence of proteins. Pus is even less favorable to the action. The salts of silver, mercury, zinc and bismuth are particularly active when bacteria are suspended in pure water. Dakin and Dunham have pointed out that the metallic ions present in the water solutions are responsible for their effect, the undissociated salts being of less importance. The activity is influenced, therefore, by the degree of ionization of each salt. As the salts of the heavy metals react chemically with proteins to form insoluble complex salts composed of both the metallic salt and the protein (see Chapter XXIV), the proteins present in wound discharges will lessen their effect as disinfectants. 

Several factors are to be considered in selecting a disinfectant for a definite purpose. One must determine the action of the disinfectant in the particular medium and the rapidity with which the action takes place. It is also important to use a substance which injures the body cells as little as possible to prevent interference with the action of the leucocytes in the wound. The irritation of the skin should also be considered. In many cases, it is undesirable to use disinfectants which coagulate protein, as phenol does. 

For this reason the hypochlorites are preferable, since they dissolve out dead tissue and are particularly valuable when used to flush out a wound. 

Metallic Salts.-Bichloride of mercury (HgCl2), is one of the most powerful germicides but its use is limited because it is very poisonous. It is rapidly absorbed and slowly excreted and, therefore, will accumulate in the system if used continuously. Its combination with soluble proteins to form insoluble mercuric proteinate has already been referred to (see Chapter XXIV). Consequently it cannot be used to disinfect stools or any discharge containing blood, pus or mucus for the disinfectant cannot penetrate beyond the surface of the mass because of the

insoluble coating which has been formed. The protoplasm of the tissues will become injured for the same reason. 

Therefore, it should not be used continuously as a hand disinfectant or as an irrigant or douche. When used as a hand disinfectant the skin must first be thoroughly washed and rinsed. Mercuric chloride will not penetrate an oily substance and because of the alkaline reaction of any soap remaining on the skin it can also be decomposed. Bichloride of mercury tablets are usually made larger than headache tablets with which they have sometimes been confused. 

They should also contain some coloring material, for example methylene blue, to give them a very different appearance. Under no condition should a stock solution of this compound be prepared without the coloring matter because of the great danger of mistaking it for boracic acid or distilled water. When preparing the solution, distilled water should be used as experimental results show that mercuric chloride is five times more active in it than it is in tap water. The treatment for bichloride of mercury poisoning has already been referred to (see Chapter XXIV). The solution cannot be used as a disinfectant for instruments because of its blackening effect. A solution of mercuric oxycyanide made up in the proportion of 1 part to 1000 parts can be used for this purpose. 

Another preparation of mercury, called mercurochrome contains 23-24% of mercury and an aniline dye. It is odorless, permanent in the air, soluble in water and 65% alcohol, but insoluble in ether and chloroform. It varies from a cherry red to a reddish yellow color when in solution. It is incompatible with acids and does not precipitate albumin. Its action is rapid, experiments proving that it kills the bacillus coli and staphylococcus aureus in one minute. It is used in a bladder irrigation in a 1% solution and in the eye in a 2.5% solution. There are said to have been untoward systemic effects from the local applica-

DISINFECTANTS 

383 tion of this preparation. The stain may be removed from the skin by sodium hypochlorite.

In the operating rooms of certain hospitals, potassium mercuric iodide, which is a double salt of iodine and mercury, is used. is used. It is formed by the direct combination of 

2 molecules of potassium iodide and 1 molecule of mercuric iodide, the formula being 2 KI.HgI₂ or K₂HgI4. It is soluble in water and in 85% alcohol. If there is a slight excess of potassium iodide, the solutions are said to be stable and there is no precipitation of the water insoluble mercuric iodide. It forms no insoluble compounds with proteins, and for that reason is better than bichloride of mercury in many cases. It is used in a 1% solution in alcohol to destroy bacteria on the skin. used for disinfecting the hands. mercury, which should never be ments, the potassium mercuric iodide may be so used, if bicarbonate of soda, one dram to each litre, is present to prevent the tarnishing of the metal. 

A 1 to 5000 solution is 

Unlike the bichloride of used to sterilize instru Harrington's solution is used in the operating room of some hospitals for the final disinfection of the hands. It consists of 80-90 grains of bichloride of mercury (HgCl₂) and 60% alcohol with 480 cc. concentrated hydrochloric acid (HCl) to each two gallons of the solution.

A metallic salt antiseptic which was used extensively during the World War was a preparation containing 1 part of bismuth subnitrate and 2 parts of iodoform mixed into a thick paste with liquid paraffin oil. It can be spread out in a thin layer and used in wounds or in fractures of the long bones. When a fresh dressing is to be made, this paste can be wiped off with wool sponges soaked in alcohol. 

The preparation is called B. I. P.

Another use for bismuth is for cholostomy wounds to keep secretions from the skin. It is also used in sinuses because it stimulates granulation of the tissue.

Oxidizing Agents.-Hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) is not used as extensively as it once was. There is an enzyme called catalase in blood, pus and body tissues which causes the hydrogen peroxide to give up oxygen at once. Consequently there is no lasting disinfecting action (see also Chapter IV). It is said that the decomposition of hydrogen peroxide may be lessened by adding a little acetanilid.

Ozone (O3) is also used to treat infected wounds, particularly cavities and sinuses in bone. 

Potassium permanganate (KMnO4) acts as a disinfectant because it is unstable and easily gives up its oxygen, which unites with bacteria, thus destroying them or inhibiting their growth. The oxygen is given up in either an acid or an alkaline medium, and as soon as the solution is reduced, its color changes from the characteristic purple color to a brown. A 1-3% solution is used to disinfect the hands, which should be held in the solution until they are dark brown, showing that the potassium permanganate has united with the organic material. The hands are then put in dilute oxalic acid or sodium bisulphite (NaHSO3) to remove the brown stain. Potassium permanganate is used in a 1-3% solution, not only for the hands but also to irrigate wounds. In a 1% solution it is used as a vaginal douche for it acts as a disinfectant and deodorant. It is also used in a 1% solution as a mouth wash and gargle.

In a solution containing 1 part potassium permanganate to 1000 parts of water, 0.1% solution, it is used as a lavage for cases of poisoning by alkaloids such as morphine, strychnine, atropine, etc. Two crystals to a glass of water makes approximately a 0.1% solution but if prepared in this way one must be sure that the crystals are thoroughly dissolved or the stomach itself may be oxidized. 

Preparations Which Contain a Halogen.-Iodine was first used as a skin disinfectant by Stretton in 1909. It is still used by many surgeons in a 2.5% solution. If it is not made up in pure alcohol iodoacetone, which is very

DISINFECTANTS 

385 irritating to the eyes of the operator, may be formed. 70% alcohol is the best strength to use for making the solution.

Iodoform (CHI) is used on gauze wicks as a drain for wounds. As it has an exceedingly disagreeable odor and has caused cases of poisoning, its use in surgical dressings has been limited. The antidote is potassium bicarbonate. 

According to one explanation, its action as a disinfectant is due to the iodine which it releases. Bliss and Olive ascribe its effect to the fact that it acts as a desiccant, and by this means uses up the secretions upon which the micro-organisms depend. It also produces chemical changes in the toxic products of bacteria, possibly rendering them harmless. As some bacteria will grow in iodoform, it is always sterilized before using it in gauze wicks for wounds. 

The chlorine compounds which are used as antiseptics can give up this element in such a way as to make it able to combine with the proteins of bacteria and body tissues. 

The amino radicals of the proteins' amino acids are the parts of the molecules which are affected. Chlorine becomes combined with nitrogen by taking the place of hydrogen so that radicals are formed which class the product with compounds called chloramines. Such a reaction is helpful for two reasons. The proteins of the micro-organisms are so changed that the latter are killed. So long as the chlorine has combined with nitrogen it is still capable of action and the product which is formed is itself a germicide. 

The chlorine compounds are important not only because of their ability to act as disinfectants but also because they are good oxidizing agents and deodorants and can decompose toxins. The following compounds are the ones most commonly used: 

Hypochlorous acid and its salts-especially sodium hypochlorite 

Chloramine-T or sodium toluene sulphonchloramide

Dichloramine-T or toluene sulphondichloramine As hypochlorous acid and its salts are unstable and very

reactive they must be frequently renewed. They are, therefore, suited to methods of irrigation. The salts of the acid are much less irritating than the acid itself, especially to the deeper tissues. The skin is more susceptible, however, than other tissues and so should be protected with some substance such as vaseline during their application. The solutions containing either the acid or the salts are prepared from bleaching powder (see Chapter XIII).

Chloramine-T is a crystalline substance, soluble in water and having no odor. Heat and light cause no appreciable decomposition in it. Its germicidal effect is greater than that of the hypochlorites without so much irritation (see also Chapter XIII). 

A preparation called "chlorazene" or paratoluene sodium sulphochloramide may be bought in tablet form (4.6 grains of this compound and 1 grain of sodium chloride in a tablet). It is used in a 1% solution for surgical cases, 1 tablet being dissolved in 1 ounce of water. Chlorazene is said to have a phenol coefficient of 50 plus. It should not be used to disinfect metal instruments.

Dichloramine-T is a crystalline substance, yellowish white in color, with a pungent odor. If kept in the dark it is stable when it is not in solution. It is dissociated in an oil medium and in this form is used as a spray. The oil called chlorcosane, obtained by the chlorination of paraffin wax, is the best solvent. Dichloramine-T is used in a 5-8% solution in it. Other solvents for dichloramine-T are eucalyptol and paraffin. It has been found that blue bottles do not prevent its decomposition so it must be kept in amber bottles. 

Dyes Which Act as Disinfectants.-There are many dyes which act as disinfectants, among them being malachitegreen, hexamethyl-violet and brilliant green. The malachite-green is used with mercuric chloride, the two solutions being put together just before using. During the World

DISINFECTANTS 

387 War it was extensively used in the Navy as a skin disinfectant and also in treating fractures, burns and in osteomyelitis. Malachite-green belongs to the triphenylmethane dyes. 

Another dye is known by several names-acriflavine, trypaflavine, or flavine. Unlike most disinfectants, its action is increased when it is mixed with serum, but like the others its activity is diminished by pus. It is used in a 1 to 1000 solution and acts more slowly than do many other disinfectants. 

Organic Preparations.-Phenol, which is more commonly called carbolic acid because of its corrosive action, was the first disinfectant used. Its chief advantage lies in the fact that it is not injurious to either fabrics or metals. There are, however, other new preparations which are replacing it because of their greater desirability. Phenol is used as a standard for measuring their efficiency, the latter being expressed by a number which represents the number of times stronger the preparation is than the standard. 

Picric acid, for example, has a coefficient of 4-6 which is represented as P/C 4-6. It means that it has from 4-6 times the activity of the phenol. Crude carbolic acid, a dark brown liquid derived from coal-tar, is used for disinfecting feces and privies. Its action is due to cresylic acid contained in it. At least 2% must be present if it is effective. Pure phenol is a white crystalline solid. It can also be bought in a liquid form. The concentration of a stock solution is 5%. This should never be made up in alcohol as a solvent, for its activity is lessened by this compound. 

The use of alcohol for carbolic acid burns and poisoning has been referred to (see Chapter IX). The action of carbolic acid as a disinfectant is probably due to its ability to precipitate proteins. Micro-organisms are, therefore, rendered lifeless. Anthrax spores, however, are resistant to it and have been known to live after having been in a

5% solution for four days. Reference has already been made to the numbing effect of carbolic acid and to the consequent danger of its use in wet dressings (see Chapter IX). 

Because of this effect it is sometimes used to allay the itching resulting from certain skin affections. If it becomes absorbed, the amount of urine is increased and its color becomes a smoky green. It is never given internally except in a combined form such as salol (phenyl salicylate). This compound acts as an intestinal disinfectant because it is decomposed into phenol and salicylic acid. Phenol is also formed as a product of protein putrefaction (see Chapter 

XXIV). To protect itself against its toxic action, the liver combines it with sulphuric acid and phenyl sulphate is formed as a result. The presence of phenol can be detected by testing with either albumin or collodian, both of which are precipitated. Another reagent, ferric chloride, will cause the formation of an amythest solution.

A number of preparations used as disinfectants contain compounds which are related to phenol, as lysol, cresol, thymol and picric acid. Lysol, P/C 2.12 is commonly used in the hospital as a disinfectant. This is a preparation of cresol held in suspension in a soap solution. It is used in a 1-2% solution in place of phenol and in weaker solutions, usually 0.5%, as vaginal douches. In preparing the douche the lysol must be thoroughly mixed with the water to prevent burning the mucous membrane of the vagina. 

There are preparations in common use in various hospitals, like the compound solution of cresol, which has a coefficient of 3. Its uses are similar to those of lysol. Thymol, or propyl methyl phenol, which is derived from the oil of thyme, is used in a 0.1% solution in sponge cups and envelope moisteners. It is also used occasionally by dentists and is a specific against hookworm. Aristol, or di-iododithymol, is used as a dusting powder, and sometimes as a substitute for iodoform whose disagreeable odor it lacks.

DISINFECTANTS 

389 Picric acid or trinitrophenol (CH₂(NO2)3OH) is used in the treatment of burns. There are other substances which are better for the treatment of extensive wounds, for although it has a phenol coefficient of probably 4 to 6, it is a toxic chemical substance. The probable explanation given by Dakin and Dunham for its successful use in burns is that it precipitates protein, thus hardening the tissues. 

It is used in some hospitals to disinfect the skin before an operation because it is supposed to have greater penetrating power than many disinfectants. Almost all acids possess some disinfecting power, this power depending on the percentage of dissociation. It is doubtless the hydrogen ion which is responsible for this action. 

Formaldehyde (CH₂O) is a gas made by oxidizing wood alcohol (CH₂OH) (see Chapters XIX and XX). The trade name “formalin” had been given to a 40% solution of the gas in water. Formaldehyde is not a good insecticide but it is an excellent gas disinfectant for rooms, for it has more penetration than some of the others and does not ordinarily injure materials in the room. The temperature must be 

65° F. in order to obtain proper action, the doors and windows properly sealed and the room itself left undisturbed for twenty-four hours. It is very irritating to the eyes and all mucous membranes, causes an increase in the flow of saliva, violent coughing and expectoration. 

Inhaling ammonium hydroxide relieves the irritation. This reagent will also help to remove the unpleasant odor if sprinkled on the floor of the room after it has been opened.

The efficiency of formaldehyde is increased by the presence of moisture, which heightens its penetrating power, and by heat, which hastens the chemical action. For this reason it is customary to use potassium permanganate with it, which is now easier to obtain than it was during the war. 

The cyanosis which occurs in cases of poisoning by formal-

dehyde is caused by changes in the red blood cells as a result of which hematin appears in the blood. 

For disinfecting articles it is customary to buy the 40% formaldehyde and add ten times as much water. The excreta to be destroyed should be covered with an equal amount of this solution and allowed to stand two or three hours. Formalin will sterilize instruments when used in a 1 to 200 solution. A solution of 1 to 500 or 1 to 1000 is usually ordered as a douche. Because it is a preservative it has been used illegitimately in milk in some cases. In the laboratories a 4% solution is used as a preserving fluid for tissues which are to be used later. It is one of the constituents of embalming fluids. The solution should be kept in a dark bottle because the light brings about a chemical change with the formation of paraformaldehyde, which is a white powder.

Reference has already been made to the use of alcohol for disinfecting purposes (see Chapter XIX). A 60 or 70% solution is used in preference to a stronger one. The 95% solution is drying to the skin and causes it to crack, thus defeating the purpose for which it is used. The weaker solution also has greater power of penetration because of its higher percentage of water.

The green soap which is used in the operating room for scrubbing the skin is made of potash soap 10.2%, olive oil 0.8%, glycerine 1%, alcohol 43% and water 45%. It is not wise to depend on the ordinary medicated soaps as a disinfectant though they are often advertised as having great disinfecting properties. Rosenau says that the carbolic acid or bichloride of mercury which is used may combine chemically in such a way that the resulting product has even less disinfecting power than the ordinary soap. 

Camphor has a slight antiseptic action and taken internally checks the formation of gas. Camphor liniment is

DISINFECTANTS 

391 used to relieve pain and camphorated oil is given hypodermically as a heart stimulant. Soap liniment contains 

5% camphor. Turpentine or gasoline can be used as disinfectants in an emergency but both are irritating.

Strong hot salt water makes an excellent gargle, for being a hypertonic solution, it draws water from the organisms in an effort to equalize the concentration, thus interfering with their water content and making it impossible for them to survive. Any chemical action is increased by heat. 

QUESTIONS 

1. Distinguish between the terms disinfectant and antiseptic. 

2. Mercuric chloride is said to have a germicidal effect. Explain what is meant. 

3. Give two examples of each of the following kinds of compounds used because of their disinfectant action: Metallic salts, oxidizing agents, chlorine compounds, coal-tar derivatives. 

4. What conditions influence the activity of a disinfectant? 

5. What factors should be considered when selecting a disinfectant? 

6. What is the common name for mercuric chloride? How can its disinfectant action be accounted for? 

7. What precautions should be observed in the use of mercuric chloride? 

8. Mention three other disinfectants which contain mercury. 

Are they preferable to mercuric chloride? Give reasons for your answers. 

9. What is B. I. P.? 

10. Why has hydrogen peroxide been replaced by other disinfectants? 

11. Describe and explain the effect of potassium permanganate when it is used as a hand disinfectant. 

12. How can one tell if a solution of potassium permanganate has deteriorated? 

13. What is iodoform? How is its action explained? 

14. What is Dakin's solution? Explain its preparation-its action.

15. Mention two other disinfectants which contain chlorine. 

16. Mention three dyes used as disinfectants. 

17. Give the common name for phenol. Give a reason for the fact that it is being replaced by other preparations. 

18. Mention three coal-tar derivatives which are used as disinfectants. 

19. Give the chemical name for picric acid. Give a reason for its effectiveness in the treatment of burns. 

20. Distinguish between formaldehyde and formalin. Mention two uses made of it. Upon what characteristic does its action depend? 

21. What is green soap? 

22. Give a reason for the effectiveness of hot salt water as a gargle.
